Chapter 11 Personality Disorders, Conduct and Impulse-Control Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 11.1 What a Personality Disorder Is: define personality disorder, trace the idea from moral insanity to the ten DSM categories, and explain the general criteria, the clusters and what is known about causes.
  2. 11.2 Cluster A: Paranoid, Schizoid and Schizotypal Personality Disorders: describe the three odd or eccentric personality disorders, tell them apart from each other and from the psychoses, and explain their link to schizophrenia.
  3. 11.3 Borderline Personality Disorder I: The Clinical Picture: trace the history of the borderline concept and apply the nine criteria, and tell borderline personality disorder apart from the disorders it is confused with.
  4. 11.4 Borderline Personality Disorder II: Defences, Causes, Course and Treatment: explain splitting and the other primitive defences, the psychodynamic, biosocial, attachment and biological accounts, the course and the treatments.
  5. 11.5 Antisocial Personality Disorder and Psychopathy: apply the antisocial criteria, trace psychopathy from Pinel to Hare's checklist, and evaluate the biological and social explanations.
  6. 11.6 Histrionic and Narcissistic Personality Disorders: describe the histrionic and narcissistic patterns, compare the Cluster B disorders with one another, and contrast Kohut's and Kernberg's views of narcissism.
  7. 11.7 Cluster C: Avoidant, Dependent and Obsessive-Compulsive Personality Disorders: describe the three anxious or fearful personality disorders and tell obsessive-compulsive personality disorder apart from OCD.
  8. 11.8 The Newer Models: Dimensions, ICD-11 and the DSM-5 Alternative Model: explain the case for dimensions, the ICD-11 severity and trait model, and the DSM-5 Alternative Model for Personality Disorders.
  9. 11.9 Oppositional Defiant and Conduct Disorders: apply the criteria for oppositional defiant and conduct disorder, including the onset types and the limited prosocial emotions specifier, and explain their course and causes.
  10. 11.10 Impulse-Control Disorders: Intermittent Explosive Disorder, Pyromania and Kleptomania: describe the impulse-control disorders, their history and how the manuals classify them.

Weightage. 32 of the 411 questions mapped in this book (7.8%) test this chapter, 13 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 10 sections is High yield.

In 1954 a young sociologist named Lee Nelken Robins (1922 to 2009) joined the department of psychiatry at Washington University School of Medicine in St Louis as a research assistant. Born in New Orleans and trained at Radcliffe College and Harvard, where she took her doctorate in sociology in 1951, she soon saw an opportunity in a store of old paper. In the 1920s the St Louis Municipal Psychiatric Clinic, a child guidance clinic, had kept careful files on the children brought to it: a social history of each family, a medical examination, psychological tests and the reason for referral. Those records were about to be destroyed. Robins saw what they could become. If she could find those children, now adults in their thirties and forties, she could ask a question nobody had answered properly: what becomes of troubled children when they grow up?

She took every child the clinic had seen between 1 January 1924 and 30 December 1929 who was white, under 18 at referral and had an IQ of at least 80: 524 children in all. Most of them were boys; many had been brought for stealing, truancy, running away and other antisocial behaviour, others for quite different problems. For comparison she drew 100 control children from the records of St Louis public elementary schools, matched with the clinic children for year of birth, sex and the average rent of the neighbourhood they lived in, and chosen to have no record of school failure, expulsion or committal to a correctional institution. Then, about thirty years after the children's first visits, the hunt began. Robins and her team searched police files, army and school records, hospitals, welfare agencies, credit bureaus and coroners' records across the country. In the end 90 percent of the sample were found or known to have died, and interviews were obtained with 82 percent of those not known to have died before 25. Before designing the interview, Robins had set criteria for failing to conform to society's norms in nineteen areas of life, all but one of them (truancy) concerning behaviour after 18: among them work, marriage, drinking, drugs, arrests, fighting, financial dependency, lying, use of false names, vagrancy and lack of guilt. A person could be considered for a diagnosis of sociopathic personality, the name of the day for what we now call antisocial personality disorder, only if he or she had failed to conform to society's norms in at least five of those areas without any sign of psychosis; the typical person given the diagnosis had failed in eleven.

The results, published in 1966 as Deviant Children Grown Up: A Sociological and Psychiatric Study of Sociopathic Personality, were stark. Of the children referred to the clinic for antisocial behaviour, about one in four had become a sociopathic adult, about one in twelve had become an alcoholic or drug addict, and about one in nine was psychotic; only 16 percent had recovered by 18 and stayed free of psychiatric problems up to 40. Children referred for other reasons were more often well as adults and rarely sociopathic, and among the control children 60 percent were well and only 2 percent sociopathic. Two further findings have been quoted ever since. Not one person diagnosed as a sociopathic adult had reached adulthood without marked antisocial behaviour in childhood. Yet fewer than half of even the most antisocial children became sociopathic adults. The childhood troubles most common among those who did were theft, incorrigibility, running away from home, truancy, keeping company with delinquent children, staying out late and discipline problems at school, but no single act predicted the outcome. The best predictor, better than the family's poverty or social class, was the number and variety of a child's antisocial behaviours. Robins later summed it up in a 1978 review of four longitudinal studies: "adult antisocial behaviour virtually requires childhood antisocial behaviour", and yet most antisocial children do not become antisocial adults. (She later checked whether the pattern held beyond her white sample by following Black men born and raised in St Louis in the early 1930s; the findings were remarkably similar.)

Two lessons from the St Louis children run through this chapter. The first is that a personality disorder is not an episode, like a bout of depression, but a way of living that starts early and lasts. The second is concrete: when the American Psychiatric Association wrote criteria for antisocial personality disorder in DSM-III in 1980, Robins's work shaped them, and the rule you will meet in Section 11.5, that the adult diagnosis needs evidence of conduct disorder beginning before age 15, is its direct descendant. (Robins went on to other landmark studies, among them her follow-up of soldiers returning from Vietnam, told in Section 10.5.6.) This chapter begins with what a personality disorder is and how the ten types are grouped, studies the odd, the dramatic and the anxious clusters in turn, meets the newer dimensional models, and ends with the disorders of childhood conduct and of impulse control, where Robins's deviant children began. Remember the St Louis children; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

11.1 What a Personality Disorder IsMedium yield · 5 Qs

Every other chapter of this book describes conditions that arrive. A depression descends in the winter of a bad year and lifts by the monsoon; a panic attack strikes in a crowded bus; a psychosis breaks out in the second year of college. Before the illness there was a person who was, in some sense, well, and treatment aims to bring that person back. The disorders in the first half of this chapter are different. They do not arrive; they were always there. They describe how a person habitually sees the world, feels, relates to others and controls (or fails to control) impulses, year after year and in every setting. There is no "before" to return to, because the disorder is woven into what the person and everyone around them would call his or her character. That simple fact explains almost everything that is difficult about personality disorders: how they are diagnosed, why the people who have them often do not think anything is wrong, why the diagnosis has been so contested, and why it is so easily misused.

Personality, traits and the point where they become a disorder

Start with personality itself. Your personality is the set of characteristic and relatively enduring ways in which you think, feel, relate to others and behave, the patterns that make you recognisably yourself from one year to the next and from one situation to another. Its building blocks are personality traits, which DSM-5-TR defines as enduring patterns of perceiving, relating to and thinking about the environment and oneself that show up across a wide range of social and personal contexts: being suspicious or trusting, orderly or careless, shy or outgoing, calm or easily stirred. How personality is organised, and the great theories of it (Freud's structural model, the trait theories, the Big Five), are the subject of the Personality book; this chapter needs only the idea of a trait as a lasting tendency. The idea is very old. Theophrastus, a pupil of Aristotle, wrote a little book called Characters, thirty short sketches of recognisable types such as the flatterer, the boor and the distrustful man who sends one servant to the market and a second to check on the first. Readers have laughed at them for more than two thousand years because everyone knows such people.

Nobody is free of awkward traits. Most of us are suspicious sometimes, sulk sometimes, cling sometimes, insist on doing things our own way sometimes. A trait becomes the stuff of a disorder only when it stops bending. DSM-5-TR puts it precisely: personality traits constitute a personality disorder only when they are inflexible and maladaptive and cause significant functional impairment or subjective distress. The healthy person who is naturally cautious can still take a risk when the situation calls for it; the person with a disorder applies the same rigid strategy to every situation, including the ones where it fails, and keeps applying it after it has failed many times. A personality disorder, then, is a pattern of inner experience and behaviour that is enduring, pervasive and inflexible, deviates markedly from what the person's culture expects, begins by adolescence or early adulthood, and leads to distress or impairment. The next subsection takes that definition apart clause by clause; first, look at what it means in a life.

Vivek is 31 and works as a software engineer in Pune. He has come to the clinic because his wife has said she will go back to her parents' home if he does not. He is articulate and polite with the psychologist, and he explains the problem clearly: other people. His manager steals credit for his work. Two previous employers were "politically motivated" against him and he resigned from both. His brother, he believes, persuaded their father to leave the family flat to him. His wife's friendliness with a colleague is "not innocent". He keeps a folder of screenshots of messages he thinks prove these things. His wife, interviewed separately, says he has been like this as long as she has known him; his mother, on the telephone, says he was the boy at school who never forgave a slight. Notice two things about Vivek. First, the same pattern appears with his boss, his brother, his wife and his schoolmates: it is not a reaction to one bad situation. Second, Vivek does not experience his suspiciousness as a symptom. To him it is simply accuracy; the problem is that the world keeps proving him right. (Which personality disorder fits him best is the business of Section 11.2.)

That second feature has a name. A symptom that the person experiences as alien, unwanted and at odds with the kind of person he or she wants to be is ego-dystonic: the woman with obsessive-compulsive disorder who cannot stop checking the gas knob knows the checking is senseless and hates it. A trait that feels natural, justified and part of the self is ego-syntonic. DSM-5-TR notes that the characteristics that define a personality disorder are often ego-syntonic and may not be considered problematic by the person at all. Psychiatric textbooks add a second, older pair of terms. A person who deals with difficulty by changing himself (worrying, blaming himself, feeling anxious and seeking help) is said to be autoplastic; a person who deals with it by trying to change the world and the people in it (arguing, demanding, manipulating, leaving) is alloplastic. The neuroses are classically autoplastic; personality disorders are classically alloplastic and ego-syntonic.

These features have practical consequences that run through the rest of the chapter. People with personality disorders seldom refer themselves for their personality. They arrive because someone else insists (a spouse, a parent, an employer, a court), or because the consequences of the pattern have produced a depression, a drinking problem, a crisis of self-harm or a lost job, and it is that consequence they want treated. Because the person may not see the pattern, the clinician cannot rely on self-report alone; DSM-5-TR recommends information from other informants and, often, more than one interview spaced over time. In India, where families usually accompany patients to the clinic, the family history is often the richest source of evidence about personality, though families have their own biases and the clinician must weigh them.

Exam trap

Personality disorder traits are typically ego-syntonic (felt as part of the self, "this is just how I am") and alloplastic (the person tries to change the environment rather than himself). Obsessions in OCD are ego-dystonic (felt as alien and resisted). This contrast is the key to the OCPD versus OCD question taught in Section 11.7. Do not reverse the pair: "ego-dystonic with good insight" describes OCD, not a personality disorder. Note too that ego-syntonic does not mean "causes no distress": people with personality disorders are often very distressed, but by the consequences of the pattern (the conflicts, the losses, the loneliness) rather than by the traits themselves.

The general definition, clause by clause

DSM-5-TR opens its chapter on personality disorders with a set of general criteria for personality disorder that every one of the ten specific disorders must also satisfy. They are worth learning thoroughly, because each clause exists to rule something out, and examiners test the clauses rather than the specific disorders surprisingly often. There are six of them, lettered A to F, and Figure 11.1 shows them as a series of gates that a clinician passes through in turn.

Criterion A: an enduring pattern that deviates markedly from the expectations of the person's culture, shown in two or more of four areas. The four areas are the heart of the definition.

  • Cognition: the ways a person perceives and interprets the self, other people and events. Vivek reads a colleague's smile as mockery and a manager's correction as a plot; another person sees himself as uniquely gifted, another as hopelessly inept.
  • Affectivity: the range, intensity, lability (changeability) and appropriateness of emotional response. One person's emotions are flat and cold; another's swing from adoration to rage within an afternoon; another laughs at the wrong moments.
  • Interpersonal functioning: how the person relates to others. Some cling, some keep everyone at a distance, some exploit, some submit.
  • Impulse control: the capacity to delay acting on urges. Some people spend, drive, drink or strike out without a pause; others are so over-controlled that they cannot act at all.

Two features of this criterion need emphasis. The first is that a personality disorder is not a disorder of any one function. It can distort thinking, emotion, relationships and self-control, and usually it touches several at once; the minimum is two of the four. The second is the phrase "deviates markedly from the expectations of the individual's culture". The yardstick is not what a psychiatrist in New York or Delhi finds normal, but what the person's own cultural group expects, a point Section 11.1.8 returns to.

Criterion B: the pattern is inflexible and pervasive across a broad range of personal and social situations. This clause rules out the reaction to a single relationship or setting. A junior doctor who is guarded and suspicious only towards a senior who has, in fact, been humiliating her has no personality disorder; a man who is guarded and suspicious towards his employer, his neighbours, his family and his doctor may have one. Inflexible means that the person keeps using the same strategy when it plainly is not working.

Criterion C: the pattern leads to clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. This is the clinical significance threshold found throughout DSM (Chapter 1). An eccentric professor who is cold and solitary but productive and content, and whose solitude troubles nobody, has a personality style, not a disorder. Remember that the distress may be felt mostly by others: Vivek's wife is suffering more than he is, but his impairment (three lost jobs, a failing marriage) is real.

Criterion D: the pattern is stable and of long duration, and its onset can be traced back at least to adolescence or early adulthood. This is the clause that makes a personality disorder a personality disorder rather than an illness that comes and goes. The clinician must be able to find the pattern in the person's history, which is why the opening story of this chapter matters so much: Robins showed that the adult antisocial pattern can be traced back into childhood. A "personality" that first appears at 45 is not a personality disorder, and DSM-5-TR warns that a change in personality in middle adulthood or later life calls for a thorough search for a medical condition or an unrecognised substance use disorder. A long-standing personality disorder can, however, come to clinical attention late, and it may worsen when the person loses the people or circumstances that had held it in check, such as a spouse who managed everything or a job with firm structure.

Criterion E: the pattern is not better explained as a manifestation or consequence of another mental disorder. Many symptoms of other disorders look like personality traits while they last. Meher, a 38-year-old schoolteacher from Lucknow, is admitted with a severe depression. On the ward she is clinging, asks the nurses to decide everything for her, cannot make the smallest choice and panics when her husband leaves after visiting hours. A trainee who saw only this would think of dependent personality disorder. But her husband and her headmistress describe a capable, independent woman who has run a household and a classroom for fifteen years, and three months later, with her depression treated, she is that woman again. Her dependency was a state, produced by the depression, not a trait. This is why DSM-5-TR asks clinicians to be cautious about diagnosing a personality disorder during an episode of depression or anxiety: the episode colours the whole picture, including the person's own account of what he or she "has always been like". The same logic applies to psychosis. For the three disorders that resemble psychotic illness (paranoid, schizoid and schizotypal) the specific criteria add that the pattern must not occur only during schizophrenia, a mood disorder with psychotic features or another psychotic disorder. If the personality disorder clearly existed before a later schizophrenia began, it is still recorded, with the word "premorbid" added: "paranoid personality disorder (premorbid)".

Criterion F: the pattern is not attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (a drug of abuse, a medication) or another medical condition such as head trauma. Zubair was a gentle, courteous mathematics teacher in Hyderabad until, at 52, his scooter was hit by a truck and he suffered a severe injury to the front of his brain. A year later he is a different man. He makes crude jokes to his daughters' friends, spends his savings on impulse, loses his temper over trifles and then seems not to care, and sits for hours doing nothing unless he is told to move. His pattern is enduring, pervasive and impairing, but it is not a personality disorder, because it is a change from a previous personality and it was caused by brain damage. DSM-5-TR has a separate diagnosis for this, personality change due to another medical condition: a persistent personality disturbance, representing a change from the person's previous characteristic pattern, that is the direct physiological consequence of a medical condition such as a frontal lobe lesion, a brain tumour, epilepsy or a stroke. It is specified by its dominant feature as labile, disinhibited, aggressive, apathetic, paranoid, other, combined or unspecified type; Zubair would be "combined type". ICD-10 called the same condition organic personality disorder (F07.0). The frontal lobe syndromes behind it, and the famous case of Phineas Gage, are described in Section 10.11.10. In the same way, the suspicious, irritable and deceitful behaviour of someone in the grip of a substance use disorder should not be counted as personality: DSM-5-TR warns against diagnosing a personality disorder from behaviour that is only a consequence of intoxication, withdrawal or the hunt for the drug.

Why must the pattern begin by adolescence or early adulthood, and why do the manuals hesitate to diagnose a personality disorder in a teenager? Because personality is still being built in childhood and adolescence, and much of what looks like a fixed trait at 14 is a phase. The moody, defiant, attention-hungry or withdrawn adolescent is common; the adult who stays that way is not. DSM-5-TR states that the traits of a personality disorder that appear in childhood will often not persist unchanged into adult life. It therefore allows personality disorder categories to be used for children and adolescents only in relatively unusual cases, where the maladaptive traits are pervasive, persistent and unlikely to be limited to a developmental stage or caused by another disorder, and it adds a time rule: for a personality disorder to be diagnosed in someone younger than 18, the features must have been present for at least 1 year. There is one exception. Antisocial personality disorder cannot be diagnosed in anyone younger than 18 at all. A 16-year-old who steals, lies, fights and has no remorse is given the diagnosis of conduct disorder (Section 11.9), and only if the pattern persists past 18 can the adult diagnosis be made, and then only if there is evidence that conduct disorder began before 15 (Section 11.5). This is the St Louis finding written into the rules.

The criteria also say something about the end of the story. "Stable" does not mean "unchangeable". DSM-5-TR notes that some personality disorders, notably antisocial and borderline personality disorder, tend to become less evident or to remit with age, while others, such as obsessive-compulsive and schizotypal personality disorder, seem to change less. The long-term studies behind that statement, which overturned the old belief that personality disorders never improve, are described in Sections 11.4 and 11.8.

Exam trap

A question asks: "Personality disorders are stable long-term conditions which can affect an individual's (a) cognition, (b) mood, (c) interpersonal functioning, (d) all of the above." The answer is (d) all of the above. The general definition lists four areas, cognition, affectivity (mood and emotion), interpersonal functioning and impulse control, and requires the pattern to show in two or more of them. Students who think of personality disorders as "relationship problems" pick (c); students who forget the fourth area miss impulse control when it appears as an option. Other favourite traps: onset is by adolescence or early adulthood (not "after 40", not "in childhood only"); under 18 the features must have lasted at least 1 year; antisocial personality disorder is never diagnosed before 18; and a personality change after a head injury is personality change due to another medical condition, not a personality disorder.

From mania without delusion to Axis II: a short history

The idea that a person can be disordered in conduct and feeling while perfectly sound in reasoning is about two centuries old. In 1801 Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826) described manie sans délire, "mania without delusion", in patients who flew into violent rages although their understanding, perception and judgement were intact. In 1835 the English physician James Cowles Prichard (1786 to 1848) widened the idea into moral insanity, a disorder of the feelings, temper, habits and moral dispositions without any defect of the intellect and without delusions. ("Moral" here meant emotional and social, not ethical.) Both stories, and the long road from them to the modern psychopath, are told in full in Section 11.5. What matters here is what happened next, because the general concept of personality disorder grew out of them.

The next step was taken in Germany. Julius Ludwig August Koch (1841 to 1908), a German asylum psychiatrist, introduced the term psychopathic inferiority in his short textbook of psychiatry in 1888 and set it out at length in Die psychopathischen Minderwertigkeiten (The Psychopathic Inferiorities), published from 1891 to 1893. He used it for people whose conduct was persistently abnormal although they had neither a mental illness with delusions or hallucinations nor an intellectual disability, and he thought the cause was constitutional, an inborn weakness. Notice the word "psychopathic". In German psychiatry of the time it meant, literally, "suffering in the mind", and covered every kind of abnormal personality; only much later did "psychopath" narrow to the cold, remorseless offender of Section 11.5.

Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926), whose classification of the psychoses you met in Chapters 1 and 4, brought these conditions into his textbook as psychopathic personalities. He saw them as lifelong, constitutional deviations from the normal range, and he stressed that the line between the normal and the pathological personality is gradual and arbitrary, an insight the dimensional models of Section 11.8 would rediscover. His seventh edition listed four types, among them the "born criminal" borrowed from the Italian criminologist Cesare Lombroso; the eighth edition (1915) listed seven: the excitable, the unstable or weak-willed, the driven (impulsive pleasure seekers and periodic drinkers), the eccentric, pathological liars and swindlers, the enemies of society, and the quarrelsome. Most of Kraepelin's types were defined by the trouble they caused, and many of the patients behind them had come to psychiatry after trouble with the law.

The most influential of the German classifications came from Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967), the same Schneider whose first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia appear in Chapter 4. In Die psychopathischen Persönlichkeiten (Psychopathic Personalities), first published in 1923, he began from a statistical idea: an abnormal personality is simply one that deviates from an average range. Not every abnormal personality is a clinical problem (a saint and a genius are abnormal too), so he defined psychopathic personalities as those abnormal personalities who suffer from their abnormality or whose abnormality makes society suffer. That two-sided test, suffering for the person or suffering for others, is an ancestor of Criterion C's "distress or impairment". Schneider insisted that these were variations of personality, not illnesses, and he described ten types: the hyperthymic, the depressive, the insecure (with sensitive and anankastic, that is obsessional, forms), the fanatical, the attention-seeking, the labile, the explosive, the affectionless, the weak-willed and the asthenic. Several live on: his anankastic type in ICD-10's anankastic (obsessive-compulsive) personality disorder, his labile and explosive types in ICD-10's emotionally unstable personality disorder, his affectionless type in the psychopath. Schneider also made a remark that sounds strikingly modern: he doubted that clinically useful abnormal types could simply be read off the extreme ends of normal personality dimensions, which is exactly the question at the centre of the debate in Section 11.8.

Psychoanalysis approached the same territory from the inside. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), in "Character and Anal Erotism" (1908), described people who are especially orderly, parsimonious and obstinate and traced the trio to the toddler's struggles over toilet training; this anal character is the ancestor of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (Section 11.7). Karl Abraham (1877 to 1925) added an oral character, and Wilhelm Reich (1897 to 1957), in Character Analysis (1933), argued that a person's whole character can work as character armour, a rigid, habitual shell of attitudes that protects against anxiety and that the patient experiences not as a symptom but as himself. Reich's point, that character defences are ego-syntonic and must first be made to feel foreign before they can change, is the psychoanalytic root of the clinical observation in Section 11.1.1. The later psychoanalytic ideas about personality organisation, especially Otto Kernberg's, belong to Sections 11.3 and 11.4.

The American manuals then turned these ideas into categories. DSM-I (1952) had a large class of "personality disorders" whose three main groups were personality pattern disturbances (inadequate, schizoid, cyclothymic and paranoid personality), personality trait disturbances (emotionally unstable, passive-aggressive and compulsive personality), and sociopathic personality disturbance, which lumped together antisocial and dyssocial reactions, sexual deviations (homosexuality among them, a story told in Section 9.10) and addiction. How loosely the class was drawn shows in a further group of "special symptom reactions" filed under the same heading: learning and speech disturbances, enuresis and sleepwalking. DSM-II (1968) listed ten personality disorders (paranoid, cyclothymic, schizoid, explosive, obsessive compulsive, hysterical, asthenic, antisocial, passive-aggressive and inadequate), each described in a short paragraph of prose that clinicians interpreted as they pleased.

The revolution came with DSM-III (1980), whose wider story is told in Section 1.7.3. For personality it did three things. First, it gave every personality disorder explicit diagnostic criteria; the criteria for antisocial personality disorder were deliberately behavioural (arrests, lying, fights, failure to work) because behaviours could be rated reliably, and they built in the childhood onset that Robins had documented. Second, it placed the personality disorders on a separate Axis II of its multiaxial system, together with the specific developmental disorders of childhood (such as developmental reading disorder); mental retardation joined them there in DSM-III-R (1987), and in DSM-IV (1994) Axis II held the personality disorders and mental retardation. The reason, explained in Section 1.7.5, was that personality disorders are lifelong background conditions that clinicians would overlook whenever a florid Axis I disorder, such as a psychotic episode or a severe depression, held their attention. Third, it grouped the disorders into three clusters. DSM-III's list was new as well as old: schizotypal, borderline, narcissistic, avoidant and dependent personality disorder appeared as disorders in their own right, hysterical personality was renamed histrionic, and cyclothymic, explosive, asthenic and inadequate personality left the list (cyclothymia moved to the mood disorders). DSM-III had eleven personality disorders, including passive-aggressive; DSM-IV (1994) moved passive-aggressive personality disorder to its appendix, leaving the ten that are still in use.

Axis II was a great success in one way and a problem in another. It made clinicians record personality, and research on personality disorders multiplied. But it also implied that personality disorders were a different kind of thing from other mental disorders, fixed and untreatable, which research did not support. DSM-5 (2013) therefore abolished the axes (Section 1.7.6): personality disorders are now listed with all other diagnoses. It kept the same ten disorders, with criteria carried over almost word for word from DSM-IV, in its main Section II, and it placed a quite different hybrid of dimensions and types, the Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders, in Section III for further study. DSM-5-TR (2022) left both unchanged. ICD-11, in force from 2022, went further and abolished the types altogether in favour of a single diagnosis graded by severity. Both new models are the subject of Section 11.8. Figure 11.2 lays out the whole history.

Exam trap

Manie sans délire is Pinel (1801); moral insanity is Prichard (1835); psychopathic inferiority is Koch (the term in 1888, the full account in 1891 to 1893); the definition "abnormal personalities who suffer, or cause society to suffer" is Kurt Schneider (1923), the same Schneider as the first-rank symptoms; character armour is Wilhelm Reich. In these early texts "psychopathic" meant any abnormal personality, not only today's psychopath. The multiaxial system and Axis II arrived with DSM-III in 1980 (not DSM-IV), and DSM-5 (2013) removed the axes. DSM-I's umbrella term was "sociopathic personality disturbance"; "antisocial personality" as a named disorder dates from DSM-II.

The ten disorders in three clusters

DSM-5-TR describes ten specific personality disorders. Each has its own set of criteria, and each set has the same shape: a stem sentence naming the core pattern, "beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts" (a compressed restatement of the general criteria; for antisocial personality disorder the stem says "occurring since age 15 years" instead), followed by a list of features of which a stated number must be present. These are polythetic criteria of the "any five of these nine" kind explained in Section 1.6.4, and they have an important consequence: two people with the same personality disorder diagnosis may share only a few of its features. Table 11.1 lists the ten, their core patterns and the number of criteria each requires. Learn the table thoroughly; it is the map for Sections 11.2 to 11.7.

Table 11.1 The ten DSM-5-TR personality disorders, their clusters, core patterns and diagnostic thresholds.
ClusterDisorderCore patternCriteria needed
A: odd or eccentricParanoidPervasive distrust and suspiciousness; others' motives read as malevolent4 of 7
ASchizoidDetachment from social relationships; restricted range of emotional expression4 of 7
ASchizotypalAcute discomfort in close relationships; cognitive or perceptual distortions; eccentric behaviour5 of 9
B: dramatic, emotional or erraticAntisocialDisregard for and violation of the rights of others since age 15 (age at least 18; conduct disorder before 15)3 of 7
BBorderlineInstability of relationships, self-image and affects; marked impulsivity5 of 9
BHistrionicExcessive emotionality and attention seeking5 of 8
BNarcissisticGrandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy5 of 9
C: anxious or fearfulAvoidantSocial inhibition, feelings of inadequacy, hypersensitivity to negative evaluation4 of 7
CDependentExcessive need to be taken care of; submissive, clinging behaviour; fear of separation5 of 8
CObsessive-compulsivePreoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and control, at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency4 of 8

The thresholds look like a list of random numbers, but there is a pattern that makes them easy to hold. Four disorders have seven criteria: paranoid, schizoid and avoidant need four of them, and antisocial personality disorder needs only three, though it adds two further conditions of its own (the age of 18 and conduct disorder before 15). The three disorders with nine criteria (schizotypal, borderline and narcissistic) each need five. Of the three with eight criteria, histrionic and dependent need five and obsessive-compulsive needs four.

DSM-5-TR groups the ten into three personality disorder clusters on the basis of descriptive similarity, an arrangement introduced by DSM-III. A popular memory aid is "weird, wild and worried".

  • Cluster A, the odd or eccentric cluster: paranoid, schizoid and schizotypal personality disorder. What they share is social detachment and, in two of the three, odd or suspicious thinking; they look like faint, non-psychotic echoes of schizophrenia, and schizotypal personality disorder in particular runs in the families of people with schizophrenia. DSM-5-TR lists schizotypal personality disorder twice, in this chapter and in the schizophrenia spectrum chapter (Section 4.5.8).
  • Cluster B, the dramatic, emotional or erratic cluster: antisocial, borderline, histrionic and narcissistic personality disorder. What they share is intense or shallow emotion that is poorly regulated, impulsivity, and relationships marked by conflict, manipulation or a hunger for attention, admiration or rescue. These are the disorders that bring people into contact with emergency departments, courts and family crises, and they take up four of the remaining sections of this chapter.
  • Cluster C, the anxious or fearful cluster: avoidant, dependent and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. What they share is chronic anxiety and the strategies people use to manage it: avoiding the people who might reject them, clinging to the people who might protect them, or controlling every detail so that nothing can go wrong.

Beyond the ten, DSM-5-TR has three further categories. Personality change due to another medical condition was met in Section 11.1.2. Other specified personality disorder is used in two situations: when a person meets the general criteria and has traits of several personality disorders without meeting the criteria for any one of them (a "mixed" picture), or when the person has a personality disorder that DSM does not include, such as passive-aggressive personality disorder; the clinician records the specific reason. Unspecified personality disorder is used when there are clearly features of a personality disorder but not enough information to be more specific, for example in an emergency department. These categories replaced DSM-IV's "personality disorder not otherwise specified", which was, embarrassingly for a system of ten carefully defined types, one of the most frequently used personality diagnoses of all (Section 11.8 explains why).

How good are the clusters? DSM-5-TR is unusually candid: the clustering system, it says, is useful in some research and teaching situations but has serious limitations and has not been consistently validated, because disorders from different clusters, or traits from several of them, often occur together in the same person. Genetic research makes the same point. In 2008 Kenneth S. Kendler and his colleagues, including the Norwegian psychologist Svenn Torgersen, interviewed 2,794 young adult twins from the Norwegian Institute of Public Health Twin Panel and modelled the genetic and environmental sources of all ten DSM-IV personality disorders together. The genetic risk factors did not follow the A, B and C typology at all. One broad genetic factor loaded on disorders from all three clusters (paranoid, histrionic, borderline, narcissistic, dependent and obsessive-compulsive), apparently reflecting a general vulnerability to personality pathology or negative emotion; a second loaded mainly on borderline and antisocial personality disorder (high impulsivity and low agreeableness); and a third on schizoid and avoidant personality disorder (introversion). Unexpectedly, the person-specific environmental factors did follow the clusters, with one exception: obsessive-compulsive personality disorder grouped with Cluster B rather than Cluster C. DSM-5-TR itself suggests that the clusters may also be viewed as dimensions, spectra of personality dysfunction on a continuum with other mental disorders, and it gives two examples of such a spectrum relationship: schizotypal personality disorder with schizophrenia, and avoidant personality disorder with social anxiety disorder.

Exam trap

There are only three clusters: A, B and C. There is no Cluster D, though it appears as an option every time. The cluster described as dramatic, emotional or erratic is Cluster B (antisocial, borderline, histrionic, narcissistic); A is odd or eccentric, C is anxious or fearful. Two favourite "which does not belong" items: among paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal and borderline personality disorder, the odd one out is borderline (Cluster B; the other three are Cluster A); and the personality disorder that is not Cluster A among paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal and avoidant is avoidant (Cluster C). Both are taught in full in Section 11.2. Finally, the thresholds: antisocial needs only 3 of 7, borderline 5 of 9.

The ICD-10 names, and how they map onto DSM

Indian hospitals and Indian question papers use both manuals, and ICD-10 gives several of the same disorders different names. Its block F60 to F69 is headed "disorders of adult personality and behaviour". Its general description is close to DSM's: deeply ingrained and enduring patterns of behaviour, shown as inflexible responses to a broad range of personal and social situations, that represent extreme or significant deviations from the way the average person in a given culture perceives, thinks, feels and relates to others, usually involving several areas of functioning (affectivity, arousal, impulse control, ways of perceiving and thinking, and style of relating to others), beginning in childhood or adolescence and continuing into adulthood, and usually associated with personal distress and problems in social and occupational performance. For most of the specific types ICD-10 usually asks for clear evidence of at least three of the traits in its description. Table 11.2 matches the ICD-10 categories with their DSM-5-TR counterparts.

Table 11.2 ICD-10 personality disorder categories and their DSM-5-TR counterparts.
ICD-10 codeICD-10 nameDSM-5-TR counterpartNote
F60.0Paranoid personality disorderParanoidSame concept
F60.1Schizoid personality disorderSchizoidSame concept
F21Schizotypal disorderSchizotypalICD-10 places it with schizophrenia, not with the personality disorders
F60.2Dissocial personality disorderAntisocialCallousness, irresponsibility, low frustration tolerance, no guilt
F60.30Emotionally unstable, impulsive typeNo direct counterpartEmotional instability and outbursts with poor impulse control
F60.31Emotionally unstable, borderline typeBorderlineAdds unclear self-image, emptiness, self-harm, fear of abandonment
F60.4Histrionic personality disorderHistrionicSame concept
F60.5Anankastic personality disorderObsessive-compulsive"Anankastic" comes from the Greek for compulsion
F60.6Anxious (avoidant) personality disorderAvoidantSame concept
F60.7Dependent personality disorderDependentSame concept
F60.8Other specific personality disordersNarcissistic (a full category in DSM)Also eccentric, haltlose, immature, passive-aggressive, psychoneurotic
F60.9Personality disorder, unspecifiedUnspecifiedNot enough information
F61Mixed and other personality disordersOther specifiedFeatures of several types
F62Enduring personality changes, not attributable to brain damage and diseaseNo counterpartAfter catastrophic experience (F62.0) or psychiatric illness (F62.1)
F07.0Organic personality disorderPersonality change due to another medical conditionCaused by brain disease or injury

Four differences deserve a sentence each. First, ICD-10 never treated schizotypal disorder as a personality disorder: it sits at F21 in the schizophrenia block, a choice ICD-11 keeps (Section 11.2). Second, ICD-10 splits borderline personality into an emotionally unstable personality disorder with two types: the impulsive type, marked by emotional instability and outbursts of violence or threatening behaviour, especially when criticised, and the borderline type, which adds a disturbed and unclear self-image, chronic feelings of emptiness, intense and unstable relationships, frantic efforts to avoid abandonment and repeated threats or acts of self-harm (Section 11.3). Third, ICD-10 has no separate narcissistic personality disorder; narcissistic personality is one of several types tucked into F60.8. Fourth, ICD-10 has a category with no DSM counterpart, enduring personality change after catastrophic experience (F62.0), for the lasting distrust, withdrawal, emptiness and sense of threat seen in some survivors of torture, concentration camps, prolonged captivity or disaster, whose personality was previously normal. ICD-11 dropped it, and much of its ground is now covered by complex post-traumatic stress disorder (Chapter 7).

Exam trap

Translate the ICD-10 names: dissocial = antisocial, emotionally unstable (borderline type) = borderline, anankastic = obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, anxious (avoidant) = avoidant. "Dissocial" has nothing to do with dissociation. Schizotypal is F21, classed with schizophrenia in ICD-10, not under F60. Narcissistic personality disorder has no code of its own in ICD-10. And "organic personality disorder" (F07.0) is the ICD-10 name for what DSM-5-TR calls personality change due to another medical condition.

How common they are, how reliably they are diagnosed, and what they do to other disorders

Counting personality disorders in the community is harder than counting most disorders. A depressive episode can be identified from a few weeks of symptoms; a personality disorder needs a picture of the whole adult life, usually from a long structured interview, and people describe their own personalities with a good deal of bias. Estimates therefore vary with the instrument and the design, but they agree on one conclusion: personality disorders are common. DSM-5-TR, reviewing epidemiological studies from several countries, gives a median prevalence of 10.5 percent for any personality disorder (3.6 percent for Cluster A, 4.5 percent for Cluster B and 2.8 percent for Cluster C), the source of the round figure, about one adult in ten, that textbooks and question papers quote. Large international studies have produced somewhat lower figures. Table 11.3 gathers the main numbers.

Table 11.3 How common personality disorders are in different populations.
StudyPopulationAny personality disorderDetail
DSM-5-TR reviewCommunity studies, several countries10.5% (median)Cluster A 3.6%, B 4.5%, C 2.8%
WHO World Mental Health Surveys (Huang and colleagues, 2009)21,162 adults in 13 countries6.1%Cluster A 3.6%, B 1.5%, C 2.7%
Winsper and colleagues (2020), meta-analysis46 community studies, 21 countries7.8%9.6% in high-income versus 4.3% in low- and middle-income countries
Zimmerman and colleagues (2005)859 psychiatric outpatients31.4% (45.5% counting NOS)Most had more than one personality disorder
Fazel and Danesh (2002), review of 62 surveys22,790 prisoners65% of men, 42% of womenAntisocial: 47% of men, 21% of women

Three things stand out. First, the rates climb steeply as you move from the community into clinics and prisons. In the Rhode Island study of Mark Zimmerman and his colleagues, in which 859 psychiatric outpatients were all given a semi-structured interview for personality disorders when they first came for treatment, almost a third had one of the ten specific disorders, and almost half had some personality disorder once the "not otherwise specified" cases were counted; avoidant, borderline and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder were the commonest. In the systematic review of prison surveys by Seena Fazel and John Danesh, about two in three male prisoners had a personality disorder and nearly half had antisocial personality disorder, about ten times the rate in the general population. Second, the community rates differ between countries: the 2020 meta-analysis led by Catherine Winsper found nearly twice the prevalence in high-income countries as in low- and middle-income countries. That may reflect real differences, or the use of instruments written in and normed for Western populations, or both; the authors found that study design (one-stage or two-stage assessment) was a significant source of the variation between studies. Third, even the relative sizes of the clusters change from study to study (Cluster B is the largest in DSM-5-TR's review and the smallest in the World Mental Health Surveys), a reminder of how much the figures depend on method.

The Zimmerman study also illustrates the problem of comorbidity among the personality disorders themselves: most outpatients who met the criteria for one specific personality disorder met the criteria for at least one more. A person with borderline personality disorder very often also meets the criteria for another Cluster B disorder, and frequently for avoidant or dependent personality disorder too. If ten disorders were really ten separate conditions, such heavy overlap would be strange; it is one of the main arguments for the dimensional models of Section 11.8.

Reliability, the extent to which two clinicians agree on a diagnosis (Section 1.6.6), was historically poor for personality disorders. In ordinary clinical practice, without a structured interview, clinicians tend to notice the one disorder that catches their attention and miss the others, and their agreement about which specific personality disorder a patient has is low. DSM-III's explicit criteria helped, and agreement improves considerably when clinicians use semi-structured interviews such as the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-5 Personality Disorders (SCID-5-PD) or the International Personality Disorder Examination (IPDE), or self-report inventories such as the Millon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory (MCMI) and the Personality Inventory for DSM-5 (PID-5); these instruments are described in the Assessment book. Even so, interviews and questionnaires often disagree with each other, and the stability of the diagnosis over time turned out to be lower than the definition assumes (Section 11.8).

Why does any of this matter to a clinician who is treating someone for something else? Because personality disorders change the course of almost every other disorder they accompany. Personality disorders co-occur heavily with depression, anxiety disorders, substance use disorders and eating disorders. In 2006 Newton-Howes, Tyrer and Johnson pooled the published studies of depressed patients who had or had not a comorbid personality disorder and found that a personality disorder roughly doubled the odds of a poor outcome for the depression (odds ratio 2.18), whichever treatment was used, except perhaps electroconvulsive therapy, for which the group was small. Personality pathology also raises the risk of suicide and self-harm (Section 11.3; Chapter 5) and complicates the treatment of substance use (Chapter 10).

Clinical link

Ask about personality in every assessment, but do it at the right time and with the right informants. A patient in the middle of a depressive episode will describe a lifelong self that is coloured by the depression (remember Meher), so the history from family and the longitudinal record matter more than a single interview. When a personality disorder is present, say so in the formulation: it predicts a slower response, a greater risk of crises and a need for longer, more structured treatment. But a personality disorder diagnosis should never be used to explain away a treatment that is not working, or to stop looking for a treatable depression underneath.

Where personality disorders come from

Each personality disorder has its own story of causes, and those stories are told in the sections that follow: the genetic link between the Cluster A disorders and schizophrenia in Section 11.2, the biosocial, attachment and trauma accounts of borderline personality disorder in Section 11.4, the fearless, under-aroused nervous system of the psychopath in Section 11.5. But some principles apply to all of them, and they follow the diathesis-stress logic of Section 1.3.4: an inherited vulnerability, expressed as temperament, meets an environment that shapes it, and the two act on each other over many years until a rigid pattern sets.

Start with genes. Twin studies show that roughly half of the variation in normal personality traits is genetic (the Personality book gives the evidence), so it would be surprising if extreme and maladaptive personality were not heritable too. The first twin study to examine the whole range of personality disorders came from Norway.

Key study: Torgersen's Norwegian twins (2000)

Question. How far are the personality disorders inherited? Method. Svenn Torgersen and his colleagues used Norway's twin and patient registries to find 92 identical (monozygotic) and 129 fraternal (dizygotic) twin pairs, interviewed each twin with the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-III-R personality disorders, and combined the results with prevalence data from a population study of more than 2,000 people to estimate how much of the variation was due to genes, to the environment the twins shared, and to experiences unique to each twin. Results. The best-fitting models gave a heritability of .60 for personality disorders in general, .37 for Cluster A, .60 for Cluster B and .62 for Cluster C, with large differences among the specific disorders (Figure 11.3). The shared family environment contributed essentially nothing in the best models, and antisocial personality disorder was too rare in the sample to be modelled. Conclusion. Personality disorders appeared to be more strongly influenced by genes than almost any Axis I disorder. Criticism. The authors themselves warned that the sample was moderate and the specific disorders uncommon, so the individual figures are imprecise. Later work on about 2,800 twins from the Norwegian Institute of Public Health Twin Panel found that the heritability of Cluster B disorders measured by interview alone was only around .30, rose to about .40 to .50 by questionnaire, and reached .63 to .71 when interview and questionnaire were combined to strip out measurement error (Torgersen and colleagues, 2012). How heritable a personality disorder looks depends heavily on how well it is measured.

Two lessons from the twin studies matter more than the individual numbers. The first is that the environment that shapes personality disorder is mostly the non-shared environment, the experiences that differ between children in the same family (a different relationship with a parent, a different school, an accident, an abuser), not the experiences siblings share; the terms are explained in Section 1.4.5. The second, from Kendler's 2008 study described above, is that the genes do not respect the DSM categories: they seem to act through broad dimensions such as negative emotionality, impulsivity and introversion, which then take different shapes in different lives.

What is inherited, then, is not a personality disorder but a temperament, the inborn, biologically based style of emotional and behavioural reactivity visible from infancy. The classic work here belongs to the Developmental and Personality books: the "easy", "difficult" and "slow-to-warm-up" babies of Alexander Thomas and Stella Chess's New York Longitudinal Study, and the shy, behaviourally inhibited toddlers of Jerome Kagan, who are at higher risk of later social anxiety. One model links temperament directly to personality disorder. The American psychiatrist C. Robert Cloninger and his colleagues (1993) distinguished four temperament dimensions (novelty seeking, harm avoidance, reward dependence and persistence), largely inherited and present early, from three character dimensions (self-directedness, cooperativeness and self-transcendence) that mature through learning in the course of life. In their account, the temperament profile colours the type of disorder (high harm avoidance suggests the anxious Cluster C pattern, high novelty seeking the impulsive Cluster B pattern), while low self-directedness and low cooperativeness, immature character, mark whether a personality disorder is present at all.

The environment enters through the relationships in which temperament develops. Attachment, the bond between a child and its caregivers described by John Bowlby (the Developmental book), gives the child its first models of what the self is worth and whether others can be trusted; insecure and especially disorganised attachment recur in the histories of people with personality disorders, and Section 11.4 shows how this idea underlies the mentalisation account of borderline personality disorder. Childhood adversity is the most consistent environmental risk factor. Jeffrey G. Johnson and his colleagues (1999) followed a representative community sample of 639 young people and their mothers in New York State from 1975 into the early 1990s, using state records as well as interviews to identify abuse and neglect. Young adults with documented childhood abuse or neglect were more than four times as likely as others to have a personality disorder, even after the parents' education and psychiatric disorders were taken into account, and ten of the twelve categories of personality disorder symptoms were linked to maltreatment. Yet adversity is neither necessary nor sufficient. Most maltreated children do not develop a personality disorder, and many people with personality disorders report no maltreatment; the same adversity can lead to depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, substance use or nothing at all, the principle of multifinality (Section 1.3.3).

Finally, temperament and environment do not act separately. A child's temperament draws particular responses from the people around it (an evocative gene-environment correlation, Section 1.5.2): an irritable, impulsive toddler provokes harsher and more inconsistent discipline, which makes him more aggressive, which provokes harsher discipline, the coercive cycle described in Section 11.9. An emotionally intense child born into a family that dismisses and punishes emotion learns neither to understand nor to regulate it, the transaction at the heart of Marsha Linehan's account of borderline personality disorder (Section 11.4). Over years such cycles harden into the inflexible, pervasive pattern the general criteria describe.

Remember

Personality disorders are moderately to substantially heritable (Torgersen 2000: about .60 overall; a larger later study found about .30 for Cluster B by interview alone, rising to .63 to .71 when interview and questionnaire were combined), with little effect of the shared family environment. Genes act through broad temperaments (negative emotionality, impulsivity, introversion) that do not follow the A, B, C clusters. Childhood abuse and neglect multiply the risk (Johnson 1999: more than four times), but most maltreated children do not develop a personality disorder. The best general model is diathesis-stress, with temperament and environment shaping each other over time.

Culture, gender and the danger of pathologising difference

A personality disorder is defined as a deviation from the expectations of the person's own culture, so the diagnosis is only as good as the clinician's knowledge of that culture. DSM-5-TR is explicit. Judgements about personality must take into account the person's ethnic, cultural and social background; personality disorders should not be confused with the problems of acculturation after migration, or with habits, customs and religious or political values that come from the person's background; and patterns that look rigid and dysfunctional may be adaptive responses to cultural constraints. Its own examples are pointed: relying on an abusive relationship in a small community where divorce is forbidden may not be pathological dependence, and conscientious political protest that puts family and friends at risk with the authorities is not necessarily pathological callousness.

Translate those principles to India and the cases write themselves. A young woman in a joint family who asks her mother-in-law before making household decisions and would not dream of contradicting her husband's elders in public is following a widely shared expectation, not showing the "needs advice and reassurance" and "cannot express disagreement" of dependent personality disorder (Section 11.7). A man who consults an astrologer before every major decision, or who says his late guru guides him in dreams, is not showing the "magical thinking" of schizotypal personality disorder if his community shares those beliefs; the criterion itself says the beliefs must be inconsistent with subcultural norms. A member of a community that has faced real discrimination who is guarded with officials and strangers is not paranoid (Section 11.2 returns to this). In each case the question is not "would this look odd in a textbook?" but "does this deviate markedly from what this person's own group expects, and does it cause distress or impairment?" DSM-5-TR adds that accuracy improves when clinicians are aware of their own cultural assumptions, avoid instruments that have not been normed on the population being assessed, take account of poverty, discrimination and acculturative stress, and seek information from informants who know the person's cultural background.

Gender raises a parallel problem. DSM-5-TR notes that some personality disorders, antisocial personality disorder above all, are diagnosed more often in men, and others, borderline, histrionic and dependent personality disorder, more often in women, though for borderline personality disorder the difference may reflect women's greater willingness to seek help (Section 11.3). It warns clinicians not to overdiagnose or underdiagnose personality disorders in either sex because of stereotypes about gender roles. Critics have long argued that the descriptions of histrionic and dependent personality disorder read like caricatures of traditional femininity, a debate taken up in Section 11.6.

The last danger is the label itself. In 1988 two British psychiatrists, Lewis and Appleby, asked a sample of psychiatrists to read a short case history of a distressed patient with suicidal urges and debts and to rate the patient and the likely management. In some versions the patient was said to have a previous diagnosis of personality disorder; otherwise the cases were the same. When the label was present, the psychiatrists judged the patient more difficult, more manipulative, more attention-seeking and annoying, more in control of the suicidal urges and less deserving of care. The authors concluded that "personality disorder" was functioning less as a clinical diagnosis than as an enduring pejorative judgement, and they proposed that the concept be abandoned. When the study was repeated with psychiatric trainees nearly thirty years later (Chartonas and colleagues, 2017), attitudes towards a patient with borderline personality disorder were still more negative than towards an otherwise identical patient with depression. The lesson is not that personality disorders are imaginary; this chapter will show that they are real, common, costly and increasingly treatable. It is that the diagnosis should open the door to the right treatment, not close the door on the patient.

Indian context

In Indian clinics the person with a personality disorder usually arrives with family, and the family's account is both indispensable and coloured by its own expectations: a daughter-in-law may be called "stubborn" or "too independent" for behaviour that is ordinary elsewhere, and a son's drinking and violence may be excused as "his nature". Before diagnosing, ask three questions: does the pattern deviate markedly from what the person's own community expects; does it appear across many settings, not only within one strained relationship; and has it been present since adolescence or early adulthood? Only when the answer to all three is yes, and the pattern causes real distress or impairment, is the diagnosis justified.

Scan Your Brain
