11.2 Cluster A: Paranoid, Schizoid and Schizotypal Personality DisordersLower yield · not asked yet

Imagine three people who all live, in their different ways, at a distance from everyone else. The first keeps his distance because he is sure that people mean him harm: a colleague's joke is a dig, a neighbour's new gate is a move in a property dispute, and his wife's late return from her sister's house needs explaining. The second keeps her distance because she simply does not want company: she is not frightened of people, she is not angry with them, she is just not much interested in them. The third keeps his distance because being close to people makes him acutely uneasy, and because his inner world is strange: he half believes that the songs on the radio carry messages for him, he dresses in a way that turns heads on the street, and his conversation drifts in vague, metaphorical loops. These three people illustrate the three personality disorders of Cluster A, the cluster that DSM-5-TR calls odd or eccentric: paranoid, schizoid and schizotypal personality disorder.

Section 11.1 explained what a personality disorder is and why the ten DSM disorders are grouped into three clusters. This section looks closely at the first cluster. You will meet each disorder through a case, learn its criteria in groups that make sense of them, see what it can be confused with, and then follow the thread that ties the whole cluster together: its kinship with schizophrenia.

The odd and eccentric cluster

What do suspiciousness, emotional coldness and magical thinking have in common? On the surface, not much. The reason they belong together is that each looks like a softened, long-lasting version of something seen in the psychoses. Pervasive suspiciousness resembles a persecutory delusion that never quite hardens into one. Detachment, a flat emotional life and a lack of pleasure resemble the negative symptoms of schizophrenia (Chapter 4, Section 4.3). Ideas of reference, odd beliefs and unusual perceptions resemble its positive symptoms, held with doubt instead of certainty. In 1991 the American psychiatrists Larry J. Siever and Kenneth L. Davis proposed a psychobiological model of the personality disorders built on four underlying dimensions (cognitive and perceptual organisation, impulsivity and aggression, affective instability, and anxiety and inhibition); in their scheme it is the first dimension, the way a person organises perception and thought, that runs from normality through the Cluster A disorders to schizophrenia. The other three dimensions map roughly onto the other two clusters.

Three practical consequences follow from this kinship. First, family studies find more Cluster A disorders, schizotypal above all, in the relatives of people with schizophrenia than in the general population, as you will see later in this section. Second, all three disorders are diagnosed only if the pattern does not occur exclusively during schizophrenia, a bipolar or depressive disorder with psychotic features, or another psychotic disorder. If the personality disorder was clearly present before a psychotic illness began, DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to record both and to add the word premorbid in brackets: "paranoid personality disorder (premorbid)". Third, under severe stress, people with any of the three can have very brief psychotic episodes lasting minutes to hours, usually too short to earn a separate diagnosis.

A fourth point is less about biology than about the clinic. People with Cluster A disorders rarely ask for help with their personalities. The paranoid person does not trust the clinician; the schizoid person sees no problem with being alone; the schizotypal person often feels misunderstood rather than unwell. When they do arrive, it is usually because of something else: depression, anxiety, a crisis at work, drinking, or a relative who has finally insisted. A clinician who wants to recognise these disorders has to listen for the personality behind the presenting complaint.

Exam trap

Cluster A is paranoid, schizoid and schizotypal, the odd or eccentric cluster; Cluster B (antisocial, borderline, histrionic, narcissistic) is dramatic, emotional or erratic; Cluster C (avoidant, dependent, obsessive-compulsive) is anxious or fearful. There is no Cluster D. Asked for the odd one out among paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal and borderline (Amity 2023), the answer is borderline, the only Cluster B disorder in the list. Asked which is NOT a Cluster A disorder among paranoid, avoidant, schizotypal and schizoid, the answer is avoidant, a Cluster C disorder; it is the classic distractor because the avoidant person, like the schizoid person, has few relationships (Section 11.2.5 explains the difference, and Section 11.7 teaches avoidant personality disorder in full).

Paranoid personality disorder: a life on guard

Suraj is 46 and has worked for twenty years as a clerk in a cooperative bank in Nagpur. He was referred to the hospital's psychology department by a physician who was treating his blood pressure and who had become worried by his anger. Suraj explains, carefully and at length, that his branch manager has been "building a file" on him for years, that two younger colleagues laugh when he passes because they have been told something about him, and that his transfer request was refused because the union secretary, whom he once corrected in a meeting, has never forgiven him. He has filed three complaints with the head office and several applications under the Right to Information Act to find out what has been written about him. He has no close friends ("Friends are people who have not yet found a use for you"), he refuses to tell the psychologist which village his family comes from ("Why do you need that?"), and he checks his wife's phone in the evenings because a cousin once mentioned seeing her talking to a man at the bus stop. He has never heard voices, and when the psychologist asks gently whether there could be any other explanation for his colleagues' laughter, he pauses and says, "There could be. But I have learnt not to be a fool." He has been like this, his wife says, for as long as she has known him.

Suraj shows the core of paranoid personality disorder: a pervasive distrust and suspiciousness of others such that their motives are read as malevolent, beginning by early adulthood and present in many contexts. The pattern must show in at least four of seven criteria. They are easiest to learn in three groups.

  • Expecting to be harmed. The person suspects, without sufficient basis, that others are exploiting, harming or deceiving him (criterion 1); is preoccupied with unjustified doubts about the loyalty or trustworthiness of friends and associates (criterion 2); and is reluctant to confide in anyone because of an unwarranted fear that the information will be used against him (criterion 3). Suraj's refusal to name his village is criterion 3 in miniature.
  • Reading threat into ordinary events. He reads hidden demeaning or threatening meanings into harmless remarks or events (criterion 4): DSM-5-TR's examples are a shop assistant's honest mistake taken as a deliberate attempt to cheat, and a compliment on an achievement heard as pressure to do even more. He perceives attacks on his character or reputation that others cannot see, and is quick to react angrily or to counterattack (criterion 6).
  • Holding on to injury. He persistently bears grudges, unforgiving of insults, injuries or slights (criterion 5), and has recurrent, unjustified suspicions about the fidelity of a spouse or sexual partner (criterion 7), sometimes gathering trivial "evidence" and trying to control the partner's movements.

A final criterion (B) protects the boundary with psychosis: the pattern must not occur only during schizophrenia, a mood disorder with psychotic features or another psychotic disorder, and must not be caused by the physiological effects of a medical condition such as temporal lobe epilepsy. Notice what is absent from the list. There are no hallucinations, no fixed false beliefs and no disorganisation. The suspicious person can usually, as Suraj did, concede that another explanation is possible, even while refusing to accept it.

DSM-5-TR describes what living with this disorder looks like beyond the criteria. People with paranoid personality disorder are hard to get along with; their suspiciousness comes out as argumentativeness, repeated complaining or a quiet, hostile aloofness. Because they cannot trust, they need to control the people around them, and they are rigid, critical of others and poor at collaboration, yet highly sensitive to criticism of themselves. They may be litigious. They are often alert to power and rank, may harbour thinly hidden grandiose fantasies, and tend to form negative stereotypes of groups other than their own. Some are drawn into tightly knit groups that share a suspicious world view.

There is a vicious circle inside this description, and it was named long before the DSM. In 1943 the American psychiatrist Norman Cameron described the paranoid pseudo-community: the suspicious person organises the ordinary actions of the people around him into an imagined conspiracy, and then acts on it. In 1962 the sociologist Edwin M. Lemert, studying people whose suspicious behaviour had led to their being excluded at work and in the community, added an uncomfortable corrective: the conspiracy is often partly real, because colleagues who find a person prickly and accusing do start to avoid him, talk about him and leave him out. DSM-5-TR makes the same point in its own words: the combative and suspicious style elicits hostility from others, which then confirms the original expectation. Suraj's colleagues may indeed laugh when he passes, and the laughter, once it starts, proves him right.

Psychodynamic writers explain the pattern through projection, the defence in which a person's own unacceptable hostile feelings are attributed to others ("I am not angry with them; they are against me"); the defence itself is described in Book 2, Section 3.4.1, and Sigmund Freud's 1911 analysis of the memoir of the German judge Daniel Paul Schreber made projection the classic account of paranoia. Cognitive therapists describe instead a set of core beliefs: other people are malicious and deceptive, they will attack if given the chance, and the only way to be safe is to stay constantly on guard. Both accounts describe the same habit of mind from different sides.

How common is the disorder? DSM-5-TR gives an estimated prevalence of 2.3 percent in a probability subsample of the American National Comorbidity Survey Replication, 4.4 percent in the National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions, and a median of 3.2 percent across six epidemiological studies; in forensic settings the figure may be as high as 23 percent. A meta-analysis of clinical and community samples found it more common in men, but the national survey found it more common in women, so the sex ratio is unsettled. The pattern may first show in childhood and adolescence as solitariness, poor relationships with peers, social anxiety, underachievement at school and hypersensitivity; an adolescent onset is associated with childhood maltreatment, bullying and later interpersonal aggression. As for causes, there is some evidence of raised rates in the relatives of people with schizophrenia and a more specific family link with delusional disorder of the persecutory type, and both longitudinal and cross-sectional studies confirm childhood trauma as a risk factor. Social adversity matters too: inequality, marginalisation and racism lower trust, and DSM-5-TR notes pointedly that in some circumstances lower trust is adaptive.

The older European psychiatry knew this personality well. Kurt Schneider's "fanatic" psychopathic personality (Section 11.1) and the "querulant" who pursues endless legal complaints are its ancestors, and Ernst Kretschmer (1888 to 1964) described in 1918 the sensitive, easily wounded person who, after a humiliation, develops a sensitive delusion of reference. ICD-10 still lists these descendants under paranoid personality disorder (F60.0), whose inclusion terms are expansive paranoid, fanatic, querulant, paranoid and sensitive paranoid personality. ICD-10's description adds two features the DSM list lacks: excessive sensitivity to setbacks and rebuffs, and a combative and tenacious sense of personal rights out of keeping with the actual situation, together with a preoccupation with "conspiratorial" explanations of events.

Treatment is difficult for the obvious reason that therapy requires trust. People with this disorder usually come only in a crisis or because of depression or anxiety, and the first task is to build an alliance. Therapists use cognitive methods to test the beliefs that everyone is malevolent and that vigilance is the only safety. But honesty requires the admission that there is still no convincing evidence that any treatment changes the disorder itself; in one survey of clinicians, only 11 percent thought that their paranoid patients would stay in therapy long enough to be helped. Low-dose antipsychotic medication is sometimes used for brief stress-related psychotic episodes, and depression or anxiety is treated in the usual way.

Clinical link

Interviewing a suspicious patient is a skill. Be transparent: explain why you ask each question, what you are writing and who will see it. Do not argue with the suspicions, and do not pretend to agree with them; acknowledge the feeling ("It sounds exhausting to feel that people are against you"). Do not be over-warm or over-familiar, which a paranoid person reads as manipulation, and keep every promise, including the small ones about appointment times. Above all, remember Lemert: some of what the patient describes may be true.

Suspicion that is not a personality disorder

Distrust is common and often justified, so paranoid personality disorder is above all a diagnosis of exclusion and of judgement. Four comparisons matter most.

Delusional disorder and the psychoses. The decisive question is conviction. A delusion is a fixed false belief held with certainty and impervious to evidence (Chapter 2, Section 2.5). The person with paranoid personality disorder suspects; the person with delusional disorder, persecutory or jealous type, knows. If Suraj one day announced that the manager had planted a listening device in his scooter and that the head office was poisoning his tea, and nothing could shake the belief, the diagnosis would move to delusional disorder (Chapter 4, Section 4.5.4). Schizophrenia adds hallucinations, disorganisation, negative symptoms and a decline in functioning; the old "paranoid schizophrenia" was a subtype of schizophrenia (ICD-10 F20.0), not a personality disorder, and DSM-5 dropped the subtypes altogether (Chapter 4, Section 4.4). DSM-5-TR's rule is that the psychotic disorders all involve a period of persistent psychotic symptoms; paranoid personality disorder can be diagnosed alongside one of them only if it was present before the psychosis began and persists when the psychosis is in remission, and then it is recorded as premorbid. Paranoid personality may indeed be the soil in which delusional disorder or schizophrenia later grows.

Understandable mistrust. DSM-5-TR devotes a long paragraph to the danger of mislabelling. Migrants, members of oppressed ethnic or racialised groups, and others who face discrimination may be guarded and defensive because they do not know the language or the rules, or because the majority society really has been hostile or indifferent. Some cultural groups develop low trust in outsiders as a reasonable response to history. Guardedness, limited show of emotion, social distance and anger in situations experienced as unfair can then be misread as paranoid traits, and the clinician's own irritation can set up a cycle of mutual mistrust. A labourer from another state who has been cheated by contractors and harassed by the police, and who now answers questions warily, is not showing a personality disorder. Barlow and Durand add prisoners, refugees, people with hearing impairment and older adults to the groups in whom suspicion may arise from circumstance.

Paranoid traits with other causes. The manual separates the disorder from suspicion that develops with persistent substance use (stimulants and cannabis are the usual culprits), from paranoid traits that follow a physical handicap such as deafness, and from a personality change due to another medical condition, in which the traits are a direct physiological consequence of an illness such as a frontal lobe tumour or Huntington's disease (Chapter 10, Section 10.11.10).

Other personality disorders. Schizotypal personality disorder shares suspiciousness, aloofness and paranoid ideation but adds magical thinking, unusual perceptions and odd thinking and speech. Schizoid personality disorder shares the aloofness but not the paranoid ideation. Borderline and histrionic personality disorders share quick anger at minor provocations but not the pervasive suspiciousness, and borderline personality disorder has far more impulsivity and self-destructiveness. The avoidant person is also reluctant to confide, but from fear of embarrassment or of being found inadequate, not from fear of malice. Antisocial acts in paranoid personality disorder are usually driven by revenge, not by a wish for gain or exploitation. And the narcissistic person who seems suspicious and withdrawn is usually afraid that his flaws will be exposed. When the criteria for more than one personality disorder are met, all are diagnosed.

Exam trap

Suspicion without delusional conviction, lifelong and trait-like, is paranoid personality disorder; a fixed false belief of persecution or infidelity is delusional disorder; suspicion plus hallucinations, disorganisation and decline is schizophrenia. Jealousy is criterion 7 of paranoid personality disorder when it is a suspicion, but delusional jealousy (Othello syndrome, Chapter 2, Section 2.6) when it is a fixed belief. "Premorbid" is the DSM label for a Cluster A disorder that was present before schizophrenia began. Do not confuse the paranoid personality with Melanie Klein's paranoid-schizoid position, a stage of normal infant development (Section 11.2.4).

Schizoid personality disorder: the observer

Firoz is 31 and works the night shift as a data analyst for a company in Hyderabad. He lives alone in a rented flat, eats the same meal from the same restaurant most days, and spends his free time writing programs to solve chess problems. His parents in Kurnool are worried because he has refused every marriage proposal they have brought him; he says, without heat, that he does not see the point. He has no friends, has never had a romantic relationship, and describes his colleagues as "fine". When his manager praised his work at the annual review, he felt nothing in particular, and when a team leader criticised him in front of others, he felt nothing then either. He came to the company's counsellor only because his manager insisted, after Firoz had declined for the third time to join a team that would require daily meetings. He is not sad, not anxious and not suspicious. He would simply like to be left to his work.

Firoz shows schizoid personality disorder: a pervasive pattern of detachment from social relationships and a restricted range of emotional expression in interpersonal settings, beginning by early adulthood and present in many contexts, with at least four of seven criteria. Again they fall into three groups.

  • No wish for closeness. The person neither desires nor enjoys close relationships, including being part of a family (criterion 1); lacks close friends or confidants other than first-degree relatives (criterion 5); and has little, if any, interest in sexual experiences with another person (criterion 3).
  • A solitary, pleasureless life. The person almost always chooses solitary activities (criterion 2), often mechanical or abstract ones such as computer or mathematical games, and takes pleasure in few, if any, activities (criterion 4). This lack of pleasure, from sensory and bodily experiences as well as from people, is a form of anhedonia.
  • Emotional flatness. The person appears indifferent to the praise or criticism of others (criterion 6) and shows emotional coldness, detachment or flattened affectivity (criterion 7), rarely returning a smile or a nod.

Criterion B excludes a pattern that occurs only during a psychotic disorder, a mood disorder with psychotic features or, importantly, autism spectrum disorder, and one caused by a medical condition. As with the paranoid type, a schizoid pattern that clearly preceded schizophrenia is recorded as premorbid.

The associated features fill in the picture. People with schizoid personality disorder find it especially hard to express anger, even when provoked, which adds to the impression that they have no feelings. Their lives can seem directionless; they drift. They react passively to adversity. They date rarely and often do not marry. Their work suffers if it requires contact with people, but they may do well when they can work in isolation, which is exactly what Firoz has arranged for himself. The American cognitive therapists Aaron Beck and Arthur Freeman (1990) caught the stance in a phrase: such people consider themselves "observers rather than participants in the world around them". And yet DSM-5-TR adds a qualification worth remembering. On the rare occasions when these individuals become comfortable enough to reveal themselves, they may admit to painful feelings, particularly about social interaction. The coldness is not always the whole story.

The disorder is uncommon in clinical settings, partly because its sufferers rarely seek help. DSM-5-TR reports 4.9 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 3.1 percent in the national epidemiological survey and a median of 1.3 percent across six studies. Some studies find it more common in men, others find no difference. It may be visible in childhood as solitariness, poor peer relationships and underachievement, which mark the child as different and invite teasing. It may be somewhat more common in the relatives of people with schizophrenia or schizotypal personality disorder, and Barlow and Durand note two further leads: childhood shyness as a possible inherited precursor, and the finding that the parents of children with autism are more likely to have schizoid personality disorder, which hints at a shared biological vulnerability for social disconnection. Very little research has been done on its causes or its treatment. When treatment is attempted it usually targets a crisis such as depression or job loss, and then the building of social skills through role-play and the gradual development of a small supportive network; outcome studies are scarce.

The word has an interesting history, and the history explains why today's schizoid category is so narrow. Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), who named schizophrenia, used "schizoid" for people with a tendency to turn away from the outer world towards an inner life, a milder version of the withdrawal he saw in his patients. Ernst Kretschmer, in Physique and Character (1921), built a typology on it. He linked a schizothymic temperament (reserved, serious, inward, typical of people with a slender, "leptosome" body build) to schizophrenia, and a cyclothymic temperament (sociable, warm, with a rounded, "pyknic" build) to manic-depressive illness; the schizoid personality was the pathological exaggeration of schizothymia. Kretschmer's schizoid had two poles: some were hypersensitive, shy and easily hurt, others cold, dull and insensitive, and most mixed the two in what he called the psychaesthetic proportion. His body-build theory has not survived, but his two poles did, in a new form. When DSM-III was drafted in the late 1970s, the American psychologist Theodore Millon (1928 to 2014) argued that the hypersensitive person who longs for company but fears rejection is quite different from the cold person who does not want it. DSM-III (1980) therefore divided the old broad schizoid category in three: the cold, detached pole kept the name schizoid; the sensitive pole became avoidant personality disorder (Section 11.7); and the eccentric variant with odd thinking and perception became schizotypal personality disorder (Section 11.2.6). That is why the DSM schizoid person is defined by indifference, not by fear.

The word has a separate life in psychoanalysis, and examiners occasionally exploit it. The Scottish analyst W. Ronald D. Fairbairn (1889 to 1964), in "Schizoid factors in the personality" (1940), used "schizoid" for a withdrawal from emotional contact in people who feel that their love is dangerous, that loving and needing others will empty or destroy them; for Fairbairn, schizoid trends lay at the root of much psychopathology. Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960) borrowed the term in 1946 for the paranoid-schizoid position, the earliest phase of normal infant development, in which the infant splits its world into a wholly good and a wholly bad object and fears persecution by the bad one. Neither usage is a DSM diagnosis; what "object relations" means is explained in Book 2, Section 3.4.3, and the splitting that Klein described returns, as the defence of borderline personality disorder, in Section 11.4.

Alone for different reasons: schizoid, avoidant, autistic, depressed

Few relationships is a description, not a diagnosis. Six quite different people can all be found eating lunch alone, and the clinician's task is to find out why each one is alone. The key question is motivational: does the person want closeness? Table 11.4 sets out the answers.

Table 11.4 Six reasons for being alone, and how to tell them apart.
ConditionWants closeness?Why the person is aloneTelling feature
Schizoid personality disorderNoIndifference: relationships bring little pleasureIndifferent to praise and criticism; flat, cold affect; no odd beliefs
Avoidant personality disorder (Cluster C)Yes, intenselyFear of criticism, rejection and humiliationFeels inadequate; hypersensitive to negative evaluation; lonely and unhappy about it
Schizotypal personality disorderLittle, and closeness is acutely uncomfortableParanoid social anxiety and a sense of not fitting inIdeas of reference, magical thinking, odd speech and appearance
Paranoid personality disorderNot with people he cannot trust, which is everyoneExpectation of malice and betrayalSuspicion, grudges, counterattack
Autism spectrum disorderOften yes, but the social skills are lackingDeficits in social communication from early childhoodRestricted, repetitive behaviour and interests; developmental onset (Chapter 3, Section 3.5)
Depression or the negative symptoms of schizophreniaInterest has been lostAn episode or illness that began at a definable timeA change from the person's usual self, not a lifelong pattern

The schizoid-avoidant contrast is the one to learn first, because it is the pair the examiners ask about most. Picture both people at a wedding. The avoidant woman stands near the wall, longing to join a group of cousins, rehearsing what she might say and certain she will say it badly; she goes home miserable. The schizoid man stands near the wall because the wall is a perfectly good place to stand; he goes home relieved and untroubled. DSM-5-TR puts it formally: in avoidant personality disorder the social isolation comes from fear of being embarrassed or found inadequate and from excessive anticipation of rejection, whereas the schizoid person has a more pervasive detachment and limited desire for intimacy.

The boundary with autism spectrum disorder is genuinely difficult, especially at the milder end, because both involve an apparent indifference to companionship; the manual's guide is that autism is marked by stereotyped behaviours and interests (and, in its parallel comparison with schizotypal personality disorder, by an even greater lack of social awareness and emotional reciprocity), with an onset in early development. The obsessive-compulsive personality may also look detached, because of devotion to work and discomfort with emotion, but DSM-5-TR notes that such people keep an underlying capacity for intimacy. Finally, introversion is not a disorder. The quiet, bookish person who prefers a few close friends to a crowd has a personality trait; only when detachment is inflexible, maladaptive and causes significant impairment or distress does it become schizoid personality disorder.

Exam trap

"Wants relationships but avoids them for fear of rejection" is avoidant (Cluster C); "does not want relationships and is indifferent to praise or criticism" is schizoid (Cluster A). Schizoid and schizotypal both have few friends and constricted affect; only schizotypal has the cognitive and perceptual distortions and the eccentricity. "Detachment from social relationships and restricted emotional expression" is the stem-phrase of schizoid personality disorder; when it appears as an option for borderline personality disorder, it is a distractor (Section 11.3).

Schizotypal personality disorder: odd beliefs below the psychotic line

Prerna is 27 and lives with her widowed mother in Kolkata. She works part time in a photocopy shop. Her mother brought her to a psychiatric outpatient department after Prerna refused to leave the house for two days because, she said, the numbers on the licence plates of the cars parked outside were "arranged as a warning". She believes she has always been able to sense when something bad is about to happen in the family, and that if she touches the door frame three times before going out the bad thing can be held off. Sometimes, when she is alone, she feels someone standing just behind her shoulder, though she knows nobody is there. She wears a heavy winter shawl in May, with an assortment of badges pinned to it, and her speech is vague and roundabout: asked why she left college, she says, "The energy of the place was not compatible with the direction of things." She has no friends and has never had one; she finds being with people "thick", and at her cousin's wedding she became more tense as the evening went on, convinced that the guests were whispering about her. When the psychiatrist asks whether the licence plates could be a coincidence, she says, "Maybe. Probably. But it felt very strong." She has been odd, her mother says, since she was a schoolgirl.

Prerna's pattern is schizotypal personality disorder: a pervasive pattern of social and interpersonal deficits marked by acute discomfort with, and reduced capacity for, close relationships, together with cognitive or perceptual distortions and eccentric behaviour, beginning by early adulthood and present in many contexts. It needs five or more of nine criteria, the only Cluster A disorder with nine criteria and a threshold of five. The nine fall naturally into three groups, and the three groups are worth noticing because they mirror the three symptom dimensions of schizophrenia.

  • Cognitive and perceptual distortions (the positive-like features). First, ideas of reference (criterion 1): the incorrect interpretation of casual incidents and external events as having a particular and unusual meaning for oneself, excluding delusions of reference. Odd beliefs or magical thinking that influence behaviour and are inconsistent with subcultural norms (criterion 2): superstitiousness, belief in clairvoyance, telepathy or a "sixth sense", or, in children and adolescents, bizarre fantasies and preoccupations. Unusual perceptual experiences, including bodily illusions (criterion 3): sensing that another person is present, or hearing a voice murmur one's name. Suspiciousness or paranoid ideation (criterion 5).
  • Interpersonal deficits (the negative-like features). Inappropriate or constricted affect (criterion 6), so that the person interacts in a stiff or odd way; lack of close friends or confidants other than first-degree relatives (criterion 8); and excessive social anxiety that does not diminish with familiarity and that tends to be associated with paranoid fears rather than negative judgements about oneself (criterion 9).
  • Eccentricity and odd thought (the disorganised-like features). Odd thinking and speech (criterion 4): vague, circumstantial, metaphorical, overelaborate or stereotyped, but without actual derailment or incoherence. Behaviour or appearance that is odd, eccentric or peculiar (criterion 7): unusual mannerisms, clothes that do not quite fit together, inattention to social conventions.

Three of these criteria repay a closer look. The first is the difference between an idea and a delusion of reference, because it is the line between this personality disorder and psychosis. Barlow and Durand's illustration is a person who believes that everyone on a passing city bus is talking about him, but who can acknowledge that this is unlikely. That acknowledgement is everything. A delusion of reference is held with full conviction and cannot be shaken; an idea of reference is entertained, felt, perhaps half believed, but doubt can be raised. Prerna's "Maybe. Probably. But it felt very strong" is the voice of an idea, not a delusion.

The second is the cultural clause in criterion 2. Magical thinking counts only if it is inconsistent with the person's subculture, and DSM-5-TR warns explicitly that pervasive culturally determined beliefs about the supernatural and religion (life after death, speaking in tongues, shamanism, mind reading, a sixth sense, the evil eye, magical beliefs about health and illness) can look schizotypal to an uninformed clinician. In India this warning has daily force. A black thread tied against nazar, a lemon and chillies hung over a shop door, consulting an astrologer before a wedding, a belief that a dead grandmother watches over the family: all are shared and sanctioned, and none is a criterion. What marks Prerna out is that her beliefs are private and idiosyncratic. Nobody else in her family believes that licence plates carry warnings, or that her touching the door frame protects them.

The third is the peculiar social anxiety of criterion 9. Most socially anxious people relax as an evening goes on and the faces become familiar. The schizotypal person, DSM-5-TR observes, does the opposite: at a dinner party he becomes more tense and suspicious as time passes, because the anxiety is rooted in suspicion of what the others are up to, not in worry about how he himself comes across. That distinguishes it from the anxiety of social anxiety disorder and of avoidant personality disorder, which centres on fear of negative evaluation of the self.

DSM-5-TR's figures put the prevalence at 3.3 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 3.9 percent in the national epidemiological survey, and a median of only 0.6 percent across five studies, a spread that shows how much depends on the method. The disorder seems slightly more common in men. It may be first apparent in childhood and adolescence as solitariness, poor peer relationships, social anxiety, underachievement, hypersensitivity, peculiar thoughts and language and bizarre fantasies, and such children attract teasing. People with the disorder usually seek help for associated anxiety or depression rather than for the personality features themselves; Barlow and Durand cite estimates that 30 to 50 percent of those who come for clinical help also meet the criteria for major depressive disorder. Under stress, transient psychotic episodes lasting minutes to hours can occur, and occasionally a full psychotic disorder develops. But the course is relatively stable, and only a small proportion go on to schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder; with obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, schizotypal personality disorder is one of the types that DSM-5-TR says are least likely to soften with age. The disorder co-occurs often with schizoid, paranoid, avoidant and borderline personality disorders.

The differential diagnosis follows from the three groups. Against schizoid and paranoid personality disorders, the presence of cognitive or perceptual distortions and marked eccentricity is decisive. Against avoidant personality disorder, the avoidant person has an active desire for relationships held back by fear, whereas the schizotypal person lacks the desire. Against borderline personality disorder, which can also produce transient psychotic-like experiences, the borderline experiences are tied to intense emotional shifts under interpersonal stress and are more dissociative (depersonalisation and derealisation), while schizotypal oddities are enduring and not bound to affect; the schizotypal person also lacks the impulsive and manipulative behaviour of the borderline person, though the two disorders often co-occur. In children, the distinction from milder autism spectrum disorder and from language and communication disorders can be very hard. And in adolescents, schizotypal features may reflect passing emotional turmoil rather than an enduring disorder, one reason for the rule that a personality disorder diagnosed under 18 needs features present for at least a year (Section 11.1).

Exam trap

Schizotypal personality disorder has ideas of reference, which the person can doubt; schizophrenia and delusional disorder have delusions of reference, held with conviction. Magical thinking counts only when it is inconsistent with subcultural norms, so shared religious and folk beliefs are not criteria. The social anxiety of schizotypal personality disorder does not diminish with familiarity and is paranoid; that of social anxiety disorder and avoidant personality disorder is fear of negative evaluation of the self. Schizotypal needs 5 of 9; paranoid and schizoid need 4 of 7.

Schizotypy and the schizophrenia spectrum

Why does a personality disorder appear in the schizophrenia chapter of DSM-5-TR? The answer is a story that runs through three ideas and one set of adoption studies.

The first idea is a word. In 1953 the Hungarian-born American psychoanalyst Sandor Rado (1890 to 1972) coined schizotype, a contraction of "schizophrenic phenotype", for people who carry an inherited predisposition to schizophrenia and show its traits in a mild, non-psychotic form, without ever (or without yet) becoming psychotic. Rado's point was that the genotype could be expressed in more than one way.

The second idea is a model. In his 1962 presidential address to the American Psychological Association, "Schizotaxia, schizotypy, schizophrenia", Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003), whom you met in Chapter 1, Section 1.3, and Chapter 4, turned Rado's word into a theory with three levels. What is inherited, Meehl proposed, is a subtle defect of neural integration, schizotaxia. In virtually any social environment a schizotaxic child develops a characteristic personality organisation, schizotypy, marked by four signs he described: cognitive slippage (a mild looseness of thought), social aversiveness, anhedonia and ambivalence. Only a minority of schizotypes, under adverse conditions and with other genetic "potentiators", go on to clinical schizophrenia; Meehl's estimate was roughly one in ten. Schizotypal personality disorder is, in this view, the visible face of schizotypy in the people who never decompensate. Notice how neatly it explains a puzzle. Most relatives of people with schizophrenia never become psychotic, yet many of them are a little odd, a little withdrawn, a little suspicious: they are the schizotypes who stayed well.

The third strand is evidence. The Danish adoption studies of Seymour Kety (1915 to 2000), David Rosenthal and Paul Wender, reporting from 1968 (Chapter 1, Section 1.4, and Chapter 4, Section 4.7), found that schizophrenia and related conditions were concentrated in the biological, not the adoptive, relatives of adoptees with schizophrenia. Among the related conditions was a cluster of milder oddities that Kety's team called borderline schizophrenia, and it was to cover this group that they coined the phrase schizophrenia spectrum. In 1979 Robert L. Spitzer (1932 to 2015), Jean Endicott and Miriam Gibbon, preparing DSM-III, reviewed the confused literature on "borderline" patients and found two separate meanings hiding under the one word. One was borderline schizophrenia in Kety's sense; the other was an unstable, impulsive personality pattern. They wrote two sets of criteria and tested them on clinicians' descriptions of 808 "borderline" and 808 control patients. The first set became DSM-III's schizotypal personality disorder, the second its borderline personality disorder. The two disorders, in other words, were born as twins from the same paper, which is why you will meet Spitzer again at the start of Section 11.3.

Did the new category really capture what Kety had found? In 1981 Kenneth S. Kendler, Alan Gruenberg and John Strauss went back to the Copenhagen interviews of the Danish adoption study and rediagnosed the relatives, blindly, using the DSM-III criteria. Schizotypal personality disorder was significantly more common in the biological relatives of the adoptees with schizophrenia than in the biological relatives of control adoptees, and it was rare and equally common in the two groups of adoptive relatives. Their conclusion was that, in that sample, schizotypal personality disorder had a strong genetic but no family-environmental relationship to schizophrenia. Later work has added that twin studies show stable genetic and rather transient environmental influences, that genetic risk variants for schizophrenia may be linked to schizotypal personality disorder, and that people with the disorder show milder versions of the memory, attention and brain-structure differences seen in schizophrenia.

The classifications drew different conclusions from the same evidence. DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR list schizotypal personality disorder in two places: among the personality disorders, where its criteria are given, and at the head of the schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorders chapter, where it is the mildest condition on the spectrum. The WHO went further and took it out of the personality disorders altogether. ICD-10 classifies schizotypal disorder (F21) in the block "Schizophrenia, schizotypal and delusional disorders", next to schizophrenia, and its inclusion terms read like a history of the concept: latent schizophrenia, borderline schizophrenia, prepsychotic and prodromal schizophrenia, pseudoneurotic and pseudopsychopathic schizophrenia, and schizotypal personality disorder. ICD-10's description is "eccentric behaviour and anomalies of thinking and affect which resemble those seen in schizophrenia, though no definite and characteristic schizophrenic anomalies occur at any stage"; its list of features adds two items the DSM list lacks, obsessive ruminations without inner resistance (often with dysmorphophobic content, that is, preoccupation with an imagined bodily defect, or with sexual or aggressive content) and occasional transient quasi-psychotic episodes; and its guidelines ask for three or four typical features, continuously or episodically, for at least two years, in a person who has never met the criteria for schizophrenia. Tellingly, ICD-10 says that the category is not recommended for general use, because it is not clearly separated from simple schizophrenia or from schizoid and paranoid personality disorders. ICD-11 keeps schizotypal disorder (6A22) in its schizophrenia block, describes it as an enduring pattern, lasting at least several years, of eccentric behaviour, appearance and speech with cognitive and perceptual distortions, unusual beliefs and interpersonal discomfort, and distinguishes negative schizotypy (constricted affect, anhedonia) from positive schizotypy (paranoid ideas, ideas of reference, perceptual disturbances) below the threshold of a psychotic disorder. Chapter 4, Section 4.5.8 places it on the psychosis side, next to attenuated psychosis syndrome. The paranoid and schizoid personality disorders, by contrast, disappear as named types in ICD-11, whose single personality disorder diagnosis is described by trait qualifiers such as detachment (Section 11.8); and of the three Cluster A disorders only schizotypal survives among the six types of the DSM-5 Alternative Model (Section 11.8).

Treatment follows the kinship. Because schizotypal patients share so much with people with schizophrenia, psychiatrists have tried antipsychotic drugs in low doses, and there is some controlled evidence. In a nine-week double-blind trial by Harold W. Koenigsberg and colleagues (2003), 25 patients with DSM-IV schizotypal personality disorder were randomly assigned to risperidone, starting at 0.25 mg a day and rising to no more than 2 mg, or to placebo; those on risperidone had lower negative and general symptom scores by week 3 and lower positive symptom scores by week 7, with tolerable side effects. Antidepressants are used for the common comorbid depression, and cognitive therapy, supportive therapy and social skills training are used to reduce isolation and to test odd beliefs gently. Because the disorder can precede psychosis, early intervention services have also turned their attention to it.

Key study: the OPUS trial and schizotypal disorder (Nordentoft and colleagues, 2006)

Can good care delay or prevent psychosis in people with schizotypal disorder? Merete Nordentoft and her colleagues in Copenhagen tested this inside OPUS, a Danish randomised trial of integrated early-intervention treatment. They took 79 patients making their first contact with services with an ICD-10 diagnosis of schizotypal disorder and randomised them to standard treatment or to integrated treatment (assertive community treatment with small caseloads, psychoeducational family treatment and social skills training). Two years later, 48.3 percent of the standard-treatment group had been diagnosed with a psychotic disorder, against 25.0 percent of the integrated-treatment group; in the statistical model integrated treatment cut the risk of transition to about a third, and male sex raised it. The conclusion: integrated treatment postponed or prevented psychosis in a substantial number of patients. The criticism: the sample was small, the patients were help-seeking first contacts with an ICD-10 diagnosis (probably more severely ill than many people with DSM schizotypal personality disorder), and two years cannot show whether psychosis was prevented or merely delayed.

Exam trap

ICD-10 and ICD-11 do not classify schizotypal disorder as a personality disorder: it is F21 and 6A22, in the schizophrenia block. DSM-5-TR lists it in both chapters, with the criteria in the personality disorders chapter. Rado coined "schizotype" (1953); Meehl proposed schizotaxia, schizotypy and schizophrenia (1962); Kety, Rosenthal and Wender found "borderline schizophrenia" in biological relatives and coined "schizophrenia spectrum"; Spitzer, Endicott and Gibbon (1979) turned it into DSM-III's schizotypal criteria. Of the Cluster A disorders, schizotypal has the strongest genetic link to schizophrenia and the highest risk of transition to psychosis; most people with it never become psychotic.

Telling the odd cluster apart

You now have three disorders that share social distance and differ in its flavour, and two psychotic disorders that lie beyond them. Figure 11.4 shows which features belong to one disorder alone and which are shared; Table 11.5 places all five conditions on the road from personality to psychosis.

Table 11.5 From personality to psychosis: the Cluster A disorders beside delusional disorder and schizophrenia.
ConditionCore picturePsychotic symptomsCourseICD-10; ICD-11
Paranoid PD (4 of 7)Pervasive distrust; malevolent motives read into ordinary events; grudges; counterattackNone lasting; brief episodes (minutes to hours) under stressLifelong trait from early adulthood; may precede delusional disorderF60.0; one personality disorder diagnosis (6D10) with trait qualifiers
Schizoid PD (4 of 7)Detachment from relationships; restricted emotion; solitary, pleasureless lifeNone lasting; brief episodes under stressLifelong trait; may precede schizophreniaF60.1; 6D10 (detachment qualifier)
Schizotypal PD (5 of 9)Discomfort with closeness; ideas of reference, magical thinking, odd perceptions; eccentricityAttenuated: ideas, not delusions; doubt can be raisedStable; a small minority develop psychosisF21; 6A22 (both in the schizophrenia block)
Delusional disorderOne or more delusions; behaviour otherwise not obviously oddDelusions for at least 1 month; Criterion A for schizophrenia never metOften chronic; onset usually in adulthoodF22; 6A24
SchizophreniaTwo or more of the five core symptoms with a decline in functioningDelusions, hallucinations, disorganisation, negative symptomsAt least 6 months; episodes and residual declineF20; 6A20

Read the table from top to bottom and you are reading the schizophrenia spectrum from its mildest to its most severe expression. Read it from left to right and you can see the three questions a clinician asks: what is the core pattern, is there real psychosis (conviction, not just suspicion; voices, not just a sense of presence), and has the person always been like this or has something changed? The last question is often the most useful of all. A personality disorder is how someone has been since adolescence; a psychotic disorder is something that has happened to them.

Exam trap

Keep the "schizo-" words apart. Schizoid: detached, cold, no odd beliefs (Cluster A). Schizotypal: detached and eccentric, with odd beliefs and perceptions (Cluster A, and the schizophrenia spectrum). Schizothymic: Kretschmer's normal reserved temperament. Schizotaxia: Meehl's inherited neural defect. Paranoid-schizoid position: Klein's stage of infancy. Schizophreniform, schizoaffective and schizophrenia are psychotic disorders (Chapter 4). Of the ten personality disorders, only schizotypal is placed by the ICD among the psychotic disorders.

11.3 Borderline Personality Disorder I: The Clinical PictureHigh yield · 10 Qs

Ask any psychiatrist or clinical psychologist who has worked in an Indian general hospital which personality disorder they meet most often, and the answer is nearly always the same. Not in the outpatient clinic, where it may hide behind a diagnosis of depression, but at night, in the emergency department, after an overdose of a mother's sleeping tablets or a razor blade drawn across a forearm, usually after a quarrel with someone the patient loves. Borderline personality disorder is the most common personality disorder in clinical settings, the most researched, the most asked about in examinations and, for a long time, the most feared by clinicians. This section is about recognising it: where its strange name came from, what its nine criteria mean and what they sound like in a real person, who receives the diagnosis, and how to tell it apart from the half-dozen disorders it is mistaken for. Why it develops, how its defences work, how it changes over a lifetime and what helps are the subject of Section 11.4.

On the border of what? The history of a word

"Borderline" is a geographical metaphor, and the first question to ask of any border is what it separates. For the first forty years of its life, the answer was neurosis and psychosis. In 1938 the New York psychoanalyst Adolph Stern published a paper on what he called the "border line group" of neuroses: patients who came for psychoanalysis looking like ordinary neurotics but who did badly on the couch. Among the features Stern listed were a deep narcissistic insecurity, an inordinate hypersensitivity to slights, rigidity, a tendency to collapse helplessly under stress instead of rallying (his memorable phrase was "psychic bleeding"), heavy use of projection, difficulties in reality testing in their relationships, and a negative therapeutic reaction, in which a correct interpretation made the patient worse, more depressed and angrier, instead of better. Stern is generally credited with the first formal description of the borderline patient.

Over the next fifteen years several writers described patients on the same frontier, each from a different angle. The Polish-born analyst Helene Deutsch (1884 to 1982) described the as-if personality in 1942: people who seemed normal and even charming but whose relationships and feelings were borrowed, imitated from whoever they were with, as if they had no core of their own. In 1949 the New York psychiatrists Paul Hoch and Phillip Polatin described pseudoneurotic schizophrenia, patients whose "pan-anxiety", "pan-neurosis" and chaotic "pan-sexuality" seemed to them to conceal a latent schizophrenia. And in 1953 Robert Palmer Knight (1902 to 1966), then medical director of the Austen Riggs Center in Massachusetts, wrote on borderline states, patients whose ego functions were seriously weakened beneath a surface that held together in structured situations; such patients could look neurotic in a conventional interview and then regress alarmingly in an unstructured one, in free association or on a projective test. Notice that in all these accounts "borderline" still meant "almost schizophrenic". You have already seen, in Section 11.2.7, how that meaning survived in Kety's "borderline schizophrenia" and in ICD-10's list of terms included under schizotypal disorder, which even names pseudoneurotic schizophrenia.

The decisive reinterpretation came from Otto F. Kernberg (born 1928), a Vienna-born psychoanalyst who trained in Chile and worked in the United States, in a paper of 1967 titled "Borderline personality organization". Kernberg proposed that "borderline" was not a halfway house on the road to schizophrenia but a stable way in which a personality is organised, one of three levels. He defined the level by three structural features. The first is identity diffusion: a poorly integrated concept of self and of significant others, so that contradictory images of the self (and of the same other person) exist side by side, unconnected. The second is the use of primitive defences, above all splitting, the keeping apart of all-good and all-bad images, with its relatives primitive idealisation, projective identification, denial, omnipotence and devaluation; Section 11.4 teaches these in full. The third is that reality testing, the capacity to tell inner experience from outer reality and to recognise what others would regard as real, is generally preserved, although it may slip under severe stress. A neurotic organisation has an integrated identity, higher-level defences centred on repression and intact reality testing; a psychotic organisation has identity diffusion, primitive defences and lost reality testing; the borderline personality organisation lies between, sharing identity diffusion and primitive defences with the psychotic level but keeping reality testing. Two cautions follow. Kernberg's borderline organisation is much broader than today's borderline personality disorder: it includes most severe personality disorders, narcissistic, antisocial, paranoid and schizoid among them. And it is a psychoanalytic account of structure, not a list of symptoms; Book 2, Section 3.4.7 explains it as the basis of his transference-focused psychotherapy.

If Kernberg gave the borderline concept a theory, Chicago gave it data. In 1968 the psychiatrist Roy R. Grinker (1900 to 1993) and his colleagues Beatrice Werble and Robert Drye published The Borderline Syndrome, the first systematic empirical study of such patients. At Michael Reese Hospital they rated the observable ego functions of about fifty hospitalised patients (secondary accounts give the number as 50 or 51) and submitted the ratings to a cluster analysis. Four subgroups emerged, running from a group close to the psychotic border, through a "core" borderline group and an affectless, "as if" group, to a group bordering on the neuroses. More important was what the groups shared: anger as the main or only emotion, a defect in affectional relationships, the absence of any consistent self-identity, and depressive loneliness. Every one of these survives in the modern criteria.

The next step was to make the diagnosis reliable. In 1975 John G. Gunderson (1942 to 2019), of McLean Hospital near Boston, and the psychologist Margaret Thaler Singer (1921 to 2003) reviewed the descriptive literature and concluded that its contradictions came from who was doing the describing, in what setting and with what samples. They identified six features that could be assessed in an initial interview: intense affect, usually depressive or hostile; a history of impulsive behaviour; a certain social adaptiveness, a surface of appropriate behaviour; brief psychotic experiences; loose thinking in unstructured situations; and relationships that swing between transient superficiality and intense dependency. Gunderson and his colleagues later built these into a semi-structured interview, the Diagnostic Interview for Borderline Patients (1981; the Assessment book describes it).

The final step, which you met from the other side in Section 11.2.7, was taken in 1979 by Robert Spitzer, Jean Endicott and Miriam Gibbon. Their review found that "borderline" was being used in two quite different senses, and they divided it: the "borderline schizophrenia" sense became schizotypal personality disorder, and the unstable, impulsive, emotionally stormy sense became borderline personality disorder. DSM-III (1980) placed both on Axis II. Its borderline criteria were eight, with five needed, and included "intolerance of being alone", a forerunner of today's abandonment criterion. DSM-IV (1994) added a ninth criterion for transient, stress-related paranoid ideation or severe dissociative symptoms, and the nine have stayed unchanged through DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022). The borderline concept had travelled from a border between neurosis and psychosis to a disorder of emotion, impulse, self and relationship. Only the name stayed behind.

The WHO never liked the name. ICD-10 (1992) calls the condition emotionally unstable personality disorder (F60.3): a marked tendency to act impulsively without considering the consequences, together with affective instability, minimal ability to plan ahead, and outbursts of intense anger that can lead to violence, especially when impulsive acts are criticised or thwarted. It has two variants. The impulsive type (F60.30) is dominated by emotional instability and lack of impulse control, with outbursts of violence or threatening behaviour, particularly in response to criticism; it includes what used to be called explosive or aggressive personality. The borderline type (F60.31) has several features of emotional instability plus disturbance of self-image, aims and internal preferences (including sexual ones), chronic feelings of emptiness, and a liability to intense and unstable relationships that causes repeated emotional crises, with excessive efforts to avoid abandonment and a series of suicidal threats or acts of self-harm. The borderline type, not the impulsive type, is the equivalent of DSM borderline personality disorder. ICD-11, which abolished the ten types, kept one link to this history, the optional borderline pattern qualifier (6D11.5), largely so that clinicians could continue to use the large body of treatment research on borderline personality disorder (Section 11.8 explains the model). Table 11.6 summarises the journey.

Table 11.6 The borderline concept from 1938 to ICD-11.
YearWho or whatIdeaWhat survives
1938Adolph Stern"Border line group" of neuroses; patients who worsen in analysisThe name; the negative therapeutic reaction
1942Helene Deutsch"As-if" personality: borrowed feelings, no coreIdentity disturbance, emptiness
1949Hoch and PolatinPseudoneurotic schizophreniaAn ICD-10 inclusion term under schizotypal disorder
1953Robert KnightBorderline states: ego weakness, regression when unstructuredBrief stress-related psychotic-like experiences
1967Otto KernbergBorderline personality organisation: identity diffusion, primitive defences, intact reality testingSplitting; transference-focused psychotherapy
1968Grinker, Werble and DryeFirst empirical study: about 50 inpatients, four subgroupsAnger, unstable relationships, poor identity, loneliness
1975Gunderson and SingerSix features usable in an interviewImpulsivity, intense affect, unstable relationships
1979 to 1980Spitzer, Endicott and Gibbon; DSM-III"Borderline" split into borderline and schizotypal PDBoth disorders, still in DSM-5-TR
1992ICD-10Emotionally unstable PD: impulsive and borderline typesF60.30 and F60.31
1994DSM-IVNinth criterion: stress-related paranoia or dissociationThe nine criteria of DSM-5-TR
