Exam trap

Adolph Stern is credited with introducing "borderline" as a diagnostic term (1938), for patients on the border between neurosis and psychosis; Kernberg described borderline personality organisation (1967), a broader structural level, not the DSM disorder; Grinker did the first empirical study (1968); Gunderson defined the criteria and built the Diagnostic Interview for Borderline Patients. ICD-10's name is emotionally unstable personality disorder, and its borderline type (F60.31), not the impulsive type, matches DSM borderline personality disorder. "Borderline" in borderline intellectual functioning (an intellectual level just above intellectual disability and not a mental disorder, Chapter 3, Section 3.2.4) has nothing to do with any of this.

Natasha: meeting the pattern

Natasha is 23 and in the second year of an MA in a college in Pune. She was brought to the emergency department of a general hospital at two in the morning by her flatmate, having cut her left forearm several times with a razor blade and swallowed ten of the sleeping tablets her mother had left after a visit. The trigger was ordinary. Her boyfriend had not replied to her messages for three hours, and when he did, he wrote that he was tired and going to sleep. Over the next hour she sent him forty-seven messages, rang two of his friends, wrote "You'll be sorry when I'm gone", and then locked herself in the bathroom.

When the psychologist saw her the next morning she was calm, ashamed, articulate and at moments funny. "I wasn't trying to die," she said. "I just needed the screaming in my head to stop. When I cut, it stops." Over two long interviews, and a conversation with her mother, a history emerged. Since about fifteen her friendships had followed one plot: a new friend was "my soulmate, the only person who gets me" within a week and "the most selfish person I've ever met" within a few months. She had started engineering, switched to fashion design and then to psychology, had changed her style, her friends and even the spelling of her name, and said, "I don't know who I am. I become whoever I'm with. When I'm alone I'm nobody. Empty, like a shell." She went on spending sprees on a credit card she could not repay, ate in uncontrollable binges late at night, drank heavily at weekends and had had unprotected sex with near strangers. Her moods changed within a day: she could wake up hopeful, be in despair by lunch after a curt message from a professor, and furious by evening. She had once thrown her phone at her flatmate. Twice, during quarrels, she had felt "as if I'm watching myself in a film", and once she had become convinced for an afternoon that her classmates were plotting to have her thrown out of college; by the evening the conviction had gone. She had seen a counsellor a year earlier and described her as "the only one who has ever understood me", until the counsellor went on leave for two weeks, after which Natasha never went back. She had never been elated for days on end, never gone without sleep and felt full of energy, and had never heard voices.

Natasha's story will run through this section, because almost every element of it is a criterion. DSM-5-TR defines borderline personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of instability of interpersonal relationships, self-image and affects, and marked impulsivity, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, as indicated by five or more of nine criteria. Read that definition slowly and you can see its architecture. There are three instabilities (relationships, self-image, affect) and one excess (impulsivity). The pattern must be pervasive (it shows at college, at home, with friends and with lovers, not only with one difficult boyfriend), it must be long-standing, traceable at least to early adulthood, and it must meet the general criteria for a personality disorder described in Section 11.1, including distress or impairment.

The five-of-nine rule makes the criteria polythetic: no single criterion is required, and many different combinations qualify. A little arithmetic shows how many. The number of ways of choosing five, six, seven, eight or nine criteria from nine is 126 + 84 + 36 + 9 + 1, which comes to 256 different combinations, all of them borderline personality disorder. Because any two sets of five drawn from nine must overlap in at least one criterion, two patients with the same diagnosis may share only a single feature. One may be defined by abandonment fears, emptiness, identity disturbance, unstable relationships and dissociation, another by anger, impulsivity, self-harm, mood reactivity and paranoid ideas under stress. This heterogeneity is one of the strongest arguments for the dimensional models of Section 11.8, and it is worth remembering when two textbook vignettes of "borderline" patients look quite different.

Exam trap

Borderline personality disorder needs five or more of nine criteria. The distractors in a "how many" question are the thresholds of other disorders: 3 (of 7) is antisocial; 4 is paranoid, schizoid and avoidant (of 7) and obsessive-compulsive (of 8); no personality disorder needs 6. The other "five" disorders are schizotypal and narcissistic (5 of 9) and histrionic and dependent (5 of 8). The definition's core is instability of relationships, self-image and affects, plus marked impulsivity, beginning by early adulthood.

Relationships and the self: criteria 1, 2, 3 and 7

Criterion 1: frantic efforts to avoid real or imagined abandonment. The word "frantic" is chosen carefully. Everybody dislikes being left; the person with borderline personality disorder experiences even a brief or imagined separation as a catastrophe and acts urgently to prevent it. DSM-5-TR's examples are telling because they are so small: sudden despair when a clinician announces that the session is ending, panic or fury when someone important is a few minutes late or has to cancel. The perception of impending separation can transform the person's self-image, feelings, thinking and behaviour, and the abandonment often means, to them, that they are "bad". Underneath lies an intolerance of being alone and a need to have other people present. Natasha's forty-seven messages in an hour, and her calls to her boyfriend's friends, are criterion 1. One rule of counting matters here: self-harm and suicidal acts done to prevent abandonment are counted under criterion 5, not criterion 1, so that the same behaviour is not scored twice.

Criterion 2: a pattern of unstable and intense relationships, alternating between extremes of idealisation and devaluation. The person may idealise a new friend, lover or carer at the first or second meeting, want to spend a great deal of time together and share intimate details very early, and then switch abruptly to seeing the same person as uncaring, withholding or cruel: the other person "does not give enough" or "is not there enough". The same person is a perfect rescuer one week and a punishing persecutor the next; the switch is often a disillusionment, when an idealised carer fails, as every real carer eventually does. DSM-5-TR adds that these individuals can empathise with and nurture others, but on the expectation that the other person will be there on demand in return. Natasha's soulmates who become the most selfish people she has ever met, and the counsellor who was "the only one who understood" until she took leave, are criterion 2. The mental operation behind this pattern, splitting, is the subject of Section 11.4.

Criterion 3: identity disturbance, a markedly and persistently unstable self-image or sense of self. Here the instability is inside, and the manual calls it identity disturbance. There are sudden and dramatic shifts in goals, values, career plans, friends, sexual identity and the kind of person one is; DSM-5-TR's vivid example is the switch from "needy supplicant for help" to "righteous avenger of past mistreatment". The self-image is usually based on a feeling of being bad or evil, and at times the person feels that they do not exist at all, which is frightening; such moments usually come when meaningful relationships and support are missing. People with the disorder may do worse in unstructured settings at work or college, and the lack of a stable identity makes it harder for them to see their own repeating patterns. Natasha's three courses, her changing style and name, and "I become whoever I'm with" are criterion 3.

Criterion 7: chronic feelings of emptiness. Patients describe a hollowness, a void, a sense of being "nobody" or "a shell", often bound up with painful aloneness and boredom; easily bored, they may seek excitement to escape the feeling. Claire, the patient in Barlow and Durand's case history, cut her own hair severely when things went badly because it helped to "fill the void". Emptiness is one of the features that best separates borderline personality disorder from histrionic personality disorder, as you will see in Section 11.3.7. Natasha's "When I'm alone I'm nobody. Empty, like a shell" is criterion 7 in her own words.

DSM-5-TR lists some associated features that belong with this group. People with the disorder may undermine themselves at the very moment a goal is about to be reached: dropping out just before graduation, regressing sharply after a session in which therapy seemed to be going well, destroying a good relationship just when it is clear that it could last. And they may feel more secure with a transitional object, a pet or an inanimate possession, than with people; the term is the paediatrician and analyst Donald Woods Winnicott's (1896 to 1971), for the soft toy or blanket that a small child uses to bear separation from its mother.

Exam trap

"Identity disturbance and fear of abandonment" (IHBAS 2025) and "feels 'no one' and 'empty inside', with chronic fears of abandonment and intense unstable relationships" (2024) both describe borderline personality disorder. The 2024 item then asks which psychoanalytic account explains this pathology of the self and keys "a failure in parental mirroring and idealising, leading to a fragmented self". Choose it, but know that it is strictly Heinz Kohut's self psychology, a close relative of object relations theory, built mainly to explain narcissistic pathology (Section 11.4 and Book 2, Section 3.5.1). The three distractors are classical Freudian explanations of other problems: oral fixation (Freud and Karl Abraham's account of dependency and depression), an unresolved Oedipal complex with castration anxiety (the core conflict of neurosis, which presupposes the integrated self the borderline person lacks) and a harsh superego producing guilt (melancholia and obsessional neurosis). In the same IHBAS item the wrong options are schizoid (no wish for relationships), histrionic (attention-seeking without identity disturbance) and antisocial (no fear of abandonment).

Affect, anger, impulsive action and stress: criteria 4, 5, 6, 8 and 9

Criterion 6: affective instability due to a marked reactivity of mood. The wording repays attention. DSM-5-TR's example is "intense episodic dysphoria, irritability, or anxiety usually lasting a few hours and only rarely more than a few days". Three things are packed into that sentence. The mood is reactive: it moves in response to events, especially events in relationships. The episodes are short: hours, not weeks. And the moods are mostly painful ones, dysphoria, irritability and anxiety, not elation. The manual adds that the basic dysphoric mood is often broken by periods of anger, panic or despair and is rarely relieved by periods of well-being or satisfaction. This short-lived, event-driven shifting is what the manual means by affective instability. Natasha's day, hopeful at waking, in despair after a curt message and furious by evening, is criterion 6. This is why an option describing "regular and predictable changes in mood" is the opposite of borderline personality disorder: borderline mood is irregular, unpredictable from the calendar and highly predictable only from what has just happened between the person and someone who matters.

Criterion 8: inappropriate, intense anger or difficulty controlling anger. The manual's examples are frequent displays of temper, constant anger and recurrent physical fights; the anger may also come out as extreme sarcasm, enduring bitterness or verbal outbursts. It is most often provoked when a carer or lover is seen as neglectful, withholding, uncaring or abandoning, and it is frequently followed by shame and guilt, which feed the person's sense of being evil. Grinker's patients, you will remember, had anger as their main or only emotion. Natasha's thrown phone is criterion 8.

Criterion 4: impulsivity in at least two areas that are potentially self-damaging. The manual lists spending, sex, substance abuse, reckless driving and binge eating; its text adds gambling. "At least two areas" matters: a person who only drinks too much has a drinking problem, not this criterion. And, as with criterion 1, suicidal and self-mutilating acts are excluded here because they belong to criterion 5. Natasha's credit card, her binges, her weekend drinking and her unprotected sex give her four areas.

Criterion 5: recurrent suicidal behaviour, gestures or threats, or self-mutilating behaviour. Recurrent suicidal thoughts and acts are often the reason a person with borderline personality disorder first reaches help, as they were for Natasha. They are usually set off by a threat of separation or rejection, or by an expectation that the person take on more responsibility. Self-mutilation, deliberate injury to one's own body by cutting or burning, is very common and may happen during dissociative states. Patients describe its effect as relief; DSM-5-TR says that the acts often bring relief by reaffirming the person's ability to feel or by expiating their sense of being evil. Natasha's "When I cut, it stops" is a common description. Two warnings belong here. First, recurrent self-harm is a criterion of the disorder, but self-harm on its own does not make the diagnosis: many people who injure themselves without suicidal intent do not have borderline personality disorder (Chapter 5, Section 5.8.2, which teaches non-suicidal self-injury). Second, the label "gesture" must never become a reason to dismiss risk. People with this disorder do die by suicide, as Section 11.3.5 shows.

Criterion 9: transient, stress-related paranoid ideation or severe dissociative symptoms. Under extreme stress, usually a real or imagined abandonment, the person may become briefly suspicious or may dissociate: depersonalisation (feeling unreal, detached from one's body, "watching myself in a film"), derealisation (the world looking unreal) or other dissociative experiences (Chapter 7, Section 7.5). These episodes last minutes or hours, are generally too mild or brief to justify a separate diagnosis, and often ease when the carer's attention returns. Natasha's afternoon of conviction about her classmates and her two experiences of watching herself from outside are criterion 9. DSM-5-TR also notes, among the associated features, that some patients develop psychotic-like symptoms under stress (hallucinations, body-image distortions, ideas of reference, hypnagogic phenomena). They are brief and tied to stress, and if they last a day or more a brief psychotic disorder may be added (Chapter 4, Section 4.5.1).

Natasha meets all nine criteria; she would need only five. Figure 11.5 groups the nine into the five domains you have just worked through, and it is a useful way to hold them in memory: two criteria for relationships, two for the self, two for affect, two for behaviour and one for cognition under stress.

Exam trap

Which is NOT a borderline criterion? "Marked aggression toward animals or persistent rule-breaking before age 15" is conduct disorder, the childhood precursor of antisocial personality disorder (Sections 11.5 and 11.9); frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, recurrent self-harm and identity disturbance are criteria 1, 5 and 3. In the Amity 2023 item, "regular and predictable changes in mood" is keyed as not a cardinal feature, because borderline mood is reactive and unpredictable; but the option "eccentric behaviour marked by oddities in thinking and communication" is not a borderline feature either: it is schizotypal personality disorder (Section 11.2.6). The remaining two options, a lack of a well-defined and stable self-image and an enduring pattern of unstable relationships, are criteria 3 and 2. Two options are strictly correct. Mark the keyed mood option, the direct inversion of criterion 6, and remember why the other is also wrong. Paranoid ideation in borderline personality disorder is only transient and stress-related (criterion 9); pervasive suspiciousness is paranoid personality disorder.

Who receives the diagnosis, and what it costs

How common is borderline personality disorder? The answer depends on where you look and how you count. DSM-5-TR gives three community figures: 1.4 percent in a probability subsample of the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, 5.9 percent in the much larger National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions, and a median of 2.7 percent across seven epidemiological studies, six of them American (the 2013 edition of DSM-5 gave a median of 1.6 percent, so you will meet both figures). Barlow and Durand settle on about 1 to 2 percent of the general population. In clinical settings the disorder is far more concentrated: about 6 percent of patients in primary care, about 10 percent of people seen in outpatient mental health clinics and about 20 percent of psychiatric inpatients. Those clinical figures explain why the disorder seems so common to clinicians: people with it use services heavily, especially in crises.

Is it a disorder of women? In clinical samples, yes, by a wide margin; older editions of the DSM put the proportion of women at about three-quarters. But DSM-5-TR now states plainly that community samples show no difference in prevalence between men and women, and suggests that the clinical excess reflects greater help-seeking by women. Men may also be diverted elsewhere: the clinical characteristics are similar in the two sexes, but men and boys show more externalising behaviour (anger, fights, substance use), which brings them to prisons and addiction services, while women and girls show more internalising behaviour, which brings them to mental health clinics. Diagnostic bias plays a part too. In a study by Leslie Morey and Enrique Ochoa (1989), 291 mental health professionals described a patient with a personality disorder they had recently seen, gave their diagnosis and listed the symptoms; when the diagnoses were checked against the clinicians' own symptom reports, patients who were female, white or poor had been diagnosed with borderline personality disorder more often than the criteria warranted.

At what age? Borderline personality disorder was long regarded as a disorder of adults, but in treatment settings adolescents as young as 12 or 13 can meet the full criteria. A personality disorder can be diagnosed under 18 if the features have lasted at least a year (Section 11.1), and borderline personality disorder is the one most often diagnosed in this way; antisocial personality disorder is the only one that can never be diagnosed under 18. Natasha's pattern, by her mother's account, began at about fifteen.

The disorder seldom travels alone. DSM-5-TR lists as common companions the depressive and bipolar disorders, substance use disorders, anxiety disorders (panic disorder and social anxiety disorder in particular), eating disorders (notably bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder), post-traumatic stress disorder and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, as well as other personality disorders. In the American national survey, up to two-thirds of people with the disorder had at least one substance use disorder, and Barlow and Durand note that almost a quarter of people with bulimia also have borderline personality disorder. These overlaps matter because each makes the other harder to treat, and because, as Section 11.3.6 shows, some of them are also its chief mimics.

The gravest cost is death. Older editions of the manual put the proportion of people with the disorder who die by suicide at 8 to 10 percent. DSM-5-TR reports more recent prospective data: in a sample of hospitalised patients followed for 24 years, around 6 percent died by suicide, against 1.4 percent of a comparison group with other personality disorders. Barlow and Durand also give about 6 percent. Suicide is most likely in people with co-occurring depressive or substance use disorders, and impulsive and antisocial behaviour raise the risk further. The same manual adds a sobering correction to the usual focus on suicide: in people with borderline personality disorder, deaths from other causes, accidents and illness, are more than twice as common as deaths by suicide. And it gives one hopeful number: in a ten-year follow-up, the proportion of patients making suicide attempts fell from 79 percent to 13 percent. What that decline says about the course of the disorder is the subject of Section 11.4. The wider consequences are what one would expect from the criteria: repeated job losses, interrupted education, separations and divorces, and physical damage from self-injury and failed suicide attempts. The childhood histories of people with the disorder more often include physical and sexual abuse, neglect, hostile conflict and early parental loss; whether and how these cause the disorder is again a question for Section 11.4.

Finally, culture. DSM-5-TR says that the borderline pattern has been identified in many settings around the world, but it warns that some social contexts (heavy demands for self-affirmation and acceptance, ambiguous or conflictual relations with authority, marked uncertainty during adaptation to a new setting) can produce impulsivity, emotional instability, explosive behaviour and dissociation that look borderline but are transient reactions. It adds that features such as the number of sexual partners, shifting between relationships or substance use must be judged against cultural norms. A clinician in India should be especially careful with the first and the last: what counts as a reckless relationship or a "shifting" sexual life differs sharply between a metropolitan hostel and a small town, and a young woman who defies her family's choice of husband is not, by that fact alone, showing an unstable identity.

Clinical link

Treat every act of self-harm in a person with borderline personality disorder as a reason for a careful suicide risk assessment, not as "attention-seeking". Ask directly about intent, method, lethality, rescue and what has changed; look for the risk factors that raise the danger (a co-occurring depressive episode, substance use, impulsive and antisocial behaviour, recent loss, discharge from hospital). Chapter 5, Sections 5.8 and 5.9 teach suicide risk and prevention; Book 2, Section 2.7 teaches the risk interview; dialectical behaviour therapy, which was built for exactly this group, is in Book 2, Section 5.8.

Borderline or bipolar? Mood that moves by the hour

The most consequential mistake in diagnosing borderline personality disorder is to confuse it with a mood disorder, in either direction. A person with borderline personality disorder whose moods change several times a day may be told that she has "rapid-cycling bipolar disorder" and be given a mood stabiliser that does little for her; a person with bipolar II disorder whose hypomanias have been missed may be told that she is "just borderline" and be denied a treatment that would help. The two can also coexist, and DSM-5-TR says that when the criteria for both are met, both should be diagnosed. So how does one tell them apart? Figure 11.6 shows the essential difference, which is a difference of time.

Five questions separate the two, and they are worth asking in this order. How long does a mood state last? Borderline shifts last hours, rarely more than a few days; a manic episode lasts at least a week, a hypomanic episode at least four days and a major depressive episode at least two weeks (Chapter 5, Sections 5.1 and 5.4). What sets it off? Borderline shifts are reactive, triggered by events in relationships, often small ones; bipolar episodes may follow stress or lost sleep but often arise on their own and then run a course independent of what happens. What is the mood? Borderline mood moves among anxiety, anger, despair and emptiness; sustained elation, grandiosity, a decreased need for sleep, racing thoughts and increased goal-directed energy belong to mania and hypomania, not to borderline personality disorder. What happens in between? In bipolar disorder there are usually periods of normal mood between episodes; in borderline personality disorder the background is a chronic dysphoria with no clean return to well-being. Is it a trait or an episode? Borderline instability is how the person has been since adolescence, in all relationships; bipolar episodes are changes from the person's usual self, with a recognisable beginning and end.

Apply the questions to Natasha. Her moods last hours; they follow messages from her boyfriend and her professor; they are despair and fury, never days of elation; she has never gone without sleep full of energy; and the pattern has been there since she was fifteen. The diagnosis is borderline personality disorder. Had her mother reported that at nineteen Natasha had spent a week barely sleeping, talking incessantly, planning to start three businesses and spending her savings on them, a bipolar disorder would also have to be considered, and perhaps both diagnoses given.

The same logic applies to depression. Borderline personality disorder very often coexists with major depressive disorder, and a depressive episode can make almost anyone look unstable, empty and hopeless for a few weeks. DSM-5-TR therefore warns against giving an additional diagnosis of borderline personality disorder on the strength of a cross-sectional presentation alone, during a depressive or bipolar episode, without having documented that the pattern began early and has lasted long. A personality disorder is diagnosed from a life history, not from a single interview on a bad day.

Exam trap

Borderline mood is reactive, brief (hours) and interpersonally triggered; bipolar episodes are sustained (days to weeks), often autonomous, and include elation, grandiosity and a decreased need for sleep. "Regular and predictable changes in mood" fits neither borderline personality disorder nor most bipolar disorder; it is the textbook wrong option for borderline personality disorder. Mood that swings several times a day in response to events points towards borderline personality disorder, not towards rapid-cycling bipolar disorder (which counts at least four whole episodes in a year). Both can be diagnosed together.

Borderline or another personality disorder?

Most examination questions on borderline personality disorder are really questions about its neighbours. The stem describes one disorder; the options describe four. Learning the neighbours is therefore half the work, and DSM-5-TR's own differential diagnosis gives the key distinction for each.

Histrionic personality disorder shares attention-seeking, manipulative behaviour and rapidly shifting emotions with borderline personality disorder. The manual says that borderline personality disorder is distinguished by self-destructiveness, angry disruptions in close relationships, and chronic feelings of deep emptiness and loneliness. The histrionic person wants an audience; the borderline person wants not to be left. Dependent personality disorder shares the fear of abandonment, and this is the pair most likely to be confused. The difference lies in the response. When a relationship is threatened, the person with borderline personality disorder reacts with emptiness, rage and demands; the person with dependent personality disorder reacts with increasing appeasement and submissiveness and, if the relationship ends, urgently seeks a replacement who will provide care and support. The dependent person's relationships are also clinging but not stormy, and her identity, though built around being looked after, is not unstable. Narcissistic and paranoid personality disorders share the angry reaction to minor slights. What distinguishes them from borderline personality disorder, in the manual's words, is the relative stability of the self-image and the relative lack of physical self-destructiveness, repeated impulsivity and profound abandonment concerns. The narcissistic person's self is grandiose and stable, however fragile beneath; the borderline person's self is unstable and often bad. Antisocial personality disorder shares manipulation, but the goal differs: the antisocial person manipulates for profit, power or some other material gratification, the borderline person to gain the concern of carers. Schizotypal personality disorder shares paranoid ideas and illusions, but in borderline personality disorder these are more transient, more interpersonally reactive and more responsive to external structure. When the criteria for more than one personality disorder are met, all are diagnosed, and borderline personality disorder co-occurs with each of these. Table 11.7 puts the main comparisons side by side.

Table 11.7 Borderline personality disorder and the Cluster B and dependent look-alikes.
DisorderCore motive or fearSelf-imageWhen a relationship is threatenedTell-tale signs
BorderlineNot to be abandonedUnstable; often "bad"; at times "no one"Panic, rage, demands, self-harmIdealise then devalue; emptiness; recurrent self-harm
Dependent (Cluster C)To be taken care ofStable but helpless and inadequateAppeasement, submission; quickly finds a replacementNeeds reassurance for everyday decisions; cannot disagree
HistrionicTo be the centre of attentionStable, image-consciousMore dramatic display; seductivenessTheatrical, shallow shifting emotion; suggestible
NarcissisticTo be admired; to protect a grandiose selfStable and grandioseScorn, cold withdrawal or rage at the slightEntitlement, envy, lack of empathy
AntisocialGain, power, pleasureStable and untroubledIndifference; moves on; exploitsDeceit, aggression, no remorse; conduct disorder before 15

The 2024 question that asks what borderline personality disorder has but narcissistic personality disorder lacks is now easy. Three of its four options are criteria of narcissistic personality disorder (Section 11.6): a grandiose sense of self-importance, a need for excessive admiration, and the belief that one is "special" and can be understood only by, or should associate only with, other special or high-status people. The fourth, pervasive instability of relationships and self-image, is the defining core of borderline personality disorder and is not part of narcissistic personality disorder; so are identity disturbance, chronic emptiness, recurrent self-harm and frantic fear of abandonment.

Exam trap

Decode the option phrases before you answer. "Detachment and social withdrawal" or "detachment from social relationships and restricted emotion": schizoid. "Social inhibition, fear of rejection, desire for acceptance": avoidant. "Eccentric beliefs and odd speech without psychosis": schizotypal. "Paranoid ideation" as the main feature: paranoid. "Obsessive perfectionism": obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. "Pervasive grandiosity and lack of empathy": narcissistic. "Excessive attention-seeking, emotional volatility, dramatic behaviour": histrionic. "Disregard for others' rights, manipulative behaviour" or "manipulation, lack of remorse, irresponsibility": antisocial. "Unstable relationships, impulsive behaviour, fear of abandonment", "emotional instability and impulsivity", "instability in relationships, self-image and affect with marked impulsivity", and a vignette of idealising and devaluing, frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, an unstable self-image and recurrent self-harm: borderline, every time.

Borderline, trauma, conduct and the adolescent

Four further boundaries come up again and again, two in adults and two in the young.

Complex post-traumatic stress disorder. ICD-11's complex PTSD (Chapter 7, Section 7.2.8) adds to the PTSD core three disturbances in self-organisation: affect dysregulation, a persistently negative self-concept and difficulty in relationships. Since many people with borderline personality disorder report childhood trauma, the overlap is large. Chapter 7 gives the distinction: in borderline personality disorder the self-image is unstable and shifting, relationships are clung to and fought over, and repeated self-harm and suicidal behaviour are central; in complex PTSD the self-image is stable and stably negative ("I am worthless"), the person tends to avoid closeness rather than cling to it, and repeated self-harm is not a defining feature (self-destructive behaviour may appear only as one example of affect dysregulation). Trauma is required for complex PTSD but not for borderline personality disorder. A latent class analysis by Marylène Cloitre and her colleagues (2014) found that the two diagnoses picked out largely different groups of patients; they can coexist, and the distinction matters because the treatments differ.

Dissociative identity disorder and the other mimics in adults. Borderline personality disorder shares identity disturbance and stress-related dissociation with dissociative identity disorder, but it has no distinct alternate identities and no amnesia between them (Chapter 7, Section 7.6). Separation anxiety disorder in adults shares the fear of losing loved ones, but DSM-5-TR notes that problems of identity, self-direction, interpersonal functioning and impulsivity are central only to borderline personality disorder. Substance use can produce every one of the criteria during intoxication or withdrawal, so the pattern must be seen outside those states. Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder shares impulsivity, but begins in childhood with inattention and restlessness and lacks the abandonment fears and identity disturbance; the two often coexist. And a personality change due to another medical condition, after a head injury, for instance, is a change from an earlier, different personality, not a pattern since adolescence.

The adolescent and the identity problem. Adolescence is a time of trying on identities, intense friendships and passionate moods, and DSM-5-TR warns that adolescents and young adults with an identity problem, especially when substance use is added, may transiently display behaviour that misleadingly suggests borderline personality disorder. The manual describes such periods as marked by emotional instability, existential dilemmas, uncertainty, anxiety-provoking choices, conflicts about sexual orientation and competing social pressures to decide on a career. An identity problem is tied to a developmental phase and is not a mental disorder. The distinction depends on pervasiveness and duration: Natasha's instability has lasted eight years and shows in every part of her life, which is why the one-year rule for diagnosing a personality disorder under 18 exists.

Conduct disorder. Here a practice question asks which feature most reliably distinguishes borderline personality disorder from conduct disorder, and the answer it keys is the right one in spirit: borderline personality disorder is a pattern of instability of identity and affect and fear of abandonment, while conduct disorder is a repetitive and persistent pattern of violating the basic rights of others or major age-appropriate rules, through aggression to people and animals, destruction of property, deceitfulness or theft, and serious violations of rules (Section 11.9). Its wrong options are easy to dismiss once the disorders are understood. The two are not identical conditions. Conduct disorder does not "always become" borderline personality disorder; in most young people it remits by adulthood, and when it does persist the adult outcome is antisocial personality disorder, which requires conduct disorder before 15 (Section 11.5), not borderline personality disorder. And neither disorder is "exclusively biological" or "exclusively environmental": both arise from genes acting with adverse environments (Sections 11.4 and 11.9). The keyed answer's ages, however, are a simplification that you should not repeat in a viva. Borderline personality disorder can be diagnosed in adolescents whose features have lasted a year, and conduct disorder can be diagnosed in a person of 18 or older if the criteria for antisocial personality disorder are not met; what truly separates the two is the content of the pattern, not the age of the patient. Table 11.8 collects the boundaries of this subsection and the last.

Table 11.8 Borderline personality disorder and its other look-alikes.
ConditionWhat it shares with BPDWhat separates it
Bipolar disorderUnstable mood, impulsivityEpisodes of days to weeks; elation and less need for sleep; normal mood between episodes
Major depressive episodeHopelessness, emptiness, suicidalityAn episode with a beginning; BPD needs a pattern since early adulthood
Complex PTSD (ICD-11)Affect dysregulation, poor self-concept, relationship problemsTrauma required; stable negative self; avoids closeness; self-harm not defining
Dissociative identity disorderIdentity disturbance, dissociationDistinct identities with amnesia between them
Separation anxiety disorder (adult)Fear of losing loved onesNo identity disturbance, impulsivity or stormy relationships

Exam trap

Borderline personality disorder versus conduct disorder: identity and affective instability with fear of abandonment versus rule violation and aggression. Conduct disorder leads, in a minority, to antisocial personality disorder, never "always" to borderline. Strictly, borderline personality disorder can be diagnosed before 18 (features present for at least a year), and conduct disorder can be diagnosed at 18 or older if antisocial personality disorder is not met; only antisocial personality disorder is barred before 18. Complex PTSD is an ICD-11 diagnosis with a stable negative self-image; borderline personality disorder has an unstable one.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  4. In schizotypal personality disorder the person has ideas of reference, which can be doubted, not of reference.

  7. The term "borderline" was introduced in 1938 by for patients on the border between neurosis and psychosis.
  8. Kernberg's borderline personality organisation combines identity diffusion, primitive defences and generally intact .

11.4 Borderline Personality Disorder II: Defences, Causes, Course and TreatmentMedium yield · 3 Qs

Section 11.3 described borderline personality disorder from the outside: nine criteria, five of which must be present, and the disorders it is most often confused with. A list of criteria tells you what to look for, but it does not tell you why a person who longs to be close to others keeps destroying the very relationships she depends on, why her picture of the people around her flips so suddenly from rescuer to betrayer, or what happens to her over the following ten or twenty years. Those are the questions of this section. We begin inside the mind, with the defences that psychoanalysts placed at the centre of the disorder; move to the four modern accounts of where it comes from (psychodynamic, biosocial, attachment and biological); and end with what happens over time and what helps.

Keep one person in mind as you read. Rhea is 24 and works at the front desk of a large hotel in Bengaluru. She was brought to a general hospital's emergency department at two in the morning after cutting her forearm, the third time in a year, when her boyfriend did not answer her calls after an argument. In her first session with the clinical psychologist the next week she said, with tears in her eyes, "You are the first person who has ever really listened to me." Three weeks later the psychologist had to move an appointment by a day. That evening Rhea sent a message: "You are exactly like everyone else. You never cared about me. I don't know why I bothered." Those two sentences, a few weeks apart, contain the defence that psychoanalysts consider the signature of the disorder.

Splitting: the defence at the centre

A defence mechanism is an unconscious mental operation that protects a person from anxiety, shame or other painful feeling by altering her awareness of a wish, a feeling, a fact or a relationship. Sigmund Freud described the first of them; his daughter Anna Freud catalogued them in 1936; and George Eman Vaillant ranked them from immature to mature in 1977 (Book 2, Sections 1.7.3 and 3.4.1 teach all three). What matters here is that defences differ not only in what they do but in how much of the mind they require. Some, such as repression, need a fairly well-integrated self that can push one wish out of awareness while keeping the rest of experience intact. Others work at a more primitive level, before such integration has been achieved. Borderline personality disorder is the disorder of the primitive defences, and the chief of them is splitting.

The idea comes from the Viennese-born British analyst Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960), whose theory of the infant mind is taught in Book 2, Section 3.4.4. Klein argued that a baby cannot at first grasp that the mother who feeds and soothes is the same person as the mother who keeps it waiting. It therefore experiences two separate mothers, a wholly good one and a wholly bad one, and keeps them apart so that its rage at the bad one cannot spoil the good. This way of organising experience she called the paranoid-schizoid position. Around the middle of the first year the infant begins to see that the two are one whole person, whom it both loves and hates, and enters the depressive position, which brings the capacity for ambivalence and concern. Klein described the mechanism that keeps the good and bad images apart, splitting, most fully in 1946 in "Notes on some schizoid mechanisms", the same paper that introduced projective identification, which you will meet in the next subsection.

Otto F. Kernberg (born 1928) took the concept from Klein and made it the hallmark of what he called borderline personality organisation (described in 1967; Section 11.3 traced this history, and Book 2, Section 3.4.7 teaches the model in full). Splitting, in Kernberg's sense, is the active keeping apart of contradictory images of the self and of other people, so that each person is experienced as all good or all bad and the two images are never brought together into a single realistic picture. The motive, on his account, is protective: the person's loving images of herself and others are fragile, and her hostile images are so charged with aggression that if the two met, the good would be destroyed. Keeping them in separate compartments keeps something good alive, at the cost of never seeing anyone, herself included, as a whole.

Listen to how it sounds. The idealised figure is not merely liked; she is "the only one who understands me". When that person disappoints, even slightly, she does not become a good person who made a mistake; she becomes someone who "never cared". The switch is total, and while one image is active the other seems to have no emotional reality at all. If you remind Rhea, on the evening of her angry message, that she called you the first person who ever listened, she may agree that she said it, but the memory carries no weight: "That was before I knew what you were really like." Splitting applies to the self as well. Rhea describes herself on good days as "a completely different person, confident, I can do anything", and on bad days as "rotten inside, evil, I deserve to be hurt", with nothing in between. This rapid alternation of all-good and all-bad images is what drives two of the criteria listed in Section 11.3, the unstable, intense relationships that swing between idealisation and devaluation and the identity disturbance.

Splitting must be told apart from two ordinary experiences. The first is ambivalence, holding loving and angry feelings about the same person at the same time ("I'm furious with my mother, and I love her"). Ambivalence is the mature achievement that splitting prevents. The second is ordinary disappointment. Everyone revises their opinion of people; what marks splitting is that the revision is sudden, total and reversible, that it happens to the same people again and again, and that the previous view is not modified but simply switched off. Notice too that splitting, unlike repression, does not hide anything from awareness. Both images are conscious; they are simply never present at the same moment. That is the key to why it is classed as primitive, and to the examiners' favourite question about the disorder.

Clinical link

Splitting does not stay inside the patient. On a ward or in a team, a patient with borderline personality disorder may idealise one nurse or doctor and denigrate another, and the staff, each seeing only their own half, can begin to quarrel about the patient: one group argues that she is being treated harshly, the other that she is manipulating everyone. This is called staff splitting. The British psychoanalyst Tom Main described how a hospital staff became divided and exhausted over certain patients in a 1957 paper, "The ailment". The remedy is regular team discussion in which every member's view is pooled, so that the team, unlike the patient, holds the good and bad pictures together. Dialectical behaviour therapy builds this in as the weekly consultation team (Book 2, Section 5.8).

Exam trap

"Borderline personality disorder is most characteristically associated with which primary defence mechanism?" The answer is splitting (Real PYQ 2025; RML 2024). The distractors are real defences that belong elsewhere: repression is the core defence of the neurotic level, sublimation is a mature defence, rationalisation is a disavowing defence used by everyone, regression is a general retreat to earlier ways of functioning under stress, and undoing is the obsessional defence of cancelling one act with another. Splitting goes with Klein (who described it in the infant mind) and Kernberg (who made it the hallmark of borderline organisation). Do not confuse splitting with the Bleulerian "splitting" of mental functions in schizophrenia (Chapter 4), which has nothing to do with all-good and all-bad images.

The other primitive defences, and the ones that are not the answer

Kernberg described splitting as the centre of a cluster of defences that support it, all of which distort the person's images of self and others rather than hiding a wish from awareness. The most important, and the hardest to grasp, is projective identification, another of Klein's 1946 concepts. In ordinary projection a person attributes his own unacceptable feeling to someone else and then disowns it: the man who is attracted to a colleague accuses her of flirting with him. Projective identification goes further in three ways. The person places an intolerable feeling in another person; she stays emotionally connected to the feeling she has placed there, so she fears and tries to control the other person; and, by the way she behaves, she pressures the other person into actually feeling or acting out what has been projected. It is an interpersonal event, not only a private fantasy.

Here is how it happened with Rhea. In the fourth session, furious but unable to own her fury, she accused the psychologist of being irritated with her ("I can see it in your face; you want to get rid of me"). When the psychologist calmly denied it, Rhea became more contemptuous, mocked her qualifications and refused to answer questions, until the psychologist did begin to feel exasperated and a little hostile. At that point Rhea said, almost with relief, "See? You do hate me." The anger had been Rhea's; by the end of the hour it was also, genuinely, the therapist's. This is why therapists who work with borderline patients pay close attention to their own reactions (their countertransference, Book 2, Section 3.3): a sudden, uncharacteristic wish to punish, rescue or get rid of a patient is often information about what the patient cannot bear to feel.

The remaining primitive defences are easier. Primitive idealisation exaggerates the goodness and power of another person so that he becomes a perfect protector against a world full of bad figures; it is not admiration but a desperate need for someone who cannot fail. Its inevitable partner is devaluation: when the idealised figure disappoints, he is dropped as worthless, which also protects the person from envying him or depending on him. Omnipotence and omnipotent control describe the patient's sense of magical power over others, who are treated as extensions of herself and must be controlled completely (the ten messages in an hour, the demand that the therapist be reachable at any time). Primitive denial, in Kernberg's special sense, is the mental partner of splitting: the person knows perfectly well that she felt the opposite about someone yesterday, but the knowledge has no emotional force and cannot soften what she feels today. Table 11.9 gathers the group.

Table 11.9 The primitive defences of borderline personality organisation, as they appeared in Rhea's sessions.
DefenceWhat happensHow it sounded
SplittingSelf and others kept all good or all bad; the two images never meet"You are the only one who listens." Then: "You never cared."
Projective identificationAn intolerable feeling is placed in another person, who is pressed into feeling or enacting it"You want to get rid of me," repeated until the therapist feels hostile
Primitive idealisationAnother person is seen as perfect and all-powerful, a rescuer"You are the best doctor in this city. You will fix me."
DevaluationThe same person is dropped as worthless at the first disappointment"You are useless, like all the others."
Omnipotent controlOthers are treated as extensions to be controlled; magical sense of power over themTen messages in an hour; "Answer me now or I'll do something."
Primitive denialContradictory feelings are both known, but the other one has no emotional force"Yes, I said that. It doesn't matter. That was before."

Why, then, are the other famous defences not the answer when an examiner asks about borderline personality disorder? The clearest way to see it is to place every defence on a ladder (Figure 11.7). The ladder shown is the seven-level Defensive Functioning Scale that DSM-IV (1994) printed in its appendix of proposals for further study; Vaillant's four levels (psychotic, immature, neurotic and mature) compress the same idea. The mature defences at the top deal with conflict while keeping reality and feeling largely intact. One step down, the neurotic defences keep a threatening wish or memory out of awareness. Lower still come the defences that distort the image of self and others, and the defences of action and of psychosis at the bottom. Splitting and projective identification sit on the "major image-distorting" rung, below the defences of ordinary neurotic people and above those of psychosis, which is exactly where Kernberg placed borderline organisation: between neurosis and psychosis.

Now take the distractors one at a time (Table 11.10). Repression, the oldest of Freud's defences, keeps an unacceptable wish, feeling or memory out of consciousness, as when a woman cannot recall how angry she was with her father in the months before he died. It requires a self integrated enough to bury one part of experience while the rest carries on, and Kernberg made it the central defence of the neurotic level of organisation, the opposite pole to splitting. Sublimation channels an unacceptable impulse into a socially valued activity (aggression into competitive sport or surgery, sexual curiosity into art or science) and is one of the mature defences. Rationalisation supplies plausible, acceptable reasons for behaviour whose real motive is unacceptable ("I didn't study because the syllabus was unfair anyway"); everyone uses it, it belongs to the disavowal level, and it is typical of no particular disorder. Regression is a return, under stress, to the ways of behaving of an earlier stage of development: the five-year-old who starts wetting the bed again when a baby brother arrives, or the competent adult who becomes demanding and childlike in a hospital bed. Borderline patients can regress, sometimes dramatically (DSM-5-TR mentions patients who regress severely just after a discussion of how well therapy is going), but regression is a general movement, not the disorder's characteristic defence. Undoing is an act that symbolically cancels an earlier unacceptable thought or act, such as repeatedly touching wood after wishing someone dead, or bringing flowers after an insult; it is the classic defence of obsessional neurosis and sits with repression on the neurotic level.

Table 11.10 The distractor defences: what each one is and why it is not the borderline answer.
DefenceWhat it doesLevelTypical of
RepressionKeeps an unacceptable wish or memory out of awarenessNeurotic (mental inhibitions)Hysteria; Kernberg's neurotic organisation
UndoingCancels a thought or act with an opposite actNeurotic (mental inhibitions)Obsessional neurosis
RationalisationGives acceptable reasons for unacceptable motivesDisavowalEveryone; no specific disorder
SublimationRedirects an impulse into valued activityMature (high adaptive)Healthy adults
RegressionReturns to earlier developmental modes under stressNot a level: a movement downIllness, stress, any disorder
SplittingKeeps all-good and all-bad images apartMajor image-distortingBorderline personality disorder
Exam trap

Two real papers ask the same thing with different distractors: "repression, splitting, sublimation, rationalisation" and "splitting, regression, undoing, sublimation". Splitting is the answer to both. If a question asks which defence is also typical of borderline personality disorder, look for projective identification, primitive idealisation, devaluation, omnipotence or primitive denial. Do not confuse projective identification with simple projection: in projection the feeling is attributed to someone else and disowned; in projective identification the person stays connected to it and induces the other person to feel it. Projection on its own is the characteristic defence of paranoid personality (Section 11.2), not of borderline personality.

Psychodynamic accounts: conflict or deficit?

Why would a person come to rely on splitting long after childhood? The psychodynamic answers fall into two families. A conflict model says that the patient has the capacities a healthy adult has, but that intense, unmanageable feelings (above all aggression) force her to use defences that distort her picture of herself and others. A deficit model says that something needed for normal development was never supplied, so a psychological capacity is simply missing. Kernberg's theory is the great example of the first; Heinz Kohut's and Gerald Adler's are examples of the second. The difference matters, because it decides what the therapist does: a conflict is interpreted, a deficit is repaired.

Kernberg's account, introduced in Section 11.3 as a level of personality organisation, is also a theory of cause. He traced borderline organisation to an excess of early aggression, arising either from the child's constitution (an inborn temperament prone to intense negative feeling) or from severe early frustration, neglect or abuse. A child flooded with rage cannot afford to bring her loving and hating images of the same mother together, because the hate would overwhelm the love; so she keeps them apart, and the splitting that Klein thought normal in infancy persists into adult life. Integration never happens, and identity remains diffuse: the adult self is a collection of contradictory part-images rather than a whole. Note that Kernberg's "borderline personality organisation" is broader than DSM's borderline personality disorder: it includes most of the severe personality disorders, among them narcissistic, antisocial and paranoid personality. His treatment, transference-focused psychotherapy, follows directly from the theory: the split images are allowed to appear in the relationship with the therapist and are clarified, confronted and interpreted until they can be integrated (Book 2, Section 3.4.7; its trials are weighed in Book 2, Section 3.11.7).

Heinz Kohut (1913 to 1981) founded self psychology, taught in full in Book 2, Section 3.5.1. On his account a child's self becomes firm and continuous only if two needs are met by the people around it, whom he called selfobjects: the need for mirroring, a parent's delighted reflection of the child's worth (the gleam in the mother's eye when the toddler shows off a scribble), and the need for an idealising relationship, a calm, strong parent whose strength the child can borrow. When both fail chronically, the self is left without structure. It is prone to fragmentation (the frightening sense of falling apart), to depletion (an emptiness and deadness), and to rage at the smallest slight. Kohut built the theory to explain the narcissistic personality disorders, and Section 11.6 compares his view of narcissism with Kernberg's. Later clinicians found that it describes borderline experience well too, above all the chronic emptiness and the sense of being nobody, which is why a question about a client who feels "no one" and "empty inside" expects Kohut's answer.

The Boston analysts Gerald Adler and Dan H. Buie proposed a second deficit model in a 1979 paper, "Aloneness and borderline psychopathology". They started from the intense, painful aloneness their borderline patients described, a feeling of being utterly alone in the world even when people who cared for them existed. They explained it with a developmental idea borrowed from Jean Piaget's work on object permanence: by about the end of the second year, most children can call up a comforting image of an absent mother and be soothed by it (evocative memory). Borderline patients, they argued, achieved this capacity only tenuously, after inconsistent care during the toddler's struggle to separate from the mother (Margaret Mahler's rapprochement subphase, Book 2, Section 3.4.6), and lose it under stress. When the therapist goes on leave or a partner fails to reply, the patient cannot summon an inner image of someone who cares, and the panic of utter aloneness follows. The theory makes sense of the frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, of the comfort some patients take in a transitional object (a photograph, a soft toy, a voice message replayed), and of the treatment advice that followed from it: be reliably available, prepare the patient for absences, and let her hold on to something of the therapist while the therapist is away. The American psychiatrist John G. Gunderson, whose diagnostic work you met in Section 11.3, reached a similar conclusion in 1996, describing intolerance of aloneness as a core deficit of the disorder.

Exam trap

A 2024 paper describes a client who feels she is "no one", is "empty inside", fears abandonment and has intense, unstable relationships, and asks what "psychoanalytic object relations theory" blames. The keyed answer, a failure in parental mirroring and idealising, leading to a fragmented self, is strictly Kohut's self psychology, not object relations theory. The two schools are close relatives (both study how early relationships build the self, and many textbooks group them), so choose it, but know the difference: object relations theory proper means Klein, Fairbairn, Winnicott and Kernberg, with their internalised images of self and others (Book 2, Section 3.4.3). The other options are classical Freudian answers: fixation at the oral stage (the psychosexual explanation of dependency, depression and addiction), an unresolved Oedipus complex with castration anxiety (the classical account of neurosis) and harsh superego demands with constant guilt (Freud's account of melancholia and obsessional neurosis, the "Guilty Man" Kohut contrasted with his "Tragic Man").

Linehan's biosocial theory

The most influential modern theory of the disorder came not from psychoanalysis but from behaviour therapy. Marsha M. Linehan (1943 to 2026) set out to treat chronically suicidal women at the University of Washington, failed with standard behaviour therapy, and built dialectical behaviour therapy on a theory she published in 1993. Book 2, Section 5.8.2 teaches the theory as the foundation of the therapy; here the question is what it says about the disorder. Linehan's biosocial theory holds that borderline personality disorder is, at root, a disorder of the emotion regulation system. It arises from a transaction, over many years, between a child who is biologically vulnerable to intense emotion and an environment that invalidates her emotional experience.

The biological half is emotional vulnerability, which has three parts: high sensitivity (a low threshold, so that a cool tone of voice or a late reply sets off an emotion other people would hardly notice), high reactivity (once triggered, the emotion is extreme and disorganises thinking and action) and a slow return to baseline (the emotion lasts, so that the next trigger arrives before the last one has faded). Linehan's own image is that such people are like patients with third-degree burns over most of the body: they have no emotional skin. The social half is the invalidating environment, a family or setting that responds to the child's account of her inner experience as wrong, exaggerated or unimportant. It dismisses or punishes emotional expression ("Stop crying, it's nothing"), it ignores ordinary distress but responds to extreme distress, so that the child learns that only extremes work, and it makes problems look easier to solve than they are ("If you really wanted to, you would just stop"). Abuse is a severe form of invalidation, but the theory does not require it: an ordinary, well-meaning family can be a poor fit for an unusually sensitive child.

The word that matters is transaction. Biology and environment do not simply add together; each changes the other over time (Figure 11.8). A highly reactive infant is hard to soothe; exhausted parents become dismissive; dismissal makes her more distressed; her escalating distress provokes more dismissal, and only her most extreme displays get a response. By adolescence she has learned neither to name her emotions, nor to trust them as valid, nor to soothe them, and she swings between suppressing them (invalidating herself as her family did) and displaying them in extreme forms. Self-harm, on this view, is not "attention-seeking" but a learned way of escaping unbearable emotion: cutting brings rapid relief, so it is negatively reinforced each time it works. Rhea said exactly this: "When I cut, the noise in my head stops." In 2009 Sheila E. Crowell, Theodore P. Beauchaine and Linehan extended the model developmentally, adding trait impulsivity in childhood as a second inherited vulnerability that the invalidating environment can shape into the full disorder.

How good is the evidence? The emotional half is well supported: people with the disorder report more intense and more variable negative emotions than other people in daily diary studies, and imaging studies (Section 11.4.6) show an overreactive emotional brain. The social half is harder to test, because "invalidation" is usually measured by asking adults to remember their childhoods, and memories coloured by the disorder itself are not neutral evidence. An exploratory study at the National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru, by J. Alafia and M. Manjula (2020), compared 34 adults with the disorder with matched controls and found more reported emotional abuse and physical punishment in the patients' childhoods, with emotional abuse and negative affect together accounting for 68.4 percent of the variation in their difficulties with emotion regulation: the pattern the theory predicts, in an Indian sample, although with the same reliance on recall. The theory's greatest strength is clinical. It is a no-blame account that families can accept, and every module of dialectical behaviour therapy was built to repair one part of the dysregulation it describes.

Exam trap

Linehan's theory is biosocial: emotional vulnerability × an invalidating environment, transacting over time, producing emotion dysregulation. Do not confuse it with George Engel's biopsychosocial model (1977), a general framework for all illness (Chapter 1, Section 1.3), or with the general diathesis-stress model. Key words that signal Linehan: "invalidating", "emotion dysregulation", "no emotional skin", "transaction". Key words that signal Kernberg: "splitting", "identity diffusion", "aggression". Key words that signal Kohut: "mirroring", "idealising", "fragmented self". Key words that signal Fonagy and Bateman (next): "mentalising", "reflective function", "psychic equivalence".

Attachment and mentalisation

Notice how many of the borderline criteria are about closeness: frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, relationships that swing between idealisation and devaluation, anger when a partner or caregiver seems neglectful, paranoid or dissociative episodes "most frequently in response to a real or imagined abandonment", as DSM-5-TR puts it. It was natural to ask whether the disorder grows out of disturbed early attachment, the bond between a child and her caregivers whose study John Bowlby began (Book 2, Section 3.5.5). Researchers have answered with the Adult Attachment Interview, which classifies the way adults talk about their own childhood attachments (Book 2, Section 3.5.6). In 2004 Agrawal, Gunderson and their colleagues reviewed 13 studies of borderline patients that had used the Adult Attachment Interview or other attachment measures. Every study found a strong association between the disorder and insecure attachment, and the most characteristic types were unresolved (disorganised talk about loss or trauma), preoccupied (entangled, angry or anxious absorption in past relationships) and fearful. In each of these types, the reviewers noted, the person longs for intimacy while dreading dependence and rejection, which is the borderline dilemma in a sentence.

Peter Fonagy (born 1952) and the British psychiatrist Anthony W. Bateman turned this finding into a theory of the disorder (taught in Book 2, Section 3.5.7, with the treatment it produced). Their key concept is mentalisation, the capacity to understand one's own and other people's behaviour in terms of mental states: feelings, wishes, beliefs and intentions. A child learns to mentalise, they argued, by finding her own mind reflected in the mind of an attuned caregiver, and secure attachment is its natural training ground. In borderline personality disorder the capacity is fragile, and it collapses precisely when the attachment system is switched on hardest: in a quarrel with a partner, at a hint of rejection, in the therapist's absence. When mentalising fails, older, prementalising ways of experiencing the mind take over. In psychic equivalence a feeling is taken as fact ("I feel you hate me, so you do"); in the teleological mode only physical action counts as evidence of a mental state ("If you cared, you would have answered within a minute"), and self-harm may become a way of making invisible pain visible; in the pretend mode thoughts float free of feeling, as in fluent therapy talk that changes nothing. On Saturday night Rhea did not think "he may be asleep, or angry, or his phone may be dead". She knew, with the certainty of psychic equivalence, that he had left her, and she cut.

Mentalisation theory is a deficit model, closer to Kohut and to Adler than to Kernberg, and it has two strengths. It links the disorder to a well-developed body of developmental research, and it explains why the symptoms come and go with relationships rather than appearing at random. Its weakness is the one shared by every attachment account: insecure attachment is common in many disorders and in many healthy people, so it can be at most one ingredient among several. Table 11.11 sets the five accounts side by side.

Table 11.11 Five accounts of borderline personality disorder.
AccountCore problemWhere it comes fromTreatment that follows
Kernberg: borderline organisation (conflict)Splitting; identity diffusionExcess early aggression, constitutional or from frustration and abuseTransference-focused psychotherapy
Kohut: self psychology (deficit)A fragmented, empty selfFailed parental mirroring and idealisingEmpathic, understanding therapy
Adler and Buie: aloneness (deficit)Cannot summon a soothing inner imageInconsistent care during separationReliable availability; transitional objects
Linehan: biosocialEmotion dysregulationEmotional vulnerability transacting with invalidationDialectical behaviour therapy
Fonagy and Bateman: mentalisationMentalising collapses under attachment stressInsecure, often disorganised attachment; traumaMentalisation-based treatment
Exam trap

The attachment types most typical of borderline personality disorder are unresolved, preoccupied and fearful, not dismissing or secure (Agrawal and colleagues' review, 2004). Mentalisation (Fonagy and Bateman) is understanding behaviour in terms of mental states; it is not mindfulness, which is non-judgemental attention to the present moment. The three prementalising modes are psychic equivalence, the teleological mode and the pretend mode. Adler and Buie's "aloneness" and Kohut's "fragmented self" are deficit models; Kernberg's is a conflict model.

Trauma, genes and the brain

For many years one cause dominated public and clinical discussion of the disorder: childhood abuse. The argument rested on studies like the one in the Key-study box, and it was put most forcefully by the American psychiatrist Judith Lewis Herman, whose book Trauma and Recovery (1992) proposed that prolonged, repeated trauma produces a "complex" form of posttraumatic stress disorder, and that many women diagnosed with borderline personality disorder are better understood as survivors of such trauma. The idea has had lasting influence: ICD-11 now includes complex PTSD as a diagnosis in its own right, and telling it apart from borderline personality disorder is a skill taught in Section 11.3 and in Chapter 7. But how strong is the evidence that abuse causes the disorder?

Key study: from recalled abuse to documented abuse (Herman 1989; Widom 2009)

The retrospective study. In 1989 Herman, J. Christopher Perry and Bessel van der Kolk interviewed 55 patients in depth about major childhood trauma: 21 with borderline personality disorder, 11 with borderline traits and 23 with closely related diagnoses. Of the borderline patients, 81 percent reported such trauma: physical abuse (71 percent), sexual abuse (68 percent) and witnessing serious domestic violence (62 percent). Abuse histories were less common in those with borderline traits and least common in the other patients. The association looked strong, but the design could not show cause: the sample was small, and the trauma was recalled by adults years after the events.

The prospective study. Cathy Spatz Widom, Czaja and Joel Paris (2009) started at the other end. They took 500 people whose physical abuse, sexual abuse or neglect in childhood had been documented by courts decades earlier, and 396 demographically similar people without such records, and assessed them for the disorder in adulthood. Significantly more of the abused and neglected group met the criteria, but the risk was clearest for physical abuse and neglect, and it ran partly through other problems in their lives: a parent with alcohol or drug problems, unemployment, leaving school early, drug abuse, depression and PTSD. Conclusion: childhood maltreatment does raise the risk, but through many pathways, and it is neither necessary nor sufficient.

That is now the consensus, and DSM-5-TR states it directly: high rates of reported childhood abuse and neglect are found in the disorder, but sexual abuse is neither necessary nor sufficient, and its higher rates in inpatients than in outpatients suggest that it predicts the severity of the disorder as much as the disorder itself. Many people with the disorder report no abuse at all, and most abused children never develop it. Abuse is best seen as one powerful form of the invalidating environment, acting on a child who is already vulnerable.

What makes a child vulnerable? Part of the answer is genetic. DSM-5-TR reports that the disorder is about five times more common in the first-degree relatives of people with it than in the general population, and that their relatives also have more substance use, anxiety, depressive and bipolar disorders and antisocial personality disorder. Family studies cannot separate genes from shared upbringing, so twin studies are needed (Chapter 1, Section 1.4). In a Norwegian study of 92 identical and 129 fraternal twin pairs interviewed with a structured diagnostic interview, Svenn Torgersen and his colleagues (2000) estimated the heritability of borderline personality disorder at 0.69, though they warned that their sample was modest and the specific disorders rare. A much larger questionnaire study by Distel and colleagues (2008), covering 5,496 twins in the Netherlands, Belgium and Australia, put the heritability of borderline features at 42 percent in both sexes and in all three countries, with the remaining 58 percent due to environmental influences unique to each twin rather than to the family the twins shared. A fair summary is that genes account for roughly 40 to 70 percent of the variation, depending on the study. What is inherited is probably not the disorder itself but traits beneath it, above all emotional reactivity and impulsivity, which is exactly what the biosocial theory assumed.

Those traits have a recognisable brain signature (the anatomy belongs to the Physiological book; only the essentials are given here). In one of the first imaging studies, Donegan and colleagues at Yale (2003) showed pictures of faces to 15 patients with the disorder and 15 controls in a functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) scanner. The patients' left amygdala, the structure that detects threat and generates negative emotion, responded more strongly to emotional faces; and when interviewed afterwards, some patients said they had found even the neutral faces threatening or hard to read. Later studies have repeatedly found the same two-part picture: an overreactive limbic system, centred on the amygdala, and weaker regulation from the prefrontal cortex (especially the anterior cingulate and orbitofrontal regions), which normally damps emotion and holds back impulsive action. Low activity of the neurotransmitter serotonin has been linked to impulsive aggression and suicidal behaviour in this and other disorders, which is one reason serotonin-acting drugs were tried (Section 11.4.8). None of these findings is a diagnostic test, and none shows that the brain differences came first rather than developing with the disorder.

Exam trap

"Childhood sexual abuse causes borderline personality disorder" is the tempting overstatement. The correct answer is that abuse and neglect are common in the histories of people with the disorder and raise the risk, but are neither necessary nor sufficient (DSM-5-TR says this of sexual abuse; the prospective studies show the same for maltreatment in general). The heritability of borderline personality disorder is substantial, about 40 to 70 percent in twin studies, and DSM-5-TR says the disorder is about five times commoner in first-degree relatives. The brain picture is amygdala hyperreactivity with weaker prefrontal control, not a lesion.

The course: better than anyone expected

For most of the twentieth century, borderline personality disorder was thought to be close to a life sentence. A personality disorder is by definition enduring, the patients were among the most troubled in any service, and clinicians who met the same people in crisis year after year concluded that they never changed. The trouble with that impression is that clinicians mostly see the patients who are still ill; those who get better stop coming. Only a prospective study, which follows a whole group from the start and keeps counting, can say what really happens. Two such studies, both begun in the 1990s, transformed the picture.

Key study: the McLean and CLPS follow-ups (2003 to 2012)

The McLean Study of Adult Development. Mary C. Zanarini and her colleagues at McLean Hospital near Boston recruited 290 inpatients who met two sets of criteria for borderline personality disorder (DSM-III-R and the Revised Diagnostic Interview for Borderlines), with 72 inpatients who had other personality disorders as a comparison group, and reinterviewed them every two years. At six years (2003), 34.5 percent met the criteria for remission at two years, 49.4 percent at four years and 68.6 percent at six years, and only 5.9 percent of those who remitted relapsed. Impulsive symptoms such as self-harm resolved fastest; affective symptoms such as anger, emptiness and dysphoria were the most chronic. At ten years (2010), 93 percent had had a remission lasting at least two years and 86 percent one lasting at least four; of those with a four-year remission only 15 percent had a recurrence. But only 50 percent had achieved recovery, defined as remission together with good social and vocational functioning, and a third of those lost it again. At sixteen years (2012), remission had reached 78 to 99 percent, depending on how long it had to last, while recovery reached only 40 to 60 percent.

The Collaborative Longitudinal Personality Disorders Study (CLPS). John Gunderson, Andrew E. Skodol and colleagues followed treatment-seeking patients aged 18 to 45 from 19 clinical settings in four cities of the north-eastern United States: 175 with borderline personality disorder, 312 with Cluster C disorders and 95 with major depression but no personality disorder. Over ten years (2011), 85 percent of the borderline patients remitted and only 12 percent relapsed, a lower relapse rate than in major depression. Social functioning, however, remained severely impaired and improved only modestly. Conclusion: the symptoms of the disorder usually remit and rarely return; a satisfying working and social life is much harder to win. Criticism: remission was defined by falling below the diagnostic threshold, so a "remitted" patient may still be quite unwell, and most patients were receiving treatment, so the studies describe treated rather than natural course.

DSM-5-TR now summarises the same evidence: stable remissions lasting one to eight years are very common; impulsive symptoms remit most rapidly and affective symptoms much more slowly; and recovery, symptomatic remission with good psychosocial functioning, is harder to achieve and less stable, with failure to recover linked to living on disability benefits and to poor physical health. The pattern makes sense of the theories above.
