The behaviours that define the diagnosis (self-harm, impulsive acts, frantic crises) are what fade first, partly through treatment and partly through ordinary maturation, while the underlying temperament, an emotional sensitivity and a lonely, empty sense of self, changes slowly. For Rhea and her family, this is the most useful single fact in this chapter: the disorder is not a life sentence, and most people with it will one day no longer meet its criteria. For her clinicians, the lesson is that treatment must aim beyond the absence of crises, towards work, friendship and a life worth living. Section 11.3 gives the figures for suicide, which remains the gravest risk along the way.

Exam trap

"Borderline personality disorder is chronic and does not improve" is false. In the McLean study 93 percent had a two-year remission within ten years, and in CLPS 85 percent remitted, with low relapse rates. What lags is recovery of social and vocational functioning (about half at ten years). Of the symptoms, impulsive ones remit fastest and affective ones (anger, emptiness, dysphoria) slowest. The McLean Study of Adult Development is Zanarini's; CLPS is the multi-site study led by Gunderson and Skodol.

Treatment

Treatment of borderline personality disorder rests on one principle that every guideline repeats: psychological treatment comes first, and medication plays a supporting role at most. The specialised psychotherapies are taught in Book 2, so here they need only be named, placed and weighed. Dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT), Linehan's treatment, combines weekly individual therapy, a weekly skills group (mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation and interpersonal effectiveness), telephone coaching in crises and a consultation team for the therapists; it has more randomised trials than any other therapy for the disorder and its clearest effect is on self-harm and suicide attempts (Book 2, Section 5.8). Mentalisation-based treatment (MBT), Bateman and Fonagy's, combines individual and group sessions over about eighteen months to strengthen mentalising (Book 2, Section 3.5.7). Transference-focused psychotherapy (TFP), Kernberg's, works twice weekly within a firm treatment contract to integrate the split images (Book 2, Section 3.4.7). Schema therapy, developed by Jeffrey Young, works on early maladaptive schemas and the rapidly shifting schema modes of borderline patients, and in a Dutch trial in 2006 it outperformed TFP (Book 2, Section 5.7). Two less intensive options complete the list. STEPPS (Systems Training for Emotional Predictability and Problem Solving), from Nancee Blum, Donald W. Black and colleagues at the University of Iowa, is a 20-week manual-based group programme that adds cognitive-behavioural skills training to the patient's usual care and teaches family members and friends how to respond. Good psychiatric management (also called general psychiatric management), developed by Gunderson, is a structured, less intensive generalist approach built on case management, psychoeducation about the disorder and realistic goals; in a Toronto trial in 2009 it did as well as DBT.

What do the trials show overall? The 2020 Cochrane review found that disorder-specific psychotherapies reduce the severity of the disorder compared with usual treatment, with DBT and MBT the most studied, but rated much of the evidence as low in certainty; and none of the specialised therapies is clearly better than the others (Book 2, Section 3.11.7 weighs the trials). The more striking lesson is that well-organised generalist care also does surprisingly well. What the successful treatments share is a clear structure, a coherent model that patient and clinician both understand, a consistent team, steady attention to the relationship, and active work on self-harm. The NICE guideline for England and Wales (2009) added a warning that matches this lesson: brief psychological interventions of less than three months should not be used specifically for the disorder.

Medication is a different story. No drug is licensed for borderline personality disorder itself. In 2001 the American Psychiatric Association's practice guideline recommended a symptom-targeted approach: selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) for affective dysregulation and impulsive behaviour, low-dose antipsychotics for the transient paranoid and dissociative symptoms, and mood stabilisers as additions. That advice has not survived: the APA's revised guideline of 2024 found no evidence that any drug treats the core symptoms, and suggests only short-term use aimed at a specific, measurable target symptom, alongside psychotherapy. Larger and better trials have been disappointing (Table 11.12). The 2010 Cochrane review found some benefit on particular symptoms for second-generation antipsychotics and mood stabilisers, but the trials were small and short. When a well-powered British trial tested the mood stabiliser lamotrigine in 276 patients (Mike Crawford and colleagues, 2018), it was no better than placebo at one year on borderline symptoms or on any other outcome. The 2022 Cochrane update, with 18 more trials than the 2010 version, concluded that no medication seems effective in treating the core pathology of the disorder. NICE was blunt even in 2009: drug treatment should not be used specifically for the disorder or for its individual symptoms (repeated self-harm, emotional instability, risk-taking, transient psychotic symptoms); antipsychotics should not be used for medium- or long-term treatment; drugs may be used for coexisting conditions such as major depression; and in a crisis a sedative may be used cautiously for no longer than a week.

Table 11.12 Medication in borderline personality disorder: what has been tried and what the evidence shows.
TargetDrugs triedWhat the evidence shows
Low mood, anger, mood swingsSSRIs such as fluoxetineNo convincing effect on the disorder; use for coexisting depression
Impulsivity, aggression, self-harmMood stabilisers: lamotrigine, topiramate, valproateSmall early trials positive; large lamotrigine trial (2018) negative
Transient paranoia, dissociationLow-dose second-generation antipsychotics: aripiprazole, olanzapineSome short-term symptom benefit; NICE: not for medium- or long-term use
Acute crisis, insomniaA sedative, such as a sedative antihistamineNICE: cautiously, agreed with the patient, no longer than one week
AnxietyBenzodiazepinesGenerally avoided: disinhibition, dependence, misuse in overdose

Two practical points follow. Polypharmacy is common and dangerous: a patient who has been given a new drug at each crisis may end up on four or five, with weight gain, sedation and a large supply of tablets for an overdose. And every prescription carries a message. A patient told that her distress is a chemical problem to be fixed by a tablet may lose the sense that she can learn to manage it herself, which is the central aim of the psychological treatments.

Crises and self-harm are where treatment is most often won or lost. The guideline approach is to plan ahead with the patient: a written crisis plan saying what she will do, whom she will call and what the service will offer; brief, goal-directed hospital admission only when risk cannot be managed in the community, with its length and purpose agreed in advance; and a calm, non-judgemental response to self-harm that treats it as a behaviour with functions to be understood, not as manipulation. Long, open-ended admissions tend to backfire, because they remove the patient from the life she has to learn to manage and can reward escalation.

Clinical link

Patients with this disorder provoke strong reactions in clinicians: the wish to rescue during idealisation, hurt and anger during devaluation, and exhaustion when crises repeat. These reactions are the ordinary consequences of splitting and projective identification, and acting on them (becoming the patient's special saviour, or quietly discharging her as "manipulative") repeats her history of unpredictable care. The protections are a clear treatment frame agreed at the start, consistent limits explained rather than imposed, and regular supervision or a consultation team. Avoid the word "attention-seeking" in notes and conversations: it is inaccurate (the behaviour usually regulates unbearable emotion) and it predicts worse care.

Exam trap

The first-line treatment of borderline personality disorder is psychological, not pharmacological. No drug is licensed for the disorder; NICE (2009) advised that drugs should not be used specifically for it or for its individual symptoms, that antipsychotics should not be used for medium- or long-term treatment, and that a sedative in a crisis should be used for no longer than a week. Match the therapy to its developer: DBT, Linehan; MBT, Bateman and Fonagy; TFP, Kernberg (with Clarkin and Yeomans); schema therapy, Young; STEPPS, Blum and Black; good psychiatric management, Gunderson.

Borderline personality disorder in India

Most Indian hospitals still record the disorder under ICD-10, as emotionally unstable personality disorder, borderline type (F60.31), and research on it in India is thin. A 2020 review by Choudhary and Gupta concluded that Indian research rests on a few case reports and small samples, and argued that culture shapes all three of the disorder's core domains, emotion, relationships and the self. Indian clinicians must judge, for example, whether a young woman's conflict with her family over a relationship or a career reflects a disorder of identity or a clash between her own choices and family expectations, and whether outbursts of anger in a crowded joint family are a fixed pattern or a reaction to a situation. Screening studies give large numbers, but they must be read carefully: in a 2019 survey of 500 medical and engineering students in Manipal, 15.2 percent scored above the cut-off on a self-report screening questionnaire for the disorder, a figure that reflects the screen's low threshold rather than the true rate of the diagnosed disorder.

In practice the disorder usually reaches services through self-harm. A young person is brought to a general hospital emergency department after cutting or an overdose, often following a quarrel with a partner or parents, and the psychiatric consultation that follows is the first chance to recognise the pattern behind the act. Since the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (section 115), a person who attempts suicide is presumed to be under severe stress and is not to be tried and punished, and the government has a duty to provide care, treatment and rehabilitation (Chapter 5 covers suicide in India). Men are recognised less often. Mihir, a 27-year-old sales executive, was brought by his parents for "anger and drinking"; only on careful history did the pattern emerge of terror of being left, rapid idealisation and hatred of girlfriends, impulsive spending and repeated cutting of his thighs, hidden from his family. Attitudes of professionals matter too: a 2025 survey of 189 mental health staff in Jammu and Kashmir found that attitudes to patients with the disorder varied by profession, with psychology and nursing interns the most positive and psychiatrists the least. Full DBT or MBT programmes need a trained team and many hours of staff time each week, which few public services anywhere can provide, so most Indian patients receive generalist care; this is one reason the good results of structured generalist treatments such as good psychiatric management matter so much in low-resource settings.

Indian context

In Indian families, invalidation often comes in loving, ordinary words: "Don't cry, people will see", "What is there to be sad about?", "Just adjust" (Book 2, Section 5.8.2). Involving the family is usually essential, because most young adults live with their parents and depend on them for treatment. Psychoeducation that presents the disorder as a problem of emotional sensitivity and learnable skills, rather than as bad character or "drama", reduces blame on both sides and makes the family an ally of treatment.

11.5 Antisocial Personality Disorder and PsychopathyMedium yield · 4 Qs

Jayant is 29, well dressed, relaxed and courteous. He has been referred by a magistrate's court in Nagpur for a psychological assessment after his third arrest for cheating: over two years he persuaded several retired schoolteachers to hand over their savings for flats in a housing project that did not exist, using two different names. He shakes the psychologist's hand warmly, compliments her on her office and explains, in fluent and plausible detail, that he has "always been misunderstood". The victims, he says, "wanted easy money; I only gave them a story, and anyway they will get some of it back." His work history is a list of jobs left after a few weeks. He has been in several fights, has two convictions for drunken, reckless driving and owes money to almost everyone he knows. His mother, interviewed separately, adds what he left out: he stole from her purse at nine, was suspended from school at twelve for bullying and threatening younger boys, regularly skipped school from the age of eleven, set fire to a neighbour's haystack at thirteen "to see it burn", and twice ran away from home overnight at fourteen. Asked how he feels about the teachers, Jayant says, "Of course I feel bad," in exactly the tone he used to praise the office.

Is Jayant bad, or is he ill? The question is older than psychiatry, and the answers given to it over two centuries explain why there are now two overlapping concepts, antisocial personality disorder and psychopathy, and why examiners keep asking you to tell them apart.

Madness without delusion: Pinel, Rush and Prichard

At the end of the eighteenth century, madness meant a disorder of the intellect: delusions, hallucinations, confused reasoning. Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826), the French physician remembered for unchaining patients at the Bicêtre and the Salpêtrière (Book 2, Section 1.3), found that this definition did not fit some of the men in his care. In his Traité médico-philosophique sur l'aliénation mentale (1801) he wrote that he had shared the view of the English philosopher John Locke that insanity is inseparable from disordered reasoning, until at the Bicêtre he was "not a little surprised" to find patients who showed no disturbance of understanding but were dominated by "a kind of instinct of fury, as if only their affective faculties had been damaged". He called the condition manie sans délire, mania without delirium or, as it is often translated, mania without delusion (in French, délire means a disorder of the intellect, not only delirium in the modern sense).

His first example is a story you will not forget. A single child was brought up by a weak and indulgent mother who gave in to every whim. As he grew older his impulses grew stronger; anyone who opposed him was attacked, and if a dog, a sheep or a horse annoyed him, he had it killed on the spot. At fairs he lost his temper and came home covered in blood. Yet when calm he was full of good sense: as an adult he ran a large estate competently, fulfilled his duties in society and was known for his charity to the poor. Lawsuits and payments of damages had been the only consequences of his violence until one day a woman swore at him and he threw her down a well. At his trial, witnesses described his long history of rages, and he was sentenced to confinement in the asylum at the Bicêtre. Notice what Pinel was describing, and what he was not. His next case was a mechanic who suffered attacks of bloodthirsty fury, warned his beloved wife to flee before one of them, was "full of remorse" and tried to kill himself with a cobbler's knife. Manie sans délire was therefore a broad category of impulsive, emotional violence with intact reasoning, not specifically the remorseless, callous person we now call a psychopath. Its importance is that it was the first medical claim that a person could be disordered in emotion and conduct while sane in intellect.

The idea spread on both sides of the Atlantic. In the United States Benjamin Rush (1746 to 1813), in his Medical Inquiries and Observations upon the Diseases of the Mind (1812), described people whose moral faculties alone seemed deranged. In England James Cowles Prichard (1786 to 1848) gave the condition its lasting name in A Treatise on Insanity and Other Disorders Affecting the Mind (1835). Moral insanity, he wrote, is "madness consisting in a morbid perversion of the natural feelings, affections, inclinations, temper, habits, moral dispositions, and natural impulses, without any remarkable disorder or defect of the intellect or knowing and reasoning faculties, and particularly without any insane illusion or hallucination". The word "moral" needs care. In Prichard's day it meant emotional and dispositional as much as ethical, the same sense as in Pinel's "moral treatment", and his category was wide enough to include what we would now call mood disorders and eccentricity. Only gradually did "moral insanity" narrow towards the lack of conscience. It had little effect in the courts, where the M'Naghten rules of 1843 tied the insanity defence to a defect of reason that moral insanity, by definition, lacked (Book 2, Section 10.9.3).

Two more strands complete the nineteenth-century picture. The Italian physician Cesare Lombroso (1835 to 1909) argued in L'uomo delinquente (1876) that some criminals are "born criminals", evolutionary throwbacks recognisable by physical stigmata such as a sloping forehead or large jaw; the theory did not survive careful measurement, but it fixed the idea of a constitutional criminal type. And in German psychiatry the word "psychopathic" took on a meaning very different from today's. Julius Koch's "psychopathic inferiorities" (1891), Emil Kraepelin's psychopathic personalities and Kurt Schneider's Psychopathic Personalities (1923) used "psychopathic" for abnormal personality of every kind, the depressive, insecure and asthenic as well as the callous; Section 11.1 tells that history. When you meet "psychopathic" in a text before about 1940, read it as "personality disordered", not as "psychopath" in Cleckley's sense.

Exam trap

Match the term to its author and year. Manie sans délire (mania without delirium): Pinel, 1801. Moral insanity as a named diagnosis: Prichard, 1835 (not Pinel; Rush and others had written of derangement of the moral faculties earlier, in a looser sense). Born criminal: Lombroso, 1876. Psychopathic inferiorities: Koch, 1891. Psychopathic Personalities (ten types): Schneider, 1923. The Mask of Sanity: Cleckley, 1941. Deviant Children Grown Up: Lee Robins, 1966. Psychopathy Checklist-Revised: Hare, 1991. In Prichard's phrase "moral" means emotional and dispositional, not only ethical.

Cleckley's mask of sanity

The modern concept of psychopathy begins with one book. Hervey M. Cleckley (1903 to 1984), a psychiatrist at the Medical College of Georgia in Augusta (and later, with Corbett H. Thigpen, co-author of The Three Faces of Eve), based The Mask of Sanity (1941) mainly on adult men he had observed for years in a closed psychiatric institution, and revised it through five editions, the last in 1976. His title states his thesis. The psychopath, he wrote, conceals "behind a perfect mimicry of normal emotion, fine intelligence, and social responsibility a grossly disabled and irresponsible personality". Ask him about love, loyalty or regret and he gives the right answers; watch what he does and there is nothing behind them. Cleckley borrowed an analogy from the neurologist Henry Head's "semantic aphasia", in which a patient produces fluent, grammatical sentences that carry no meaning: the psychopath, by analogy, produces the outward form of a human life without its inner meaning. Two later researchers, Johns and Quay (1962), put the same idea in a phrase that stuck: the psychopath knows the words but not the music.

Cleckley's case of "Tom" shows the pattern. Tom was 21, healthy, pleasant, alert and entirely at ease, the kind of young man in whom a prospective employer would see "strong indications of character". His family and the legal authorities hoped that some psychiatric disorder could be found in him, so that he might escape a jail sentence for stealing. His trouble had begun in childhood. Although he was generously provided for, he stole his father's chickens and the family silver and sold them for a pittance; he was often truant; he seemed to resent punishment and to want to avoid it, yet no punishment changed what he did. Notice how closely Tom resembles Jayant, and notice two details that would shape the research of the next half century: Tom was not driven by any strong temptation, and punishment taught him nothing.

From cases like Tom's, Cleckley drew the sixteen characteristics in Table 11.13, which researchers called the "Cleckley criteria". Read them in groups. The first three describe the mask: charm and intelligence, no psychosis and, surprisingly, no anxiety. The middle group describes the conduct: unreliability, lying, antisocial acts with little motive, poor judgement and failure to learn from experience, outrageous behaviour with drink, no life plan. The rest describe the emotional void: no remorse, an egocentricity that makes love impossible, shallow emotions, no insight, indifference to the people who help him, an impersonal sex life. Two items surprise students: "absence of nervousness", which inspired the fearlessness research below, and "suicide rarely carried out". Cleckley also insisted that psychopathy is not the same as criminality. His book has chapters on the psychopath as businessman, as man of the world, as gentleman, as scientist, as physician and as psychiatrist, people whose disorder was disguised by position and success.

Table 11.13 Cleckley's sixteen characteristics of the psychopath (The Mask of Sanity), with what each means.
No.CharacteristicWhat it means
1Superficial charm and good "intelligence"Makes an excellent first impression
2Absence of delusions and other signs of irrational thinkingNot psychotic
3Absence of "nervousness" or psychoneurotic manifestationsCalm, unanxious, even under threat
4UnreliabilityCannot be counted on, even when it matters
5Untruthfulness and insincerityLies easily, with no sign of discomfort
6Lack of remorse or shameNo guilt for harm done
7Inadequately motivated antisocial behaviourCheats or steals for trivial or no gain
8Poor judgement and failure to learn by experienceRepeats the same mistakes despite punishment
9Pathologic egocentricity and incapacity for loveOthers matter only as means
10General poverty in major affective reactionsEmotions shallow and short-lived
11Specific loss of insightCannot see himself as others see him
12Unresponsiveness in general interpersonal relationsIndifferent to kindness or trust shown him
13Fantastic and uninviting behaviour with drink and sometimes withoutCrude, reckless conduct when drinking
14Suicide rarely carried outThreats, but seldom completed suicide
15Sex life impersonal, trivial and poorly integratedCasual relationships without attachment
16Failure to follow any life planNo consistent goals; drifts and wrecks chances
Exam trap

Cleckley described psychopathy (16 characteristics, 1941); he did not build a rating instrument. The checklist that turned his description into a measure is Robert Hare's (Section 11.5.6). Cleckley's psychopath is not anxious ("absence of nervousness"), is not psychotic, and is not necessarily a criminal. Do not mix up his two famous books: The Mask of Sanity is about psychopathy; The Three Faces of Eve (with Thigpen, 1957) is about multiple personality (Chapter 7).

From sociopathy to antisocial personality disorder: the manuals

The American manuals took up the concept in three stages. DSM-I (1952) placed it under sociopathic personality disturbance, a heading that also contained sexual deviation and addiction, and divided the antisocial people themselves into an "antisocial reaction" (chronically in trouble, profiting from neither experience nor punishment, without loyalty) and a "dyssocial reaction" (people who had grown up in an abnormal moral environment and were loyal to their own criminal group). The word "sociopathic" stressed that the disorder shows itself in damage to society. DSM-II (1968) renamed it antisocial personality and described it in Cleckley's language of traits: selfish, callous, irresponsible, impulsive, unable to feel guilt or to learn from punishment.

DSM-III (1980) made the decisive change. Its authors wanted diagnoses that different clinicians would agree on, and they judged that traits such as "callous" or "insincere" were hard to rate reliably, especially in a person who lies. So they defined antisocial personality disorder (ASPD) almost entirely by behaviour that can be checked: arrests, fights, debts, abandoned jobs. They also built in a developmental rule taken from the follow-up research of Lee Nelken Robins (whose St Louis study opened this chapter): the antisocial pattern had to have begun in childhood, before the age of 15. DSM-IV (1994) shortened the criteria into the form that DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR still use. The behavioural approach worked as intended, and reliability rose, but at a cost that Robert Hare and others were quick to point out: the criteria describe persistent offenders well and psychopaths less well, so they include most prisoners and miss the charming, unanxious, successful psychopath Cleckley described. The Alternative Model in Section III of DSM-5 (Section 11.8) tries to bring the traits back, with a specifier "with psychopathic features" for the bold variant marked by low anxiousness, low withdrawal and high attention seeking.

Exam trap

DSM-I (1952) used sociopathic personality disturbance (with antisocial and dyssocial reactions); DSM-II (1968) used antisocial personality; DSM-III (1980) introduced antisocial personality disorder with behavioural criteria and the childhood-onset rule, influenced by Lee Robins's follow-up studies. The behavioural criteria were chosen for reliability; their cost is that they capture persistent offenders better than Cleckley's psychopaths.

The DSM-5-TR criteria

DSM-5-TR defines antisocial personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of disregard for, and violation of, the rights of others, occurring since age 15, shown by three or more of seven features. DSM-5-TR notes that the same pattern has also been called psychopathy, sociopathy or dyssocial personality disorder. The seven features fall naturally into three groups, and Jayant shows all of them.

  • Lawlessness and deceit. The person repeatedly fails to conform to social norms with respect to lawful behaviour, performing acts that are grounds for arrest whether or not he is ever arrested (criterion 1). He is deceitful, lying repeatedly, using aliases or conning others for profit or pleasure (criterion 2). Jayant's two names and his housing scheme meet both. Because deceit is central, DSM-5-TR advises that information from collateral sources (family, records, police reports) be combined with the interview, which is why his mother's account mattered so much.
  • Impulsivity and aggression. He acts impulsively or fails to plan ahead, changing jobs, homes or partners on the spur of the moment (criterion 3); he is irritable and aggressive, with repeated physical fights or assaults (criterion 4; fights in self-defence or in defence of others do not count); and he shows reckless disregard for the safety of himself or others, in drunken or dangerous driving, risky sex or drug use, or neglect of a child (criterion 5).
  • Irresponsibility and lack of remorse. He is consistently irresponsible, failing to keep up steady work or to honour financial obligations (criterion 6), and he lacks remorse, being indifferent to having hurt, mistreated or stolen from others or offering glib rationalisations ("they wanted easy money"; "life's unfair"; "he had it coming") (criterion 7).

Three further rules complete the diagnosis, and they are the ones examiners test (Figure 11.9). The person must be at least 18 years old (criterion B), because a personality pattern in a teenager is still forming; an adolescent with the same behaviour is diagnosed with conduct disorder (Section 11.9). There must be evidence of conduct disorder with onset before age 15 (criterion C): aggression to people or animals, destruction of property, deceitfulness or theft, or serious violations of rules. This is the developmental rule that DSM-III introduced: adult antisocial behaviour that begins out of nowhere in a person with a normal childhood is usually something else, such as the effect of a substance use disorder, a situational response or ordinary crime. Jayant's mother's account (bullying at twelve, truancy from eleven, the haystack fire at thirteen, running away twice at fourteen) supplies the childhood evidence. Finally, the antisocial behaviour must not occur only during the course of schizophrenia or bipolar disorder (criterion D), because the manic patient who spends wildly and fights, or the psychotic patient who assaults a neighbour he believes is poisoning him, is not showing a personality pattern.

Two more rules keep the diagnosis honest. For a person over 18, conduct disorder is diagnosed only if the criteria for ASPD are not met. And criminal behaviour on its own is not a personality disorder: a person who commits crimes for gain without the personality features of the disorder receives the DSM-5-TR code for adult antisocial behaviour, listed among the "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention", not a mental disorder at all.

Telling ASPD apart from its neighbours depends on motive, which DSM-5-TR describes precisely. People with narcissistic personality disorder share the antisocial person's toughness, glibness, exploitation and lack of empathy, but lack his impulsivity, aggression and deceit, are hungrier for admiration and envy, and usually have no history of conduct disorder or crime. People with histrionic personality disorder are impulsive, seductive and manipulative too, but more emotional and not characteristically antisocial. People with borderline personality disorder may be manipulative, but to gain nurturance, whereas the antisocial person manipulates for profit, power or pleasure; he is also less emotionally unstable and more aggressive. People with paranoid personality disorder may break the law, but usually from a wish for revenge rather than for gain. And all of these can be diagnosed together when their criteria are met: ASPD commonly co-occurs with borderline, histrionic and narcissistic features, all members of Cluster B.

Exam trap

The age rules: the pattern of disregard for others' rights is counted since age 15; the person must be at least 18 (not 16, and there is no rule that he must be over 21); and there must be evidence of conduct disorder with onset before 15, not before 12, 16 or 18. A question offering "conduct disorder before age 15 and a pattern of disregard for others' rights from age 18" is loosely worded (the pattern is counted from 15; 18 is the minimum age for the diagnosis) but is still the right answer; "onset only after 40", "hallucinations and delusions" and "fear of abandonment and identity disturbance" (borderline) are wrong. "Manipulation, lack of remorse and irresponsibility" is antisocial, not borderline (manipulates for nurturance, fears abandonment), dependent (submissive, clinging, needs reassurance) or narcissistic (exploits for admiration, but is not impulsive, aggressive or deceitful and has no conduct-disorder history). Three of seven criteria are needed.

Dissocial personality disorder: the ICD view

ICD-10 calls the condition dissocial personality disorder (F60.2) and describes it in a style closer to Cleckley's than to DSM's: callous unconcern for the feelings of others; gross and persistent irresponsibility and disregard for social norms, rules and obligations; an incapacity to maintain enduring relationships, though there is no difficulty in establishing them; a very low tolerance of frustration and a low threshold for aggression, including violence; an incapacity to experience guilt or to profit from experience, particularly punishment; and a marked proneness to blame others or to offer plausible rationalisations for the behaviour. The category explicitly includes the older terms amoral, antisocial, asocial, psychopathic and sociopathic personality. Notice two differences from DSM-5-TR. ICD-10 does not require conduct disorder in childhood; it says only that conduct disorder in childhood and adolescence, though not invariably present, may further support the diagnosis. And its emphasis falls on callousness and guiltlessness, the affective core of psychopathy, rather than on arrests and debts. ICD-11 abolished the separate personality disorder types, so dissocial personality survives there only as dissociality, one of the five trait domains that describe a personality disorder's style (Section 11.8). Table 11.14 sets the three definitions side by side.

Table 11.14 Three ways of defining the antisocial personality.
FeatureDSM-5-TR ASPDICD-10 dissocial PDPsychopathy (PCL-R)
Defined mainly byBehaviour (arrests, fights, debts)Traits and behaviourTraits plus lifestyle and criminal history
Childhood ruleConduct disorder before 15 requiredSupportive, not requiredEarly behaviour problems scored as one item
Minimum age18No fixed minimum; usually from late adolescenceAdult version; a youth version exists
Core emphasisViolation of others' rightsCallousness, guiltlessness, low frustration toleranceCallous, remorseless use of others
StatusA personality disorder diagnosisA personality disorder diagnosisA research and forensic construct, measured by rating
Exam trap

ICD-10's name is dissocial personality disorder (F60.2); DSM-5-TR lists "dyssocial personality disorder" as an older synonym, and DSM-I's "dyssocial reaction" meant the loyal member of a criminal group. Unlike DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 does not require conduct disorder in childhood. In ICD-11 the separate type is gone, and dissociality survives as one of five trait domains (with negative affectivity, detachment, disinhibition and anankastia; Section 11.8).

Psychopathy and Hare's checklist

Cleckley's description was vivid but impossible to use in research as it stood: two clinicians could read the same case and disagree about whether a man had "general poverty in major affective reactions". The Canadian psychologist Robert D. Hare (born 1934), who spent his career at the University of British Columbia studying prison inmates, set out to turn the description into a reliable measure. His research scale, the Psychopathy Checklist (PCL), appeared in 1980 with 22 items; the revised version, the Psychopathy Checklist-Revised (PCL-R), was published in 1991 with 20 items, and a second edition followed in 2003. A trained rater scores each item 0 (absent), 1 (possibly or partly present) or 2 (definitely present) from a semi-structured interview together with a review of the person's files, which matters because a psychopath's own account cannot be trusted. Totals therefore run from 0 to 40. A score of 30 or more is the usual cut-off for psychopathy in North America; British and European researchers often use 25. In this book the PCL-R matters as the instrument that defines the research construct of psychopathy; how it is administered and validated, and its screening and youth versions (the 12-item PCL:SV and the PCL:YV for adolescents), belong to the Assessment book.

Factor analysis of the items by Harpur, Hare and Hakstian (1989) found two correlated factors. Factor 1, interpersonal and affective, captures what Hare called the "selfish, callous and remorseless use of others": glibness, grandiosity, lying, manipulation, lack of remorse, shallow emotion, callousness. Factor 2, social deviance, captures a "chronically unstable, antisocial and socially deviant lifestyle": impulsivity, irresponsibility, need for stimulation, early behaviour problems, delinquency. In the 2003 edition each factor was split into two facets (Table 11.15). The structure is the key to the relation between psychopathy and ASPD. Factor 2 overlaps heavily with the DSM criteria; Factor 1 is what DSM's behavioural approach left out.

Table 11.15 The structure of the PCL-R: two factors, four facets, with example items.
FactorFacetExample items
1 Interpersonal-affectiveInterpersonalGlibness and superficial charm; grandiose sense of self-worth; pathological lying; conning and manipulation
1 Interpersonal-affectiveAffectiveLack of remorse or guilt; shallow affect; callousness and lack of empathy; failure to accept responsibility
2 Social devianceLifestyleNeed for stimulation; parasitic lifestyle; no realistic long-term goals; impulsivity; irresponsibility
2 Social devianceAntisocialPoor behavioural controls; early behaviour problems; juvenile delinquency; revocation of conditional release; criminal versatility

The two concepts overlap without coinciding, and the overlap is lopsided. Almost every psychopath who comes into contact with the criminal justice system also meets the criteria for ASPD, because his lifestyle and antisocial facets satisfy the behavioural criteria. But most people with ASPD are not psychopaths, because many offenders have the behaviour without the callous, unemotional core. DSM-5-TR reports ASPD in more than 70 percent of the most severe samples of men from prisons, forensic settings and substance use clinics, whereas psychopathy as defined by the PCL-R is found in a minority of prisoners; Hare's own summary (1996) is that ASPD applies to the majority of criminals, while only about 15 to 20 percent of offenders score 30 or more on the PCL-R. Psychopathy is also the stronger predictor of what happens next. In a study of 169 mentally disordered offenders released from a maximum-security hospital in Ontario, Grant T. Harris, Marnie E. Rice and Catherine Cormier (1991) found that 40 percent of the whole group committed a further violent offence, against 77 percent of those classified as psychopaths. That predictive power is why the PCL-R carries so much weight in forensic risk assessment (Book 2, Section 10.9.5), and also why its misuse worries critics: the label is stigmatising, scores can differ depending on which side of a court case the rater is working for, and applying it to adolescents, whose personalities are still forming, is especially risky.

Exam trap

The PCL-R was developed by Robert Hare (PCL 1980; PCL-R 1991; second edition 2003), building on Cleckley's description. It is not "Hervey Cleckley alone, 1941" (Cleckley wrote The Mask of Sanity but built no checklist), not Aaron Temkin Beck (1921 to 2021; cognitive therapy and the Beck Depression Inventory) and not Hans Jürgen Eysenck (1916 to 1997; the PEN model, the Eysenck Personality Questionnaire and an arousal theory of crime, Section 11.5.9). PCL-R facts: 20 items, each scored 0, 1 or 2, maximum 40, cut-off 30 (often 25 in Britain and Europe), rated from interview plus file review; two factors (interpersonal-affective and social deviance), four facets (interpersonal, affective, lifestyle, antisocial). Most psychopathic offenders meet ASPD criteria; most people with ASPD are not psychopaths.

Primary and secondary psychopaths, successful psychopaths and the triarchic model

Not all antisocial people are alike, and the oldest attempt to divide them is still useful. In 1941, the year of Cleckley's book, the American psychiatrist Benjamin Karpman argued that psychopathy should be separated into two clinical types. In primary psychopathy (Karpman's "idiopathic" type) no psychological cause can be found: the person is callous, guiltless and unanxious from the start, a constitutional lack of conscience. In secondary psychopathy (the "symptomatic" type) antisocial behaviour arises from some other disturbance, such as neurotic conflict, hostility born of abuse or a disorder of mood, and the person is anxious, emotionally reactive and often guilty afterwards. Compare Jayant with Qasim, 23, who was beaten throughout his childhood by an alcoholic father. Qasim steals, drinks and gets into fights, but he is tense, quick to rage when he feels slighted, sleeps badly and weeps with shame after hurting his younger sister. Both meet the criteria for ASPD. Only Jayant resembles Cleckley's psychopath, and the two will respond very differently to punishment and to treatment.

Cleckley had also insisted that some psychopaths never reach prison. In a clever study, Cathy Spatz Widom (1977) recruited such people by placing advertisements in underground newspapers: "Wanted: charming, aggressive, carefree people who are impulsively irresponsible but are good at handling people and at looking after number one." The people who answered resembled imprisoned psychopaths on questionnaires of empathy and socialisation, and many had parents with alcoholism or other problems, but many of them had stable jobs and had managed to stay out of prison. Such successful psychopaths are hard to study precisely because they are successful; what seems to separate them from their imprisoned counterparts is better impulse control, higher intelligence and, perhaps, a world of work that rewards their boldness.

The most influential modern attempt to reconcile these varieties is the triarchic model of Christopher J. Patrick, Don C. Fowles and Robert F. Krueger (2009), which describes psychopathy as a combination of three dispositions. Boldness is social dominance, emotional resilience under stress and venturesomeness: the fearless, unflappable, charming quality of Cleckley's mask. Meanness is callousness, cruelty, lack of empathy and the exploitation of others. Disinhibition is impulsivity, poor self-control, irresponsibility and impatience. On this view the criminal psychopath of the prison studies is high in meanness and disinhibition, which is why he also meets ASPD criteria; the successful psychopath is high in boldness and meanness but lower in disinhibition; and DSM-5-TR's ASPD criteria capture mainly disinhibition and meanness and leave out boldness. The Alternative Model's "with psychopathic features" specifier (low anxiousness, low withdrawal, high attention seeking) is in effect an attempt to add boldness back. Whether boldness belongs at the heart of psychopathy is still argued: some studies find that it adds little to meanness and disinhibition in predicting harmful behaviour.

Exam trap

The primary (idiopathic) versus secondary (symptomatic) division of psychopathy is Karpman's (1941): the primary psychopath is callous and unanxious, the secondary one anxious, emotionally reactive and often driven by conflict or trauma. The triarchic model (boldness, meanness, disinhibition) is Patrick, Fowles and Krueger's (2009); do not confuse it with Sternberg's triarchic theory of intelligence. Widom (1977) found "successful" psychopaths through newspaper advertisements.

Who develops antisocial personality disorder, and what becomes of them

How common is the disorder? DSM-5-TR gives three figures: a prevalence of 0.6 percent in a probability subsample of the American National Comorbidity Survey Replication, 3.6 percent in the National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions, and a median of 3.6 percent across seven epidemiological studies, six of them American. (The earlier DSM-5 of 2013 gave a 12-month prevalence of 0.2 to 3.3 percent, a range you may still meet in question banks.) The figure rises steeply in selected settings: above 70 percent among the most severe samples of men with alcohol use disorder, in substance use clinics, prisons and other forensic settings. The disorder is about three times as common in men as in women. Women with ASPD are more likely than men to have suffered sexual and other abuse, and more often have coexisting mood and anxiety disorders, whereas men more often present with irritability, aggression and reckless disregard for others; because the conduct disorder criteria emphasise aggression, DSM-5-TR warns that the disorder may be underdiagnosed in women. Prevalence is higher where there is poverty, urban deprivation and migration, but DSM-5-TR also warns against misdiagnosis: in neighbourhoods with high levels of violence and discrimination, joining a gang may be a survival strategy rather than a sign of disordered personality, and members of oppressed groups are more likely to have been labelled with conduct disorder in adolescence, which is the gateway to the adult diagnosis.

The disorder rarely travels alone. Substance use disorders are the commonest companion, found in about 60 percent of people with ASPD in one review (Taylor and Lang, 2006), and anxiety, depressive, somatic symptom and gambling disorders are also more frequent. The risk of developing the adult disorder is greatest in children whose conduct disorder began before age 10 and who also had attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3). People with ASPD are more likely than others to die prematurely, from natural causes as well as from suicide, accidents and violence. In a 50-year follow-up of the Boston delinquent and non-delinquent boys first studied by Sheldon Glueck and Eleanor Glueck, John H. Laub and George Vaillant (2000) found that the delinquent boys were more than twice as likely as the others to die an unnatural death.

The course has one hopeful feature. DSM-5-TR describes ASPD as chronic, but notes that it may become less evident or remit as the person grows older, often by about age 40, particularly in criminal behaviour, and with some decrease in the whole range of antisocial behaviour and substance use. Hare, McPherson and Forth (1988) showed the same thing in criminal records. The conviction rates of non-psychopathic offenders stayed roughly constant from age 16 to 45; those of psychopaths stayed high until about 40 and then fell markedly. Nobody knows exactly why: the burning out of a high-energy, stimulation-seeking temperament, the accumulated cost of prison, and the stabilising effect of relationships and work have all been suggested. Note that the fall is in offending, the Factor 2 behaviour; the interpersonal and affective traits of Factor 1 seem to change much less with age.

Exam trap

ASPD is about three times commoner in men (DSM-5-TR). Its prevalence is given as 0.6 percent (NCS-R) and 3.6 percent (NESARC and the median of seven studies) in DSM-5-TR, and as 0.2 to 3.3 percent (12-month) in DSM-5; it exceeds 70 percent in the most severe male prison and addiction samples. It tends to remit by about age 40, especially the criminal behaviour. Do not confuse this with borderline personality disorder, which is diagnosed more often in women in clinical samples (Section 11.3).

Fearless and underaroused: the biological explanations

Two of Cleckley's characteristics set the agenda for the laboratory: the psychopath is not anxious, and he fails to learn from experience, above all from punishment. Perhaps, researchers reasoned, these are the same fact. Most of us are kept out of trouble less by reasoning than by anticipatory fear: the knot in the stomach before we lie to a parent or take what is not ours. If a person did not feel that fear, threats of punishment would mean little to him, and he would go on doing what brings immediate reward. The first to test the idea was a graduate student at the University of Minnesota.

Key study: Lykken's fearless psychopaths (1957)

Question. David T. Lykken (1928 to 2006) asked whether primary psychopaths, the unanxious type Cleckley described, are deficient in fear and therefore in learning to avoid punishment. Method. He compared three groups: prison inmates whom staff judged to fit Cleckley's picture closely (primary sociopaths), antisocial inmates who did not fit Cleckley's picture in important respects (neurotic sociopaths), and non-offender controls. He measured anxiety by questionnaire, and measured conditioned fear by recording skin conductance (sweating of the skin, an index of autonomic arousal) to a signal that was paired with electric shock. The ingenious part was a "mental maze": a sequence of 20 choice points, at each of which the subject pressed one of four levers. One lever was correct and moved him on; one of the three wrong levers also delivered a shock. Subjects were told only to learn the route; nobody mentioned that shocks could be avoided. Result. The primary psychopaths learned the correct route about as well as the others, but they learned much less to avoid the shocked errors; they reported the least anxiety; and their skin conductance responses to the signal for shock were the smallest. Conclusion. Lykken proposed the fearlessness hypothesis: psychopaths have an unusually high threshold for fear, so punishment teaches them little, and much of the rest of the syndrome follows. Criticism. The groups were small, the selection of "primary" psychopaths rested on staff judgement, and later work showed that the deficit depends on the task: psychopaths learn to avoid losing money as well as other people do, and their avoidance learning is poorest when they are also pursuing a reward.

Lykken's finding was extended in two directions. The first was autonomic. Hare (1965) showed that psychopathic prisoners had lower resting skin conductance than other inmates and that their skin conductance rose less steeply as an announced shock approached: their bodies did not prepare for punishment. This led to the underarousal hypothesis, which says that antisocial people have chronically low levels of autonomic and cortical arousal and seek stimulation (fights, drugs, risks, crime) to raise it to a comfortable level, much as a bored child misbehaves. Adrian Raine and his colleagues tested this prospectively. Measuring schoolboys at age 15, Raine, Venables and Williams (1990) found that those who had a criminal record by 24 had shown lower resting heart rate, lower skin conductance activity and more slow-wave electroencephalographic activity years before; arousal measures alone classified 74.7 percent of the boys correctly. In a meta-analysis of 40 studies of 5,868 children, Ortiz and Raine (2004) found low resting heart rate to be the best-replicated biological correlate of antisocial behaviour in children and adolescents, with an even larger effect when heart rate was measured during a stressor. And in a 20-year study of 1,795 children, Gao, Raine and colleagues (2010) found that those who had a criminal record at 23 had shown significantly poorer fear conditioning at the age of three than matched children who did not offend, which points to an early deficit in the amygdala's threat learning.

The second direction was theoretical. Hans Jürgen Eysenck (1916 to 1997) built an arousal theory of crime in Crime and Personality (1964). Conscience, he argued, is a set of conditioned anxiety responses acquired in childhood when misbehaviour is punished; extraverts are cortically underaroused and condition poorly, so they acquire weaker consciences, and people high in extraversion and neuroticism (and, in his later editions, psychoticism) should be over-represented among offenders. Jeffrey Alan Gray (1934 to 2004) proposed that behaviour is governed partly by a behavioural inhibition system, sensitive to signals of punishment and non-reward, and a behavioural activation (reward) system; Don Fowles (1980) applied the model to psychopathy, arguing that psychopaths have a weak inhibition system with a normal or strong reward system, which explains Lykken's result neatly. Joseph P. Newman offered a cognitive alternative, the response modulation hypothesis. In a card-playing task by Newman, Patterson and Kosson (1987), the chance of winning began at about 90 percent and fell steadily to zero; psychopathic offenders kept playing and losing long after non-psychopathic offenders had stopped. On Newman's account the problem is not lack of fear but a difficulty in shifting attention from a goal they are pursuing to the warning signs around it.

The brain has been examined directly too (the anatomy belongs to the Physiological book). Raine and colleagues (2000) scanned 21 community volunteers with ASPD and found an 11 percent reduction in prefrontal grey matter, without any obvious brain lesion, together with reduced autonomic responses while they gave a videotaped speech about their own faults. The prefrontal cortex, with the amygdala, is needed for linking emotion to decisions, and its damage in adults can produce a "pseudopsychopathic" change in personality, as in the famous case of Phineas Gage and in the frontal lobe syndrome (Chapter 10, Section 10.11.10). Table 11.16 gathers the findings.

Table 11.16 The biological findings in antisocial personality and psychopathy.
FindingStudyWhat it suggests
Poor avoidance of shocked errors; low anxiety; small conditioned skin conductance responsesLykken, 1957Fearlessness: punishment teaches little
Low resting skin conductance; little rise as shock approachesHare, 1965Bodies do not prepare for punishment
Low heart rate, skin conductance and slow EEG at 15 predict crime at 24Raine, Venables and Williams, 1990Underarousal comes before the offending
Low resting heart rate across 40 studies of childrenOrtiz and Raine, 2004The best-replicated biological marker
Poor fear conditioning at age 3 in later offendersGao and colleagues, 2010An early amygdala deficit
Keep playing a losing card gameNewman, Patterson and Kosson, 1987Response modulation: cannot switch from a goal to warnings
11 percent less prefrontal grey matterRaine and colleagues, 2000Weak prefrontal control of emotion and behaviour
Exam trap

The fearlessness hypothesis is Lykken's (1957; the mental maze and avoidance of shock); the underarousal hypothesis says that low arousal drives stimulation seeking (low resting heart rate and skin conductance; Raine). They are related but not the same: one is about too little fear, the other about too little arousal. Eysenck's theory (1964) is that underaroused extraverts condition poorly and acquire weak consciences; Gray's behavioural inhibition system was applied to psychopathy by Fowles; the card-playing perseveration task is Newman's.

Genes, families and society

Family, twin and adoption studies all show a genetic contribution to antisocial behaviour, and DSM-5-TR notes that the disorder is more common in the first-degree relatives of affected people; in such families, it adds, the men more often have ASPD and substance use disorders, while the women more often have what used to be called somatisation disorder. Adoption studies make the most interesting point, because they show genes and environment working together rather than separately. Crowe (1974) studied children of female felons who had been adopted by other families soon after birth. As adults they had more arrests, convictions and antisocial personality than adopted children of non-offending mothers, which suggests a genetic influence; but the adoptees of felons who went on to become criminals had also spent longer in temporary orphanage care before adoption, a hint that the genes mattered most in a poor early environment. Remi J. Cadoret and his colleagues in Iowa (1995) made the interaction explicit. In 95 male and 102 female adoptees separated at birth from their biological parents, a biological parent with ASPD predicted adolescent aggression, conduct disorder and adult antisocial behaviour; an adverse adoptive home (adoptive parents with marital, legal or psychiatric problems) independently predicted adult antisocial behaviour; and the adverse home raised aggression and conduct disorder only in adoptees who also had the biological background of ASPD. A molecular version of the same lesson is the Dunedin finding of Avshalom Caspi, Terrie E. Moffitt and colleagues (2002) that maltreated boys with a low-activity form of the MAOA gene were the most likely to become antisocial, told as a Key study in Chapter 1, Section 1.5.

Which environments matter? DSM-5-TR names child abuse or neglect, unstable or erratic parenting and inconsistent discipline as factors that make it more likely that conduct disorder will evolve into adult ASPD. Gerald R. Patterson's account of coercive family processes, in which parent and child train each other into escalating aggression, is taught with conduct disorder in Section 11.9, together with Terrie Moffitt's distinction between antisocial behaviour that persists from early childhood through life and the far commoner kind that is limited to adolescence. The adult disorder is the end of the life-course-persistent path. Intelligence and opportunity also count: in a New Zealand cohort, White, Moffitt and Silva (1989) found that of the five-year-olds judged at high risk of delinquency, only 16 percent had been in trouble with the police by fifteen, and those who had tended to be the children with lower IQs. Poverty, deprived neighbourhoods and deviant peers add further risk.

Put together, the evidence supports an integrative picture. A child inherits a temperament that is fearless, underaroused and impulsive; harsh, inconsistent or neglectful care fails to build the conscience that fear-based learning would normally lay down, or actively teaches that aggression works; the child's behaviour drives away prosocial friends and attracts deviant ones, ends his schooling early and closes off legitimate work; and the resulting adult life of failure and frustration feeds further antisocial acts. No single factor is sufficient, which is exactly why prevention aims at the earliest links in the chain.

Exam trap

The Iowa adoption study showing a gene-environment interaction for aggression and conduct disorder (biological ASPD plus an adverse adoptive home) is Cadoret and colleagues (1995); the MAOA gene and maltreatment finding in Dunedin boys is Caspi, Moffitt and colleagues (2002). Both say the same thing: genetic risk shows itself most in a harmful environment.

Treatment, and antisocial personality in Indian law

Clinicians are pessimistic about treating adults with ASPD, for good reasons. The person rarely thinks he needs help, usually arrives because a court, an employer or a family has insisted, and may use therapy to learn what to say. The most sobering study came from a maximum-security therapeutic community at Oak Ridge in Ontario. Marnie Rice, Grant Harris and Catherine Cormier (1992) compared offenders who had been through its intensive programme with matched offenders who had not, over a follow-up averaging more than ten years. For non-psychopaths, treatment was associated with less violent reoffending; for psychopaths, it was associated with more. One interpretation is that a programme built on insight and empathy taught some psychopaths to understand and manipulate others better without making them care.

That is not the whole story. In a study of 152 high-risk violent offenders treated in an intensive cognitive-behavioural violence reduction programme in Saskatoon, Canada, the men who made more therapeutic change committed less violence over about five years of follow-up, although men who scored higher on the PCL-R's "selfish, callous and remorseless use of others" made less change (Olver, Lewis and Wong, 2013). The NICE guideline for ASPD (2009) recommends group-based cognitive and behavioural interventions (for example, programmes such as "reasoning and rehabilitation") to address impulsivity, interpersonal difficulties and offending, both in the community and in institutions; it advises that pharmacological interventions should not be routinely used for ASPD or for its associated aggression, anger and impulsivity, though coexisting depression, anxiety and substance use should be treated in their own right; and it notes that positive, reinforcing approaches are more likely to succeed than punitive ones. Contingency management for coexisting drug use appears to work at least as well in these patients as in others, perhaps because it rewards immediate, concrete gains, the kind of reinforcement to which they respond. The greatest hope, though, lies earlier: parent management training and related programmes for children with conduct problems (Section 11.9; Book 2, Sections 4.10.4 and 10.3) aim to break the chain before the adult disorder forms.

What about the law? In India the insanity defence is section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023 (formerly section 84 of the Indian Penal Code): nothing is an offence if, at the time, by reason of unsoundness of mind, the person was incapable of knowing the nature of the act, or that it was wrong or contrary to law (Book 2, Section 10.9.4 teaches it in full). The test is cognitive, and India has no irresistible impulse limb and no general defence of diminished responsibility. Antisocial personality disorder therefore offers no defence. Jayant knew exactly what he was doing: he used false names, hid the money and planned his story, and the courts treat precisely this kind of planning and concealment as evidence that a person knew his act was wrong. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 defines mental illness as a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgement, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life, a definition that a personality disorder alone rarely meets. In practice, the clinician's role with antisocial clients in India lies in prisons, in addiction services and in court-ordered assessments, where the diagnosis informs risk and management rather than excusing the act.

Clinical link

Assessing a person who may be lying for gain changes how you gather evidence. Interview more than once and check for consistency; always obtain collateral information (family, school and police records, previous hospital notes), as DSM-5-TR advises; separate in your report what you observed, what you were told and what you infer; and notice your own reactions, since being charmed or flattered in the first meeting is itself data. Remember too that Jayant and Qasim share a diagnosis but not a mind: the anxious, remorseful, traumatised offender may benefit from treatment of his trauma, mood and drinking in ways that the callous, unanxious one will not.

Exam trap

Adult ASPD has no approved drug treatment; NICE (2009) recommends group-based cognitive and behavioural programmes for offending and advises against routine medication for the disorder or its aggression and impulsivity. In the Oak Ridge therapeutic community study, treatment was followed by more violent reoffending in psychopaths. In Indian law the insanity defence (BNS section 22, formerly IPC section 84) is a cognitive test, so ASPD, in which the person knows what he is doing and that it is wrong, is not a defence.

11.6 Histrionic and Narcissistic Personality DisordersMedium yield · 4 Qs

Walk into any large wedding in India and, sooner or later, you will find the person the evening seems to revolve around. Perhaps it is the cousin who arrives late in the most striking outfit in the hall, hugs people she met once at a naming ceremony as if they were sisters, cries openly during the vidaai and is laughing again five minutes later. Perhaps it is the uncle who mentions, within a minute of meeting you, that the minister is a personal friend, that his son's firm is "the best in the country", and that he was, naturally, seated at the main table. Both of them want to be noticed. Most people like this are simply lively, proud or a little vain, and the wedding would be duller without them. But for a small number the need to be noticed is rigid, all-consuming and costly, and it shapes every relationship they have. Those are the two disorders of this section.

Histrionic and narcissistic personality disorder are the two attention-drawing members of Cluster B, the group that DSM-5-TR describes as "dramatic, emotional or erratic" (Section 11.1 sets out the clusters; the other two members, borderline and antisocial personality disorder, are taught in Sections 11.3 to 11.5). The question this section answers is the one students find hardest: if both people want attention, what exactly is the difference? The short answer is that the histrionic person wants to be the centre of attention and will take it in any form, even as a fragile or pitiable figure, while the narcissistic person wants attention of one kind only, admiration for being superior. To see why that difference matters, and why it is not the only one, you need to meet each disorder properly: where its name came from, what its criteria are trying to capture, what it looks and sounds like in a consulting room, and how the theorists have tried to explain it.

From hysteria to histrionic: the history of a name

The word "histrionic" comes from the Latin histrio, an actor, and it means theatrical in manner. It is a recent word for an old idea. For more than two thousand years, a dramatic, emotional, attention-seeking style was folded into the much larger concept of hysteria, from the Greek hystera, the womb. The ancient Greek physicians thought that many unexplained complaints of women were caused by a wandering uterus, and the word kept its female associations long after that theory had been abandoned. Chapter 7, Section 7.1 tells the story of hysteria in full, from the wandering womb to Jean-Martin Charcot's demonstrations at the Salpêtrière and Freud's idea of conversion. What matters here is that the word came to carry two quite different meanings. One was a set of physical symptoms without an organic cause: paralyses, blindness, fits and anaesthesias, which later became conversion disorder. The other was a type of character: vain, excitable, seductive, emotionally changeable and hungry for attention. Psychoanalysts in the first half of the twentieth century wrote at length about this "hysterical character" and assumed that it was the soil in which conversion symptoms grew.

That assumption was challenged in 1958 by two American psychiatrists, Paul Chodoff and Henry Lyons, in a paper whose title says it all: "Hysteria, the hysterical personality and 'hysterical' conversion". They pointed out that the one word was being used for two different things, and that conversion symptoms and the hysterical personality often occurred apart: many patients with conversion symptoms did not have a hysterical personality at all. Chodoff went on to argue that the textbook picture of the "hysterical female" was less a description of a disorder than a caricature of femininity drawn by male observers, and that the name itself was pejorative.

The official manuals followed slowly. The first edition of the American Psychiatric Association's manual, DSM-I (1952), had no category called hysterical personality; some of its features were placed under a diagnosis called emotionally unstable personality. DSM-II (1968), strongly influenced by psychoanalysis, introduced hysterical personality, a pattern of excitability, emotional instability, over-reactivity and self-dramatisation that was attention seeking and often seductive, and gave "histrionic personality disorder" in brackets as an alternative name. DSM-III (1980), which set out to describe disorders by what can be observed rather than by their presumed causes and which broke hysteria apart (Chapter 7, Section 7.1.8), made the bracketed name the official one. Since then histrionic personality disorder has been the DSM's heir to the old hysterical personality, and it has kept its place in every edition up to DSM-5-TR (2022), where its code is F60.4. ICD-10 (1992) also lists histrionic personality disorder under F60.4, and its inclusion terms tell you the history in miniature: "hysterical personality" and "psychoinfantile personality". ICD-11 (2022) abolished all the named personality disorder types, so histrionic personality disorder has no ICD-11 equivalent at all, and it is also one of the four types (with paranoid, schizoid and dependent) left out of the six retained in DSM-5's Alternative Model for Personality Disorders (Section 11.8 explains both new models). Table 11.17 sets out the changes of name.

Table 11.17 From hysteria to histrionic: how the dramatic personality was named.
SourceName usedWhat changed
Ancient Greek medicineHysteria (from hystera, womb)Unexplained complaints of women blamed on the uterus; the female association begins
Early psychoanalysisHysterical characterA personality type assumed to underlie conversion symptoms
Chodoff and Lyons, 1958Hysterical personality versus conversionThe personality and the bodily symptoms shown to occur apart
DSM-I, 1952Emotionally unstable personalityNo hysterical personality category; some features placed here
DSM-II, 1968Hysterical personality (histrionic personality disorder)Attention-seeking, seductive, self-dramatising pattern named
DSM-III, 1980, to DSM-5-TR, 2022Histrionic personality disorder"Hysteria" dropped; behavioural criteria; Cluster B
ICD-10, 1992Histrionic personality disorder (F60.4)Includes "hysterical" and "psychoinfantile" personality
ICD-11, 2022NoneNamed types abolished; severity and trait domains instead (Section 11.8)
Exam trap

Do not confuse three things that share a word. Hysterical neurosis (DSM-II) is the old name for conversion and dissociative symptoms, now Chapter 7's disorders. Hysterical personality (DSM-II) is the old name for histrionic personality disorder. And the "emotionally unstable personality" of DSM-I, a forerunner of the histrionic pattern, is not ICD-10's emotionally unstable personality disorder (F60.3), which is ICD-10's name for the borderline and impulsive patterns (Section 11.3). A person can have histrionic personality disorder and never have a conversion symptom, and the reverse.

The histrionic pattern: eight criteria in four groups

Kiara is 29 and works for a public relations firm in Bengaluru. She is referred by a physician who has seen her five times in two months for headaches, palpitations and a pain in her side that has now moved to her back; every test is normal. In the waiting room she is already chatting with the receptionist, and when the psychologist calls her in she says warmly, "Oh, I love your kurta, you have such good taste, I knew you'd understand me." She is dressed with great care and checks her reflection in the glass of the bookcase. Asked why she has come, she describes the end of a relationship: the man, whom she had known for three weeks, was "my soulmate, honestly the love of my life", and her friends were all "devastated for me". When the psychologist asks what was special about him, she pauses and says, "He was just... amazing, you know? So real. Everyone said so." Her tears come quickly and stop just as quickly when she remembers a funny story about the office party. She calls the physician who referred her by his first name and refers to him as "a very dear friend". Her manager has spoken to her twice about being flirtatious with clients, and the women in her team have quietly stopped including her in their lunches. She cannot understand why. "I'm the one who makes everything fun," she says. "Without me that office would be a morgue."

Nothing Kiara does is bizarre, and none of it, taken alone, is a symptom. Many lively people compliment strangers, dress to be noticed and cry at films. What makes her pattern a disorder is that it is pervasive (it appears at work, with friends, with doctors and in love), inflexible (she cannot switch it off when it costs her), long-standing (her mother says she was "always the star, always performing"), and impairing (she has lost two jobs and every close female friendship). DSM-5-TR defines histrionic personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of excessive emotionality and attention seeking, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, shown by five or more of eight criteria. The eight criteria are easier to remember and to understand if you group them by what each is trying to capture.

The need for the spotlight. Three criteria describe the drive to be seen. The person is uncomfortable, or feels unappreciated, in situations in which she or he is not the centre of attention (criterion 1). Such people are often charming and lively at first meeting, the "life of the party", but when attention drifts elsewhere they may do something dramatic to bring it back: tell a story, make a scene, develop a new and vivid symptom. The person consistently uses physical appearance to draw attention (criterion 4), spending a great deal of time, energy and money on clothes and grooming, fishing for compliments, and being upset out of all proportion by a critical remark about her looks or an unflattering photograph. And interaction with others is often marked by inappropriate sexually seductive or provocative behaviour (criterion 2). The important word is inappropriate: the seductiveness is not confined to people the person is romantically interested in but spills into professional and social relationships where it does not belong, such as Kiara's dealings with clients. Clinicians usually understand the seductiveness as a way of holding attention rather than an expression of sexual desire, and DSM-5-TR notes that people with the disorder may have difficulty achieving real emotional intimacy in romantic or sexual relationships.

Emotion on the surface. Two criteria describe how emotion is displayed. The person shows rapidly shifting and shallow expression of emotions (criterion 3), and self-dramatisation, theatricality and exaggerated expression of emotion (criterion 6). These two go together but are not the same. Criterion 6 is about size: embracing casual acquaintances with excessive ardour, sobbing uncontrollably on minor sentimental occasions, temper tantrums. Criterion 3 is about depth and speed. The emotions seem to be switched on and off too quickly to be deeply felt, which is why other people often accuse the person of faking. Notice the descriptive term here. Chapter 2, Section 2.8 distinguishes shallow affect (emotion shown without depth or conviction) from labile affect (emotion that changes rapidly); histrionic display is both at once, intense on the surface and quickly gone.

A style of thinking. Two criteria describe a cognitive style, and they are the ones students forget. The person has a style of speech that is excessively impressionistic and lacking in detail (criterion 5). Strong opinions are delivered with dramatic flair, but the reasons behind them are vague: a person is "a wonderful human being", but no example can be given of anything wonderful she has done. Kiara's "He was just... amazing, you know?" is the textbook case. Aaron Beck and his colleagues described this impressionistic cognitive style as a tendency to see situations in global, black-and-white terms, without the details that would allow a more careful judgement. The person is also suggestible (criterion 7): easily influenced by other people and by passing fads, overly trusting of strong authority figures who seem able to solve their problems magically, quick to play hunches and adopt new convictions. In this sense suggestibility means a readiness to take on the opinions and feelings of others, not the hypnotic sense of the word.

Imagined closeness. The last criterion describes how relationships are perceived: the person considers relationships to be more intimate than they actually are (criterion 8), calling almost every acquaintance "my dear, dear friend" or referring to a doctor met once or twice by his first name. The irony is that the person who feels close to everyone is usually close to almost no one.

Beyond the criteria, DSM-5-TR describes several associated features. Impairment in histrionic personality disorder tends to be lower than in many other personality disorders, and it is mainly interpersonal. The interpersonal style is one of social dominance, which can range from a "warmer" dominance that is intrusive and exhibitionistic to a "colder" dominance that is arrogant, controlling and even aggressive. Romantic relationships suffer most: people with histrionic symptoms are more likely to divorce or never to marry. Same-sex friendships are strained because a provocative style can look like a threat to the friend's own relationships, and friends are worn out by the constant demand for attention. The person craves novelty and excitement, becomes bored with routine, finds delayed gratification hard, starts jobs and projects with great enthusiasm that soon fades, and may neglect long-standing relationships for the excitement of new ones. The person often becomes depressed when not the centre of attention, and although the actual suicide risk is not known, clinical experience suggests an increased risk of suicidal gestures and threats. Histrionic personality disorder often occurs with borderline, narcissistic, paranoid, dependent and antisocial personality disorders and with substance misuse, and it is thought to be related to somatic symptom disorder, conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder) and major depressive disorder. Kiara's travelling pain is typical: a vivid physical complaint is a reliable way to be attended to.

How common is it? The answer depends heavily on the survey. In a probability subsample of the American National Comorbidity Survey Replication the estimated prevalence was 0.0 percent, in the National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions (NESARC) it was 1.8 percent, and across five epidemiological studies the median was 0.9 percent. Figures that range from zero to almost two percent for the same disorder in the same country are themselves a lesson: the result depends on the interview used, on how strictly each criterion is scored, and on how much distress or impairment is required. It is one reason the newer models of Section 11.8 have moved away from counting criteria.

The differential diagnosis follows directly from the criteria. Borderline personality disorder can also involve attention seeking, manipulation and rapidly shifting emotion, but it is distinguished by self-destructiveness, angry disruptions in close relationships, chronic feelings of deep emptiness and identity disturbance (Section 11.3). Antisocial and histrionic personality disorder share impulsivity, superficiality, excitement seeking, recklessness, seductiveness and manipulation, but the histrionic person is more exaggerated in emotion and does not characteristically behave antisocially; above all, DSM-5-TR says, the histrionic person manipulates to gain nurturance, whereas the antisocial person manipulates to gain profit, power or some other material gratification. Narcissistic personality disorder also craves attention, but wants praise for superiority (Section 11.6.5). The dependent person relies heavily on others for praise and guidance but without the flamboyant, exaggerated emotion. A manic or hypomanic episode can look histrionic, with expansive, flirtatious, dramatic behaviour, but it is an episode, a change from the person's usual self that comes with elevated or irritable mood, reduced need for sleep and pressured speech (Chapter 5), not a lifelong style. Finally, as for every personality disorder, the pattern must be distinguished from a personality change due to another medical condition and from the effects of persistent substance use, and many people have histrionic traits without the disorder; only when the traits are inflexible, maladaptive and persisting, and cause significant impairment or distress, do they amount to histrionic personality disorder.
