Exam trap

The core of histrionic personality disorder is excessive emotionality and attention seeking: five of eight criteria. Its signature extras, the ones that separate it from its neighbours, are seductive or provocative behaviour, shallow, rapidly shifting emotion, impressionistic speech, suggestibility and seeing relationships as more intimate than they are. Grandiosity is not a histrionic criterion (it is narcissistic); self-harm, emptiness and identity disturbance are not histrionic criteria (they are borderline); antisocial acts are not histrionic. The histrionic person is happy to be seen as fragile or dependent if that brings attention; the narcissist never is.

Is histrionic a woman's diagnosis? Sex bias, causes and treatment

Because the disorder descends from hysteria, it has carried a question with it from the start: is histrionic personality disorder a real pattern that happens to be commoner in women, or is it a description of exaggerated femininity that clinicians are too ready to see in women? The criteria themselves invite the suspicion. Concern with appearance, emotional expressiveness, seductiveness and suggestibility are close to a list of what many societies have expected, and sometimes demanded, of women. In 1983 the psychologist Marcie Kaplan made the point with a famous piece of satire in the American Psychologist, "A woman's view of DSM-III". She invented criteria for an "independent personality disorder", worded as a mirror image of DSM-III's dependent personality disorder but built from stereotypically masculine traits: the person puts work (career) above relationships with loved ones, is reluctant to take others' needs into account when making decisions about career or leisure, and passively allows others to take responsibility for major areas of social life because of an inability to express the necessary emotion (letting a spouse do most of the child care, for example). If those traits are not a disorder, Kaplan asked, why are their feminine mirror images?

Satire makes an argument; it does not test one. The question of whether clinicians actually diagnose differently depending on the patient's sex needs an experiment, and the classic one was done a few years later.

Key study: Ford and Widiger's case histories (1989)

Maureen R. Ford and Thomas A. Widiger wanted to know whether the different rates of histrionic and antisocial personality disorder in women and men reflect real differences or bias in the people making the diagnosis, and whether DSM-III's explicit criteria protect against that bias. They sent case histories to 354 psychologists. Some cases were written to fit antisocial personality disorder and some to fit histrionic personality disorder, with varying degrees of ambiguity, and in different versions the same patient was described as male, as female, or with the sex unspecified; everything else was identical. One group of psychologists made diagnoses; another rated how well features of the cases met individual histrionic and antisocial criteria. When the antisocial case was labelled male, most psychologists diagnosed antisocial personality disorder; when exactly the same case was labelled female, many diagnosed histrionic personality disorder instead. Labelling the histrionic case female also made the histrionic diagnosis more likely. Yet when the psychologists rated the separate criteria, no sex bias appeared. The conclusion was subtle and important: the bias did not lie in how clinicians read each criterion but in the overall diagnostic judgement, where expectations about base rates in men and women crept in. The criticism is the usual one for vignette studies: written case histories are not patients, and the criteria were those of DSM-III.

DSM-5-TR takes a cautious line. In clinical settings, it says, histrionic personality disorder has been diagnosed more often in women, but the sex ratio is not significantly different from the proportion of women in those clinical settings; and some studies using structured assessments report similar prevalence in men and women. In other words, a good part of the apparent female excess may simply reflect the fact that more women than men come to clinics, together with the kind of diagnostic judgement Ford and Widiger exposed. The male histrionic patient exists, and he may be missed: the bodybuilder who must be the centre of every gathering, the man whose exaggerated stories and flirtation with every colleague are read as charm rather than pathology.

A related idea, proposed by C. Robert Cloninger and developed by Scott O. Lilienfeld (1960 to 2020), is that histrionic and antisocial personality may be sex-typed expressions of one shared underlying disposition. Lilienfeld and colleagues reported in 1986 that roughly two-thirds of people with histrionic personality also met criteria for antisocial personality disorder, and the suggestion followed that the same impulsive, attention-hungry temperament might come out as a histrionic pattern in women and as an antisocial pattern in men. The hypothesis remains controversial.

Beyond that, surprisingly little is known about the causes of histrionic personality disorder, given its long history. A Norwegian twin study by Svenn Torgersen and colleagues (2000), with 221 twin pairs, estimated the heritability of histrionic personality disorder at about 0.67, but the sample was small, and later and much larger Norwegian twin studies have generally found more modest heritabilities for personality disorders: in 1,386 Norwegian twin pairs, Torgersen and colleagues (2008) put the heritability of histrionic traits at 31 percent and of narcissistic traits at 24 percent (Section 11.7.7 gives the figures for Cluster C). In one of those larger studies, Kenneth S. Kendler and colleagues (2008) found that histrionic traits loaded on a broad genetic factor shared with paranoid, borderline, narcissistic, dependent and obsessive-compulsive personality disorders, a general vulnerability rather than a histrionic-specific one. Psychoanalysts saw the hysterical character as a search for the love and attention that a parent had withheld. Theodore Millon (1928 to 2014), whose biosocial learning theory shaped the DSM-III personality disorders, placed histrionic personality next to dependent personality. In his 1969 textbook Modern Psychopathology he crossed an active versus passive dimension with four ways of seeking reinforcement (detached, dependent, independent and ambivalent), giving eight basic patterns; the passive-dependent pattern, which he called submissive, is the ancestor of dependent personality disorder, and the active-dependent pattern, which he called gregarious, is the ancestor of histrionic personality disorder. Both rely on others for their sense of worth, but where the dependent person waits passively to be looked after, the histrionic person actively courts attention and approval. DSM-5-TR makes a related point: histrionic people usually have less insight than dependent people into their own dependency needs.

Treatment research is just as thin. Much has been written about helping people with histrionic personality disorder, but little of the research demonstrates success. An early behavioural report by Kass, Silvers and Abrams (1972) described five women, four of whom had been admitted after suicide attempts, who were rewarded for appropriate interaction and "fined" for attention-getting behaviour; the therapists saw improvement at an 18-month follow-up but collected no controlled data. Most therapy works on the problematic relationships: helping the person see how the short-term gains of a dramatic, charming or complaining style turn into long-term costs, and learning more direct ways of asking for what she or he needs. A large Dutch trial of schema therapy (Bamelis and colleagues, 2014; see Section 11.7.7) included patients with histrionic personality disorder among others and found that results did not differ by diagnosis. No medicine treats the disorder itself; medicines are used, if at all, for an accompanying depression (Book 2, Section 5.7 describes schema therapy, and Book 2, Chapter 5 the cognitive therapies).

Clinical link

The histrionic pattern shows itself in the consulting room first. DSM-5-TR notes flattery, gifts and dramatic descriptions of symptoms that are replaced by new ones at each visit. The flattery is pleasant and the drama is engaging, and both can pull a young clinician into a special, overly personal relationship. Two habits help. Ask for detail, gently and repeatedly ("What exactly did he say? What happened next?"), because the impressionistic style hides the facts and the facts are where the work is. And keep the frame steady (time, place, fees, boundaries), so that attention in therapy comes from the work rather than from performance.

Indian context

DSM-5-TR warns that norms for emotional expressiveness, appearance and interpersonal style vary widely across cultures, genders and age groups. Open weeping at a daughter's farewell or at a funeral, elaborate dress at a festival, and warm terms of endearment for near-strangers ("didi", "beta") are expected in many Indian settings and are not evidence of anything. DSM-5-TR also asks clinicians to separate a lasting histrionic pattern from the reactive, context-bound display that competitive peer groups can produce, including the "need to be liked". A college student performing for her followers on social media is not, on that evidence, histrionic; the question is always whether the pattern is pervasive, inflexible and impairing across her whole life.

From Narcissus to narcissistic personality disorder

The second disorder of this section takes its name from a Greek myth, best known in the Roman poet Ovid's Metamorphoses. Narcissus was a youth of great beauty who spurned everyone who loved him, among them the nymph Echo, and was punished by falling in love with his own reflection in a pool. Unable to leave it and unable to possess it, he wasted away beside the water, and where he died a flower grew. The myth is worth remembering accurately, because it already contains the paradox of the modern disorder: Narcissus is not content with himself. He is trapped by an image he can never reach.

The myth entered psychology at the end of the nineteenth century. The English sexologist Havelock Ellis (1859 to 1939) in 1898 compared a form of auto-erotism, sexual interest turned towards one's own body, to Narcissus, and the German psychiatrist Paul Näcke (1851 to 1913) turned the comparison into a term, Narcismus, in 1899. Otto Rank (1884 to 1939) published the first psychoanalytic paper on narcissism in 1911, linking it to vanity and self-admiration. The decisive text was Sigmund Freud's (1856 to 1939) essay "On Narcissism: An Introduction" (1914). Freud distinguished two kinds. What he called primary narcissism is the infant's original investment of libido in itself, a necessary stage, he said, between auto-erotism and love for other people. In secondary narcissism, libido that had been invested in other people is withdrawn from them and turned back onto the self, as Freud believed happened in schizophrenia (Chapter 4 tells that part of the story). Freud also concluded that patients whose libido was turned inwards in this way could not form a transference and so could not be analysed, a pessimism that later analysts would overturn.

The idea of narcissism as a type of personality came next. The Austrian psychoanalyst Robert Waelder (1900 to 1967) described what he called a narcissistic personality in 1925, with a case study. But the term "narcissistic personality disorder" belongs to Heinz Kohut (1913 to 1981), who used it in a 1968 paper on the psychoanalytic treatment of such patients and developed his ideas in The Analysis of the Self (1971). Otto F. Kernberg (born 1928) gave a rival account in Borderline Conditions and Pathological Narcissism (1975). The debate between the two men was at its height when DSM-III was being written, and narcissistic personality disorder entered the manual for the first time in DSM-III in 1980. It has stayed in every edition since, though not without a fight: in 2010 the work group preparing DSM-5 proposed dropping it, strong objections from clinicians and researchers followed, and it was kept. It also survives as one of the six types in DSM-5's Alternative Model (Section 11.8). In DSM-5-TR its code is F60.81.

The World Health Organization never gave the disorder the same standing. ICD-10 mentions narcissistic personality disorder only by name, as one of the inclusion terms under F60.8, "other specific personality disorders", alongside such conditions as eccentric, "haltlose" (drifting, aimless), immature, passive-aggressive and psychoneurotic personality, without any description of its own. ICD-11 has no narcissistic category, because it has no named types at all.

Exam trap

Narcissistic personality disorder has no separate code in ICD-10; it is listed only by name under F60.8, other specific personality disorders. Histrionic personality disorder, by contrast, has its own ICD-10 code (F60.4). The term "narcissistic personality disorder" is usually credited to Kohut (1968); "On Narcissism" (primary and secondary narcissism) is Freud (1914); the word Narcismus is Näcke's (1899) after Havelock Ellis's comparison (1898). NPD entered the DSM in DSM-III (1980).

The narcissistic pattern: the core triad and nine criteria

Aryan is 41. Three years ago he founded an education-technology company in Gurugram, and for a while it was the subject of admiring articles. Last month it collapsed. His wife persuaded him to come to the clinic after finding him drinking alone at three in the morning and hearing him say that he "would rather be dead than pitied". He arrives ten minutes late, does not apologise, and asks the psychologist where she trained and whether "the head of the department" was not available. For forty minutes he talks without a pause: the company failed because the investors were "small men who could not see the vision"; a college friend whose firm is thriving "got lucky, and everyone knows he took my ideas"; he has been compared, he says, to the greatest founders of his generation. When the psychologist mentions that his wife sounded frightened on the phone, he looks puzzled. "She is very lucky to be married to me. She knows that." He has not asked the psychologist a single question about herself, and when she has to end the session on time he is visibly offended. Only at the door does something else show. He says, quietly, that he has not slept properly in weeks, that he replays the board meeting at which he was removed every night, and that he cannot bear anyone from his old circle to see him now.

DSM-5-TR defines narcissistic personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of grandiosity (in fantasy or behaviour), need for admiration and lack of empathy, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, shown by five or more of nine criteria. Those three phrases are the core triad of narcissistic personality disorder, and nearly everything that sets the disorder apart from its neighbours comes back to the triad. The nine criteria fall naturally into the three groups, as Table 11.18 shows.

Table 11.18 The nine DSM-5-TR criteria for narcissistic personality disorder grouped under the core triad (five or more are needed).
Core themeCriterion (in brief)What it can sound like
Grandiosity1. Grandiose sense of self-importance; expects to be recognised as superior without matching achievements"Frankly, the company was me."
Grandiosity2. Preoccupied with fantasies of unlimited success, power, brilliance, beauty or ideal love"In five years they will be studying my model at Harvard."
Grandiosity3. Believes he or she is special and unique and can be understood only by, or should associate only with, high-status people or institutions"I asked for the head of the department."
Grandiosity9. Arrogant, haughty behaviours or attitudesSnobbish, disdainful, patronising
Need for admiration4. Requires excessive admirationFishes for compliments; expects his arrival to be greeted with fanfare
Need for admiration8. Often envious of others, or believes others envy him or her"He got lucky; everyone knows he took my ideas."
Lack of empathy5. Sense of entitlement: unreasonable expectation of especially favourable treatment or automatic complianceDoes not expect to wait in line; furious when he must
Lack of empathy6. Interpersonally exploitative: takes advantage of others to achieve his or her own endsOverworks his staff without regard to their lives
Lack of empathy7. Lacks empathy: unwilling to recognise or identify with the feelings and needs of others"She is lucky to be married to me."

Each group deserves a closer look, because each criterion has a reason for being there. Grandiosity is an exaggerated sense of one's own importance, value or ability, and it may be lived out in behaviour (boasting, pretension, expecting to be treated as superior) or kept mostly in fantasy (rehearsing scenes of future triumph, comparing oneself with the famous). Its constant companion is an under-valuing of other people's contributions. The belief in being special leads to an insistence on the "top" doctor, lawyer, teacher or institution, and DSM-5-TR makes a revealing remark here: the person's own self-esteem is enhanced, or "mirrored", by the idealised value he assigns to the people he associates with, and he may devalue the credentials of those who disappoint him. Aryan's question about the head of department is criterion 3 at work.

The need for admiration looks at first like confidence. DSM-5-TR says the opposite: the person's self-esteem is almost invariably very fragile, and the struggle with severe internal self-doubt, self-criticism and emptiness is what drives the constant search for admiration. Envy belongs in the same group because it is the shadow of admiration. If my worth depends on being admired more than others, another person's success is a direct threat to me, and so I must either begrudge it, devalue it or assure myself that others envy me.

The lack of empathy group explains how the person treats other people. The first of these, entitlement, is the unreasonable expectation of especially favourable treatment or of automatic compliance with one's wishes; when it combines with insensitivity to others' needs it leads, consciously or not, to exploitation, in which friendships and relationships are formed mainly when the other person seems likely to advance one's purposes or enhance one's self-esteem. DSM-5-TR is precise about what kind of empathy is missing. People with narcissistic personality disorder tend to have some cognitive empathy, the ability to understand another person's perspective intellectually, but lack emotional empathy, the ability to feel what another person is feeling. That is why the narcissistic person can often read a room very well (and use what he reads) while being oblivious to the hurt his remarks inflict, exuberantly telling a former partner that he is now in "the relationship of a lifetime", or boasting of his health to a friend who is ill. When other people's needs are noticed, they tend to be seen as signs of weakness.

Behind the criteria lies the feature that the criteria themselves do not list, and that the vignette's last minute revealed: vulnerability. Because the self-esteem is so fragile, the person is very sensitive to criticism or defeat. Such experiences may leave him feeling ashamed, humiliated, degraded, hollow and empty, even if he does not show it. He may react with disdain, rage or a defiant counterattack; or he may withdraw socially, or put on an appearance of humility that masks and protects the grandiosity. Some people with the disorder are highly successful, combining professional ability with self-control and an interpersonal distance that lets them marry and keep jobs; others have very low occupational functioning because they will not risk competing where they might fail. High perfectionist standards are common, together with a fear of having one's imperfections exposed, and sustained shame, envy and humiliation can lead to social withdrawal, emptiness and depressed mood. Section 11.6.6 shows how these two sides, the grandiose and the vulnerable, have been turned into two faces of the disorder.

The numbers again depend on the survey: an estimated 0.0 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 6.2 percent in NESARC, and a median of 1.6 percent across five epidemiological studies. Among adults diagnosed with the disorder, 50 to 75 percent are men. Narcissistic traits are particularly common in adolescents and do not by themselves predict the adult disorder, which is one reason for the general caution about diagnosing personality disorders in the young (Section 11.1). The disorder often first comes to clinical attention, or gets worse, during a crisis that punctures the grandiosity: a bankruptcy, a demotion or loss of work, a divorce. Aryan's collapse is typical. Ageing can be hard, because the physical and occupational limits that come with it are difficult to accept. But DSM-5-TR also offers a hopeful note: new durable relationships, real achievements and tolerable disappointments and setbacks can all be corrective and help people change. Culture matters as well: narcissistic traits may be higher in societies that emphasise individualism and personal autonomy over collective goals, and in such societies they may attract less clinical attention.

Aryan's remark that he would "rather be dead than pitied" should be taken seriously. When a person with narcissistic personality disorder is exposed to failure, imperfection and overwhelming emotion, especially under severe stress, suicidal thinking can follow, and DSM-5-TR notes that suicide attempts by people with this disorder tend to be less impulsive and of higher lethality than attempts by people with other personality disorders. The quiet, ashamed, high-achieving man after a public humiliation is a recognised high-risk picture, and it does not look like the dramatic, repeated self-harm of borderline personality disorder (Chapter 5, Sections 5.8 and 5.9 cover suicide risk assessment).

The differential diagnosis turns on one word. DSM-5-TR says that the most useful feature for telling narcissistic personality disorder apart from histrionic, antisocial and borderline personality disorder, whose interactive styles it describes as coquettish, callous and needy respectively, is the grandiosity of narcissistic personality disorder. Compared with borderline personality disorder, the narcissistic person also has a relatively stable self-image, more self-control, and relatively little self-destructiveness, impulsivity, separation insecurity or emotional over-reactivity (Section 11.3 develops this comparison). Compared with the histrionic person, the narcissist shows excessive pride in achievements, relatively little emotional display, and disdain for other people's sensitivities; all three may need a great deal of attention, but the narcissist needs that attention to be admiring. Antisocial and narcissistic personality disorder share a tough-minded, glib, superficial, exploitative and unempathic style, but narcissistic personality disorder does not necessarily include impulsive aggression and deceitfulness, and it usually lacks the childhood history of conduct disorder that antisocial personality disorder requires, and the adult criminal behaviour that so often goes with it (Section 11.5). Both narcissistic and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder may profess perfectionism, but the obsessive-compulsive person is immersed in order and rigidity and is usually self-critical, while the narcissistic person sets perfectionist standards for appearance and performance and is more likely to believe he has achieved perfection (Section 11.7.5). Suspiciousness and social withdrawal usually point to schizotypal, avoidant or paranoid personality disorder; when they appear in a narcissistic person they come from shame and from fear of having flaws exposed. Grandiosity also occurs in manic and hypomanic episodes, but there it comes with a change of mood and functioning and is episodic. And when a narcissistic injury leaves a lasting sense of inferiority and shame that resembles persistent depressive disorder, both diagnoses can be made if both are met. The disorder is associated with depressive disorders, anorexia nervosa and substance use disorders, especially cocaine use, and with histrionic, borderline, antisocial and paranoid personality disorders. Finally, many highly successful people show narcissistic traits; only when those traits are inflexible, maladaptive and persisting and cause significant impairment or distress do they constitute the disorder.

Exam trap

The DSM-5 core triad of narcissistic personality disorder is grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of empathy (five of nine criteria). Learn the distractors as the triads of other disorders: "impulsivity, identity disturbance and anger" is borderline; "suspiciousness, social anxiety and eccentricity" mixes paranoid and schizotypal (excessive social anxiety is a schizotypal criterion); "submissiveness, fear of separation and reassurance seeking" is dependent (Section 11.7.3). Asked which Cluster B disorder shows grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of empathy, answer narcissistic: borderline is instability and fear of abandonment, histrionic is emotionality and attention seeking, antisocial is disregard for and violation of others' rights. And remember the empathy detail: the narcissist often has cognitive empathy but lacks emotional empathy.

Two faces of narcissism: grandiose and vulnerable

Clinicians had long noticed that not every narcissistic patient looks like Aryan in his first forty minutes. Some are loud, boastful and impervious; others are shy, hypersensitive and quietly convinced that they deserve far more recognition than the world gives them. In 1989 the American psychiatrist Glen O. Gabbard argued that the DSM-III-R criteria, which describe the first kind, were too narrow to capture the range of narcissistic disturbance described by psychoanalysts. He proposed that narcissistic personality disorder lies on a continuum between two extremes. At one end is the oblivious narcissist: arrogant, aggressive, self-absorbed, needing to be the centre of attention, talking at people rather than with them and seemingly unaware of their reactions. At the other end is the hypervigilant narcissist: inhibited, shy, intensely sensitive to how others react, scanning them for any sign of criticism, easily hurt and ashamed, and avoiding the spotlight he secretly craves. Gabbard said the two could be told apart by the transference and countertransference they produce; roughly speaking, the clinician with an oblivious patient feels like an unnoticed audience, while the clinician with a hypervigilant patient feels watched for every slight.

Two years later the personality psychologist Paul Wink gave the distinction an empirical footing. In "Two faces of narcissism" (1991) he analysed six narcissism scales drawn from the Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI) and found that they did not measure one thing but two independent factors. One, which he called Grandiosity-Exhibitionism, went with extraversion, self-assurance, exhibitionism and aggression. The other, Vulnerability-Sensitivity, went with introversion, defensiveness, anxiety and vulnerability to life's traumas. Yet both factors shared a common core of conceit, self-indulgence and disregard of others. Wink argued for a distinction between overt and covert narcissism, and the names most used today, grandiose narcissism and vulnerable narcissism, descend from that work. Table 11.19 sets them side by side.

Table 11.19 Grandiose and vulnerable narcissism.
FeatureGrandiose (overt, oblivious)Vulnerable (covert, hypervigilant)
Outward mannerExtraverted, self-assured, exhibitionistic, aggressiveIntroverted, defensive, anxious, inhibited
AttentionSeeks the spotlight openlyCraves recognition but avoids exposure
Response to criticismSeems not to register it, or counterattacks with disdain and rageDeeply wounded; shame, brooding resentment, withdrawal
What the clinician feelsAn unnoticed audienceWatched for every slight
Shared coreConceit, self-indulgence, entitlement, disregard of othersThe same
Fit with DSM-5-TR criteriaClose: the nine criteria describe this facePartial: described mainly in the associated features

Are these two disorders or one? Many writers now treat them as two faces of the same fragile self-esteem, and DSM-5-TR's own description supports that view: the grandiose person "may react with disdain, rage, or defiant counterattack" to criticism, but the same experiences "can also lead to social withdrawal or an appearance of humility that may mask and protect the grandiosity". The same person may show both faces at different times. Figure 11.10 shows how. While admiration flows, the person feels special and superior. Sooner or later reality intrudes in the form of criticism, failure or simply being ignored, and that wound, often called a narcissistic injury, produces shame, fury or withdrawal. The pain then drives a new search for admiration to repair the self, and the cycle turns again. Aryan in the first forty minutes was the grandiose face; Aryan at the door was the vulnerable one.

Much of the research on narcissism in ordinary people uses a questionnaire, the Narcissistic Personality Inventory (NPI), first published by Robert Raskin and Calvin S. Hall in 1979 and best known in its later 40-item forced-choice form (Raskin and Terry, 1988), a measure of normal-range, mostly grandiose narcissism (tests belong to the Assessment book). Later factor analyses split it into leadership and authority, which tends to go with adaptive outcomes, and grandiose exhibitionism and entitlement and exploitativeness, which tend to go with maladaptive ones. That point matters when you read the next study: a high NPI score is not a diagnosis of narcissistic personality disorder.

Key study: is there a narcissism epidemic? (2008)

In The Culture of Narcissism (1979) the American historian Christopher Lasch (1932 to 1994) argued that modern Western society, with its stress on short-term pleasure, individualism, competition and success, was breeding narcissistic people. Thirty years later the psychologist Jean M. Twenge (born 1971) and her colleagues put the claim to a test. They gathered 85 samples of American college students, 16,475 in all, who had completed the NPI between 1979 and 2006, and plotted the mean score against the year of testing. Scores rose with time, by about a third of a standard deviation from 1982 onwards, so that almost two-thirds of recent students scored above the mean of 1979 to 1985. Twenge and W. Keith Campbell later popularised the finding as The Narcissism Epidemic (2009). In the same year as the first paper, Kali H. Trzesniewski, M. Brent Donnellan and Richard W. Robins analysed NPI data from 26,867 college students from the 1980s to 2007 and found no evidence of an increase, and no rise in high school students' self-enhancement from 1976 to 2006 in more than 400,000 students. The two groups have argued since about which samples can fairly be compared. The lesson for a clinician is narrower and safer: whatever is happening to average NPI scores, they measure a normal-range trait, and neither side showed that the disorder itself has become commoner.

One more variant needs a name. Erich Fromm (1900 to 1980) coined the phrase malignant narcissism in The Heart of Man (1964) for a solipsistic, destructive form of narcissism, and Otto Kernberg proposed it as a clinical syndrome in 1984: narcissistic personality disorder combined with antisocial features, paranoid traits and ego-syntonic aggression or sadism. It is not a DSM or ICD diagnosis. Kernberg saw it as lying between narcissistic and antisocial personality disorder, with a worse outlook than ordinary narcissistic personality disorder, and it is the clinical picture people usually have in mind when they speak of narcissistic tyrants.

Kohut and Kernberg: two readings of the narcissistic patient, and other causes

Why does a person develop this pattern? The most famous answers came from the two psychoanalysts whose names have already appeared, and their disagreement is one of the great debates of modern psychoanalysis. Book 2 teaches both theories in full: Kohut's self psychology, with its selfobjects, mirroring, idealising and twinship, in Book 2, Section 3.5.1, where Table 3.7 compares the two men's views of narcissism and of technique, and Kernberg's borderline personality organisation and transference-focused psychotherapy in Book 2, Section 3.4.7. Section 11.4 of this chapter uses the same two theorists to explain borderline personality disorder. Here the question is narrower: looking at a patient like Aryan, what does each man see?

Kohut sees a self whose normal development was arrested. Every child begins with an archaic grandiose self ("Look at me!") and a wish to merge with an idealised, all-powerful parent. Given enough empathic mirroring (the gleam in the mother's eye) and a parent calm and admirable enough to idealise, these archaic forms are gradually transformed into healthy ambition, ideals, self-esteem and empathy. When parents chronically fail to respond, the archaic grandiosity is never transformed; it persists, split off and unrealistic, and the self beneath it is enfeebled, prone to feeling empty, depleted and fragmented, and liable to narcissistic rage when slighted. On this reading Aryan's boasting is the cry of a starved self, and the right response is empathic understanding that lets the old needs reappear in the therapy and slowly mature. Kohut kept the narcissistic personality disorders apart from the borderline states, which he regarded as more severe disorders of the self.

Kernberg sees something quite different: not an arrested normal narcissism but a pathological structure. In his account the narcissistic person has built a pathological grandiose self, a fusion of three images that should have remained separate: the real self (the child's genuine specialness), the ideal self (fantasies of power, wealth and beauty that consoled the child) and the ideal object (the fantasy of an endlessly loving, admiring parent). The grandiose self defends against intense early rage and envy, and the person protects it by devaluing anyone who might be envied and by exploiting others. Kernberg described the typical parents as cold and indifferent, with covert aggression, yet prizing the child for some special quality, such as looks or a talent, which became the child's refuge from feeling unloved. For Kernberg most narcissistic patients rest on a borderline level of personality organisation, with primitive defences such as splitting and devaluation, but function better on the surface than borderline patients because the grandiose self holds them together. On this reading Aryan's contempt for his investors and his envy of his friend are the heart of the matter, and they must be confronted and interpreted, not soothed. Table 11.20 compares the two readings.

Table 11.20 How Kohut and Kernberg read the narcissistic patient (compare Book 2, Table 3.7, for their techniques).
QuestionKohut (self psychology)Kernberg (object relations)
What is pathological narcissism?Normal narcissism whose development was arrestedA pathological structure, different in kind from normal narcissism
The grandiosityThe archaic grandiose self, never transformed because never mirroredA pathological grandiose self: real self, ideal self and ideal object fused
What lies beneathDeficit: emptiness, depletion, fragmentationConflict: rage, envy and devaluation of others
The parentsChronically unempathic; failed to mirror or to be idealisableCold, covertly aggressive, yet prizing a special quality in the child
Relation to borderlineDistinct from, and milder than, the borderline statesUsually built on borderline personality organisation
Patient each fits bestThe vulnerable, shame-prone narcissistThe grandiose, envious, exploitative narcissist
What therapy must doEmpathic immersion; let the selfobject transferences unfoldConfront and interpret grandiosity, envy and negative transference

Notice the row on which patient each theory fits best. Many writers have pointed out that the two men may simply have been describing different patients: Kohut's analysands in Chicago were often high-functioning, depleted, shame-ridden people, much like the vulnerable face of Table 11.19, while Kernberg's patients, many of them seen in hospital settings, were often more aggressive and more disturbed. Both theories may be partly right about different people.

Both theories agree on one thing: the cause lies in a lack of parental warmth and empathy. That shared claim has now been tested directly, at least in children, and it did not do well.

Key study: where does narcissism in children come from? (2015)

Eddie Brummelman, Brad J. Bushman and their colleagues set two theories against each other. Psychoanalytic theory, as you have just seen, says narcissism grows from a lack of parental warmth. Social learning theory, the view associated with Theodore Millon, says children become narcissistic when parents overvalue them, teaching them that they are superior to other children and entitled to privileges. The researchers followed 565 children aged 7 to 12, the age at which individual differences in narcissism first appear, and their parents, over four waves six months apart, measuring the children's narcissism and self-esteem and the parents' overvaluation and warmth. Narcissism was predicted by parental overvaluation, not by a lack of warmth. Self-esteem showed the opposite pattern: it was predicted by parental warmth, not by overvaluation. The study separates two things people often confuse: a child who feels "I am as worthy as others" (self-esteem) and a child who feels "I am better than others" (narcissism). Its limits: it measured normal-range narcissism in children over 18 months, not the adult disorder, and correlational panel data cannot prove cause.

Genes contribute too. In the small Norwegian twin study of Torgersen and colleagues (2000), narcissistic personality disorder had the highest heritability estimate of all the disorders studied, about 0.79, but with only 221 twin pairs the estimate is uncertain, and the larger study by Kendler and colleagues (2008) found narcissistic traits loading on the same broad genetic factor as histrionic, borderline, paranoid, dependent and obsessive-compulsive traits. Culture probably matters as well, as DSM-5-TR's note on individualistic societies suggests, though as the narcissism epidemic debate shows, that is harder to prove than to assert.

Treatment research is limited in both the number of studies and the reports of success. People with narcissistic personality disorder seldom come for treatment of the disorder itself; they come, like Aryan, after a collapse, often depressed, and treatment usually begins with the depression. Cognitive therapists work on the grandiose fantasies, the hypersensitivity to evaluation and the lack of empathy, for example by shifting attention from imagined triumphs to attainable everyday satisfactions and by practising attention to other people's feelings. Psychodynamic therapists follow Kohut or Kernberg (Book 2, Sections 3.5.1 and 3.4.7), and transference-focused psychotherapy has been adapted for pathological narcissism. The Dutch schema therapy trial of Bamelis and colleagues (2014) included narcissistic patients and found that results did not differ by diagnosis (Section 11.7.7). No medicine treats the disorder; antidepressants are used for accompanying depression, and suicide risk after a narcissistic injury must be assessed carefully.

Clinical link

Your own reactions are diagnostic information. With a grandiose patient you may feel bored, invisible or quietly irritated, as if you were an audience paid to listen; with a vulnerable patient you may feel that every word is weighed for an insult. You may also be idealised for a few sessions ("you are the only one who has ever understood me") and then abruptly devalued when you disappoint. Expect it, do not take either role personally, and remember the man at the door: the patient who devalues you most loudly is often the one most terrified of being found ordinary.

Exam trap

Kohut: narcissism is a deficit (developmental arrest after failed mirroring and idealising); treat with empathy; school: self psychology. Kernberg: narcissism is a defensive structure (the pathological grandiose self against rage and envy), usually on a borderline organisation; treat by confrontation and interpretation; school: object relations. "Malignant narcissism" is Fromm's phrase (1964) made into a syndrome by Kernberg (1984); it is not a DSM diagnosis. Brummelman's study (2015) supports parental overvaluation, not lack of warmth, as a source of childhood narcissism.

Telling the Cluster B disorders apart

You have now met all four members of Cluster B: antisocial personality disorder (Section 11.5), borderline personality disorder (Sections 11.3 and 11.4), and the histrionic and narcissistic patterns of this section. DSM-5-TR groups them because people with these disorders often appear dramatic, emotional or erratic, and they also frequently occur together: a single patient may meet criteria for two or three of them. That overlap is also what makes them so easy to confuse. A clinician meets a person who seeks attention, manipulates others or has stormy relationships and must decide which pattern fits best, and dependent personality disorder from Cluster C often belongs on the shortlist too, because the dependent person also needs other people intensely.

The most reliable way through is to ask what each person wants from other people, because that is where the four disorders really differ. DSM-5-TR itself points the way when it describes the interactive styles of the histrionic, antisocial and borderline person as coquettish, callous and needy, and singles out grandiosity as the mark of the narcissist. Figure 11.11 turns that into a picture: for each pattern, what other people are for.

Read the figure from top to bottom. The histrionic person needs an audience; any attention will do, and she will happily appear fragile, ill or helpless if that keeps eyes on her. The narcissistic person needs a mirror; attention is wanted only if it admires, and appearing weak is intolerable. The borderline person needs a lifeline; the other person is first a rescuer, idealised, and then, at the first sign of distance, a betrayer, devalued, and the fear underneath is of being abandoned. The antisocial person needs marks; other people are means to profit, power or pleasure, and their rights and feelings do not count. And the dependent person, who belongs to Cluster C, needs caretakers; the other person is someone to make decisions and provide protection, and the price paid is submission. Table 11.21 fills in the details.

Table 11.21 The Cluster B disorders compared, with dependent personality disorder as the common distractor.
DisorderCore motiveView of selfRelationships and emotionTypical trap
Antisocial (3 of 7; age 18 or over)Profit, power or pleasure; others' rights do not countAbove the rules; tough and entitledExploitative and deceitful; irritable, aggressive; no remorseRequires conduct disorder before 15; "manipulation, lack of remorse, irresponsibility"
Borderline (5 of 9)Not to be abandonedUnstable; empty; "I am no one"Idealise then devalue; intense mood swings lasting hours; self-harmInstability of self-image and relationships is borderline, not narcissistic
Histrionic (5 of 8)To be the centre of attention, in any formExists in others' eyes; will play fragileSeductive, theatrical, shallow; acquaintances called intimate friendsAdmiration seeking with emotionality and seduction, not grandiosity
Narcissistic (5 of 9)Admiration for being superiorGrandiose on top, fragile beneathEntitled, exploitative, envious; little emotional empathyCore triad: grandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy
Dependent, Cluster C (5 of 8)To be taken care ofInadequate; helpless aloneSubmissive, clinging; cannot disagree; quickly finds a new caretakerFears separation but has a stable identity; docile, not flamboyant

Now push a case through the table, because this is how the distinction is made in practice. A client shows a persistent pattern of seeking admiration, but her behaviour is marked by excessive emotionality, attention seeking and seductive behaviour rather than grandiosity. Start with the motive column. She wants to be looked at and responded to, and she uses emotional display and seduction to get it: that is the histrionic motive. The word "admiration" in the stem is bait for narcissistic personality disorder, but the stem has deliberately removed grandiosity, the feature DSM-5-TR calls the most useful for diagnosing narcissism, and has added the coquettish style that belongs to histrionic personality. Borderline personality disorder would need instability of self-image and relationships, fear of abandonment, impulsivity or self-harm, none of which is mentioned. Dependent personality disorder would need submissiveness, clinging and a need for others to make decisions, and the dependent person is self-effacing, not seductive. The answer is histrionic personality disorder.

Two further cautions. First, these disorders are not mutually exclusive. DSM-5-TR says plainly that when a person meets the criteria for more than one personality disorder, all can be diagnosed, and in clinics histrionic, narcissistic and borderline features very often travel together. The comparisons in this section are for telling patterns apart, not for forcing every patient into a single box. Second, the clusters themselves are descriptive conveniences with weak empirical support, as Section 11.1 explains; Section 11.7.7 shows how the twin studies have pulled Cluster C apart, and Section 11.8 shows how ICD-11 has abandoned the named types altogether, keeping for this territory a "borderline pattern" specifier and trait domains such as dissociality, which captures much of the antisocial and narcissistic picture.

Exam trap

"Admiration seeking, but with excessive emotionality, attention seeking and seductive behaviour rather than grandiosity" is histrionic personality disorder, not narcissistic (no grandiosity), borderline (no instability, abandonment fears or self-harm) or dependent (no submissiveness or clinging). The Cluster B group, described as dramatic, emotional or erratic, is antisocial, borderline, histrionic and narcissistic; Cluster A is odd or eccentric and Cluster C anxious or fearful, and there is no Cluster D (Section 11.1). What borderline has that narcissistic does not is pervasive instability of relationships and self-image, with self-harm, emptiness and fear of abandonment (Section 11.3); grandiosity, need for admiration and the belief in being special are narcissistic.

11.7 Cluster C: Avoidant, Dependent and Obsessive-Compulsive Personality DisordersMedium yield · 2 Qs

The people of Cluster B fill a room; the people of Cluster C are more likely to be found at its edges, or not in it at all. DSM-5-TR groups avoidant, dependent and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder together because people with them often appear anxious or fearful. Each of the three is organised around a fear and a strategy for managing it. The avoidant person fears being found inadequate and rejected, and so stays away from people. The dependent person fears being left to fend for himself, and so clings to people and gives in to them. The obsessive-compulsive person fears error, disorder and the loss of control, and so tries to control everything: tasks, time, money, feelings and other people. Keep those three sentences in mind and most puzzles about Cluster C solve themselves.

Theodore Millon, whom you met in Section 11.6.3, gave this way of thinking its most systematic form. In his 1969 scheme of eight basic patterns, the avoidant person is actively detached, keeping away from others because contact has come to mean pain, whereas the schizoid person is passively detached, simply uninterested; the dependent person is passively dependent (Millon's "submissive" pattern), waiting to be cared for; and the compulsive person is passively ambivalent (his "conforming" pattern), torn between leaning on others and asserting himself against them, and resolving the conflict by rigid conformity to rules. The cluster has also changed shape over time. DSM-III (1980) and DSM-III-R (1987) included a fourth Cluster C disorder, passive-aggressive personality disorder, a pattern of passive resistance to demands; DSM-IV (1994) moved it to an appendix as "negativistic personality disorder", and it has not returned. As you will see in Section 11.7.7, the twin studies have since cast doubt on whether even the three remaining disorders belong together.

Avoidant personality disorder: wanting people and fearing them

Vaani is 26 and works as a medical transcriptionist from her room in Coimbatore. Last year she turned down a promotion to team leader because the job meant running a weekly video meeting; the thought of eight people watching her speak kept her awake for a week. She longs for friends. She joined an online book club eight months ago, reads every book, drafts long comments and deletes them unsent. When a colleague did not reply to her "good morning" message, she spent the evening concluding that the colleague found her pathetic, and she has not written to that colleague since. Her parents have arranged several meetings with prospective grooms; she has refused all but one, and at that one she barely spoke, because "he would have seen what I am really like". Asked to describe herself she says, without drama, "Boring. Plain. Nothing to say. People are kind to me because they feel sorry for me." Her mother remembers a child who hid behind her sari at every function and who cried on the first day of every school year. What Vaani wants most in the world is to belong somewhere. What she does, every day, is make sure nobody can reject her by never giving anybody the chance.

DSM-5-TR defines avoidant personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy and hypersensitivity to negative evaluation, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, shown by four or more of seven criteria. Notice that the definition has three parts, a behaviour (inhibition), a self-image (inadequacy) and a sensitivity (to negative evaluation), and the seven criteria map onto them in three groups.

Keeping out of harm's way. The person avoids occupational activities that involve significant interpersonal contact because of fears of criticism, disapproval or rejection (criterion 1): promotions are declined, as Vaani's was, because the new responsibilities might bring criticism. The person is unwilling to get involved with people unless certain of being liked (criterion 2); other people are assumed to be critical and disapproving until they pass stringent tests proving otherwise, and group activities are avoided. And the person is unusually reluctant to take personal risks or engage in new activities because they may prove embarrassing (criterion 7), exaggerating the dangers of ordinary situations, so that life becomes narrow and predictable.

Guarded intimacy. The person shows restraint within intimate relationships because of fear of being shamed or ridiculed (criterion 3). This criterion is important for the differential diagnosis, because it says that people with avoidant personality disorder can form intimate relationships when they are assured of uncritical acceptance, but even then they hold back, reluctant to talk about themselves or show their feelings for fear of exposure.

The inner world of expected rejection. The person is preoccupied with being criticised or rejected in social situations (criterion 4), with a markedly low threshold for detecting it: a slightly disapproving look can wound deeply, and a neutral gesture or a message left unanswered may be read as contempt. The person is inhibited in new interpersonal situations because of feelings of inadequacy (criterion 5), and views himself or herself as socially inept, personally unappealing or inferior to others (criterion 6). DSM-5-TR adds an observation that matters for working life: some people find strangers hardest, but many find repeated contact harder, because as colleagues get to know them the chance grows that their inferiority will be "revealed". Over weeks or months a conviction builds that the others see them as worthless, the distress becomes intolerable, and they resign; a history of repeated job changes may follow.

The associated features complete the picture. People with avoidant personality disorder vigilantly watch the faces and movements of those around them, are likely to misread social responses as critical, and so collect constant confirmation of their self-doubt. Others describe them as shy, timid, lonely and isolated. They usually lack a wide support network to help them through crises. Crucially, they desire affection and acceptance and may fantasise about idealised relationships. Research on attachment describes them as having insecure styles, variously called "preoccupied" or "fearful": a wish for closeness combined with a fear that others will not value them or will hurt them.

Avoidant personality disorder is one of the more common personality disorders: an estimated 5.2 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 2.4 percent in NESARC, and a median of 2.1 percent across six epidemiological studies. Community surveys find it slightly but consistently more common in women. It often begins in infancy or childhood as shyness, isolation and fear of strangers and new situations. Most shy children grow out of their shyness, but those who go on to develop the disorder become increasingly shy and avoidant during adolescence and early adulthood, exactly when new relationships matter most. There is some evidence that it becomes less evident or remits with age; the estimated prevalence in adults over 65 is 0.8 percent. Because shyness and avoidance can be developmentally normal, DSM-5-TR says the diagnosis should be used with great caution in children and adolescents.

Culture shapes the picture too. Cultural and ethnic groups differ in how much diffidence and avoidance they regard as appropriate, and avoidance may arise from the difficulties of acculturation after migration. DSM-5-TR also notes that in some sociocultural contexts marked avoidance follows social embarrassment, a "loss of face", or a failure to meet major life goals, rather than temperamental shyness; the avoidance may be a deliberate way of preserving social harmony. A young man who withdraws from his community after failing the examination his whole family was counting on is not, on that evidence, avoidant in the personality sense.

The diagnosis has a short history. The German psychiatrist Ernst Kretschmer (1888 to 1964) had suggested that the schizoid temperament comes in two forms: an anaesthetic form, cold and insensitive, and a hyperaesthetic form, timid, sensitive and easily hurt (Section 11.2 discusses Kretschmer and the schizoid concept). Millon built on this distinction, separating the people who are asocial because they are indifferent from those who are asocial because they are anxious and afraid of rejection, and his proposal entered DSM-III in 1980 as avoidant personality disorder, with the indifferent group left as schizoid personality disorder. Not everyone was convinced. In 1985 W. John Livesley and his colleagues argued, in a historical comment in the American Journal of Psychiatry, that the split cut a continuous distribution in two without good historical grounds and obscured important features of the schizoid personality. ICD-10 calls the disorder anxious (avoidant) personality disorder (F60.6), and its description adds two features that DSM leaves implicit: persistent and pervasive feelings of tension and apprehension, and restrictions in lifestyle because of a need for physical security.

Exam trap

Avoidant personality disorder is a Cluster C (anxious or fearful) disorder, not Cluster A, even though, like the schizoid and schizotypal person, the avoidant person ends up socially isolated. In a list of paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal and avoidant, the one that is not Cluster A is avoidant. Its definition has three parts: social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy and hypersensitivity to negative evaluation, four of seven criteria. ICD-10's name is anxious (avoidant) personality disorder, F60.6.

Avoidant, schizoid or socially anxious?

Vaani spends her evenings alone. So does a schizoid man who has lived contentedly by himself for twenty years, and so does a young woman with social anxiety disorder who dreads the college canteen. What separates them is not what they do but why they do it, and how they feel about it. Table 11.22 sets out the comparison.

Table 11.22 Three ways of ending up alone: avoidant personality disorder (PD), schizoid PD and social anxiety disorder.
FeatureAvoidant PD (Cluster C)Schizoid PD (Cluster A)Social anxiety disorder (Chapter 6)
Wants relationships?Yes, deeply; feels loneliness keenlyNo; content with or prefers solitudeYes
Why the avoidanceFear of rejection, humiliation, being found inadequateLack of interest in peopleFear of scrutiny and negative evaluation in social or performance situations
Reaction to criticismHypersensitive; deeply hurtIndifferent to praise and criticismFeared, anticipated, replayed afterwards
View of selfPervasively inept, unappealing, inferiorLittle concern with others' viewsNegative, but often less pervasive and less stable
Scope and onsetPervasive; shyness from early childhoodPervasive; emotionally cold, restricted affectCan be limited to performance situations; median onset about 13
In Millon's schemeActively detachedPassively detachedNot a personality pattern

The schizoid comparison is the easy one, and the one that matters most: the schizoid person does not want company, while the avoidant person longs for it but fears rejection. DSM-5-TR puts it plainly: people with avoidant personality disorder want relationships and feel their loneliness deeply, whereas people with schizoid or schizotypal personality disorder may be content with, and even prefer, their social isolation (Section 11.2 teaches the schizoid and schizotypal patterns). The two disorders can nonetheless be diagnosed together, and in the twin data described in Section 11.7.7 they share a genetic factor that the researchers interpreted as introversion.

The comparison with social anxiety disorder is genuinely hard, and nobody has settled it. Chapter 6, Sections 6.3.6 and 6.3.7 teach social anxiety disorder and its differential diagnosis from the anxiety side. DSM-5-TR describes a great deal of overlap between the two. They may be different expressions of the same underlying problem, or avoidant personality disorder may be a more severe form of social anxiety disorder, on a continuum. Against that, differences have been described, especially in self-concept: in avoidant personality disorder the sense of inferiority is pervasive and entrenched, whereas the negative self-concept in social anxiety disorder may be less stable. Avoidant personality disorder also frequently occurs without social anxiety disorder, and some separate risk factors have been identified, which is why the manual keeps two categories. When the two occur together, which is often, both are diagnosed: social anxiety disorder is more often comorbid with avoidant personality disorder than with any other personality disorder, and avoidant personality disorder is more often comorbid with social anxiety disorder than with any other anxiety disorder.

Two more neighbours need a line each. Both avoidant and paranoid personality disorder involve a reluctance to confide in others, but the avoidant person fears humiliation or being found inadequate, whereas the paranoid person fears that the information will be used maliciously. Both avoidant personality disorder and agoraphobia involve avoidance, and they often occur together, but the motive differs: the agoraphobic person avoids places for fear of panic or physical harm with no escape (Chapter 6, Section 6.2.6). Avoidant personality disorder is also commonly accompanied by depressive disorders, and it carries an increased rate of substance use disorders, similar to the rate seen in the generalised form of social anxiety disorder.

What causes it? Three strands of evidence point in the same direction. The first is temperament. Jerome Kagan's behaviourally inhibited toddlers, who freeze and withdraw from unfamiliar people and situations, are at higher risk of later social anxiety (Chapter 6, Section 6.7.2), and childhood shyness is, as DSM-5-TR says, a common precursor of avoidant personality disorder. The second is early experience. Millon suggested that a child born with a difficult or timid temperament may be rejected, or at least not given enough uncritical affection, by parents, and that the rejection breeds low self-esteem and social alienation that persist into adulthood. In support, adults with the disorder questioned by Stravynski, Elie and Franche (1989) remembered their parents as more rejecting, more guilt-engendering and less affectionate than controls did, and Meyer and Carver (2000) found more reported childhood isolation, rejection and conflict. The obvious caution is that these are adults' memories, and an adult who sees rejection everywhere may also remember it everywhere. The third strand is genetic: avoidant personality disorder is moderately heritable, and, interestingly, it occurs more often in the relatives of people with schizophrenia, a reminder of its old link with the schizoid concept.

Exam trap

Schizoid: does not want relationships, indifferent to praise or criticism (Cluster A). Avoidant: wants relationships but fears rejection, hypersensitive to criticism (Cluster C). Social anxiety disorder: fear of scrutiny in social or performance situations, an anxiety disorder (Chapter 6), which may be the same condition as avoidant personality disorder at a different point on a continuum and often coexists with it. Paranoid: will not confide because others are malicious; avoidant: will not confide because others will see how inadequate I am.

Dependent personality disorder: the need to be taken care of

A 29-year-old man is brought to a clinic in Lucknow by his wife after what turns out to be a panic attack: his parents had gone to a cousin's wedding in another city and, for the first time in his life, he had spent a night alone in the house. He works as an accounts assistant in his uncle's firm, a job his father found for him. He phones his mother several times a day, at work as well as at home, to ask what he should eat for lunch, whether to accept a client's invitation, whether the shirt he bought was a mistake. He has been offered a transfer with a large rise in pay and has been unable to decide for three months; he is waiting for his father to tell him. His wife says he has never once disagreed with his mother, even when he privately thought she was wrong, and that he volunteers to do all his cousins' tax paperwork, unpaid, at weekends, because "they will be upset otherwise". When his first engagement was broken off, he asked his parents within a fortnight to find him someone else, "anyone you think is suitable". He is gentle, polite and apologetic in the interview, and when asked what frightens him most he answers at once: "That one day my parents will not be there, and I will not know how to manage anything."

Look at that vignette with Indian eyes before you diagnose anything. Many young Indian adults live with their parents, consult them about major decisions and defer to them in public, and arranged marriage is an ordinary institution, not a symptom. The question is not whether this man depends on his family; almost everyone does. It is whether his dependence goes far beyond what his own family and community expect of a man of his age, whether it is pervasive, and whether it costs him. He cannot spend one night alone, cannot choose lunch, cannot take a promotion and fears his own competence. That is beyond any cultural norm.

DSM-5-TR defines dependent personality disorder as a pervasive and excessive need to be taken care of that leads to submissive and clinging behaviour and fears of separation, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, shown by five or more of eight criteria. The manual adds an explanation of the whole pattern that is worth memorising: the dependent and submissive behaviours are designed to elicit caregiving, and they arise from a self-perception of being unable to function adequately without the help of others. The eight criteria fall into three groups.

Handing over decisions and responsibility. The person has difficulty making everyday decisions without an excessive amount of advice and reassurance from others (criterion 1): which shirt to wear, whether to carry an umbrella. The person needs others to assume responsibility for most major areas of life (criterion 2), such as where to live, what job to take and whom to befriend, and this goes beyond age-appropriate and situation-appropriate requests for help. And the person has difficulty initiating projects or doing things on his or her own (criterion 4), because of a lack of self-confidence in judgement or abilities rather than a lack of motivation or energy. That last clause is there to rule out depression, in which a person also fails to start things, but because energy and interest are gone. People with dependent personality disorder may even fear becoming more competent, because competence might cost them the support they rely on, and because others always handle their problems they never learn the skills of independent living, so the dependency perpetuates itself.

Keeping the supporter at any cost. The person has difficulty expressing disagreement because of fear of losing support or approval (criterion 3); such people will agree with things they believe are wrong rather than risk losing help, and they do not express anger towards those they depend on. The criterion carries a note: do not include realistic fears of retribution. A woman who does not argue with a violent husband is being prudent, not dependent. The person also goes to excessive lengths to obtain nurturance and support, to the point of volunteering for unpleasant tasks (criterion 5), submitting to unreasonable demands, making extraordinary sacrifices, or tolerating verbal, physical or sexual abuse. Here too DSM-5-TR adds a caution: tolerating abuse should be counted as evidence of the disorder only when it is clear that other options are available to the person. In a society where a woman may have no income, no legal help and nowhere to go, staying is not a personality trait.

The terror of being alone. The person feels uncomfortable or helpless when alone because of exaggerated fears of being unable to care for himself or herself (criterion 6). When a close relationship ends, the person urgently seeks another as a source of care and support (criterion 7), attaching quickly and indiscriminately to someone new. And the person is unrealistically preoccupied with fears of being left to take care of himself or herself (criterion 8). The word "unrealistically" matters: DSM-5-TR's own example is an elderly man with cancer who moves into his son's household for care, whose dependence is appropriate to his circumstances.

People with dependent personality disorder are often pessimistic and full of self-doubt, belittle their own abilities, take criticism as proof of their worthlessness, seek overprotection and dominance from others, and avoid positions of responsibility. In treatment they can look like ideal patients, attentive, compliant and grateful, which is itself the problem, as Section 11.7.7 explains. The disorder is the least common in the cluster: an estimated 0.6 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 0.5 percent in NESARC, and a median of 0.4 percent across six epidemiological studies. It has been diagnosed more often in women than in men in both clinical and community settings, a difference that raises the same questions about gender socialisation and diagnostic expectation as histrionic personality disorder did (Section 11.6.3). As with avoidant personality disorder, DSM-5-TR says the diagnosis should be used with great caution, if at all, in children and adolescents, for whom dependent behaviour may be developmentally appropriate. It brings an increased risk of depressive, anxiety and adjustment disorders, and it often occurs with borderline, avoidant and histrionic personality disorders.

The idea has a psychoanalytic ancestry. Classical theory traced dependency to the oral stage of psychosexual development: frustration or over-gratification in infancy was said to produce an oral fixation and an oral character, passive, needy and hungry for nurturance, a type described by Karl Abraham (1877 to 1925). DSM-I (1952) listed a passive-dependent type under passive-aggressive personality, and dependent personality disorder as a category in its own right arrived with DSM-III in 1980, the submissive pattern of Millon's scheme. ICD-10 lists dependent personality disorder under F60.7 and includes under it the older names asthenic, inadequate, passive and self-defeating personality.

The modern evidence on causes has been gathered above all by the American psychologist Robert F. Bornstein. In an influential review in 1992 he concluded that dependency appears to grow from overprotective and authoritarian parenting: a parent who does everything for the child and a parent who demands obedience both teach the child that he cannot cope alone and that the way to be safe is to please the powerful. Dependency, Bornstein found, goes with suggestibility, conformity, compliance, interpersonal yielding and heightened sensitivity to other people's cues, and he proposed that the core motive behind all the varied dependent behaviours is a strong wish to obtain and keep nurturing, supportive relationships. Earlier writers had emphasised the loss of a parent, neglect or rejection in childhood. Genes also play a part: the small Norwegian twin study of Torgersen and colleagues put the heritability of dependent personality disorder at about 0.57, and the larger studies described in Section 11.7.7 give lower but still substantial figures. DSM-5-TR adds that chronic physical illness or a persistent separation anxiety disorder in childhood or adolescence may predispose a person to the disorder, which makes sense: a child who has been ill for years, or terrified of every separation, has had both reason and opportunity to learn that safety lies in being looked after.

Dependence, abandonment and culture

Fear of losing the people one depends on appears in several disorders, and the clearest way to separate them is to ask what the person does when the fear comes true, or seems about to. DSM-5-TR gives the decisive contrast with borderline personality disorder. Both fear abandonment, but the borderline person reacts to abandonment with feelings of emotional emptiness, rage and demands, whereas the dependent person reacts with increasing appeasement and submissiveness and urgently seeks a replacement relationship to provide care and support. The borderline person also has a typical pattern of unstable and intense relationships and an unstable identity; the dependent person's relationships are stable, if lopsided, and his sense of who he is, however diminished, is not in turmoil. Section 11.3 teaches the borderline side of this comparison.

The dependent and the histrionic person both have a strong need for reassurance and approval and may seem childlike and clinging. But the dependent person is self-effacing and docile, while the histrionic person is gregarious and flamboyant, with active demands for attention, and has less insight into his or her own dependency needs. The dependent and the avoidant person share feelings of inadequacy, hypersensitivity to criticism and a need for reassurance, and the two disorders often occur together, but their behaviour runs in opposite directions: the avoidant person withdraws until certain of acceptance, while the dependent person seeks out and holds on to important others. Even similar behaviour has different motives. Both may be unassertive, but the avoidant person's unassertiveness comes from fear of rejection or humiliation, the dependent person's from a wish not to be left to fend for himself.

Two non-personality conditions also need to be ruled out. Adults with separation anxiety disorder are typically over-concerned about the safety of their children, spouses, parents or even pets, and are distressed when separated from them; the dependent person is distressed when alone because of exaggerated fears of being unable to look after himself (Chapter 6, Section 6.4.7 teaches separation anxiety disorder). And dependency can be a consequence of another mental disorder, such as depression, panic disorder or agoraphobia, or of a medical condition, in which case it began with the illness and should lift with it.

Indian context

No personality disorder is more tangled with culture than this one. DSM-5-TR warns that the degree to which dependent behaviour is considered appropriate varies substantially across age and sociocultural groups, that an emphasis on passivity, politeness and deference is characteristic of some societies and may be mistaken for the disorder, and that societies may foster dependence differently in men and women. The Indian psychiatrist Jaswant Singh Neki (1925 to 2015), professor of psychiatry at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences and later director of the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, made a related argument decades earlier. In a 1973 paper on the guru-chela relationship he proposed that the traditional bond of teacher and disciple, in which the disciple's dependence is accepted and gradually transformed rather than treated as immaturity, could serve as a model for therapy in India, and in 1976 he examined the cultural relativity of dependence in social and therapeutic relationships. The American psychoanalyst Alan Roland, comparing Indian, Japanese and American patients in In Search of Self in India and Japan (1988), described an Indian familial self, experienced as part of a network of family relationships, in contrast to the Western individualised self. None of this means that dependent personality disorder does not occur in India. It means that the threshold must be judged against the person's own reference group: DSM-5-TR describes the disorder as a pervasive inability to make decisions, continuous feelings of subjugation, lack of initiative, silence and social distancing far in excess of the usual cultural norms of politeness and purposeful passivity.

Exam trap

"Submissiveness, fear of separation and reassurance seeking" is dependent personality disorder: a pervasive need to be taken care of, five of eight criteria, Cluster C. Dependent and borderline both fear abandonment: the dependent person responds with appeasement and a quick replacement relationship and has a stable identity; the borderline person responds with rage, emptiness and demands, and has identity disturbance. Dependent and avoidant both feel inadequate: the dependent person clings, the avoidant person withdraws. Do not count realistic fears of retribution, or tolerance of abuse when there is no alternative, as dependent traits.

Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder: the need for control

Keshav is 47, a senior section engineer with the railways in Bhubaneswar, and he has come because his cardiologist sent him: his blood pressure will not come down and he sleeps four hours a night. He arrives exactly on time with a typed list of his symptoms, numbered and dated. At work he is known for two things: nothing that leaves his desk is ever wrong, and nothing leaves his desk on time. His inspection reports are rewritten four or five times until every figure has been checked against the register by hand, and his team's monthly returns are always the last in the division. When his list of pending tasks went missing last week, he spent most of an afternoon searching for it instead of writing it out again from memory. He signs everything himself because his deputy "does things his own way", and his juniors have learned to wait. He has not taken his earned leave in four years, and on the one family trip he did take he spent the mornings correcting files in the hotel room. At home his wife and son live by his timetable. The storeroom is full of old newspapers, broken calculators and twenty years of railway circulars that he will not let anyone throw away because "you never know". He keeps a ledger of household expenses to the rupee, lives far more frugally than his salary requires, and refused to let his son buy a new cricket bat because the old one "can be repaired". When a junior asked for a small advance to pay a hospital bill, he refused on principle: "Rules are rules. If I make one exception, there will be fifty." His wife puts it simply: "He is not a cruel man. He is just never wrong, and he cannot stop." Asked whether any of this troubles him, Keshav seems surprised. "If everybody did their work properly," he says, "I would have no stress at all."

DSM-5-TR defines obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD) as a pervasive pattern of preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and mental and interpersonal control, at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, shown by four or more of eight criteria. Read the definition twice, because each half matters. The first half says what the person is preoccupied with: order, perfection and control, of the mind as well as of other people. The second half says what is sacrificed for them: flexibility, openness and, ironically, efficiency. The eight criteria can be grouped into four.

Order that defeats its own purpose. The person is preoccupied with details, rules, lists, order, organisation or schedules to the extent that the major point of the activity is lost (criterion 1), and shows perfectionism that interferes with task completion, for example being unable to finish a project because his own overly strict standards are not met (criterion 2). These are Keshav's late returns and his lost list. DSM-5-TR describes how time is poorly allocated, the most important tasks are left to the last moment, deadlines are missed or met only by working through the night, and other areas of life fall into disarray.

A life given over to work and duty. The person is excessively devoted to work and productivity, to the exclusion of leisure and friendships, and this is not accounted for by obvious economic necessity (criterion 3); a daily-wage labourer who works every day to feed his family does not meet the criterion.
