Holidays are postponed until they never happen, leisure is approached as a serious task to be mastered, and play becomes work, as when a parent corrects a toddler for riding a tricycle in a crooked line. The person is also over-conscientious, scrupulous and inflexible about matters of morality, ethics or values, and this is not accounted for by cultural or religious identification (criterion 4); he is mercilessly self-critical about his own mistakes, harshly judgemental of other people's, rigidly deferential to rules and authority, and allows no exceptions for extenuating circumstances, as in Keshav's refusal of the advance.

Control of other people. The person is reluctant to delegate tasks or to work with others unless they submit to exactly his or her way of doing things (criterion 6), gives detailed instructions on the one correct way to load a dishwasher or file a report, micromanages, and rejects help even when behind schedule because nobody else can do it properly. And the person shows rigidity and stubbornness (criterion 8): plans are made in meticulous detail and not changed, other people's viewpoints are hard to acknowledge, and compromise is refused even when it would be in his own interest, because it is "the principle of the thing".

Holding on to things and money. The person is unable to discard worn-out or worthless objects even when they have no sentimental value (criterion 5), because "you never know when you might need it", and adopts a miserly spending style towards both self and others, viewing money as something to be hoarded for future catastrophes (criterion 7), and living well below what he can afford.

Beyond the criteria, DSM-5-TR describes how decisions become slow and painful whenever rules do not supply the answer; how the person becomes upset or angry when unable to control the physical or interpersonal environment, though the anger is usually not expressed directly (he will not complain about poor service in a restaurant but will brood about how small a tip to leave), or erupts as righteous indignation over a minor matter; how attentive he is to dominance and submission, deferring excessively to authorities he respects and resisting those he does not; and how hard he finds it to share emotion, expressing affection in a stiff, controlled way, rarely paying compliments, and feeling uncomfortable around emotionally expressive people. OCPD is common: an estimated 2.4 percent in the National Comorbidity Survey Replication subsample, 7.9 percent in NESARC, and a median of 4.7 percent across five epidemiological studies, which makes it one of the commonest personality disorders in community surveys. Unlike most of the others, it appears to be equally common in men and women in large population studies.

Two of the exclusion clauses deserve an Indian illustration. DSM-5-TR warns that some cultural communities place great emphasis on work and productivity, and that members of certain religious groups, professions and migrant communities may rigidly embrace codes of conduct, work demands and standards of moral scrupulosity that their group reinforces; such behaviour should not on its own be counted. A household that follows strict rules of ritual purity in the kitchen, an orthodox priest who keeps every rule of his tradition exactly, or an audit officer whose job is to find every error in a ledger is not showing OCPD on that evidence. The question, as always, is whether the pattern goes beyond what the person's own group expects, runs across every area of life, and causes impairment or distress. DSM-5-TR adds a fair point in OCPD's favour: in moderation, obsessive-compulsive traits can be highly adaptive, especially where high performance is rewarded. Many good surgeons, accountants and editors have a touch of them.

The pattern was described long before it was given its present name. In a short paper of 1908, "Character and anal erotism", Sigmund Freud described people who are notably orderly, parsimonious and obstinate, a trio he linked to the anal character. Orderly covers cleanliness, conscientiousness and reliability; parsimonious, which can shade into avarice, covers thrift with money and possessions; obstinate covers defiance and stubbornness. Map Freud's three words onto the DSM-5-TR criteria and they fit remarkably well: orderliness to the preoccupation with details and perfectionism (criteria 1 and 2), parsimony to the hoarding of money and objects (criteria 5 and 7), and obstinacy to rigidity and the refusal to delegate (criteria 6 and 8). Freud traced the trio to the anal stage of psychosexual development, the second and third years of life, when battles over toilet training make control, cleanliness and holding on the central issues, and to defences such as reaction formation (Chapter 6, Section 6.8.10 explains these defences in the context of obsessional neurosis). Karl Abraham extended the description of the anal character in a paper of 1921. The observation has lasted; the theory of its origin has found little support, since there is little good evidence that people with these traits had distinctive toilet training.

The manuals changed the name several times. DSM-I (1952) called it compulsive personality; DSM-II (1968) called it obsessive compulsive personality, with "anankastic personality", the equivalent term in the WHO's ICD-8, in double brackets beside it, and warned that it must be distinguished from obsessive compulsive neurosis; DSM-III (1980) went back to compulsive personality disorder; and DSM-III-R (1987) adopted the present name, obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. ICD-10 uses the term anankastic personality disorder (F60.5), from the Greek anankē, necessity or compulsion, and includes under it "compulsive" and "obsessional" personality. ICD-11 has no OCPD category, but it keeps the idea alive as one of its five trait domain qualifiers, anankastia, a narrow focus on one's own rigid standard of perfection and of right and wrong, and on controlling one's own and other people's behaviour (Section 11.8).

Now to the question at the heart of OCPD: what motivates it? The DSM-5-TR text answers directly. People with the disorder, it says, attempt to maintain a sense of control through painstaking attention to rules, trivial details, procedures, lists, schedules or form. Control is the thread through all eight criteria. The lists and rules control the task; the perfectionism controls the risk of error; the devotion to work controls time; the moral rigidity controls conscience; the refusal to delegate and the stubbornness control other people; the hoarding of objects and money controls the future. Even the emotional stiffness is control, of one's own feelings. Keshav's "If I make one exception, there will be fifty" is a man defending his control of the world against the chaos he believes lies beyond it.

Exam trap

People with OCPD are primarily motivated by a need for control (RML 2022). Look at why the other options are wrong. Efficiency is what OCPD sacrifices: the definition reads "at the expense of flexibility, openness, and efficiency". Achievement is tempting because of the devotion to work, but perfectionism stops tasks being finished and often lowers achievement; the person who works for achievement and admiration is closer to the narcissistic pattern. Autonomy is tempting for another reason: Erik Erikson's second psychosocial stage, autonomy versus shame and doubt, corresponds to Freud's anal stage. But the person with OCPD is not seeking freedom; he is bound by rules and binds others with them. Also remember Freud's trio for the anal character: orderly, parsimonious, obstinate (1908).

The differential diagnosis within the personality disorders follows from the motive. Both narcissistic and obsessive-compulsive people may profess perfectionism and believe that others cannot do things as well, but the narcissistic person is more likely to believe he has achieved perfection, whereas the obsessive-compulsive person is usually self-critical (Section 11.6.5). Narcissistic and antisocial people may lack generosity but will indulge themselves, whereas the obsessive-compulsive person is miserly towards self and others alike. Schizoid and obsessive-compulsive people may both seem formal and socially detached, but in OCPD this comes from discomfort with emotion and devotion to work, while in schizoid personality disorder there is a fundamental lack of capacity for intimacy. Hoarding disorder should be considered when the saving is extreme, with stacks of worthless objects creating a fire hazard or blocking the house, and both diagnoses are recorded when both are met (Chapter 6, Section 6.6.4). Many features of OCPD overlap with the "type A" pattern of preoccupation with work, competitiveness and time urgency (Book 2, Section 9.3.3 discusses type A behaviour and heart disease), and Keshav's blood pressure is not a coincidence. OCPD is also more likely in people with anxiety disorders and obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), and it may be associated with depressive and bipolar disorders and with eating disorders, especially the perfectionism of anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8). The comparison that matters most, with obsessive-compulsive disorder itself, needs a subsection of its own.

OCPD and OCD: one word, two different problems

Chapter 6, Section 6.5.9 compared obsessive-compulsive disorder with OCPD from the OCD side, with Table 6.16; this subsection recaps that comparison from the personality side and adds what the personality chapter needs. The core point is DSM-5-TR's: despite the similar names, the two are usually easily distinguished, because OCD involves true obsessions and compulsions and OCPD does not. OCPD is not characterised by intrusive thoughts, images or urges, or by repetitive behaviours performed in response to them; it is an enduring, pervasive, maladaptive pattern of excessive perfectionism and rigid control.

The deeper difference is in how the person relates to the problem, and Figure 11.12 shows it. In OCD the obsession arrives from outside the self, as it feels to the person: an intrusive, unwanted thought ("I may have left the gas on"; "my hands are contaminated") that is experienced as senseless or alien and is resisted, and the compulsion is performed to neutralise the anxiety the obsession brings, giving brief relief before the cycle starts again. The symptoms are ego-dystonic, at odds with the person's view of himself. In OCPD there is no intruder. The rules, the perfectionism and the need for control are part of the self, and they feel right, rational and even admirable; they are ego-syntonic (Section 11.1 defines both terms). The person with OCD is at war with his symptoms. The person with OCPD is at peace with his traits, and it is his family and colleagues who are at war with him.

Several practical consequences follow, and Table 11.23 gathers them. Because OCPD feels right, insight is usually limited: the person sees his traits as virtues and other people's objections as carelessness. He rarely seeks help for the traits themselves; like Keshav, he comes for the stress, the blood pressure, the depression or the marriage. Because OCD feels wrong, most people with it recognise that their beliefs are excessive, although DSM-5-TR's insight specifier allows for good or fair, poor, and absent insight with delusional beliefs (Chapter 6, Section 6.5.5). The repetitive behaviour differs too: in OCD it is a ritual that neutralises an obsession; in OCPD it is thoroughness, rule following and rechecking for errors, driven by the wish to get things right rather than by an intrusive fear. And the course differs: OCD tends to wax and wane, whereas OCPD is a stable trait pattern present since early adulthood. One historical wrinkle is worth knowing: ICD-10's description of anankastic personality disorder did include the intrusion of insistent and unwelcome thoughts or impulses, but not of the severity of OCD, which shows how blurred the boundary once was.

Table 11.23 OCPD and OCD from the personality side (compare Chapter 6, Table 6.16).
FeatureOCPDOCD
Typical words"This is the right way; others are careless""I know it is senseless, but I cannot stop"
Relation to the selfEgo-syntonic: felt as right and rationalEgo-dystonic: intrusive, unwanted, resisted
InsightUsually limited; traits seen as virtuesUsually good or fair; poor or absent in some
Obsessions and compulsionsNone in the true senseDefining features
What drives the repetitionGetting things right; controlNeutralising the anxiety of an obsession
Who seeks help, and whyOften sent by family or doctor, for stress or depressionUsually the person, for the symptoms
CourseStable trait pattern from early adulthoodWaxing and waning
ClassificationPersonality disorder, Cluster C; ICD-10 anankastic F60.5Obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (Chapter 6)
Main treatmentsPsychotherapy; drug evidence thinExposure and response prevention; serotonergic antidepressants (SSRIs)
Together?Can coexist: both are then recorded23 to 32 percent of one treated OCD sample had OCPD

The two can occur together. When the criteria for both are met, DSM-5-TR says both diagnoses should be recorded, and in one American study of 214 treatment-seeking adults with OCD, comorbid OCPD was found in 23 to 32 percent of them over the course of follow-up. But DSM-5-TR also notes that the majority of people with OCD do not have a pattern that meets criteria for OCPD, and Barlow and Durand describe OCPD as only distantly related to OCD. The shared name misleads more than it informs.

Exam trap

Compared with OCD, OCPD is ego-syntonic with limited insight. Reject the distractors one by one. "Ego-dystonic and accompanied by significant insight" describes OCD (with good or fair insight), the reverse of OCPD. "Only present during manic episodes" is wrong for any personality disorder, which is an enduring pattern, not an episode; a manic person's expansive overactivity is not OCPD. "Acute in onset and rapidly remitting" contradicts the definition of every personality disorder: stable, long-standing and beginning by adolescence or early adulthood. And do not let the shared name fool you: OCPD has no true obsessions or compulsions, and when both disorders are present, both are diagnosed.

Cluster C compared: does it hold together, and what helps?

Table 11.24 brings the three anxious or fearful disorders together, with the fear at the centre of each, the strategy each uses against it and what that strategy costs, the ICD-10 name, and the look-alike it is most often confused with.

Table 11.24 The Cluster C personality disorders (PD) compared.
Disorder (criteria)Core fearStrategy and its costICD-10 nameMain look-alike
Avoidant (4 of 7)Rejection, humiliation, being found inadequateWithdraws until sure of acceptance; loneliness and a narrowed lifeAnxious (avoidant), F60.6Schizoid PD (no wish for people); social anxiety disorder
Dependent (5 of 8)Being left to care for oneselfClings and submits; loses initiative, may tolerate mistreatmentDependent, F60.7Borderline PD (rage, not appeasement); histrionic PD
Obsessive-compulsive (4 of 8)Error, disorder, losing controlControls tasks, people, money; loses flexibility and efficiencyAnankastic, F60.5OCD (true obsessions, ego-dystonic); narcissistic perfectionism

Read across the rows and the family resemblance is clear: in each disorder anxiety drives a strategy that brings short-term relief and long-term cost. But is the resemblance more than skin deep? The best test comes from the Norwegian twin studies. In 2007 Ted Reichborn-Kjennerud and his colleagues, Kendler and Torgersen among them, interviewed 1,386 young adult twin pairs from the Norwegian Institute of Public Health Twin Panel and measured the three Cluster C disorders as dimensions, by counting criteria. The heritability of each was modest, between 27 and 35 percent, and for dependent and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder much lower than the small earlier study had suggested; there was no effect of shared family environment. A common genetic factor accounted for 83 percent of the genetic variance in avoidant personality disorder and 48 percent in dependent personality disorder, but only 15 percent in OCPD. OCPD, the authors concluded, is largely distinct in its causes from the other two, and the DSM-IV Cluster C construct is not supported in its present form. A year later Kendler's broader analysis of all ten disorders found that genetic risk does not follow the A, B and C clusters at all, although environmental risk largely does, with one telling exception: the environmental factors behind OCPD grouped with those of Cluster B. Avoidant personality disorder, meanwhile, shared a genetic factor with schizoid personality disorder that looked like introversion. So the "anxious or fearful" label describes how these people appear, not a shared cause, which is one of the arguments Section 11.8 uses for abandoning categories altogether. In DSM-5's Alternative Model for Personality Disorders (AMPD), avoidant and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder survive among the six retained types while dependent personality disorder does not, and in ICD-11 only OCPD has a direct descendant, the anankastia trait domain.

Treatment for Cluster C is better studied than for most personality disorders, and the news is reasonably good. The psychological treatments themselves belong to Book 2: cognitive and cognitive behavioural therapy in Book 2, Chapter 5 (with the Clark and Wells model of social anxiety in Book 2, Section 5.6.4), and schema therapy in Book 2, Section 5.7. What matters here is what the trials show.

Key study: dynamic versus cognitive therapy for Cluster C (Svartberg, Stiles and Seltzer, 2004)

Martin Svartberg and his colleagues in Norway randomly assigned 50 outpatients who had one or more Cluster C personality disorders, and no other personality disorder, to 40 weekly sessions of either short-term dynamic psychotherapy or cognitive therapy. Most also had anxiety or depressive disorders. The therapists were experienced full-time clinicians following manuals under supervision, and symptoms, interpersonal problems and core personality pathology were measured repeatedly during treatment and over two years afterwards. Both groups improved significantly on every measure, and kept improving after treatment ended. There were no significant differences between the two therapies at any point. Two years after treatment, 54 percent of the dynamic therapy patients and 42 percent of the cognitive therapy patients had recovered symptomatically, and about 40 percent in both groups had recovered in interpersonal problems and personality functioning. The authors concluded that both therapies have a place, and that factors other than the type of therapy may better predict who does well. The limitation is size: with 50 patients, a modest real difference between the therapies could have been missed.

For avoidant personality disorder specifically, a Dutch trial by Paul M. G. Emmelkamp and colleagues (2006) randomly assigned 62 patients to 20 weekly sessions of brief dynamic therapy, 20 weekly sessions of cognitive behavioural therapy, or a waiting list. Cognitive behavioural therapy did better than both the waiting list and brief dynamic therapy on several measures, and the gains were maintained at follow-up; brief dynamic therapy did no better than waiting. Because avoidant personality disorder and social anxiety disorder overlap so heavily, the same exposure-based and cognitive methods are used for both, and the strength of the therapeutic alliance appears to be an important predictor of success. The largest trial was of schema therapy. Lotte L. M. Bamelis, Arnoud Arntz and their colleagues (2014) treated 323 patients with Cluster C, paranoid, histrionic or narcissistic personality disorder in twelve Dutch mental health institutes, assigning them to 50 sessions of schema therapy, to treatment as usual, or to clarification-oriented psychotherapy. Three years after treatment began, judged by interviewers who did not know which treatment each patient had received, significantly more of the schema therapy patients had recovered from their personality disorder than in either other group. They also dropped out less often than patients given treatment as usual, and at follow-up had less depression and better general and social functioning. Results did not differ by diagnosis.

Drugs play a smaller part. No medicine is approved for any personality disorder as such. When avoidant personality disorder occurs with social anxiety disorder, clinicians use the medicines that work for social anxiety, chiefly the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and serotonin and noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) (Chapter 6, Section 6.9.4), but trials in avoidant personality disorder itself are few and have not established a drug treatment for it. For dependent personality disorder and OCPD, medication is used, if at all, for an accompanying depression or anxiety disorder; the evidence for any drug acting on OCPD itself is thin, which is one more practical reason not to confuse it with OCD, where SSRIs are a first-line treatment.

Clinical link

Each Cluster C pattern recreates itself in the therapy room. The dependent client is the "ideal patient": punctual, grateful, eager to be told what to do, and that very eagerness defeats the goal of greater independence. The therapist must resist becoming the new caretaker and hand decisions back to the client a little at a time. The avoidant client watches the therapist's face for disapproval and may silently drop out after one session that felt critical, so the alliance must be built carefully and checked openly ("Was there anything today that felt uncomfortable?"). The obsessive-compulsive client may argue over the details of the treatment plan, intellectualise rather than feel, and do homework so thoroughly that it is never finished; the work is often to practise "good enough".

Exam trap

Cluster C is anxious or fearful: avoidant (4 of 7), dependent (5 of 8) and obsessive-compulsive (4 of 8). It once had a fourth member, passive-aggressive personality disorder (DSM-III and DSM-III-R), moved to an appendix as "negativistic" in DSM-IV. The ICD-10 names are anxious (avoidant) F60.6, dependent F60.7 and anankastic F60.5. Twin studies show OCPD to be largely distinct in its causes from avoidant and dependent PD, so the cluster is descriptive, not causal. In the DSM-5 Alternative Model, avoidant and obsessive-compulsive PD are retained; dependent PD is not.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Antisocial, borderline, histrionic and narcissistic personality disorders form Cluster , described as dramatic, emotional or erratic.
  2. A style of speech that is excessively impressionistic and lacking in detail is a criterion of personality disorder.
  3. The DSM-5 core triad of narcissistic personality disorder is grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of .
  4. People with narcissistic personality disorder often have some cognitive empathy but lack empathy.
  5. Gabbard (1989) described two subtypes of narcissistic personality disorder, the oblivious and the .
  6. In ICD-10, narcissistic personality disorder has no code of its own and is listed by name under F60.8, other personality disorders.
  7. Brummelman and colleagues (2015) found that narcissism in children was predicted by parental , not by a lack of parental warmth.
  8. The avoidant person wants relationships but fears rejection; the person has little wish for relationships at all.
  9. Faced with abandonment, the borderline person reacts with emptiness and rage, whereas the dependent person reacts with and urgently seeks a replacement relationship.
  10. Freud's anal character (1908) was orderly, parsimonious and obstinate; people with OCPD are primarily motivated by a need for , and their traits are ego-syntonic.

Answers: (1) B, (2) histrionic, (3) empathy, (4) emotional (affective), (5) hypervigilant, (6) specific, (7) overvaluation, (8) schizoid, (9) appeasement (submissiveness), (10) control.

11.8 The Newer Models: Dimensions, ICD-11 and the DSM-5 Alternative ModelLower yield · not asked yet

Meet Manish. He is 31, an accountant in a Pune firm, and he has come to the outpatient department because his third serious relationship in five years has just ended and he has started punching the wall of his rented room at night. Over three long interviews his clinician gathers a clear picture. His moods swing within hours, usually after something a colleague or a girlfriend has said; he flares into rages he regrets; he describes a hollow feeling "like a room with no furniture". He is also deeply suspicious: he is sure that his manager is quietly building a case against him, he reads insults into harmless remarks, and he still has not forgiven a cousin for something said at a wedding in 2019. And he keeps to himself, avoiding the office lunch group because he expects to be laughed at, although he would like to belong.

Now try to give Manish one of the ten diagnoses you have met in this chapter. He meets four of the nine borderline criteria (Section 11.3), one short of the five required. He meets three of the seven paranoid criteria (Section 11.2), one short of four. He meets three of the seven avoidant criteria (Section 11.7), again one short. Under the rules of DSM-5-TR Section II he has no specific personality disorder at all. Yet he is plainly unwell, his problems have shaped his whole adult life, and they are costing him his relationships and perhaps his job. The best the manual can offer him is "other specified personality disorder", a label that tells the next clinician almost nothing about what Manish is like.

Manish is not a rare case. He is the typical case, and the newer models of personality disorder were built for people like him. This section explains why so many researchers lost faith in the ten categories, what the dimensional alternative is, and how the two newest systems, the ICD-11 classification that has been official since 2022 and the Alternative Model printed in Section III of DSM-5, describe personality pathology instead. By the end you will be able to diagnose Manish three different ways, and you will see that the three answers are telling the same story in different languages.

What is wrong with ten boxes

Section 1.6.4 set out the general case against purely categorical diagnosis: no zones of rarity between disorders, too much comorbidity, too many "other" and "unspecified" diagnoses, and so on. The personality disorders are where every one of those problems is at its worst, and it is worth seeing exactly how. The ten Section II categories were carried over almost unchanged from DSM-IV, and DSM-5-TR itself admits, in the opening pages of its alternative model, that "symptoms meeting criteria for a specific personality disorder frequently also meet criteria for other personality disorders" and that the residual diagnosis "is often the correct (but mostly uninformative) diagnosis".

The first problem is artificial comorbidity. A patient who meets the criteria for one personality disorder very often meets them for a second or a third. That is not because people collect personality disorders the way some people collect illnesses. It is because the criteria sets overlap (suspiciousness appears in paranoid, schizotypal and, as stress-related paranoid ideation, borderline personality disorder; social withdrawal appears in schizoid, schizotypal and avoidant personality disorder), and because a few broad traits, above all a tendency to strong negative emotion, run through many of the categories at once. When one underlying dimension is sliced into several named boxes, a person high on that dimension lands in several boxes. The comorbidity is produced by the classification, not by the patient.

The second problem is heterogeneity within a single diagnosis. Borderline personality disorder needs any five of nine criteria. Work out the arithmetic: there are 126 ways of choosing exactly five of nine criteria, 84 ways of choosing six, 36 of seven, 9 of eight and 1 of all nine, a total of 256 different combinations that all earn the same label. Two patients can share the diagnosis while sharing only one criterion: one may meet criteria 1 to 5, the other criteria 5 to 9. If you were told only that a new patient "has borderline personality disorder", you would not know whether to expect self-harm, dissociation, frantic clinging or explosive anger.

The third problem is arbitrary thresholds. Why five borderline criteria and not four or six? Why four of seven for paranoid personality disorder? The cut-offs were set by committees, not discovered in nature, and nothing special happens to a person between criterion four and criterion five. Manish, with four borderline features, is no less impaired than a patient with five; studies that treat the criteria as a simple count generally find that the count predicts impairment and outcome better than the yes-or-no diagnosis does. A category treats a person one criterion below the line exactly like a person with no features at all.

The fourth problem follows from the first three. Because real patients so often have a mixture of features, or fall just short of several thresholds, the residual category becomes one of the busiest in the chapter. In 2004 Roel Verheul and Thomas A. Widiger pooled 51 studies of the DSM-IV diagnosis "personality disorder not otherwise specified" (PD NOS, the forerunner of today's other specified and unspecified personality disorder). In studies using structured interviews it ranked third among all the personality disorder diagnoses, and in studies relying on ordinary clinical interviews it was often the single commonest. When the most frequent answer a classification gives is "none of the above", the classification is not carving nature at its joints.

Several further weaknesses complete the case. Unstructured clinical diagnosis of personality disorders agrees poorly from one clinician to the next; structured interviews such as the SCID-5-PD and the International Personality Disorder Examination (Assessment book) agree better but take hours. The diagnoses are less stable than the definition (a pattern "stable and of long duration") promises: Section 11.4 shows that most patients with borderline personality disorder slip below the threshold within a few years, often by losing one or two criteria, while the underlying traits and the problems in work and relationships change much more slowly. And the three clusters, which Section 11.1 uses to organise the chapter, are by DSM-5-TR's own account useful for teaching but "not consistently validated": disorders from different clusters co-occur all the time. Table 11.25 gathers the problems in one place.

Table 11.25 Why many researchers lost faith in the ten categorical personality disorders.
ProblemWhat it meansExample
Artificial comorbidityOne person meets several personality disorder diagnoses because the criteria sets overlapA suspicious, withdrawn man qualifies for paranoid, schizotypal and avoidant personality disorder at once
HeterogeneityPolythetic rules let very different people share one label256 combinations of five or more of the nine borderline criteria; two patients may share only one
Arbitrary thresholdsCut-offs set by committee; impairment rises smoothly with each criterionFour borderline criteria: no diagnosis; five: a diagnosis
Overuse of the residual categoryMany patients fit no type, so "not otherwise specified" or "other specified" is the commonest answerVerheul and Widiger's 2004 meta-analysis of 51 studies
Poor reliabilityClinicians using ordinary interviews often disagreeStructured interviews help but are long
InstabilityDiagnoses come and go faster than the definition impliesRemission of borderline personality disorder within a few years (Section 11.4)
Unvalidated clustersCluster A, B and C disorders co-occur across clustersDSM-5-TR's own warning about the clustering system
Exam trap

Despite all these problems, DSM-5-TR's official personality disorders are still the ten categories in Section II, carried over from DSM-IV; the dimensional Alternative Model sits in Section III and is not the official diagnostic system. It is ICD-11, not DSM-5-TR, that made a dimensional model official. There is no Cluster D, and the clusters themselves are described by the manual as having "serious limitations". "Personality disorder not otherwise specified" was the DSM-IV name; DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR replaced it with other specified and unspecified personality disorder.

The dimensional alternative: personality disorder as normal personality taken to an extreme

If the boxes do not fit, what should replace them? DSM-5-TR states the alternative in one sentence. The categorical perspective holds that personality disorders are "qualitatively distinct clinical syndromes"; the dimensional perspective holds that they are "maladaptive variants of personality traits that merge imperceptibly into normality and into one another". On this view nobody simply has or lacks a personality disorder, any more than anybody simply has or lacks height. Everyone sits somewhere on a set of continuous trait dimensions, and what we call a personality disorder is a position far out on one or more of them, combined with the rigidity and the damage to self and relationships that the general definition in Section 11.1 describes. Figure 11.13 shows the idea.

Which dimensions? The obvious candidate was the most thoroughly replicated description of normal personality, the five-factor model (the "Big Five": neuroticism, extraversion, openness to experience, agreeableness and conscientiousness), whose history and measurement belong to the Personality book. In 1994 Widiger and Paul T. Costa Jr. argued in the Journal of Abnormal Psychology that the personality disorders could be understood as extreme and maladaptive positions on these same five dimensions, and in the same year Costa and Widiger edited a book of studies testing the claim. The idea was elegant: instead of a separate science of abnormal personality, there would be one science of personality, with the disorders as its far ends.

Does the evidence support it? In 2004 the Australian psychologists L. M. Saulsman and A. C. Page pooled 15 independent samples in which people had been measured on both the five factors and the ten DSM-IV personality disorders. Two findings came out. First, each disorder had a five-factor profile that was "meaningful and predictable" from its criteria: avoidant personality disorder was high in neuroticism and low in extraversion, obsessive-compulsive personality disorder high in conscientiousness, and so on. Second, the most consistent thread running through many of the disorders was high neuroticism and low agreeableness, with extraversion "a more discriminating dimension" because it pointed in opposite directions for different disorders (low in schizoid and avoidant patients, high in histrionic ones). That second finding is exactly what you would predict if artificial comorbidity is real: many personality disorders share a high-neuroticism, low-agreeableness core, so they overlap. Table 11.26 gives the general pattern.

Narcissistic-Somewhat high (assertiveness)Low-
AvoidantHighLow--
DependentHigh-Inconsistent (high compliance in theory)-
Obsessive-compulsive---High

The five-factor model was not the only dimensional candidate. Some researchers argued that a model of normal personality would miss the clinically important extremes, and built their dimensions from personality pathology itself. The psychiatrist W. John Livesley, working in Vancouver, collected the features clinicians use to describe personality disorders and reduced them statistically to a self-report questionnaire, the Dimensional Assessment of Personality Pathology-Basic Questionnaire (DAPP-BQ: 290 items, 18 lower-order traits), whose items group into four higher-order factors: emotional dysregulation, dissocial behaviour, inhibitedness and compulsivity. Other groups produced the Personality Psychopathology Five (PSY-5), scored from the MMPI-2, and several more (all of them Assessment book material). The striking thing is how similar the answers are. Again and again the same four or five broad dimensions come out: a tendency to intense negative emotion, a tendency to withdraw from people, a tendency to be callous and antagonistic, a tendency to act without restraint, and (in some models) a tendency to rigid control or to odd thinking. Hold on to that list; you are about to see it reappear, under slightly different names, in ICD-11 and in DSM-5's alternative model. At the broadest level still, the Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology (HiTOP) places these personality dimensions inside spectra that cover all mental disorders (Section 1.6.4).

A dimensional description has clear advantages. It keeps information that a cut-off throws away (Manish's four borderline features are recorded, not discarded); it describes every patient, so there is no residual category; ratings on a continuum are more reliable than yes-or-no judgements; it explains comorbidity instead of multiplying it; and it links the disorders to a large research literature on the genetics and development of normal traits (the broad traits are substantially heritable, Section 11.1). Its disadvantages are practical. Clinicians think and talk in syndromes; "high negative affectivity, moderate detachment" is harder to say across a ward round than "borderline". Decades of treatment research, including all the trials of dialectical behaviour therapy and mentalisation-based treatment (Section 11.4), were done with patients selected by category. And courts, insurers and health services want a name. The two newest systems are attempts to keep the advantages while softening the disadvantages.

Exam trap

The Big Five (neuroticism, extraversion, openness, agreeableness, conscientiousness) describe normal personality; DSM-5's five pathological domains (negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, psychoticism) are their maladaptive variants, not the same list under new names. The five-factor account of personality disorders is associated with Widiger and Costa (1994). Livesley's instrument is the DAPP-BQ, with four higher-order factors (emotional dysregulation, dissocial behaviour, inhibitedness, compulsivity). The dimensional view says personality disorders "merge imperceptibly into normality and into one another"; the categorical view says they are "qualitatively distinct clinical syndromes".

ICD-11: one personality disorder, graded by severity

The World Health Organization took the bolder step. ICD-10 had listed eight specific personality disorders (F60.0 to F60.7, from paranoid to dependent, Section 11.1), and in practice they were used very unevenly: two of them, emotionally unstable personality disorder and dissocial personality disorder, were recorded far more often than the rest, and the overlap among the types produced the artificial comorbidity described in Section 11.8.1. The working group that WHO set up to revise the personality disorders for ICD-11 was chaired by the British psychiatrist Peter Tyrer, and in 2011 Tyrer, Michael Crawford, Roger Mulder and their colleagues published the case for a complete reclassification. Its central argument is one that DSM-5-TR itself now concedes in its alternative model: "Generalized severity may be the most important single predictor of concurrent and prospective dysfunction in assessing personality psychopathology." If what matters most for prognosis and treatment is how severe a person's personality problems are, the classification should ask that question first, and treat the style of the problems as a secondary description.

That is the shape of the ICD-11 classification, which the World Health Assembly adopted in 2019 and which came into effect on 1 January 2022 (Section 1.9). The ten types are gone. In their place is a single diagnosis, personality disorder (6D10), which the clinician makes in three steps: first decide whether the general requirements are met, then grade the severity, and then, if it helps, describe the style with trait qualifiers.

The general requirements will feel familiar from Section 11.1, but read them closely, because they differ from DSM-5-TR in instructive ways. There must be an enduring disturbance in the functioning of aspects of the self (identity, self-worth, the accuracy of one's view of oneself, self-direction) and/or interpersonal dysfunction (the ability to develop and maintain close and mutually satisfying relationships, to understand other people's perspectives and to manage conflict). The disturbance must have persisted over an extended period of time, which the guidelines illustrate as 2 years or more. It shows itself in patterns of cognition, emotional experience, emotional expression and behaviour that are maladaptive (for example, inflexible or poorly regulated), and it appears across a range of personal and social situations rather than in one relationship or one role. The patterns must not be developmentally appropriate and must not be explained primarily by social or cultural factors, "including socio-political conflict", a clause that protects, for example, a young man whose mistrust and hostility grew up in a region of armed conflict. They must not be due to a substance or medication, or be better explained by another mental, neurological or medical condition. And they must cause substantial distress or significant impairment.

Notice what is missing. ICD-11 does not require the pattern to have begun in adolescence or early adulthood, as DSM-5-TR does. Its guidelines say that personality disorder usually shows itself first in childhood or adolescence, but that it is only "relatively" stable after young adulthood: a person with a personality disorder at 25 may no longer have one at 50, and a person who never had one may develop one later in life, sometimes when the loss of a spouse, a job or a supportive family removes the scaffolding that had compensated for a fragile personality. Notice, too, that the ICD-11 definition is built around the self and relationships, the same two pillars as Criterion A of DSM-5's alternative model, which you will meet in Section 11.8.5.

Once the general requirements are met, the clinician grades severity. ICD-11 judges severity from four things: how widely and deeply the functioning of the self is disturbed; how widely and deeply relationships are disturbed across contexts (romantic, family, work, friendship); how pervasive, severe and chronic the emotional, cognitive and behavioural manifestations are (including how accurately the person appraises situations under stress, and whether intense emotion leads to self-harm or violence); and how much distress and impairment all this causes. Table 11.27 sets out the levels.

Table 11.27 The ICD-11 severity levels. Personality difficulty sits below the threshold for a disorder and is not a mental disorder.
Level (code)Areas of personality functioning affectedRelationships and rolesHarm to self or others
Personality difficulty (QE50.7)Pronounced traits that cause problems only intermittently or at low intensity, for example under stress; present for at least about 2 yearsNot severe enough to cause notable disruption of work, friendships or intimate relationshipsNot expected
Mild personality disorder (6D10.0)Some areas but not others (for example, self-direction disturbed while identity and self-worth are intact); may not be apparent in some contextsProblems in many relationships or roles, but some relationships maintained and some roles carried outTypically not substantial
Moderate personality disorder (6D10.1)Multiple areas (identity, intimacy, control of impulses), though some are less affectedMarked problems in most relationships; most social and occupational roles compromised to some degreeSometimes
Severe personality disorder (6D10.2)Severe disturbance of the self (no sense of who one is, or a self so rigid that life shrinks; self-contempt, grandiosity or marked eccentricity); most if not all areasVirtually all relationships seriously affected; ability or willingness to perform roles absent or severely compromisedOften

There is also a code for personality disorder, severity unspecified (6D10.Z). The lowest step deserves a sentence. Personality difficulty describes pronounced personality traits that cause some trouble, perhaps only when the person is under strain, without reaching the level of a disorder. It is coded as QE50.7 in Chapter 24 of ICD-11 (factors influencing health status or contact with health services), not in the mental disorders chapter at all: ICD-11 records it as a reason for clinical attention, not as an illness. Its existence is itself a dimensional statement. The line between a difficult personality and a disordered one is drawn on a continuum, and ICD-11 gives a name to the zone just below the line, the zone Person A occupies in Figure 11.13.

Exam trap

ICD-11 has one personality disorder diagnosis (6D10) graded mild, moderate or severe; there are three severity grades of disorder, not four or five. Personality difficulty (QE50.7) is not a mental disorder; it is coded outside Chapter 6. ICD-11 asks for a disturbance lasting 2 years or more and, unlike DSM-5-TR, sets no age of onset. The model was developed by the WHO working group chaired by Peter Tyrer; it is ICD-11's official system, not a research appendix.

The ICD-11 trait domains and the borderline pattern

Severity says how much; the trait domain qualifiers say what kind. ICD-11 offers five, each coded under 6D11 ("prominent personality traits or patterns"). They are optional, the clinician may record as many as are prominent, and they may be attached to personality difficulty as well as to personality disorder. Each describes an enduring disposition, not a symptom:

  • Negative affectivity (6D11.0): a tendency to experience a broad range of negative emotions with a frequency and intensity out of proportion to the situation. It covers anxiety, anger, worry, fear, vulnerability, hostility, shame, depression, pessimism, guilt, low self-esteem and mistrustfulness. Once upset, such a person has great difficulty regaining composure.
  • Detachment (6D11.1): a tendency to keep interpersonal distance (social detachment: avoiding social contact, few friends, avoidance of intimacy) and emotional distance (emotional detachment: reserve, aloofness, limited emotional expression and experience). Such a person may deliberately seek out work that involves no dealings with others.
  • Dissociality (6D11.2): disregard for the rights and feelings of others, with two components. Self-centredness covers entitlement, grandiosity, expecting admiration and attention seeking; lack of empathy covers being deceptive, manipulative, exploitative, ruthless, mean, callous and physically aggressive, sometimes taking pleasure in others' suffering. Notice that this one domain absorbs much of what Section II divides between antisocial and narcissistic personality disorder.
  • Disinhibition (6D11.3): a tendency to act rashly on immediate external or internal stimuli (sensations, emotions, thoughts) without considering the consequences: impulsivity, distractibility, irresponsibility, recklessness and lack of planning, seen in reckless driving, dangerous sports, substance use, gambling and unplanned sex.
  • Anankastia (6D11.4): a narrow focus on one's own rigid standard of perfection and of right and wrong, and on controlling one's own and other people's behaviour, and situations, to make them conform. It has two parts: perfectionism (concern with rules, details, hyper-scheduling, orderliness, neatness) and emotional and behavioural constraint (rigid control of emotional expression, stubbornness, risk avoidance, perseveration, deliberateness). The name comes from the Greek ananke, necessity or compulsion, and survives from ICD-10's anankastic personality disorder, the ICD name for obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (Section 11.7). The person who stubbornly redoes a colleague's work because it falls short of his standards is the typical example.

One domain is conspicuously absent. There is no trait qualifier for odd, eccentric or psychotic-like thinking, and no schizotypal personality disorder anywhere in the ICD-11 personality chapter. ICD-11, like ICD-10 before it, classifies schizotypal disorder (6A22) with schizophrenia and the other primary psychotic disorders (Sections 4.5.8 and 11.2). Where a person's personality disturbance includes transient psychotic-like beliefs or perceptions, or marked eccentricity, ICD-11 counts these towards the severity rating rather than treating them as a separate trait, which echoes Kernberg's old idea (Section 11.4) that impaired reality testing marks the most severe level of personality organisation.

The sixth qualifier is a compromise, and it is honest to call it one. The original ICD-11 proposal had no place for borderline personality disorder at all. Many clinicians and researchers objected: borderline personality disorder has by far the largest research literature of any personality disorder, specific psychological treatments have been tested on it (Section 11.4), and in many health systems the label opens the door to those treatments. WHO therefore added the borderline pattern qualifier (6D11.5), which may be applied when the personality disturbance is characterised by a pervasive pattern of instability of relationships, self-image and affects, with marked impulsivity, shown by five or more of nine features that closely follow the DSM criteria taught in Section 11.3 (frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, unstable intense relationships, identity disturbance, self-damaging impulsivity, recurrent self-harm, affective instability, chronic emptiness, intense anger, and transient dissociative or psychotic-like features under high emotional arousal). The inclusion is often described as a "pragmatic compromise". Critics have argued that the pattern adds little, since borderline features are largely captured by high negative affectivity combined with disinhibition and dissociality, and some studies find borderline criteria hard to separate from negative affectivity at all. Figure 11.14 puts the whole ICD-11 model on one page.

Now return to Manish. Does he meet the general requirements? Yes: his self-image and his relationships have been disturbed for well over 2 years, across work, family and romance, and the disturbance is costing him dearly. How severe? Several areas are affected (his sense of self, his close relationships and his control of anger), he has marked problems in most relationships, his work is suffering, and his anger has led to some harm (the wall, and very nearly a colleague), but some areas are less affected: he works competently and keeps one old school friendship. That is moderate personality disorder, 6D10.1. Which traits are prominent? His mood swings, rages, emptiness, low self-worth and mistrust all fall under negative affectivity (6D11.0), and his avoidance of the lunch group and of new friendships falls under detachment (6D11.1). The borderline pattern qualifier does not apply, because he shows only four of its nine features. So ICD-11's description of Manish is "moderate personality disorder with prominent negative affectivity and detachment": a short phrase that tells the next clinician more than "other specified personality disorder" ever could.

Clinical link

The ICD-11 model was designed partly for clinics like a busy Indian psychiatry outpatient department, where a full structured interview for ten personality disorders is rarely possible. The first two questions (is there a personality disorder, and how severe is it?) can be answered from a good history and an informant in a few minutes, and the severity grade carries most of the practical information: it guides how intensive treatment needs to be, how closely risk must be watched, and what the prognosis is. The trait qualifiers then point towards what to work on: emotion regulation for negative affectivity, engagement and trust for detachment, consequences and empathy for dissociality, impulse control for disinhibition, flexibility for anankastia.

Exam trap

The five ICD-11 trait domains are negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition and anankastia. Dissociality and anankastia are ICD-11 words; antagonism and psychoticism are DSM-5 Alternative Model words. ICD-11 has no psychoticism domain and no schizotypal personality disorder: schizotypal disorder is 6A22, in the schizophrenia block. The borderline pattern is a qualifier (6D11.5), not a separate personality disorder diagnosis, and it needs five or more of nine features. "Anankastic" in ICD-10 (F60.5) means obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, not OCD.

The DSM-5 Alternative Model I: Criterion A, the level of personality functioning

Section 1.8.5 introduced the Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders (AMPD) in a paragraph. Here is the full model, and the story of how it came to be printed at the back of the manual rather than in the front. The DSM-5 Personality and Personality Disorders Work Group, chaired by Andrew E. Skodol with John M. Oldham as co-chair and Robert F. Krueger coordinating the text, set out to replace the DSM-IV categories with a model built on impairment and traits. Its first draft, posted on the DSM-5 website in February 2010, proposed deleting five of the ten personality disorders (paranoid, schizoid, histrionic, dependent and narcissistic). The deletion of narcissistic personality disorder provoked a storm of protest, and a revised draft in June 2011 reinstated it. The arguments continued inside and outside the work group, two of whose members, Livesley and Verheul, resigned in 2012. Finally, in December 2012, the Board of Trustees of the American Psychiatric Association decided to keep the DSM-IV categories, virtually unchanged, as the official diagnoses in Section II and to print the work group's model in Section III as an alternative, "to preserve continuity with current clinical practice, while also introducing an alternative approach". DSM-5-TR (2022) kept that arrangement and describes the result as a hybrid dimensional-categorical model.

The model rests on seven general criteria, A to G. The essential two are Criterion A, moderate or greater impairment in personality (self and interpersonal) functioning, and Criterion B, one or more pathological personality traits. Criteria C to G then repeat, in new words, the familiar conditions of Section 11.1: the impairment and traits are relatively inflexible and pervasive across situations (C) and relatively stable over time, with onset traceable to at least adolescence or early adulthood (D); they are not better explained by another mental disorder (E), not solely due to a substance or another medical condition such as severe head injury (F), and not better understood as normal for the person's developmental stage or sociocultural environment (G).

Criterion A is the heart of the model, because the work group regarded disturbances of the self and of relationships as "the core of personality psychopathology". It is rated with the Level of Personality Functioning Scale (LPFS), developed by Donna S. Bender, Leslie C. Morey and Skodol and published in 2011. The scale has four elements, two for the self and two for relationships:

  • Identity: experiencing oneself as a unique person with clear boundaries between self and others; stable self-esteem and accurate self-appraisal; the capacity to experience and regulate a full range of emotions.
  • Self-direction: pursuing coherent and meaningful short-term and life goals; using constructive and prosocial internal standards of behaviour; being able to reflect productively on oneself.
  • Empathy: understanding and appreciating other people's experiences and motives; tolerating differing perspectives; understanding the effects of one's own behaviour on others.
  • Intimacy: the depth and duration of connection with others; the desire and capacity for closeness; mutual regard shown in the way one behaves towards people.

For each element the scale describes five levels: 0, little or no impairment (healthy, adaptive functioning); 1, some impairment; 2, moderate impairment; 3, severe impairment; and 4, extreme impairment. The clinician chooses the level that best captures the person's overall current functioning. A personality disorder requires at least level 2, moderate impairment. The manual explains that this threshold was chosen on evidence: in the work group's studies (Morey and colleagues, 2011) the moderate level was the point that best allowed clinicians to identify personality disorder pathology accurately and efficiently. The level also carries information beyond the diagnosis. Greater impairment predicts having more than one personality disorder, or one of the typically more severe ones, and the scale can be used on its own as a global index of personality functioning, even in someone with no personality disorder.

Look at what the four elements describe and you will see why the AMPD and ICD-11 are close cousins. Identity and self-direction are ICD-11's "functioning of aspects of the self"; empathy and intimacy are its "interpersonal dysfunction". Both systems have concluded, from the same research, that a single dimension of personality functioning (how integrated a person's sense of self is and how capable they are of real relationships) carries most of what clinicians need to know about severity. Psychodynamic readers will recognise an old idea in modern dress: Kernberg's levels of personality organisation (Section 11.4) also ranked people by the integration of their identity and the quality of their relationships.

Exam trap

Criterion A of the AMPD has two areas and four elements: self (identity, self-direction) and interpersonal (empathy, intimacy). The LPFS runs from level 0 (little or no impairment) to level 4 (extreme impairment), five levels in all, and a disorder needs moderate impairment, level 2, or worse. Do not confuse the LPFS levels with ICD-11's grades: ICD-11 has personality difficulty plus mild, moderate and severe disorder. The AMPD is in Section III of DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR; it was placed there by the APA Board of Trustees in December 2012.

The DSM-5 Alternative Model II: Criterion B, the pathological traits, and the six types

Criterion B describes the style of a person's personality pathology in terms of pathological personality traits. The manual defines a trait as a tendency to feel, perceive, behave and think in relatively consistent ways across time and situations; it is not the same as a single act (one impulsive purchase does not make a person impulsive) or a symptom (symptoms such as poor concentration come and go with episodes of illness, whereas traits are relatively stable). Traits are arranged in a hierarchy. Broad traits are called trait domains; narrower traits that tend to occur together within a domain are called trait facets. The AMPD has five domains containing 25 facets. Each domain is a pole of a dimension whose healthy opposite the manual names in brackets, and the manual states plainly that the five are "maladaptive variants of the five domains" of the five-factor model:

  • Negative affectivity (versus emotional stability): frequent and intense experience of a wide range of negative emotions (anxiety, depression, guilt and shame, worry, anger) and their behavioural and interpersonal expressions, such as self-harm and dependency.
  • Detachment (versus extraversion): avoidance of socioemotional experience, both withdrawal from people (from casual daily contact to intimate relationships) and restricted emotional experience and expression, especially a limited capacity for pleasure.
  • Antagonism (versus agreeableness): behaviour that puts the person at odds with others, including an exaggerated sense of self-importance with an expectation of special treatment, and a callous antipathy towards others, both unaware of their needs and ready to use them.
  • Disinhibition (versus conscientiousness): an orientation towards immediate gratification, so that behaviour is driven by current thoughts, feelings and stimuli without regard for past learning or future consequences.
  • Psychoticism (versus lucidity): a wide range of culturally incongruent odd, eccentric or unusual behaviours and cognitions, both in process (perception, dissociation) and in content (beliefs).

Table 11.28 lists the 25 facets under their domains. Three details repay attention. Some facets belong to two domains: hostility sits in both negative affectivity and antagonism, and depressivity and suspiciousness are defined under detachment but also contribute to negative affectivity. Restricted affectivity is defined under detachment and appears under negative affectivity only as a "lack of" facet, pointing the opposite way. And rigid perfectionism is listed under disinhibition as a "lack of" facet: it is the extreme opposite pole of disinhibition, too much conscientiousness rather than too little. That is how the AMPD represents what ICD-11 calls anankastia.

Table 11.28 The five trait domains and 25 trait facets of the DSM-5 Alternative Model (DSM-5-TR Section III).
Domain (healthy opposite)Facets
Negative affectivity (emotional stability)Emotional lability, anxiousness, separation insecurity, submissiveness, hostility, perseveration; also depressivity and suspiciousness; lack of restricted affectivity
Detachment (extraversion)Withdrawal, intimacy avoidance, anhedonia, depressivity, restricted affectivity, suspiciousness
Antagonism (agreeableness)Manipulativeness, deceitfulness, grandiosity, attention seeking, callousness; also hostility
Disinhibition (conscientiousness)Irresponsibility, impulsivity, distractibility, risk taking; lack of rigid perfectionism
Psychoticism (lucidity)Unusual beliefs and experiences, eccentricity, cognitive and perceptual dysregulation

The traits are measured, where time allows, with the Personality Inventory for DSM-5 (PID-5), a 220-item self-report questionnaire built by Krueger and colleagues in 2012, with an informant version for a spouse or relative (Assessment book). The manual compares the clinical use of the trait model to the review of systems in general medicine: whatever the presenting complaint, review all five domains, lest an important area be missed.

From Criteria A and B the model derives six specific personality disorders, the six for which the work group judged the evidence of validity and clinical usefulness strongest. Each is defined by characteristic difficulties in at least two of the four Criterion A elements, plus a specified set of traits (Table 11.29).

Table 11.29 The six specific personality disorders of the Alternative Model. Each also needs moderate or greater impairment in two or more of identity, self-direction, empathy and intimacy, and the general criteria C to G.
DisorderTypical featuresCriterion B (traits required)
AntisocialFailure to conform to lawful and ethical behaviour; egocentric, callous lack of concern for others6 or more of 7: manipulativeness, callousness, deceitfulness, hostility, risk taking, impulsivity, irresponsibility; age at least 18; may add "with psychopathic features"
AvoidantSocial avoidance and inhibition from feelings of ineptitude; anxious preoccupation with rejection and ridicule3 or more of 4, one of which must be anxiousness: anxiousness, withdrawal, anhedonia, intimacy avoidance
BorderlineInstability of self-image, goals, relationships and affects, with impulsivity, risk taking or hostility4 or more of 7, at least one being impulsivity, risk taking or hostility: emotional lability, anxiousness, separation insecurity, depressivity, impulsivity, risk taking, hostility
NarcissisticVariable, vulnerable self-esteem regulated by attention and approval seeking; overt or covert grandiosityBoth grandiosity and attention seeking
Obsessive-compulsiveDifficulty with close relationships, rigid perfectionism, inflexibility, restricted emotional expression3 or more of 4, one of which must be rigid perfectionism: rigid perfectionism, perseveration, intimacy avoidance, restricted affectivity
SchizotypalImpaired capacity for close relationships; eccentric cognition, perception and behaviour; suspiciousness4 or more of 6: cognitive and perceptual dysregulation, unusual beliefs and experiences, eccentricity, restricted affectivity, withdrawal, suspiciousness

Two of these deserve a comment because they change the familiar picture. The AMPD's narcissistic personality disorder is defined around variable and vulnerable self-esteem, with grandiosity that may be overt or covert, which brings the vulnerable narcissism described in Section 11.6 into the definition. And the antisocial type carries a specifier, with psychopathic features, for the variant often called primary psychopathy (Section 11.5): low anxiousness and low withdrawal combined with high attention seeking, so that a bold, socially dominant and stress-immune style masks the antisocial behaviour. This is the AMPD's way of recording psychopathy; it is not the same thing as the "with limited prosocial emotions" specifier of conduct disorder (Section 11.9.4).

Everyone who meets the general criteria but does not match one of the six types receives personality disorder, trait specified (PD-TS). The diagnosis needs moderate or greater impairment in at least two of the four elements of personality functioning and one or more pathological trait domains or facets, and the person's particular traits are always recorded, so that each PD-TS diagnosis is in effect a personal profile. Paranoid, schizoid, histrionic and dependent personality disorder, the four Section II types that did not survive as named types, are represented by PD-TS with the relevant traits (for example, suspiciousness and hostility for a paranoid style, withdrawal and restricted affectivity for a schizoid one, attention seeking and emotional lability for a histrionic one, submissiveness and separation insecurity for a dependent one). The PD-TS diagnosis does the job that "other specified personality disorder" fails to do: it says exactly what is wrong.

Push Manish through the model one last time. Criterion A: his sense of self is unstable and empty (identity), and his close relationships are intense, conflicted and short-lived (intimacy), so he has moderate impairment in two of the four elements, level 2 on the LPFS. Criterion B: the PID-5 and the interview show elevated emotional lability, anxiousness, depressivity and hostility, together with suspiciousness and withdrawal. Now check the type rules. For the AMPD borderline type he needs four of seven traits, one of them impulsivity, risk taking or hostility: emotional lability, anxiousness, depressivity and hostility make four, and hostility is among them. So, although Section II gave him no specific diagnosis, the alternative model diagnoses borderline personality disorder, and the clinician adds the traits not required for it (suspiciousness, withdrawal) as specifiers. The example is a useful warning as well as a demonstration. The two DSM models were designed to identify roughly the same people, but they reach their verdicts by different routes, and in individual patients they can disagree.

Exam trap

The AMPD has 5 trait domains and 25 facets, and keeps six types: antisocial, avoidant, borderline, narcissistic, obsessive-compulsive and schizotypal. The four that became PD-TS are paranoid, schizoid, histrionic and dependent (a favourite "which is not retained" option). Everyone else gets personality disorder, trait specified. The domains are negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition and psychoticism. The PID-5 measures the traits; the LPFS rates Criterion A. Narcissistic personality disorder was dropped in the 2010 draft but restored in 2011, so it is one of the six.

Three systems side by side

You can now line up the three systems that a clinician in India may meet on a single day: DSM-5-TR Section II in a research paper, ICD-10 or ICD-11 on the hospital case sheet, and the AMPD in a journal article or a training programme. Table 11.30 matches their dimensions; Table 11.31 compares their architecture.

Table 11.30 The trait domains of the DSM-5 Alternative Model and ICD-11, matched to the normal personality dimensions of the five-factor model.

FeatureDSM-5-TR Section IIDSM-5 Alternative ModelICD-11
StatusOfficial DSM diagnosesAlternative in Section IIIOfficial WHO classification since 2022
First question askedWhich of ten types?How impaired is personality functioning?Is there a personality disorder, and how severe?

SeverityNot ratedLPFS, levels 0 to 4; level 2 neededDifficulty, then mild, moderate, severe
