How well do the new systems work? Their great strength is that they rest on the research of the previous thirty years rather than on the clinical traditions of the previous century. The five broad domains turn up again and again, in many countries, whether researchers start from normal personality, from personality pathology or from the PID-5. DSM-5-TR itself accepts that general severity may be the best single predictor of how a person will fare. And both systems remove the residual category: nobody falls outside them. Their weaknesses are practical and political. The AMPD is complicated: two criteria, four elements, five levels, five domains, 25 facets and six rule-sets. WHO, while noting that ICD-11's model is conceptually close to it, judged the AMPD "too complicated for implementation in most clinical settings around the world", which is one reason ICD-11 has no facets. Critics of both systems point out that the line between "impairment in functioning" and "pathological traits" is hard to draw (is chronic emptiness a disturbance of identity or a facet of depressivity?), that clinicians are unfamiliar with the new language, and that abandoning the named types cuts the link with decades of treatment research, which is exactly why ICD-11 kept a borderline pattern and the AMPD kept six types. The likely future is a period in which both languages are spoken, and the clinician who can translate between them, as you have just done for Manish, is the one who understands personality disorder best.

Exam trap

A 2021 paper asked: "Personality disorders are stable long-term conditions which can affect an individual's (a) cognition, (b) mood, (c) interpersonal functioning, (d) all of the above." The answer is (d) all of the above, and every system in this section agrees. DSM-5-TR Section II requires the pattern in two or more of cognition, affectivity, interpersonal functioning and impulse control (Section 11.1); ICD-11 requires it in patterns of cognition, emotional experience, emotional expression and behaviour, with disturbance of the self and/or relationships; the AMPD rates the self and interpersonal functioning and traits that span emotion, thought and behaviour. Matching pairs to learn: negative affectivity, detachment and disinhibition appear in both manuals; antagonism (AMPD) corresponds to dissociality (ICD-11); psychoticism is AMPD only; anankastia is ICD-11 only.

11.9 Oppositional Defiant and Conduct DisordersLower yield · 2 Qs

It is a Saturday morning at a child guidance clinic in a large Indian city, and three new families are waiting. Veer is seven. His mother says he "simply will not listen": he argues about everything, throws a tantrum whenever the tablet is taken away, blames his little sister for whatever goes wrong and drives his class teacher to distraction by humming when she asks for silence. Daksh is twelve and has been sent by his school after a series of fights, the last one with a cricket bat; neighbours say he has hurt stray dogs, and his father admits, with a mixture of anger and fear, that he has never once seen the boy sorry. Falguni is fifteen. Until last year she was a quiet, ordinary student; then she fell in with an older group, began lying about where she was, took money from her aunt's purse and twice ran away overnight.

All three children are in trouble with the adults around them, and a hurried clinician might give them all the same label, "behaviour problem". This section shows why they almost certainly have three different conditions: oppositional defiant disorder for Veer, conduct disorder of childhood onset with limited prosocial emotions for Daksh, and conduct disorder of adolescent onset for Falguni. It also shows why that matters, because the three diagnoses carry very different prospects for the adults these children will become. The section ends where Section 11.5 began, at the door of antisocial personality disorder.

The disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders: why these sit together

DSM-5 (2013) created a new chapter, disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders, and DSM-5-TR keeps it. The manual defines the family by a shared problem and a shared consequence. The problem is poor self-control of emotions and behaviour. Many disorders involve that, so the defining feature is the consequence: in these disorders the failures of control show themselves in behaviour that violates the rights of others (aggression, destruction of property) or brings the person into significant conflict with social norms or authority figures. The chapter contains oppositional defiant disorder, intermittent explosive disorder, conduct disorder, antisocial personality disorder (whose criteria are printed in the personality chapter but listed here as well, because it is the adult end of the same externalising spectrum), pyromania, kleptomania, and other specified and unspecified disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorder for impairing presentations that miss the thresholds.

The disorders differ in the balance between the two kinds of self-control. At one end, the criteria for conduct disorder are almost entirely about behaviour: acts that violate other people's rights or major social rules, which may or may not spring from poorly controlled emotion (some of the coldest aggression in the chapter comes from constricted, not excessive, emotion). At the other end, intermittent explosive disorder is almost entirely about emotion: outbursts of anger out of all proportion to the provocation. Oppositional defiant disorder sits between them, with criteria shared between emotion (anger and irritability) and behaviour (argumentativeness and defiance). Pyromania and kleptomania are different again: poor control over one specific act, fire setting or stealing, that relieves inner tension. Intermittent explosive disorder, pyromania and kleptomania are taught in Section 11.10; this section takes oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder.

The chapter's members share more than a definition. They are all commoner in boys and men, they usually begin in childhood or adolescence (conduct disorder and oppositional defiant disorder almost never begin in adulthood), and they are linked, in the manual's words, to "a common externalizing spectrum associated with the personality dimensions of disinhibition and negative emotionality", and inversely with constraint and agreeableness. Those are the very dimensions you met in Section 11.8, and they explain why these disorders co-occur so often with one another, with substance use disorders and with antisocial personality disorder. The manual also adds a warning that runs through everything that follows. Almost every symptom in this chapter (a tantrum, a lie, a fight, a night out without permission) is something ordinary children and teenagers do from time to time. What makes it a symptom is its frequency, persistence, spread across situations and the impairment it causes, judged against what is normal for the child's age, gender and culture.

The history of the names is short but examined. DSM-III (1980) introduced conduct disorder, divided into four subtypes by two dimensions (undersocialised or socialised, aggressive or nonaggressive), and a milder condition called oppositional disorder; DSM-III-R (1987) renamed it oppositional defiant disorder. DSM-IV (1994) replaced the four subtypes with the childhood-onset and adolescent-onset types still used today, and placed both disorders with ADHD among the "attention-deficit and disruptive behavior disorders" of childhood. DSM-5 moved them into the new chapter alongside the old impulse-control disorders and added the "with limited prosocial emotions" specifier. ICD-10's "socialised" and "unsocialised" conduct disorders (Section 11.9.8) preserve the DSM-III idea.

Exam trap

The DSM-5-TR chapter "Disruptive, Impulse-Control, and Conduct Disorders" contains oppositional defiant disorder, intermittent explosive disorder, conduct disorder, antisocial personality disorder (listed in two chapters), pyromania, kleptomania and the other specified and unspecified categories. It does not contain ADHD (a neurodevelopmental disorder, Section 3.7), disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (a depressive disorder, Section 5.3.2), gambling disorder (now with the addictions, Section 10.1.7) or trichotillomania (obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, Section 6.6.5). Conduct disorder is the most behavioural member, intermittent explosive disorder the most emotional, and oppositional defiant disorder lies between.

Oppositional defiant disorder

Oppositional defiant disorder (ODD) is a frequent and persistent pattern of angry or irritable mood, argumentative or defiant behaviour, or vindictiveness, lasting at least 6 months and shown by at least four symptoms from any of three groups, during interaction with at least one person who is not a sibling. The eight symptoms are worth learning in their groups, because the groups have different futures.

  • Angry or irritable mood: often loses temper; is often touchy or easily annoyed; is often angry and resentful.
  • Argumentative or defiant behaviour: often argues with authority figures (for a child or adolescent, with adults); often actively defies or refuses to comply with requests from authority figures or with rules; often deliberately annoys others; often blames others for his or her own mistakes or misbehaviour.
  • Vindictiveness: has been spiteful or vindictive at least twice within the past 6 months.

Every rule in the criteria has a reason. The sibling rule exists because squabbling, provoking and refusing are so common between brothers and sisters that they would make the diagnosis meaningless; the pattern must show with a parent, a teacher, a peer or some other person. The word "often" is given a number, because every child sometimes loses his temper. For children under 5 the behaviour should occur on most days for at least 6 months; from age 5 onwards, at least once a week for at least 6 months (vindictiveness has its own rule of twice in 6 months). Even then the clinician must ask whether the frequency and intensity are outside the range normal for the child's developmental level, gender and culture: weekly tantrums are common in preschool children, but daily tantrums occur in only about 10 percent of them. The behaviour must also cause distress to the child or to the people around him, or impair his social, educational or other functioning. And it must not occur only during a psychotic, substance use, depressive or bipolar disorder, and must not meet the criteria for disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, which takes precedence.

Severity is graded not by the number of symptoms but by how far the pattern has spread. Mild means the symptoms are confined to one setting (home, school, work or peers); moderate, some symptoms in at least two settings; severe, some symptoms in three or more. The manual's logic is that pervasiveness is the best indicator of severity, and it adds a useful clinical observation: many children with ODD show their symptoms only at home and only with family members, and because the behaviour is most visible with adults the child knows well, it may not appear at all during the clinic interview. A charming, polite seven-year-old in the consulting room tells you nothing either way.

Veer shows how the criteria work. Over the past year, two or three times a week, he has lost his temper when told to stop playing (symptom 1); he is touchy and quick to take offence (2); he argues with his parents and teacher (4); he refuses homework instructions "because nobody can tell me what to do" (5); he hums to annoy his teacher (6); he blames his sister for everything (7); and twice in the past 6 months he took revenge on a classmate who had reported him, once by spreading a story that got the boy scolded (8). That is seven symptoms, well over four, lasting more than 6 months, at least weekly, at home and at school, with adults who are not his siblings, and causing real trouble in both places: ODD, moderate. Notice, too, what Veer does not do. He does not hit people, hurt animals, destroy property, steal or run away; those belong to conduct disorder. Between his outbursts he is a cheerful boy, which matters for the next distinction. And children with ODD typically do not see themselves as angry or defiant at all; like Veer, they explain that the demands made of them are unreasonable.

Oppositional defiant disorder is common. The manual gives a cross-national prevalence of 1 to 11 percent, with an average of about 3.3 percent; before adolescence it is somewhat commoner in boys (about 1.6 to 1), a difference not consistently found in adolescents and adults. The first symptoms usually appear in the preschool years and rarely later than early adolescence. Its future is the most important fact about it, and it divides along the symptom groups. Most children with ODD never develop conduct disorder, although ODD often precedes conduct disorder, especially the childhood-onset type. The defiant, argumentative and vindictive symptoms carry most of the risk of conduct disorder; the angry and irritable mood symptoms carry most of the risk of later anxiety and depressive disorders, even without conduct disorder. Adults who had persistent ODD as children have more troubled relationships, less education and more workplace stress. ODD is also associated with an increased risk of suicide attempts, even after other disorders are allowed for.

The diagnosis depends on telling ODD apart from its many look-alikes, set out in Table 11.32. Three deserve a sentence each. A child with ADHD may also fail to do what he is told, but he fails because the task demands sustained attention or sitting still, not because he resents being told; the two disorders often coexist, and both can then be diagnosed (Section 3.7.5). A child with disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (Section 5.3.2) has severe temper outbursts, on average three or more times a week, and a persistently irritable or angry mood between them, nearly every day; such a child usually meets the ODD criteria too, but when DMDD is diagnosed ODD is not, even if all its criteria are met. That is why Veer's good humour between outbursts is diagnostically important. And conduct disorder, as you will see next, involves aggression to people or animals, destruction of property, theft or deceit, which ODD does not; yet ODD can be at least as impairing, and when a child meets the criteria for both, DSM-5-TR allows both diagnoses (DSM-IV did not).

Table 11.32 Oppositional defiant disorder and the conditions it is confused with.
ConditionWhat it shares with ODDWhat tells them apart
Normal defianceArguing, tantrums, refusingFrequency, persistence and impairment beyond what is normal for age, gender and culture
ADHDNot complying with requestsNon-compliance only where sustained effort or sitting still is demanded; often coexists
Disruptive mood dysregulation disorderIrritability, temper outburstsSevere outbursts three or more times a week with persistent irritable mood between; if met, ODD is not diagnosed
Conduct disorderConflict with authorityAggression to people or animals, destruction, theft, deceit; both may be diagnosed
Intermittent explosive disorderHigh levels of angerSerious aggression towards others (Section 11.10)
Depressive and bipolar disordersIrritability, negative moodODD not diagnosed if symptoms occur only during the mood disorder
Adjustment disorderDefiance after a stressorClear link to the stressor; settles within 6 months of its end
Language disorder, hearing loss, social anxietyNot following directions, refusingFailure to understand, or fear of negative evaluation, not defiance
Exam trap

ODD needs four or more of eight symptoms for at least 6 months, shown with at least one person who is not a sibling. Its three groups are angry/irritable mood (three symptoms), argumentative/defiant behaviour (four) and vindictiveness (one: spiteful at least twice in 6 months). Severity depends on the number of settings, not the number of symptoms. ODD involves no aggression to people or animals, no destruction of property and no theft; those suggest conduct disorder. If the criteria for DMDD are met, ODD is not diagnosed. Most children with ODD do not go on to conduct disorder.

Conduct disorder: the fifteen criteria

Conduct disorder (CD) is a repetitive and persistent pattern of behaviour in which the basic rights of others, or major age-appropriate social norms or rules, are violated. The two halves of that definition matter. "The basic rights of others" covers what the child does to people, animals and property; "major age-appropriate norms" covers the rules that a child of that age is expected to keep, such as coming home at night and going to school. The pattern is shown by at least three of 15 criteria in the past 12 months, with at least one present in the past 6 months, from any of four groups (Table 11.33). The behaviour must cause clinically significant impairment in social, academic or occupational functioning. And if the person is 18 or older, the criteria for antisocial personality disorder must not be met; conduct disorder can be diagnosed in an adult, though onset after 16 is rare, but once the adult pattern of antisocial personality disorder is present that diagnosis replaces it.

Table 11.33 The 15 criteria for conduct disorder in their four groups, with what each looks like.
GroupCriterionWhat it looks like
Aggression to people and animals1. Often bullies, threatens or intimidates othersExtorts tiffin money from younger boys; threatening messages on social media
Aggression to people and animals2. Often initiates physical fightsStarts the fight, not merely defends himself
Aggression to people and animals3. Has used a weapon that can cause serious harmBat, brick, broken bottle, knife, gun
Aggression to people and animals4. Has been physically cruel to peopleTies up and hurts a smaller child
Aggression to people and animals5. Has been physically cruel to animalsTortures stray dogs or cats
Aggression to people and animals6. Has stolen while confronting a victimMugging, chain or purse snatching, extortion, armed robbery
Aggression to people and animals7. Has forced someone into sexual activitySexual assault
Destruction of property8. Has deliberately set fires intending serious damageSets fire to a school store room (compare pyromania, Section 11.10)
Destruction of property9. Has deliberately destroyed others' property in other waysSmashes car windows, vandalises school property
Deceitfulness or theft10. Has broken into someone else's house, building or carBreaks into a neighbour's flat
Deceitfulness or theft11. Often lies to obtain goods or favours or to avoid obligations ("cons" others)Borrows money with false stories and never repays
Deceitfulness or theft12. Has stolen items of nontrivial value without confronting a victimShoplifting, forgery, fraud
Serious violations of rules13. Often stays out at night despite parental prohibitions, beginning before age 13Out until 2 a.m. at age 11
Serious violations of rules14. Has run away from home overnight at least twice, or once without returning for a lengthy periodDoes not count if running from physical or sexual abuse
Serious violations of rules15. Is often truant from school, beginning before age 13Bunks school regularly from Class 6

Read the wording of the criteria and you will notice two kinds. Some begin with "often" (bullies, initiates fights, lies, stays out, is truant): they describe repeated behaviour, because one fight or one lie means little. Others begin with "has" (has used a weapon, has been cruel, has stolen while confronting a victim, has forced someone into sex, has set fires, has broken in): these are acts serious enough that a single occurrence in the past year counts. The two rules with an age, staying out at night and truancy, must have begun before age 13, because a 16-year-old who comes home late or misses lectures is breaking rules that are far less "age-appropriate" than an 11-year-old doing the same. And running away counts only if it happened at least twice overnight, or once for a long period, and not if the child was fleeing abuse: a girl who runs from a beating is protecting herself, not violating a norm.

Falguni shows the criteria applied. In the past year she has repeatedly lied to her parents and relatives to obtain money (criterion 11), stolen a substantial sum from her aunt's purse when no one was looking (12), and run away overnight twice (14), the last time three months ago. That is three criteria in 12 months with at least one in the past 6, and her schooling and family life are badly impaired: conduct disorder. She has also been bunking school, but that began when she was 14, so it does not count towards criterion 15. Nothing in her history suggests conduct problems before the age of 10, a fact that the next subsection shows to be the most important thing about her.

The manual grades severity by both the number of problems and the harm done. Mild: few if any problems beyond those needed for the diagnosis, causing relatively minor harm (lying, truancy, staying out late, other rule breaking). Moderate: intermediate in number and effect (stealing without confronting a victim, vandalism). Severe: many problems beyond those required, or problems that cause considerable harm (forced sex, physical cruelty, use of a weapon, stealing while confronting a victim, breaking and entering). Falguni's stealing makes her moderate; Daksh's cruelty and the cricket bat make him severe.

Conduct disorder is common and costly. In the United States and other largely high-income countries the one-year prevalence ranges from 2 percent to more than 10 percent, with a median of 4 percent; one American survey found a lifetime prevalence of 12.0 percent in men and 7.1 percent in women. Rates rise from childhood into adolescence, and few children with impairing conduct disorder receive any treatment. The disorder looks different in the two sexes. Boys more often fight, steal, vandalise and get into trouble for school discipline; girls more often lie, are truant, run away, and are drawn into prostitution; both use relational aggression (harming another person's friendships and reputation), but girls show much less physical aggression. Substance misuse is a common associated feature, particularly in adolescent girls. Suicidal thoughts, attempts and suicide are more frequent than expected; a ten-year Taiwanese follow-up found that adolescents with conduct disorder made more suicide attempts even after mood, anxiety and substance use disorders were allowed for. The functional costs (suspension and expulsion, injuries, early pregnancy, sexually transmitted infections, police contact) may carry into poorer health in midlife.

Exam trap

Conduct disorder needs three or more of 15 criteria in the past 12 months, at least one in the past 6 months, from four groups: aggression to people and animals (7 criteria), destruction of property (2), deceitfulness or theft (3) and serious violations of rules (3). Staying out at night and truancy count only if they began before 13. Fire setting with intent to cause serious damage is a conduct disorder criterion (destruction of property); pyromania is a separate disorder of fire setting for gratification or relief, and is not diagnosed when conduct disorder better explains the fires (Section 11.10). Conduct disorder can be diagnosed at 18 or over, but only if the criteria for antisocial personality disorder are not met.

Two specifiers that predict the future: age at onset and limited prosocial emotions

Of all the facts a clinician can gather about a child with conduct disorder, the age at which the problems began is one of the most informative, and DSM-5-TR builds it into the diagnosis as three subtypes. In the childhood-onset type the child showed at least one symptom of conduct disorder before the age of 10. In the adolescent-onset type no symptom appeared before 10. The unspecified onset type is used when the history is too thin to say. Either onset type can be mild, moderate or severe.

The two onset types describe two different kinds of child. Children with the childhood-onset type are usually boys, often had oppositional defiant disorder in early childhood, usually meet the full criteria before puberty, are more physically aggressive, have disturbed relationships with other children, and very often have ADHD or another neurodevelopmental difficulty as well. Their families more often show the risk factors described in Section 11.9.6, and they are much more likely to carry the disorder into adulthood, with criminal behaviour, substance use and antisocial personality disorder. Children with the adolescent-onset type have more ordinary peer relationships, even if they misbehave in company, and most of them, especially those with fewer and milder problems, settle into adequate social and working lives as adults. Daksh, whose fights and cruelty to animals began when he was eight, has the childhood-onset type. Falguni, who was an ordinary student until fourteen, has the adolescent-onset type. If you had to bet on which of them will be in serious trouble at thirty, the onset type tells you where to put your money; Section 11.9.5 explains why.

The second specifier is newer, and it is the most examined fact in this section. It grew out of a line of research led by the American psychologist Paul J. Frick, who later served as text coordinator of the DSM-5 work group on ADHD and the disruptive behaviour disorders and as section editor for this chapter of DSM-5-TR. Frick and his colleagues showed that within the large and varied group of children with serious conduct problems there is a smaller group marked by what they called callous-unemotional traits: a lack of guilt and empathy, a cold and shallow emotional life, and indifference to how well they do. These children resemble adult psychopaths in miniature (Section 11.5). DSM-5 turned the idea into the specifier with limited prosocial emotions. To qualify, the child must have shown at least two of the following four characteristics persistently over at least 12 months, in more than one relationship and setting:

  • Lack of remorse or guilt. The child does not feel bad or guilty after doing something wrong and shows a general lack of concern about the consequences of his actions. Remorse expressed only when caught or facing punishment does not count.
  • Callous lack of empathy. The child disregards and is unconcerned about other people's feelings; he is described as cold and uncaring, and seems more concerned about what his actions do to himself than about the harm they do to others.
  • Unconcerned about performance. The child does not care about doing poorly at school, at work or in other important activities, does not make the effort needed even when expectations are clear, and typically blames others for poor results.
  • Shallow or deficient affect. The child does not express feelings to others, or does so only in ways that seem shallow, insincere or superficial (emotions switched "on" and "off" quickly, or actions contradicting the emotion shown), or uses emotional displays for gain, to manipulate or intimidate.

Every word of the specifier guards against a mistake. "Persistently over at least 12 months" and "in multiple relationships and settings" rule out a child who is cold to one disliked teacher or unrepentant after one incident; the specifier describes the child's typical way of relating, not an occasional state. And because children with these traits rarely admit to them in an interview, the manual insists on multiple sources of information: besides the child, people who have known him over long periods and in different settings, such as parents, teachers, relatives and peers. Daksh qualifies. His father has never seen genuine remorse ("he says sorry only when he is caught, and then he smiles"), teachers describe him as cold, he does not care about failing his examinations, and his tears, when they come, appear and vanish as needed. That is four of the four characteristics, at home and at school, for well over a year.

Why does the specifier matter? The manual lists the reasons. Only a minority of children with conduct disorder qualify for it; in one study by Kahn, Frick and colleagues (2012), between 10 and 50 percent of youth with conduct disorder met the threshold, depending on the sample and on who was reporting. Those who do qualify are more likely to have the childhood-onset type and a severe rating, and more likely to show aggression that is planned for gain (instrumental aggression) rather than the hot-headed, reactive aggression of a frustrated child. Other features travel with them: thrill seeking, fearlessness and insensitivity to punishment. And their disorder is more likely to persist. Treatment research points the same way. In 2005 David J. Hawes and Mark R. Dadds followed 56 young boys (average age about six) through a 10-week behavioural parent-training programme. Boys with high callous-unemotional traits had more severe conduct problems to begin with and did worse at 6-month follow-up, even after the severity of their problems was allowed for; they responded less to time-out and showed less emotional reaction to it. Punishment, it seems, teaches these children less, which fits the fearlessness account of adult psychopathy that Section 11.5 develops from Lykken's work.

Clinical link

Assessing the specifier is a lesson in interviewing children. A twelve-year-old who has learned that adults want to hear "I am sorry" will say it; the question is whether he says it only when cornered, and whether his behaviour changes. Ask the parent, the class teacher and, where possible, the grandmother or the tuition teacher who sees the child in another setting: "When he hurts someone, what does he do next? Does he ever seem upset about it when no one is watching?" Record exactly who said what. A child who is distressed and guilty after his fights, however frequent, does not have limited prosocial emotions, and the distinction changes both the treatment plan and what you tell the family about the future.

Exam trap

In DSM-5-TR, "with limited prosocial emotions" is a specifier for conduct disorder only: not for antisocial personality disorder (whose Section III version has its own specifier, "with psychopathic features", Section 11.8.6), not for oppositional defiant disorder and not for intermittent explosive disorder. Its four characteristics are lack of remorse or guilt, callous lack of empathy, unconcern about performance, and shallow or deficient affect; two or more are needed, for 12 months, in more than one relationship or setting. Wrong options in the papers include "hyperactivity and inattention" (that is ADHD), "comorbid major depression" and "onset before age 5" (no such rule; the onset types turn on age 10). Childhood-onset type means at least one symptom before age 10. Two traps from other sources: some textbooks (Barlow and Durand among them) give the DSM-5 specifier as "with a callous-unemotional presentation", and the DSM-5 drafts proposed "with significant callous-unemotional traits", whereas the published manual says "with limited prosocial emotions"; and in ICD-11 the limited prosocial emotions qualifier can be applied to both conduct-dissocial disorder and oppositional defiant disorder (Section 11.9.8). A question that names DSM-5 or DSM-5-TR wants conduct disorder.

Two paths through adolescence: Moffitt's developmental taxonomy

Why should the age at onset matter so much? The most influential answer came from the American psychologist Terrie E. Moffitt (born 1955), in a 1993 paper in Psychological Review titled "Adolescence-limited and life-course-persistent antisocial behavior: a developmental taxonomy". Moffitt began with a puzzle that had troubled criminologists for decades. On the one hand, antisocial behaviour is remarkably continuous: the aggressive five-year-old is far more likely than his classmates to be the delinquent fifteen-year-old and the violent thirty-year-old. On the other hand, antisocial behaviour is remarkably common in adolescence: its prevalence rises almost tenfold during the teenage years and then falls away sharply in early adulthood, the famous age-crime curve. If antisocial behaviour is a stable trait, why do so many teenagers who were never troublesome as children suddenly start, and then just as suddenly stop?

Moffitt's answer was that the puzzle dissolves once you see that "delinquents" are two different groups of people who happen to look alike for a few years. The first is a small group whose antisocial behaviour begins in early childhood and persists at every stage of life, changing only its form: biting and hitting at four, shoplifting and truancy at ten, selling drugs and stealing cars at sixteen, robbery and fraud at twenty-two, and violence towards a partner and children at thirty. She called this life-course-persistent antisocial behaviour. Its roots, she argued, are neuropsychological problems present early in life (difficulties with verbal ability and with executive functions such as self-control, often with a difficult temperament and hyperactivity), which meet and are magnified by a criminogenic environment: parents who are themselves struggling, harsh or inconsistent discipline, poverty. A difficult infant provokes coercive parenting, which makes the child more difficult; each failure (at school, with peers) narrows the options for the next stage, so the problems accumulate and become, in her words, a pathological personality.

The second group is much larger. Its members had ordinary childhoods and begin offending around puberty. Moffitt called this adolescence-limited antisocial behaviour. Her explanation is one of the most quoted ideas in developmental psychology: the maturity gap. Modern teenagers are biologically adult years before society allows them adult privileges (money, independence, sex, respect), and the life-course-persistent youths around them seem to have exactly these things. So ordinary teenagers imitate them, a process Moffitt called social mimicry: smoking, drinking, vandalism and minor theft become ways of announcing that one is grown up. On this view adolescence-limited delinquency is normative and even adaptive, and it fades when adult roles (a job, a marriage, a home) offer the real thing. It fades, that is, unless the teenager is caught in one of the snares that can trap an adolescence-limited youth in antisocial behaviour: a criminal record, an addiction, dropping out of school, an early pregnancy. Figure 11.15 shows the two paths.

Key study: the Dunedin cohort and the two paths (1993 to 2008)

The test bed for Moffitt's theory was the Dunedin Multidisciplinary Health and Development Study, which follows 1,037 people born between April 1972 and March 1973 in the New Zealand city of Dunedin, a study founded by Phil Silva and assessed repeatedly from age 3 into middle age with remarkably few drop-outs. Moffitt and her husband and colleague Avshalom Caspi sorted the cohort's delinquents by the age at which their problems began. In 2001 they reported that the childhood-onset group had the predicted childhood risks (inadequate parenting, neurocognitive problems, difficult temperament and behaviour problems) and the adolescent-onset group did not; the male to female ratio was about 10 to 1 for childhood-onset delinquency but only about 1.5 to 1 for adolescent onset. At age 26 (2002) the childhood-onset men were the most impaired on every measure: psychopathic traits, mental health problems, substance dependence, financial and work problems, and drug-related and violent crime, including violence against women and children. The adolescent-onset men were less extreme, but they were not in the clear: they still showed impulsive traits, substance problems, financial difficulties and property offences. A third group, aggressive as children but not very delinquent as teenagers, became anxious, depressed, isolated low-level offenders. By age 32 (Candice Odgers and colleagues, 2008) four trajectories could be seen in both sexes, and life-course-persistent women, like the men, had serious problems with violence, mental and physical health and money. Criticism: the data show more than two groups, so "taxonomy" is a simplification; adolescence-limited offending is less benign and less limited than the name implies; and onset age is a marker of risk, not destiny.

The theory maps neatly onto the DSM onset types. The childhood-onset type of conduct disorder is, roughly, the clinical face of the life-course-persistent path, and the adolescent-onset type of the adolescence-limited path, which is why DSM-5-TR says the childhood-onset type predicts a worse prognosis. Moffitt herself sat on the DSM-5 work group for ADHD and the disruptive behaviour disorders. For the clinician the lesson is practical: a history taken carefully back to early childhood (school reports, an old teacher's memory, a grandmother's account of the toddler years) can matter more than the details of the latest incident.

Exam trap

Terrie Moffitt (1993) proposed life-course-persistent versus adolescence-limited antisocial behaviour, using the Dunedin (New Zealand) study; not Patterson (coercive family process), not Robins (follow-up of St Louis child guidance clinic children) and not Frick (callous-unemotional traits). Life-course-persistent: rare, begins in early childhood, rooted in neuropsychological deficits plus adverse environments, mostly male, persists. Adolescence-limited: common, begins at puberty, explained by the maturity gap and social mimicry, sex ratio nearer equal, usually desists unless caught in snares.

Why some children develop conduct disorder: genes, bodies, families and peers

No single cause produces conduct disorder. What the research shows instead is a set of risk factors, biological, psychological, family and social, which add up and interact, and which are both more common and more severe in children with the childhood-onset type. It helps to take them in the order in which they come into a child's life.

Genes. Conduct disorder runs in families, and twin and adoption studies show that this is partly genetic. Risk rises when a biological or adoptive parent or a sibling has conduct disorder (the adoptive parent is a reminder that families also pass on environments), and the disorder is more common in the children of biological parents with severe alcohol use disorder, depressive and bipolar disorders, schizophrenia, ADHD or conduct disorder. In 2002 Soo Hyun Rhee and Irwin Waldman pooled 51 twin and adoption studies of antisocial behaviour. In their best-fitting model, additive genetic influences accounted for 32 percent of the variation, non-additive genetic influences for 9 percent, the shared environment (what siblings in one family have in common) for 16 percent, and the non-shared environment (experiences unique to each child) for 43 percent. Genes and environment, in other words, each matter a great deal. Genetic influence appears stronger for the aggressive symptoms than for rule breaking, and a family history is particularly typical of the childhood-onset type. The best-known gene-environment interaction in psychiatry, between a variant of the MAOA gene and childhood maltreatment in the Dunedin cohort (Caspi and colleagues, 2002), is told as a Key study in Chapter 1 (Section 1.5) and recalled in Section 11.5.

Temperament, intelligence and the body. A difficult, undercontrolled temperament in infancy and lower than average intelligence, especially verbal intelligence, predict later conduct disorder; these are exactly the early neuropsychological problems at the root of Moffitt's life-course-persistent path. Two physiological findings are unusually robust. A slower resting heart rate has been reliably found in children with conduct disorder, and DSM-5-TR notes that this marker is not characteristic of any other mental disorder; reduced autonomic fear conditioning, especially low skin conductance responses, is also well documented. Brain-imaging studies consistently find structural and functional differences in the circuits that regulate and process emotion, particularly the connections between the ventral prefrontal cortex and the amygdala. None of these findings is diagnostic, and none can be used to identify an individual child. What they mean (a fearless, under-aroused child who learns little from punishment and seeks stimulation) is the subject of the fearlessness and stimulation-seeking theories of antisocial personality in Section 11.5.

Families and the coercive cycle. The family-level risk factors listed by DSM-5-TR read like a catalogue of childhood adversity: parental rejection and neglect, inconsistent child-rearing, harsh discipline, physical or sexual abuse, lack of supervision, early institutional living, frequent changes of caregiver, large family size, parental criminality and parental substance use disorders. The most detailed account of how parenting and a child's behaviour shape each other came from Gerald R. Patterson (1926 to 2016) and his colleagues at the Oregon Social Learning Center, who spent years observing aggressive children and their families at home, recording every exchange. In his 1982 book Coercive Family Process Patterson described what he called the coercive family process. Picture it in an ordinary Indian flat. A mother tells her eight-year-old to switch off the television and do his homework. He ignores her. She repeats the instruction more loudly; he whines and argues; she shouts; he screams, kicks the sofa and calls her names. At last, exhausted and embarrassed in front of the neighbours, she gives up and walks into the kitchen. Peace returns. Both have learned something. The boy has learned, by negative reinforcement, that escalating his aversive behaviour makes the demand go away. The mother has learned, also by negative reinforcement, that giving in makes the screaming stop. Each partner's behaviour is rewarded by the other, so the cycle repeats and escalates: the child learns that the louder he fights the more often he wins, and the parent learns that the only way to "win" is to withdraw all demands, or to explode in turn. Add poor monitoring of where the child is and what he is doing, parental depression and low involvement, and the pattern is maintained.

Patterson and his colleagues (Patterson, DeBaryshe and Ramsey, 1989) traced what happens next. A child trained in coercion at home carries it to school, where his aggression and defiance bring academic failure and rejection by ordinary classmates in middle childhood. Rejected children drift together, and by late childhood and adolescence the child has committed himself to a deviant peer group, which supplies the models, opportunities and approval for delinquency. That sequence, home to school to peers, is the early-starter path, and it is the social half of Moffitt's life-course-persistent theory. Its practical corollary is parent management training (Section 11.9.9), which teaches parents to break the cycle.

How the child reads the world. The American psychologist Kenneth A. Dodge studied what happens inside an aggressive child's head in the moment before he hits. In his social information-processing studies, aggressive children shown an ambiguous provocation (a classmate's ball strikes them from behind, a drink is spilled on their work) were much more likely than other children to conclude that it was done on purpose, and then to retaliate. This hostile attribution bias, which DSM-5-TR describes in its account of conduct disorder, makes aggression feel justified to the child: he believes he is only defending himself. Dodge and John Coie (1987) also distinguished reactive aggression, hot-headed retaliation to perceived threat, which goes with hostile attributions, from proactive aggression, cool and planned use of force to get something, which is closer to the instrumental aggression of children with limited prosocial emotions.

Peers, neighbourhood and culture. At the community level the risk factors are peer rejection, membership of a delinquent peer group, neighbourhood disadvantage and exposure to violence. Co-occurring ADHD and substance use make persistence more likely. Two cautions about culture complete the picture. Disruptive behaviour can be near normal in some settings, such as very threatening, high-crime areas or war zones, and the diagnosis should not be applied to a child whose behaviour is an adaptation to such a place. And DSM-5-TR warns that in youth from marginalised groups, anger and resistance in reaction to discrimination can be misdiagnosed as conduct disorder by an uninformed clinician; an Indian clinician should hold the same caution about children who face discrimination by caste, religion or class. On the other side of the ledger, first-generation immigrants and refugees often have fewer conduct problems than their peers, despite their adversities.

Exam trap

The coercive family process (negative reinforcement of child aggression and parental giving-in) is Gerald Patterson's (1982), not Moffitt's or Bandura's. The hostile attribution bias is Kenneth Dodge's. A slower resting heart rate is the most reliable physiological marker of conduct disorder and, according to DSM-5-TR, is not characteristic of any other mental disorder; but no biological finding is diagnostic. Rhee and Waldman's meta-analysis put genetic influences on antisocial behaviour at roughly 40 percent (32 additive plus 9 non-additive), with environment accounting for the rest.

Course and outcome: the road to antisocial personality

The single most hopeful fact about conduct disorder is in the manual: in a majority of individuals the disorder remits by adulthood. Many people with conduct disorder, especially those with the adolescent-onset type and those with few and milder symptoms, achieve adequate social and occupational adjustment as adults. The childhood-onset type predicts a worse prognosis, with a higher risk of criminal behaviour, persisting conduct problems and substance-related disorders; persistence is also more likely with limited prosocial emotions, co-occurring ADHD and substance use. The order in which symptoms appear tells part of the story. The first to emerge tend to be the less serious (lying, shoplifting) and the last the most severe (rape, theft while confronting a victim), and physically aggressive symptoms are commoner in childhood while nonaggressive ones become commoner in adolescence; a child who shows the most damaging behaviours early has the worst outlook.

The developmental chain that runs through this chapter therefore links three diagnoses, but only for a minority at each step. Oppositional defiant disorder often precedes the childhood-onset type of conduct disorder, yet most children with ODD never develop conduct disorder. Conduct disorder is the necessary precursor of antisocial personality disorder, yet most children with conduct disorder never become antisocial adults. Lee Robins, whose follow-up of the St Louis child guidance clinic children opens this chapter, summarised the paradox in 1978: adult antisocial behaviour virtually requires childhood antisocial behaviour, yet most antisocial children do not grow up to be antisocial adults. DSM-5-TR writes the first half of that finding into its rules. Antisocial personality disorder (Section 11.5) can be diagnosed only in a person at least 18 years old who has shown evidence of conduct disorder with onset before the age of 15, and the pervasive pattern of disregard for others' rights must have been present since age 15. When a person with conduct disorder reaches adulthood and the aggression, destruction, deceit and rule breaking continue at work and at home, including violence towards partners and children, the diagnosis moves from conduct disorder to antisocial personality disorder.

Antisocial personality is not the only adult outcome. People who had conduct disorder are at increased risk of mood disorders, anxiety disorders, posttraumatic stress disorder, impulse-control disorders, psychotic disorders, somatic symptom disorders and substance-related disorders, and of the social consequences of a disrupted youth: incomplete education, unstable work, early parenthood and poor health in midlife. The Dunedin data showed that this is true of women as well as men: by 32, women on the life-course-persistent path had serious problems with violence, mental and physical health and money, just as the men did, although fewer women follow that path. One distinction that the papers like to test does not belong to this chain at all. Conduct disorder in childhood leads, in those whom it follows into adulthood, to antisocial personality disorder, not to borderline personality disorder; how the two differ (a youthful pattern of rule violation and aggression versus an adult pattern of unstable identity, affect and relationships with fear of abandonment) is taught in Section 11.3.

Exam trap

The majority of children with conduct disorder remit by adulthood, and most do not develop antisocial personality disorder; the childhood-onset type and limited prosocial emotions predict persistence. The link runs one way in the rules: antisocial personality disorder needs evidence of conduct disorder with onset before 15 and an age of at least 18 (Section 11.5 sets out the age traps). Childhood conduct disorder leads to antisocial, not borderline, personality disorder; a mock-paper question that contrasts the two wants "borderline personality disorder is an adult pattern of identity and affective instability with fear of abandonment; conduct disorder is diagnosed in youth and centres on rule-violating, aggressive behaviour" (Section 11.3).

Conduct disorders in ICD-10 and ICD-11

ICD-10 classified the conduct disorders (F91) among the behavioural and emotional disorders with onset usually in childhood and adolescence (F90 to F98). Its general description is a repetitive and persistent pattern of dissocial, aggressive or defiant conduct, more severe than ordinary childish mischief or adolescent rebelliousness, lasting 6 months or longer. Its subtypes were organised not by age of onset but by the setting and the child's social integration, the idea DSM-III had used. Conduct disorder confined to the family context (F91.0) is antisocial behaviour shown entirely, or almost entirely, at home or towards members of the household. Unsocialised conduct disorder (F91.1) combines persistent dissocial or aggressive behaviour with a pervasive abnormality in the child's relationships with other children: he is not integrated into any peer group and is isolated or rejected. Socialised conduct disorder (F91.2) occurs in a child who is generally well integrated into a peer group, often a group of delinquent friends. Oppositional defiant disorder (F91.3) was placed inside the conduct disorders as their mildest form, characteristically seen in children below the age of about 9 or 10 years, with defiant, disobedient, provocative behaviour but without severe dissocial or aggressive acts. ICD-10 also offered a mixed disorder of conduct and emotions (F92), for conduct disorder combined with marked depression (F92.0, depressive conduct disorder) or other emotional symptoms such as anxiety (F92.8), and hyperkinetic conduct disorder (F90.1) for children who meet the criteria for both hyperkinetic disorder and conduct disorder.

ICD-11 places these conditions in a block called disruptive behaviour or dissocial disorders. Oppositional defiant disorder (6C90) is divided into ODD with chronic irritability-anger (6C90.0) and without it (6C90.1). The irritability qualifier is ICD-11's answer to the problem DSM-5 solved with disruptive mood dysregulation disorder: ICD-11 has no DMDD, and records the persistently irritable, angry child as ODD with chronic irritability-anger instead (Section 5.3.2). Conduct disorder becomes conduct-dissocial disorder (6C91), with a childhood-onset type (6C91.0, features before adolescence, for example before age 10) and an adolescent-onset type (6C91.1). And ICD-11 attaches its own limited prosocial emotions qualifier, describing what are sometimes called callous and unemotional traits, to both disorders: each ODD and conduct-dissocial code can be completed as "with limited prosocial emotions" or "with typical prosocial emotions" (6C91.00, for instance, is childhood-onset conduct-dissocial disorder with limited prosocial emotions). Table 11.34 sets the three systems side by side.

Table 11.34 Oppositional defiant and conduct disorders in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-10ICD-11
Where classifiedDisruptive, impulse-control and conduct disordersChildhood-onset behavioural and emotional disorders (F90 to F98)Disruptive behaviour or dissocial disorders (6C90 to 6C9Z)
Oppositional defiant disorderSeparate disorder; both ODD and CD may be diagnosedA subtype of conduct disorder (F91.3), mainly under 9 or 106C90, with or without chronic irritability-anger
Conduct disorder subtypesChildhood-onset (before 10), adolescent-onset, unspecified onsetConfined to the family context, unsocialised, socialisedConduct-dissocial disorder, childhood onset or adolescent onset

Combined categoriesBoth diagnoses given (for example, CD and ADHD)Hyperkinetic conduct disorder (F90.1); mixed disorders of conduct and emotions (F92)Both diagnoses given
Exam trap

The same code numbers mean different things in different books. DSM-5-TR uses the American ICD-10-CM codes, in which F91.1 is conduct disorder, childhood-onset type, F91.2 adolescent-onset type and F91.9 unspecified onset. In the WHO's ICD-10 (the version on most Indian case sheets), F91.1 is unsocialised and F91.2 socialised conduct disorder. In both, F91.3 is oppositional defiant disorder. ICD-10's subtypes rest on peer integration and setting; DSM's on age at onset. ICD-11's name is conduct-dissocial disorder, and its limited prosocial emotions qualifier also applies to ODD.

Treatment, and conduct disorder in Indian law

The treatments with the best evidence for oppositional defiant and conduct disorders are psychological, and they are taught in Book 2; here, what each is and where the evidence stands. Parent management training, which grew directly out of Patterson's work, teaches parents to give clear instructions, attend to and reward good behaviour, ignore minor provocations, use brief non-violent consequences such as time-out consistently, and supervise their children, so breaking the coercive cycle; it is the best-supported treatment for younger children with ODD and conduct problems (Book 2, Sections 4.10.4 and 10.3.5). For school-age children, problem-solving skills training, developed by Alan E. Kazdin, and anger-management programmes such as John E. Lochman's Coping Power teach the child to stop, read social situations without a hostile bias, generate alternatives and weigh consequences, directly targeting the processing errors Dodge described (Book 2, Section 10.3.4). For serious adolescent offenders, multisystemic therapy, developed by Scott W. Henggeler and colleagues, is an intensive home- and community-based treatment that works on the young person's family, school, peers and neighbourhood at once. Book 2, Section 10.4.4 brings these together for oppositional and conduct problems. Two general findings run through the research: the younger the child, the better the response, and children with limited prosocial emotions respond less well to standard programmes, especially to their punishment components. A third is a warning: grouping antisocial adolescents together for treatment can make them worse, because the group teaches and rewards deviance (Thomas Dishion and colleagues described such iatrogenic peer effects in 1999).

No medicine treats conduct disorder itself. The nearest thing to an approval is narrow: in the United Kingdom and Europe risperidone is licensed only for the short-term treatment (up to 6 weeks) of persistent aggression in conduct disorder in children from 5 years and adolescents with below-average intellectual functioning. The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guideline on antisocial behaviour and conduct disorders in children and young people (CG158, 2013) advises against using drugs for the routine management of behaviour problems. It allows the antipsychotic risperidone to be considered for the short-term management of severely aggressive behaviour, in young people with explosive anger and severe emotional dysregulation, when psychosocial interventions have failed, after a full assessment and baseline physical investigations, with regular monitoring of effect and side effects. Those side effects are the familiar ones of second-generation antipsychotics (Section 4.10): weight gain and metabolic changes, raised prolactin, sedation, and less often movement disorders, which is why the guideline asks for height and weight to be plotted on a growth chart. When ADHD coexists, as it so often does in the childhood-onset type, treating the ADHD according to its own guidance, with methylphenidate or atomoxetine (Section 3.8), often reduces the conduct problems as well. Mood stabilisers have been tried for aggression, but the evidence is weaker.

Indian context

In India, a person under 18 who is alleged or found to have committed an offence is a child in conflict with law under the Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015, which received presidential assent on 31 December 2015 and came into force on 15 January 2016. Such children are dealt with by a Juvenile Justice Board, not an ordinary criminal court, and the emphasis is on care, rehabilitation and reintegration. The Act's most debated change followed public anger over the December 2012 Delhi gang rape, in which one accused was a few months short of 18. Under Section 15, when a child who has completed 16 years is alleged to have committed a heinous offence (one carrying a minimum punishment of seven years' imprisonment or more), the Board must make a preliminary assessment of the child's mental and physical capacity to commit the offence, ability to understand its consequences and the circumstances in which it was committed, and it may take the assistance of experienced psychologists, psycho-social workers or other experts. On that assessment the case may be transferred to a Children's Court and the child tried as an adult. The Supreme Court has stressed that this is a delicate task needing expertise (Barun Chandra Thakur versus Master Bholu, 2022), and the National Commission for Protection of Child Rights issued guidelines for it in 2023. Clinical psychologists are therefore increasingly asked to assess adolescents with conduct problems for the courts (Book 2, Section 10.9).
