11.10 Impulse-Control Disorders: Intermittent Explosive Disorder, Pyromania and KleptomaniaMedium yield · 2 Qs

Most people know the moment. A car cuts across you on the flyover and for a second you want to lean on the horn and shout something unprintable. A box of sweets sits unattended on a colleague's desk and your hand drifts towards it. You stand by the bonfire on Lohri night and feel an odd pull to throw on one more stick, just to watch the flames leap. For nearly everyone the moment passes. Something like a brake engages, the urge is weighed against what it would cost, and the urge loses. This section is about people in whom, again and again, the urge wins: the man whose rage at a trivial slight ends in a broken door or a bleeding stranger, the young man who sets fires because fire itself thrills him, and the woman who steals lipsticks she could easily afford and then throws them away. Their acts look like crimes, and many of them end in court, yet the motive that drives ordinary crime, gain, is missing. What drives them instead is the question of this section.

Section 11.9 explained why DSM-5-TR places these three conditions in one chapter with oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder. Here you will go further: first what an impulse-control disorder is and how the idea grew out of the "monomanias" of nineteenth-century Paris, then each of the three disorders in turn, and finally the wider family of impulse disorders as ICD-10 and ICD-11 draw it, including the two conditions that have left the family (gambling and hair pulling) and the one that ICD-11 has added (compulsive sexual behaviour disorder).

The failure to resist: what an impulse-control disorder is

Begin with the word. An impulse is a sudden urge to act, felt before there has been time to think the act through. The tendency to act on such urges is impulsivity. A widely used definition, from the American psychiatrist F. Gerard Moeller, the psychologist Ernest S. Barratt and their colleagues in 2001, calls it "a predisposition toward rapid, unplanned reactions to internal or external stimuli without regard to the negative consequences of these reactions" to the person or to others. Everybody has some impulsivity, and a little of it is useful: a person who never acts without a week of deliberation misses every chance that comes and goes quickly. Too much of it runs through half of psychiatry. You have met it in attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3), in the reckless spending and sexual behaviour of mania (Chapter 5), in the binges of bulimia nervosa (Chapter 8) and in the substance use disorders (Chapter 10), and in this chapter it is one of the nine criteria of borderline personality disorder and one of the seven of antisocial personality disorder. Impulse control is even one of the four areas in which the general definition of a personality disorder can show itself (Section 11.1).

Impulsivity is not one thing. In 2001 the psychologists Stephen P. Whiteside and Donald R. Lynam analysed the many questionnaires that claim to measure it and found four separable tendencies: urgency (acting rashly when gripped by strong feeling), lack of premeditation (acting without thinking ahead), lack of perseverance (abandoning tasks that have become boring) and sensation seeking (craving excitement and novelty). The disorders in this section draw on different parts of that mixture. The man who explodes at a minor provocation shows urgency in its purest form. The fire setter who is fascinated by flames, fire engines and the crowd that gathers looks much more like a sensation seeker. The shoplifter who could easily have paid is caught by an urge she cannot explain even to herself.

What, then, turns an impulse into a disorder? DSM-IV (1994) gave the classic answer. The essential feature of an impulse-control disorder, it said, is "the failure to resist an impulse, drive, or temptation to perform an act that is harmful to the person or to others". For most of these disorders, it went on, the person feels an increasing sense of tension or arousal before the act, experiences pleasure, gratification or relief while committing it, and afterwards may or may not feel regret, self-reproach or guilt. That sequence, drawn in Figure 11.16, is the impulse cycle. Learn it stage by stage, because most examination questions about these disorders are really questions about where in the cycle a symptom belongs.

Look at what each stage contributes. The first is the urge, usually set off by something quite ordinary: boredom, a quarrel, a lonely evening, the sight of an open display counter. The tension builds, sometimes over minutes and sometimes over days, and the person may fight it. In the second stage the fight is lost. The third stage is the reason the cycle repeats. The act brings pleasure (a thrill, a "high") or relief (the tension drops away), and often both. In the language of learning theory, which Book 1 teaches, the pleasure is positive reinforcement and the removal of an unpleasant state of tension is negative reinforcement, and an act that is reinforced at once becomes more likely next time. The fourth stage, regret, comes too late to compete: a punishment that arrives hours later, or only when the police arrive months later, suppresses a behaviour far less than a reward that arrives in the same second. Then the tension begins to build again.

Notice also how the act feels while it is happening. At that moment it is wanted; the person is not fighting it but welcoming it. Clinicians say the act is ego-syntonic at the time it is done, even if the person condemns it bitterly afterwards. This is the sharpest difference between an impulsive act and a compulsion, the repetitive act of obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD; Chapter 6, Section 6.5.2). A compulsion is driven by dread, not desire. The woman who washes her hands forty times does so to undo the contamination she fears, and the washing relieves anxiety but never gives pleasure; she experiences it as alien and senseless, that is, ego-dystonic (Section 6.5.4). In the 1990s the American psychiatrist Eric Hollander and his colleagues proposed that the disorders of repetitive behaviour could be arranged along a single dimension. At the compulsive end people overestimate harm and act to avoid it; at the impulsive end they underestimate harm and act to obtain pleasure or arousal. OCD sits at one end. Pyromania, kleptomania, pathological gambling and explosive outbursts sit towards the other. Hair pulling, which can be pleasurable and yet is fought against, sits in between, a middle position that fits with its later move out of the impulse-control disorders, as the next subsection explains. Table 11.35 sets the two kinds of act side by side.

Table 11.35 Impulsive and compulsive acts compared.
FeatureImpulsive actCompulsive act
What sets it offAn urge, a craving or a flash of angerAn obsession, or a feeling that something is not right
What the person seeksPleasure, arousal or relief of tensionPrevention of harm, or relief of anxiety
How it feels during the actWanted at that moment (ego-syntonic)Unwanted and senseless (ego-dystonic)
Attitude to riskUnderestimates harm; risk seekingOverestimates harm; harm avoidant
AfterwardsOften regret, guilt or shameBrief relief, then the doubt returns
ExamplesStealing in kleptomania, fire setting in pyromania, explosive outburstsChecking, washing and ordering in OCD

One more point about the cycle: not every disorder in the family shows all of it. DSM-5-TR still requires the rise of tension before the act and the pleasure or relief that follows it for pyromania and kleptomania; those two criteria are the heart of both diagnoses. It does not require them for intermittent explosive disorder, which is defined by the outbursts themselves, although many people with that disorder describe their outbursts as attacks or spells in which pressure builds, bursts and drains away, leaving remorse behind. And when DSM-5 rehoused trichotillomania it dropped the tension and relief criteria altogether, because a substantial number of people who pull their hair do not experience that sequence (Section 6.6.5).

Exam trap

The impulse cycle is tension or arousal before the act, then pleasure, gratification or relief during it, then (sometimes) regret. Anxiety relief without pleasure, in response to an obsession, is the mark of a compulsion, not an impulse. "Ego-syntonic at the time of the act" describes the impulse-control disorders; "ego-dystonic" describes OCD. The rising tension and the relief are formal criteria for pyromania and kleptomania, not for intermittent explosive disorder, and DSM-5 removed them from trichotillomania.

From monomania to a chapter of its own: the history and the classification

The idea that a person might be driven to one particular harmful act while remaining sane in every other respect is a child of the early nineteenth century. Philippe Pinel had already described patients whose behaviour was violently disordered without any delusion, his manie sans délire (Section 11.5 tells that story). His pupil Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840) went further with the concept of monomania, a partial insanity confined to one sphere of the mind while the rest stays intact. Among its forms was an "instinctive" monomania, in which the will is driven to acts that reason condemns, and French physicians began to give each such impulse its own name, built from a Greek root and the word mania. In 1816 the Swiss physician André Matthey described thieves who stole things they did not need and called the condition klopémanie, stealing madness. In 1833 Charles Chrétien Henri Marc (1771 to 1841; some French sources date his death to January 1840), a Paris physician and a leading figure in French legal medicine, published a long medico-legal study of monomanie incendiaire (incendiary monomania) and used the word pyromanie, pyromania, from the Greek pyr, fire. And in 1838 Esquirol and Marc renamed Matthey's condition kleptomanie, from the Greek kleptein, to steal. The English word kleptomania comes from them.

These names were made in the courtroom as much as in the clinic, and that origin explains the suspicion that has followed them ever since. If a farmhand who set fire to a barn was suffering from incendiary monomania, he was ill rather than wicked, and could not justly be hanged. Critics answered that nobody can tell an impulse that could not be resisted from one that simply was not resisted, the same objection that later dogged the "irresistible impulse" test of insanity (Book 2, Section 10.9.3). The psychiatrist Jeffrey L. Geller and his colleagues, in a 1986 history of pyromania in America, traced how the diagnosis rose in the era of moral insanity in the 1840s, when it was called a monomania, an impulsive mania or an instinctive mania; faded once physicians began to argue that there could be diseased brains but not diseased minds, so that fire setting without a brain lesion became a moral failing to be punished; and returned with psychoanalysis between the 1920s and the 1950s, when it was explained as an irresistible impulse, an obsession or a "urethral-erotic" character trait. That last phrase goes back to Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), who in a footnote to Civilization and Its Discontents (1930) speculated that primitive man could tame fire only after he had renounced the pleasure of putting it out by urinating on it. The theory linked fire setting with bedwetting for decades; there has never been good evidence for it.

The great German classifier Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) gathered the impulsive acts into a single chapter on "impulsive insanity" (das impulsive Irresein) in the eighth edition of his textbook, published between 1909 and 1915. Its common forms were impulsive fire setting, stealing, poisoning, child killing and running up debts. His descriptions tell you as much about his society as about his patients. Impulsive fire setting, he wrote, was mostly a disorder of inexperienced young women, predisposed by low intelligence, a parent's alcoholism or a "hysterical" make-up, and placed in positions of dependence they were trying to escape; impulsive theft, especially from the new department stores, was a weakness of otherwise respectable women, worst during menstruation. The young Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969), later famous for his General Psychopathology, wrote his doctoral dissertation, published in 1909 as Heimweh und Verbrechen (Homesickness and Crime), on the link between homesickness and impulsive crimes, especially among young servant girls. Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) carried Kraepelin's category into his own textbook, which is one reason his name turns up as a distractor in questions about kleptomania. Notice the pattern. Until well into the twentieth century both pyromania and kleptomania were imagined as disorders of women, explained by the womb, menstruation and the menopause, and the department-store "lady thief" was a stock figure in the newspapers of Paris, London and New York, with sceptics complaining that kleptomania was simply an excuse for wealthy women caught shoplifting. Today kleptomania is still diagnosed more often in women, but fire setting is far commoner in men.

The American manuals took a long time to settle the family. DSM-I (1952) mentioned kleptomania only as a supplementary term, not a formal diagnosis, and DSM-II (1968) left it out altogether. The explosive outbursts lived for two editions among the personality disorders: in DSM-I as the "aggressive type" of passive-aggressive personality, and in DSM-II as "explosive personality". DSM-III (1980) created a group called "disorders of impulse control not elsewhere classified", whose members included pathological gambling, kleptomania, pyromania and intermittent explosive disorder, the first time the explosive outbursts had been a disorder in their own right rather than a type of personality. The phrase "not elsewhere classified" mattered: problems of impulse control that belonged to another group (substance use, paraphilias, binge eating, the impulsivity of a personality disorder) stayed where they were, and the new group took only the orphans. DSM-III-R (1987) added trichotillomania, and DSM-IV (1994) kept the five disorders plus "impulse-control disorder not otherwise specified".

DSM-5 (2013) broke the group up. Pathological gambling, renamed gambling disorder, moved to the chapter on substance-related and addictive disorders, because gambling activates the brain's reward systems much as drugs do and produces symptoms like those of the substance use disorders (Chapter 10, Section 10.1.7). Trichotillomania moved to the new obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, because it runs in families with OCD, co-occurs with it and responds to some of the same treatments (Chapter 6, Section 6.6.5). The three that remained, intermittent explosive disorder, pyromania and kleptomania, joined oppositional defiant disorder, conduct disorder and antisocial personality disorder (which is listed in both places) in a new chapter, "Disruptive, Impulse-Control, and Conduct Disorders". Section 11.9 explains why. In one sentence: all the disorders in that chapter are failures of emotional and behavioural self-control that violate the rights of others or bring the person into conflict with society's norms. Within it, DSM-5-TR places intermittent explosive disorder at the "emotion" end (poorly controlled anger), conduct disorder at the "behaviour" end, and describes pyromania and kleptomania as poor control over one specific behaviour, fire setting or stealing, that relieves inner tension. Even the codes tell the story. DSM-5-TR prints ICD-10-CM codes, and intermittent explosive disorder (F63.81), pyromania (F63.1) and kleptomania (F63.2) still carry numbers from ICD-10's block of habit and impulse disorders, F63, which Section 11.10.8 describes.

Exam trap

Who named what: Matthey, klopémanie (1816); Marc, pyromanie (1833); Esquirol and Marc, kleptomanie (1838); Esquirol, monomania; Kraepelin, impulsive insanity (eighth edition, 1909 to 1915). The DSM-IV "impulse-control disorders not elsewhere classified" were five: intermittent explosive disorder, kleptomania, pyromania, pathological gambling and trichotillomania. In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR only the first three remain impulse-control disorders, in the chapter "Disruptive, Impulse-Control, and Conduct Disorders"; gambling is an addictive disorder and trichotillomania an obsessive-compulsive and related disorder. An option that still lists gambling or hair pulling as a DSM-5 impulse-control disorder is out of date.

Intermittent explosive disorder: the clinical picture

Uday is 29 and works as a software tester in Hyderabad. His wife calls him "the gentlest man in the world, ninety-nine percent of the time". The other one percent has cost him dearly. In the past four months he has screamed abuse at a delivery boy who rang the bell twice, hurled his phone onto the sofa when a video call froze, and shouted at his mother until she wept because she had put too much salt in the dal. These verbal storms come two or three times a week; each blows up in seconds and blows out within a quarter of an hour. Twice in the past year the storm was worse. After an argument about money he kicked in the panel of a cupboard door, and last month, when an auto-rickshaw driver refused to go by the meter, he grabbed the man by the collar and split his lip. Afterwards Uday is ashamed. "It is as if something switches on," he says. "I feel it boiling up, and then I am watching myself do it. Afterwards I am drained, and then I feel sick with myself." He is sober when it happens, he is not depressed, he has never had a head injury, and he gains nothing from the outbursts except a cupboard to repair, a police complaint and a frightened family.

Uday has intermittent explosive disorder (IED): recurrent behavioural outbursts that represent a failure to control aggressive impulses. The word intermittent is chosen with care. Between outbursts the person's mood and behaviour may be quite unremarkable, and that is one of the things that separates IED from the conditions it resembles. DSM-5-TR's criteria can be learned in three groups.

What the outbursts must look like (Criterion A). There are two routes, and either is enough (Figure 11.17). The first is frequent and relatively mild: verbal aggression (temper tantrums, tirades, verbal arguments or fights) or physical aggression towards property, animals or other people that causes no damage and no injury, occurring twice a week on average for 3 months. Uday's shouting meets this route. The second is infrequent and severe: three outbursts within 12 months that damage or destroy property or that injure an animal or a person. Uday's cupboard and the driver's lip make two; he does not yet meet the second route, and does not need to. DSM-5-TR notes that many people show both patterns, with milder outbursts in between occasional destructive ones. The value of the damaged object does not matter; smashing a cheap plastic chair counts.

What makes the outbursts disordered (Criteria B, C and D). The aggression must be grossly out of proportion to the provocation or to any stressor that set it off (Criterion B): a salty dal, a doorbell rung twice. It must not be premeditated, that is, it is impulsive or anger-based, and it must not be committed to achieve some tangible objective such as money, power or intimidation (Criterion C). And it must cause marked distress, impair the person's work or relationships, or bring financial or legal consequences (Criterion D). Criterion C is the one that does the most work, because it separates two kinds of aggression that psychologists have long distinguished. The first, reactive aggression, also called hostile or affective aggression, is hot: a furious response to a perceived provocation or threat. The second, proactive aggression, also called instrumental aggression, is cold: aggression used as a tool to obtain something. The developmental psychologist Kenneth A. Dodge (Section 11.9), who studied both in aggressive children in the 1980s, showed that reactively aggressive children tend to read hostile intent into ambiguous situations (a push in the queue is taken as an attack), the hostile attribution bias. IED is a disorder of reactive aggression. The bully who threatens a classmate for his lunch money is showing proactive aggression, and that belongs with conduct disorder (Section 11.9).

Who can receive the diagnosis (Criteria E and F). The person must be at least 6 years old, or at an equivalent developmental level, because tantrums are normal in small children (Criterion E). The outbursts must not be better explained by another mental disorder (major depressive disorder, bipolar disorder, disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, a psychotic disorder, antisocial or borderline personality disorder), must not be the result of a medical condition such as head trauma or Alzheimer's disease, and must not be the effect of a drug or medication (Criterion F). In children and adolescents aged 6 to 18, aggression that is part of an adjustment disorder does not count. A note to the criteria adds that IED can be diagnosed in addition to ADHD, conduct disorder, oppositional defiant disorder or autism spectrum disorder when the outbursts go beyond what is usual in those disorders and need attention in their own right.

DSM-5-TR describes the typical outburst exactly as Uday experiences it: a rapid onset with little or no warning, a duration of less than 30 minutes, and a trigger that is usually a minor provocation by someone close, a spouse, a parent, a colleague. Depressive, anxiety and substance use disorders often accompany IED, but they usually begin later than the outbursts. These criteria are new. DSM-IV demanded "several discrete episodes" of failure to resist aggressive impulses that resulted in serious assault or destruction of property, so a man who only shouted, however often and however destructively for his family, could not qualify. DSM-5 widened the diagnosis to include verbal aggression and non-damaging physical aggression, and added the frequency thresholds. The changes followed research criteria first published in 1998, and later revised, by the American psychiatrist Emil F. Coccaro and his colleagues, which, Coccaro argued in 2012, identified people with higher levels of aggression, impulsivity, family history of aggression and biological markers of aggression better than the DSM-IV criteria did. Barlow and Durand note the controversy that has always surrounded the diagnosis: critics fear that a general category for aggressive behaviour could be offered as an insanity defence for any violent crime.

How common is it? Far commoner than was believed when DSM-III introduced it as a rare disorder. In the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, a household survey of 9,282 American adults carried out between 2001 and 2003, Ronald C. Kessler, Coccaro and their colleagues (2006) found that 7.3 percent met DSM-IV criteria at some point in life and 3.9 percent in the past year. The average person with IED had had 43 attacks in a lifetime, causing about 1,359 dollars of property damage, and there were 180 injuries for every 100 cases; the average age at onset was 14; and although most had at some time been treated for some emotional or substance problem, only 28.8 percent had ever been treated for their anger. Surveys that define the disorder differently give different figures, and DSM-5-TR gives a 1-year prevalence of about 2.6 percent in the United States and a lifetime prevalence of 4.0 percent. The disorder is commoner in people younger than about 35 to 40 than in those over 50, and in people with a high-school education or less; some studies find it commoner in men and boys and others find no difference. Lower rates in parts of Asia and the Middle East, says DSM-5-TR, may mean that such behaviour is less often present, or simply that it is less often reported when people are asked; a clinician anywhere, India included, has to ask about outbursts directly, because patients and families tend to call them temper rather than illness.

The outbursts usually begin in late childhood or adolescence and rarely start for the first time after 40. The course is chronic and persistent over many years, sometimes episodic, with stretches of frequent outbursts. The costs are what you would expect: lost friends, broken marriages, demotion and dismissal, the price of what was smashed, civil suits and criminal charges. The risk of suicide is real too. In one study of 1,460 research volunteers, people with IED who also had posttraumatic stress disorder had a lifetime rate of suicide attempts of 41 percent.

Because angry outbursts occur in so many conditions, the diagnosis is largely a matter of exclusion, and Table 11.36 sets out the look-alikes. Two are worth spelling out. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (Chapter 5, Section 5.3.2) also has severe temper outbursts, but between them the child's mood is persistently irritable or angry most of the day, nearly every day; it must begin before age 10 and cannot be diagnosed for the first time after 18; and the two diagnoses are never given together. Borderline and antisocial personality disorders both bring outbursts, but as one part of a much wider pattern, and DSM-5-TR notes that the level of impulsive aggression in them is lower than in IED. Popular culture has a name for one look-alike, amok, the Malay pattern of brooding after an insult followed by a violent rampage, which Chapter 1 (Section 1.10.5) describes among the cultural syndromes.

Table 11.36 Intermittent explosive disorder and its look-alikes.
ConditionWhat separates it from IED
Disruptive mood dysregulation disorderPersistent irritable or angry mood between outbursts; onset before 10; never diagnosed together with IED
Oppositional defiant disorderChronic anger and defiance, mainly towards authority; tantrums and arguments rather than assaults
Conduct disorder, antisocial personality disorderAggression often planned and used to get something; a wide pattern of rule breaking
Borderline personality disorderAnger is one part of unstable mood, self-image and relationships; impulsive aggression is less severe
Depressive, bipolar or psychotic episodeOutbursts occur only during the episode
Substance intoxication or withdrawalOutbursts almost always when drunk, high or withdrawing
Medical causesHead injury, complex partial epilepsy, delirium, dementia, or personality change due to another medical condition, aggressive type
Adjustment disorder (ages 6 to 18)Aggression arises in response to a stressor and is part of the adjustment reaction
Exam trap

IED aggression can be verbal or physical; an option saying "only verbal, never physical" (or the reverse) is wrong. The two routes are twice a week on average for 3 months (no damage or injury) or three damaging or injurious outbursts in 12 months. The minimum age is 6, so "IED is diagnosed only in adults" is wrong, and no comorbid mood disorder is required. The outbursts are not premeditated and gain the person nothing tangible; planned, instrumental aggression points to conduct disorder or antisocial personality disorder. The specifier "with limited prosocial emotions" belongs to conduct disorder only, never to IED (Section 11.9).

Intermittent explosive disorder: causes and treatment

Why does the brake fail? The oldest and best-supported biological answer concerns serotonin, and it began with a study of men in prison.

Key study: Linnoila and the impulsive offenders (1983)

A joint American and Finnish team led by Markku Linnoila and Matti Virkkunen asked whether violent men differ in their brain chemistry, and whether it matters how they were violent. They studied 36 violent offenders and divided them by the nature of their crimes: some had acted impulsively, in a sudden rage, and others had premeditated their acts. In each man they measured, by lumbar puncture, the monoamines and their breakdown products in the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), including 5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid (5-HIAA), the main breakdown product of serotonin and an index of how much serotonin the brain is turning over. The impulsive offenders had relatively low CSF 5-HIAA; the premeditating offenders did not. The other monoamines did not differ between the groups, and the lowest levels of all were in impulsive offenders who had also attempted suicide. The authors concluded that low CSF 5-HIAA may be a marker of impulsivity rather than of violence as such. The sample was small and entirely male, and later research found the link between serotonin and aggression weaker and more complicated than the early studies suggested, but the distinction it drew, between hot impulsive aggression and cold planned aggression, became the backbone of the modern IED diagnosis.

The same idea of a failed brake shows up in brain imaging. DSM-5-TR summarises the findings: serotonergic abnormalities, both globally and in particular regions of the limbic system (the anterior cingulate cortex) and the orbitofrontal cortex; greater responses of the amygdala to angry faces during functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) in people with IED than in healthy people; and reduced volume of grey matter in several frontolimbic regions, which correlates with measures of aggression, although these differences are not always found. Put simply, the amygdala raises the alarm too loudly at signs of threat or insult, and the orbitofrontal and cingulate cortex, which normally weigh the alarm against its social consequences, damp it too weakly. You will recognise the pattern from Phineas Gage, whose damaged orbitofrontal cortex turned a reliable foreman into a man given to profanity and outbursts (the Physiological book tells his story). The genes contribute: first-degree relatives of people with IED are at increased risk, and twin studies show a substantial genetic influence on impulsive aggression. So does experience. People who suffered physical and emotional trauma in the first 20 years of life are at increased risk, and in some refugee populations long displacement from home and separation from family are risk factors too. Psychologically, people with IED share the hostile attribution bias of reactively aggressive children: an ambiguous remark or an accidental jostle is read as a deliberate insult, and the anger that follows feels to them entirely justified.

Treatment combines psychological and drug approaches, although for a long time there were almost no controlled trials of either. The first randomised trial of psychotherapy was reported in 2008 by Michael S. McCloskey, Coccaro and their colleagues in Chicago. They assigned 45 adults with IED to 12 weeks of group cognitive-behavioural therapy (CBT), individual CBT, or a waiting list. The therapy, adapted from an anger-management programme by the psychologist Jerry L. Deffenbacher, combined relaxation training, cognitive restructuring of hostile thoughts and rehearsal of coping skills. Group and individual therapy did about equally well, and both reduced aggression, anger, hostile thinking and depressive symptoms and improved anger control, with large effects that were maintained at the 3-month follow-up. These programmes descend from the anger-control version of stress inoculation training developed by Raymond W. Novaco in the 1970s (Book 2, Section 5.5.2 explains stress inoculation). In practice the person learns to notice the early bodily signs of rising anger, to identify the triggers and the hostile interpretations that feed it, to leave the situation before the explosion, to relax, and to rehearse other responses.

On the drug side, the serotonin story led naturally to the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs). In a double-blind trial in which 100 people with IED were randomly given fluoxetine or a placebo (Coccaro, Lee and Kavoussi, 2009), fluoxetine produced a sustained reduction in aggression and irritability that was visible by the second week, and it did so whether or not the person was depressed or anxious, so it was not working simply as an antidepressant. Yet only 46 percent of those given fluoxetine reached full or partial remission of their outbursts. Smaller trials have tested anticonvulsant mood stabilisers with mixed results, and no medicine is approved specifically for IED. Two practical points complete the picture: alcohol and other drugs that release inhibition make outbursts more likely and should be cut down or stopped, and where children or a partner are at risk, safety comes before any treatment plan.

Clinical link

Many people who seek help for IED are sent by a frightened family or by a court, and many describe their outbursts in the passive voice: "it just happened", "something switched on". The diagnosis explains a pattern, but it does not remove responsibility, and a clinician who treats IED must still ask about violence at home, about children who witness it, and about weapons. Criterion C is also a useful clinical check: if the "explosions" happen only at home, never in front of the boss, and reliably get the person his way, they are not as impulsive as they look.

Exam trap

Low CSF 5-HIAA (the serotonin metabolite) was found in impulsive violent offenders but not in premeditating ones (Linnoila and colleagues, 1983): it marks impulsivity, not violence in general. The brain picture of IED is an over-reactive amygdala with weak orbitofrontal and anterior cingulate control. Fluoxetine reduces impulsive aggression, but fewer than half of patients remit; CBT for anger has the best psychological evidence.

Pyromania: fire setting for its own sake

Mukund is 20, a second-year polytechnic student in Nagpur. His father has brought him to the outpatient department because the police have found him, for the third time, standing beside a burning heap of tyres and scrap behind a warehouse. He did not run; he was watching. Over two years, he admits, he has set perhaps fifteen fires: piles of dry leaves and rubbish, an abandoned shed, the dry grass along the railway line in May. He plans them, carrying a small bottle of kerosene and a lighter in his bag for days and waiting for an evening when the wind is right. For hours beforehand he feels "tight and restless, like the night before a result". When the flames catch, the tightness melts into an excitement he struggles to describe: "It is the most alive I ever feel." He stays to watch the fire engines arrive, knows which fire station will answer a call from each part of the city, and has twice applied to train as a fireman. He has never set a fire for money, to hurt anyone or to get back at anyone; he feels guilty afterwards about the shed, and frightened that one day someone will be inside. He was sober each time, he has never had a manic episode, and apart from the fires he does not lie, steal or fight, and he was not a troublesome child.

Mukund has pyromania. The criteria fall into three groups. First, the act and the feelings around it: deliberate and purposeful fire setting on more than one occasion (Criterion A); tension or affective arousal before the act (Criterion B); fascination with, interest in, curiosity about or attraction to fire and its situational contexts, its paraphernalia, uses and consequences (Criterion C); and pleasure, gratification or relief when setting fires or when witnessing or taking part in their aftermath (Criterion D). Second, what the fire must not be for (Criterion E): monetary gain, the expression of a sociopolitical ideology, the concealment of a crime, the expression of anger or vengeance, the improvement of one's living circumstances (a tenant who burns a flat to be rehoused), a response to a delusion or a hallucination, or the result of impaired judgement in major neurocognitive disorder, intellectual disability or intoxication. Third, a rule of precedence (Criterion F): the fire setting must not be better explained by conduct disorder, a manic episode or antisocial personality disorder. Read the criteria as a funnel. A and E together say that the fire must be deliberate yet serve no outside purpose; B, C and D say what it serves instead, an inner state of tension, fascination and release.

DSM-5-TR adds associated features that make the picture vivid. People with pyromania are often regular "watchers" at fires in their neighbourhood, may set off false alarms, and take pleasure in the institutions, equipment and personnel of firefighting; some spend time at the fire station, set fires in order to be associated with the fire service, or even become firefighters. They may make considerable advance preparation before starting a fire, and they may be indifferent to the damage and danger to life, or take satisfaction from the destruction. That point about preparation matters for the examination. An impulse-control disorder is not defined by how quickly the act follows the urge; it is defined by the person's failure to resist the urge at all. Mukund's urge is impulsive, but his fires are planned. ICD-11 describes pyromania (6C70) in almost the same words, as a recurrent failure to control strong impulses to set fires in the absence of an apparent motive, with mounting tension, persistent fascination with fire and pleasure, excitement, relief or gratification during and after the act. Its list of motives that rule the diagnosis out adds one that DSM-5-TR mentions only in its differential diagnosis: setting a fire to attract attention or recognition, as in the person who lights a fire in order to "discover" it and be the hero who raised the alarm.

How common is pyromania? Probably very rare, and this is the most important fact about it. Fire setting itself is not rare. In one population sample, DSM-5-TR reports, the lifetime prevalence of fire-setting behaviour was 1.0 to 1.1 percent, 1.7 percent in men and 0.4 percent in women. But most fire setters do not have pyromania, as one careful Finnish study showed.

Key study: Looking for pyromania (Lindberg and colleagues, 2005)

Nina Lindberg, Virkkunen and their colleagues at the University of Helsinki reviewed the records and forensic psychiatric reports of all 90 men with repeated arson who had been referred for pretrial psychiatric assessment at the Helsinki University Hospital Department of Forensic Psychiatry between 1973 and 1993. If pyromania exists anywhere, it should exist among repeat arsonists. The commonest diagnoses were personality disorders, psychoses and intellectual disability, often with alcoholism, and 68 percent had been intoxicated during the index fire. Men with psychosis or intellectual disability tended to be "pure" arsonists, guilty of nothing but fires, whereas those with personality disorders committed many kinds of crime and arson was just one of them. Only three of the 90 (3.3 percent) met the criteria for pyromania. The authors concluded that, as defined, pyromania is an extremely rare phenomenon, and they questioned whether intoxication should automatically exclude it, since so many repeat fire setters drink.

The figures from clinics are similar: in an American study of adults hospitalised for psychiatric reasons, 3.4 percent met the full criteria for current pyromania. And in the largest clinical description of people with the disorder, Jon E. Grant and Suck Won Kim (2007) at the University of Minnesota interviewed 21 adults and adolescents with lifetime pyromania. Their average age at onset was 18.1 years; 18 of the 21 reported urges to set fires; on average they set a fire every 5.9 weeks; much of their fire setting did not reach the legal threshold of arson; 61.9 percent had a current mood disorder and 47.6 percent another impulse-control disorder. Ten of the 21 were women, which fits DSM-5-TR's caution: fire setting is commoner in men, but whether this holds for pyromania itself is unknown. DSM-5-TR suggests late adolescence as the typical age at onset; incidents come and go episodically; and the long-term course is unknown. The comorbid conditions are substance use disorders, gambling disorder, depressive and bipolar disorders and other disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders.

The commonest fire setters of all are children and adolescents: in the United States more than 40 percent of those arrested for arson are younger than 18. Yet pyromania in childhood appears to be rare. Juvenile fire setting usually belongs to conduct disorder, ADHD or an adjustment disorder, or is simply developmental experimentation with matches and lighters. Compare Mukund with Wasim, a 14-year-old in Surat who set fire to a neighbour's scooter after the neighbour complained about him to his father. Wasim also fights at school, steals from shops, lies his way out of trouble and stays out all night. His fire was revenge, and it is one item in a broad pattern of violating other people's rights. It counts towards conduct disorder, whose criterion of deliberate fire setting with the intention of causing serious damage is taught in Section 11.9, and Criterion F forbids a separate diagnosis of pyromania. Figure 11.18 shows the sequence of questions a clinician asks about any fire setter.

What causes pyromania is almost unknown, because so few people have it that research has had to study fire setters in general. The research that Barlow and Durand review has examined family histories of fire setting and the comorbid antisocial personality disorder and alcoholism of arsonists. The serotonin story reappears here too. In 1987 Virkkunen, Linnoila and their colleagues compared 20 imprisoned arsonists with 20 habitually violent offenders and 10 healthy volunteers and found significantly lower CSF levels of 5-HIAA, and of a breakdown product of noradrenaline, in the arsonists; and the lower an arsonist's blood sugar fell in a glucose tolerance test, the more severe his fire setting had been. The authors read both findings as signs of poor impulse control. Treatment is generally cognitive-behavioural: the person learns to recognise the signals that start the urge and practises ways of coping with it without fire. For children, programmes of fire-safety education and work with the family are the usual first step. None of these has been tested in controlled trials of people with pyromania itself.

Clinical link

In 1963 the psychiatrist John M. Macdonald, in a paper called "The threat to kill", mentioned in passing that childhood fire setting, cruelty to animals and bedwetting turned up in the histories of some of the patients he had studied who had threatened to kill. The "Macdonald triad" became a fixture of crime fiction as a predictor of adult violence, but research has not supported it, and Macdonald's own later follow-up did not find the association he had suggested. A child who sets fires deserves a careful assessment, for conduct disorder, ADHD, abuse or neglect, and a family in crisis, not a label as a future murderer.

Exam trap

The hallmark difference: pyromania is deliberate, purposeful fire setting, often prepared in advance, for the gratification or relief that fire itself brings; intermittent explosive disorder is impulsive aggression grossly out of proportion to the trigger. Fire set for money, revenge, ideology, concealment of a crime, a better home, or (in ICD-11) attention is not pyromania; nor is fire set in psychosis, intoxication, dementia or intellectual disability. A teenager's fire setting within a pattern of antisocial behaviour counts towards conduct disorder (Section 11.9). Pyromania has no minimum or maximum age, but it is very rare in childhood and typically begins in late adolescence; older books say it is "mostly male", while DSM-5-TR says fire setting is commoner in men and the sex ratio of pyromania itself is unknown.

Kleptomania: stealing what one does not need

Abhay is 38, a respected lecturer in commerce at a college in Kochi, married and well paid. For about eight years he has been stealing from supermarkets and stationery shops: a packet of gel pens, a keychain, a tube of lip balm, a pack of batteries. He never takes anything expensive and never anything he needs. Most of it ends up in a locked drawer in his staffroom cupboard, and some he drops in a bin on the way home. "When I see it on the shelf, something starts," he says. "My heart races, my palms sweat, I tell myself no, and the more I say no the worse it gets. When it is in my pocket there is a rush, a release. Then on the road home I feel disgusted with myself." He has tried avoiding the shops, dropping money into a shop's charity box to make up for what he took, and once creeping back to return the things to the shelf. Last month a store manager caught him with a bar of chocolate worth forty rupees in his pocket and three thousand rupees in his wallet. He was let off with a warning, and he has not told his wife.

Abhay has kleptomania. He is unusual in one way, because most people seen in clinics with the disorder are women, and typical in every other. The DSM-5-TR criteria are short. There must be a recurrent failure to resist impulses to steal objects that are not needed for personal use or for their monetary value (Criterion A); an increasing sense of tension immediately before the theft (Criterion B); and pleasure, gratification or relief at the time of committing it (Criterion C). The stealing must not be done to express anger or vengeance, and not in response to a delusion or a hallucination (Criterion D), and it must not be better explained by conduct disorder, a manic episode or antisocial personality disorder (Criterion E). Notice how closely B and C copy the impulse cycle of Figure 11.16, and how Criterion A is worded. It does not say the objects are worthless. It says they are not stolen because they are needed or valuable; the value is simply not the point.

The clinical features follow from that. The objects are usually of little value to the person, who could have afforded them, and they are often given away or thrown away, occasionally hoarded or secretly returned. People with kleptomania generally avoid stealing when arrest is immediately likely (they do not steal in full view of a police officer), but they do not usually preplan the thefts or fully weigh the chance of being caught, and they steal alone, without help from or collaboration with anyone else. They know that the act is wrong and senseless, they usually try to resist it, and they frequently fear arrest and feel depressed or guilty afterwards. ICD-11 describes kleptomania (6C71) in the same terms, as a recurrent failure to control strong impulses to steal objects in the absence of an apparent motive, with mounting tension beforehand and pleasure, excitement, relief or gratification during and immediately after the theft, and it adds a coding note that the diagnosis is not given separately when stealing occurs within conduct-dissocial disorder or a manic episode.

Kleptomania is rare. DSM-5-TR puts its prevalence in the general population of the United States at about 0.3 to 0.6 percent, and among people arrested for shoplifting in the United States and Canada at about 4 to 24 percent; women outnumber men by about three to one. Studies of psychiatric patients find it more often than you might expect: in the survey of 204 consecutively admitted adult inpatients by Grant and his colleagues (2005), 7.8 percent had current kleptomania, the second commonest impulse problem after compulsive buying. The age at onset varies, but the disorder often begins in adolescence, and it can begin in childhood, in adulthood or, rarely, late in life. Three courses have been described: sporadic, with brief episodes and long remissions; episodic, with long periods of stealing and periods of remission; and chronic, with some fluctuation. The disorder may continue for years despite repeated convictions. Its comorbid companions are compulsive buying, depressive and bipolar disorders (especially major depression), anxiety disorders, eating disorders (particularly bulimia nervosa), personality disorders, substance use disorders (especially alcohol use disorder) and other impulse-control disorders. First-degree relatives have higher rates of alcohol use disorder than the general population, and kleptomania is associated with an increased risk of suicide attempts.

The main differential diagnosis is ordinary theft, and Table 11.37 sets them apart. Ordinary theft, whether planned or impulsive, is deliberate and motivated by the usefulness or the money value of the object; some adolescents also steal on a dare, as an act of rebellion or as a rite of passage. Kleptomania is rare, whereas shoplifting is relatively common, so most shoplifters do not have it. In malingering, a person caught stealing may claim the symptoms of kleptomania to escape prosecution. In antisocial personality disorder and conduct disorder, the theft is part of a general pattern of antisocial behaviour. And stealing that occurs during a manic episode, in response to delusions or hallucinations, or in a major neurocognitive disorder belongs to that condition; when respectable people begin to shoplift for the first time in middle or late life, a clinician should think of a dementia (Chapter 10).

Table 11.37 Kleptomania and ordinary theft.
FeatureKleptomaniaOrdinary theft or shoplifting
Why the object is takenNot for use or money; to satisfy an urgeFor its use or its money value; sometimes on a dare
What happens to itDiscarded, given away, hoarded or returnedUsed, kept or sold
Feelings around the actTension before, pleasure or relief during, guilt afterNo characteristic tension and release
Planning and companyUsually unplanned; always aloneOften planned; may be done with others
Attitude to the actKnows it is wrong and senseless; tries to resistAccepts it as worth the risk
How commonRare (about 0.3 to 0.6 percent)Relatively common

The name itself is a favourite examination question. Section 11.10.2 told its history; the box that follows turns it into the answer, and teaches the distractors that the question sets beside it.

Exam trap

"The term kleptomania was given by" Marc (Charles Chrétien Henri Marc, with Jean-Étienne Esquirol, 1838), the keyed and correct answer; Matthey's earlier word was klopémanie (1816). The distractors are famous for other words. Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) coined schizophrenia (1908 to 1911) and the terms ambivalence and autism (Chapter 4); he did not name kleptomania, and simply carried Kraepelin's "impulsive insanity" into his textbook. Enrico Morselli (1852 to 1929) named dysmorphophobia in 1891, the fear of ugliness now called body dysmorphic disorder (Chapter 6). Alistair Munro described monosymptomatic hypochondriacal psychosis in a series of papers in the 1970s and 1980s: a single, encapsulated hypochondriacal delusion, such as the conviction of being infested by parasites or of giving off a foul smell, which he found often responded to the antipsychotic pimozide. A second trap: the objects in kleptomania need not be worthless; they are simply not stolen for their use or their value. And unlike the fire setter, the person with kleptomania usually does not plan the theft and always steals alone.

Kleptomania: explanations and treatment

Why would anyone steal what they do not want? The psychoanalysts gave the first answers, reading the stolen object as a symbol: a substitute for love that was missing, compensation for a loss, an act with a hidden sexual meaning. Such accounts are hard to test, and they have faded. Two modern ideas have more support. The first treats the theft as a way of regulating mood. Kleptomania is strongly comorbid with mood disorders, and some writers have called it an "antidepressant" behaviour, an act that briefly lifts an unpleasant feeling (Barlow and Durand cite Fishbain, 1987). The second idea treats kleptomania as close kin to the addictions. The urge to steal behaves like a craving: it is set off by cues (the open counter, the crowded aisle), it builds, it is relieved by the act, and the act brings a "rush". People with kleptomania score high on measures of impulsivity, reflecting their difficulty in weighing the immediate gratification of stealing against its long-term costs of arrest and shame (Grant and Kim, 2002). DSM-5-TR notes that the neurotransmitter pathways involved in the behavioural addictions, serotonin, dopamine and the brain's own opioids, appear to play a part in kleptomania as well, and one small imaging study found abnormalities in the white matter of the inferior frontal regions, areas associated with decision making. The addiction idea led directly to the best-tested treatment.

Key study: naltrexone for kleptomania (Grant, Kim and Odlaug, 2009)

Naltrexone blocks opioid receptors, and in alcohol dependence it is thought to blunt the "high" that drinking brings (Chapter 10, Section 10.4). If the rush of stealing also runs through the brain's opioid system, Jon Grant, Suck Won Kim and Brian L. Odlaug at the University of Minnesota reasoned, naltrexone should weaken the urge to steal. They randomly assigned 25 adults with kleptomania to naltrexone (50 to 150 mg a day) or a placebo for 8 weeks, under double-blind conditions, and 23 of them completed the trial. Those on naltrexone showed significantly greater reductions in the urge to steal, in actual stealing and in overall severity than those on placebo; the mean effective dose was about 117 mg a day, and the drug was well tolerated. Before it, as the authors pointed out, kleptomania had no empirically validated treatment at all. Its limits are clear: it was small and short, and at doses above those used for alcohol the liver has to be monitored.

The serotonin reuptake inhibitors have a weaker record. Several case series and open trials reported improvement, but in the most informative study (Lorrin M. Koran and colleagues at Stanford, 2007, with 24 patients), 79 percent improved on open-label escitalopram, and when the responders were then randomly switched to placebo or kept on the drug, relapse was about as common on escitalopram (43 percent) as on placebo (50 percent); the SSRI added nothing that could be shown. No medicine is approved for kleptomania. Psychological treatment is cognitive-behavioural, and most of the evidence comes from case reports. The classic technique is covert sensitisation, in which the person imagines the build-up to a theft and then imagines vividly its worst consequences, being caught, handcuffed and named in the newspaper, so that the urge becomes linked with aversion rather than relief (Book 2, Section 4.4.3 explains the method). Imaginal desensitisation, exposure to the shop with the urge resisted, and the relapse-prevention methods of addiction treatment (Chapter 10) are also used. As Grant and Odlaug pointed out in 2008, there have been no controlled trials of any psychotherapy for kleptomania.

Indian context

Fire setting and theft are crimes in India as everywhere, and a diagnosis of pyromania or kleptomania does not by itself excuse them. The insanity defence, now section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 (formerly section 84 of the Indian Penal Code), protects only a person who, because of unsoundness of mind, was incapable of knowing the nature of the act or that it was wrong or contrary to law (Book 2, Section 10.9.4). People with kleptomania, by definition, know that the act is wrong; Indian law, unlike some American states, has no "irresistible impulse" test. The diagnosis matters instead for treatment, for how families and employers respond, and sometimes in sentencing.

You now have the three disorders side by side, and Table 11.38 sets them out in the form that questions most often take: what is done, what precedes it, what follows it, whether it is planned, and what rules it out.

Table 11.38 The three DSM-5-TR impulse-control disorders compared.
FeatureIntermittent explosive disorderPyromaniaKleptomania
The actVerbal or physical aggressive outburstsDeliberate fire setting, more than onceStealing objects not needed for use or value
Before the actAnger rising within seconds; tension not requiredTension or arousal; fascination with fireIncreasing tension immediately before
During or afterOften distress and remorse afterwardsPleasure, gratification or reliefPleasure, gratification or relief; then guilt
Planned?Never premeditatedOften prepared in advanceUsually not planned; done alone
Key exclusionsTangible goal; DMDD; mood, psychotic, medical or substance causeMoney, revenge, ideology, concealment; psychosis; impaired judgementAnger or vengeance; delusion or hallucination
Not better explained byOther disorders listed in Criterion FConduct disorder, mania, antisocial PDConduct disorder, mania, antisocial PD
Who and how manyAbout 2.6 percent a year (US); onset late childhood or adolescenceVery rare; onset late adolescence; sex ratio unknown0.3 to 0.6 percent; women 3 to 1
Exam trap

The only drug with a positive placebo-controlled trial in kleptomania is the opioid antagonist naltrexone, not an SSRI. Kleptomania, pyromania and intermittent explosive disorder do not need a comorbid mood disorder, although depression is common in all three. A thief who steals out of anger or revenge, or a manic patient who walks out of a shop with goods, does not have kleptomania, and the knowledge that stealing is wrong, which people with kleptomania have, is exactly why the diagnosis does not make out an insanity defence in Indian law.

The wider family: habit and impulse disorders in ICD-10, impulse control disorders in ICD-11

The two international classifications draw the family differently from DSM-5-TR, and the differences are tested. ICD-10 (1992) placed the impulse disorders among the "disorders of adult personality and behaviour" (F60 to F69), in a block called habit and impulse disorders (F63). Its definition is worth reading slowly, because it is unusually honest. These are disorders characterised by "repeated acts that have no clear rational motivation, cannot be controlled, and generally harm the patient's own interests and those of other people", which the patient reports are associated with impulses to act; their cause is not understood, and they are grouped together "because of broad descriptive similarities, not because they are known to share any other important features". Habitual excessive use of alcohol or drugs, and impulse and habit disorders of sexual behaviour, are excluded. The block contains pathological gambling (F63.0), pathological fire-setting [pyromania] (F63.1), pathological stealing [kleptomania] (F63.2), trichotillomania (F63.3), other habit and impulse disorders (F63.8) and an unspecified category (F63.9). Intermittent explosive disorder has no code of its own in ICD-10; it is listed as an inclusion term under F63.8. Notice ICD-10's own names. Its definition of pathological fire-setting speaks of multiple acts or attempts at setting fire to property without apparent motive, a persistent preoccupation with fire, tension before and intense excitement after, and it excludes fire setting by an adult with dissocial personality disorder, in conduct disorder, in intoxication, in organic mental disorders and in schizophrenia. Its pathological stealing excludes stealing in a depressive disorder.

ICD-11 (2022) rebuilt the block. Its impulse control disorders (6C70 to 6C7Z) are "characterised by the repeated failure to resist an impulse, drive, or urge to perform an act that is rewarding to the person, at least in the short-term, despite consequences such as longer-term harm either to the individual or to others, marked distress about the behaviour pattern, or significant impairment" in personal, family, social, educational, occupational or other important areas of functioning. Read that definition beside DSM-IV's: the key new word is "rewarding". The block has four named members, pyromania (6C70), kleptomania (6C71), compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72) and intermittent explosive disorder (6C73), with other specified (6C7Y) and unspecified (6C7Z) categories. ICD-11's intermittent explosive disorder is described without DSM-5-TR's frequency thresholds, as repeated brief episodes of verbal or physical aggression or destruction of property, grossly out of proportion to the provocation, that are not part of a pattern of chronic anger and irritability (such as oppositional defiant disorder) and that cause significant impairment.

Equally important is what ICD-11 sends elsewhere, with a "coded elsewhere" note. Gambling disorder (6C50) and gaming disorder (6C51) are disorders due to addictive behaviours (Chapter 10, Section 10.1.7). Trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are body-focused repetitive behaviour disorders (6B25) in the obsessive-compulsive group (Chapter 6, Section 6.6.5). Oppositional defiant disorder (6C90) and conduct-dissocial disorder (6C91) form a separate block of disruptive behaviour or dissocial disorders; unlike DSM-5-TR, ICD-11 does not put them in the same block as the impulse disorders. And ICD-11 has a category for impulse symptoms that are the direct physiological effect of a medical condition, secondary impulse control syndrome (6E66): prominent stealing, fire setting, aggressive outbursts, compulsive sexual behaviour or excessive gambling judged to be the direct result of a health condition outside the mental disorders, such as a brain injury or a neurological disease, and recorded alongside that condition. How directly brain chemistry can produce such behaviour is shown by the dopamine-boosting medicines used for Parkinson's disease, which can set off pathological gambling (Section 10.1.7) and other impulsive behaviour in people who never had it before.

The newcomer is compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72): a persistent pattern of failure to control intense, repetitive sexual impulses or urges, resulting in repetitive sexual behaviour that has become a central focus of life, that the person has repeatedly and unsuccessfully tried to reduce, and that continues despite adverse consequences and even when it brings little satisfaction, over an extended period (for example 6 months or more), with marked distress or impairment. Distress that comes entirely from moral judgements and disapproval of sexual impulses or behaviour does not count. ICD-11 deliberately placed it among the impulse control disorders, not among the paraphilic disorders (its content is usually conventional) and not among the addictive behaviours. DSM-5-TR has no such diagnosis: the proposal for a "hypersexual disorder" made for DSM-5 by Martin Kafka in 2010 was not adopted. Chapter 9, Section 9.5.7 tells this story in full.

One more urge has knocked at the door of both manuals without being let in: compulsive buying, repeated and uncontrollable shopping that leads to distress, debt and family conflict. Kraepelin described it in his textbook under the name oniomania (buying mania), Bleuler described it after him, and it is common: a 2004 American telephone survey of 2,513 adults put its point prevalence at 5.8 percent, and in the inpatient study by Grant and his colleagues it was the commonest impulse problem of all (9.3 percent). In 1994 Susan L. McElroy and her colleagues described 20 compulsive buyers; 19 of them had a lifetime diagnosis of a major mood disorder, and the authors proposed operational criteria so that the condition could be studied as a disorder in its own right. It is still not a diagnosis with its own code and criteria in either manual. DSM-5-TR mentions compulsive buying only as a frequent companion of kleptomania; ICD-11 lists "compulsive buying-shopping disorder" as an example under other specified impulse control disorders (6C7Y), so that is where it is coded, although some researchers argue that it belongs with the addictive behaviours instead. Table 11.39 closes the section by tracing every member of the family, past and present, through the four manuals.

Table 11.39 The impulse-control family through four manuals.

Exam trap

ICD-11's impulse control disorders are exactly four: pyromania (6C70), kleptomania (6C71), compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72) and intermittent explosive disorder (6C73). Gambling and gaming are addictive behaviours (6C50, 6C51); trichotillomania is a body-focused repetitive behaviour disorder (6B25). ICD-10 called the group habit and impulse disorders (F63), used the names "pathological fire-setting" and "pathological stealing", and still listed gambling (F63.0) and trichotillomania (F63.3) in it; intermittent explosive disorder sat under F63.8. DSM-5-TR has no compulsive sexual behaviour disorder, and ICD-11 puts it among the impulse control disorders, not the paraphilic disorders or the addictions.

The case of the St Louis children, revisited

Read Lee Robins's results again with this chapter behind you, and each of them now has a name. The adults she called sociopathic personalities are what DSM-5-TR calls antisocial personality disorder (Section 11.5): a pervasive pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others, counted from age 15 and shown by three or more of seven features. Those features are behavioural because DSM-III (1980) built them, as Robins had built her nineteen areas of life, from things that can be checked: arrests, fights, debts, abandoned jobs. Her finding that not one sociopathic adult had reached adulthood without marked antisocial behaviour in childhood became the rule that the adult diagnosis needs evidence of conduct disorder with onset before 15, in a person at least 18 years old. And her best predictor, the number and variety of a child's antisocial acts rather than any single one of them, is the logic of the conduct disorder criteria (Section 11.9): three or more of fifteen, drawn from four groups, with severity graded by the number of problems and the harm done.

The other half of her paradox, that most antisocial children do not become antisocial adults, is the subject of Sections 11.9.4 to 11.9.7. In a majority of children conduct disorder remits by adulthood. What predicts that it will not is exactly what a clinic file can record: problems that began early (the childhood-onset type, with a symptom before 10), callous-unemotional traits (the specifier with limited prosocial emotions), and what Terrie Moffitt (1993) called the life-course-persistent path, rare and rooted in neuropsychological deficits and adverse environments, as against the far commoner antisocial behaviour that is limited to adolescence. The St Louis children also illustrate the general definition with which the chapter began (Section 11.1): a personality disorder is not an episode but an enduring pattern that can be traced back at least to adolescence. The files that were about to be destroyed held the childhood half of hundreds of adult lives, and Robins's achievement was to see that a personality disorder can only be understood across the whole of a life.

Chapter summary

11.1 What a Personality Disorder Is. A personality disorder is an enduring pattern of inner experience and behaviour that deviates markedly from the expectations of the person's culture in two or more of four areas (cognition, affectivity, interpersonal functioning and impulse control), is inflexible and pervasive, stable and long-standing with onset by adolescence or early adulthood, and causes distress or impairment; its traits are usually ego-syntonic. Under 18 the features must have lasted a year, and antisocial personality disorder is never diagnosed before 18. The idea runs from Pinel's manie sans délire (1801) and Prichard's moral insanity (1835) through Schneider (1923) to Freud's anal character and Reich's character armour; DSM-III (1980) placed the disorders on Axis II. DSM-5-TR has ten disorders in three clusters, A (odd or eccentric), B (dramatic, emotional or erratic) and C (anxious or fearful), with no Cluster D. The median prevalence is 10.5 percent, and Torgersen's twins gave a heritability of .60.

11.2 Cluster A: Paranoid, Schizoid and Schizotypal Personality Disorders. The odd or eccentric disorders share social distance and a kinship with schizophrenia. Paranoid personality disorder (four of seven criteria) is pervasive distrust and suspiciousness without delusional conviction; Cameron's paranoid pseudo-community (1943) and Lemert's corrective (1962) show how suspicion creates real enemies. Schizoid personality disorder (four of seven) is detachment from social relationships with restricted emotional expression: the schizoid person does not want closeness, the avoidant person wants it but fears rejection, a split Millon brought into DSM-III. Schizotypal personality disorder (five of nine) adds acute discomfort in close relationships, ideas of reference, magical thinking, odd speech and eccentric behaviour. Rado's schizotype (1953), Meehl's schizotaxia, schizotypy and schizophrenia (1962) and the Danish adoption studies of Kety, Rosenthal and Wender placed it in the schizophrenia spectrum, which is why ICD-10 and ICD-11 class it with schizophrenia (F21, 6A22).

11.3 Borderline Personality Disorder I: The Clinical Picture. Borderline personality disorder is a pervasive pattern of instability of interpersonal relationships, self-image and affects, with marked impulsivity, shown by five or more of nine criteria: frantic efforts to avoid abandonment, relationships swinging between idealisation and devaluation, identity disturbance, self-damaging impulsivity, recurrent suicidal behaviour or self-mutilation, affective instability from marked reactivity of mood, chronic emptiness, intense anger, and transient stress-related paranoid ideation or dissociation. Stern named the "border line" group (1938), Kernberg described borderline personality organisation (1967), Grinker made the first empirical study (1968), and Gunderson and Singer (1975) and Spitzer, Endicott and Gibbon (1979) led to the DSM-III criteria. It is diagnosed more often in women in clinical samples, though not in the community, and carries a real suicide risk. Its mood shifts within hours, unlike bipolar episodes, and it must be told apart from the other Cluster B disorders, complex PTSD, an adolescent identity problem and conduct disorder.

11.4 Borderline Personality Disorder II: Defences, Causes, Course and Treatment. Splitting, the keeping apart of all-good and all-bad images of self and others, comes from Klein and is the defence Kernberg placed at the centre of borderline organisation, supported by projective identification, primitive idealisation, devaluation, omnipotence and primitive denial; in a ward it appears as staff splitting (Main, 1957). Repression, sublimation, rationalisation, regression and undoing are not the borderline answer. Kernberg's conflict model contrasts with the deficit models of Kohut (failed mirroring and idealising, a fragmented self) and of Adler and Buie (aloneness). Linehan's biosocial theory (1993) has emotional vulnerability transacting with an invalidating environment; Fonagy and Bateman trace the disorder to failed mentalisation. Abuse raises the risk but is neither necessary nor sufficient. The McLean and CLPS follow-ups showed that most patients remit. Psychotherapy (DBT, MBT, TFP, schema therapy, STEPPS, good psychiatric management) comes first; no drug is licensed for the disorder.

11.5 Antisocial Personality Disorder and Psychopathy. Antisocial personality disorder is a pervasive pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others since age 15, shown by three or more of seven features (unlawful acts, deceitfulness, impulsivity, aggression, recklessness, irresponsibility, lack of remorse), in a person at least 18 with evidence of conduct disorder before 15. The concept runs from Pinel and Prichard to Cleckley's The Mask of Sanity (1941) and Robert Hare's PCL-R (1991, 20 items, two factors and four facets), the instrument Cleckley never built. DSM-III's behavioural criteria drew on Robins; ICD-10 calls the disorder dissocial. Psychopathy and the disorder overlap without coinciding; Karpman separated primary from secondary psychopaths, and Patrick and colleagues proposed the triarchic model. Lykken's fearlessness (1957), Raine's underarousal, Eysenck's poor conditioning and Cadoret's gene-environment interaction explain it. It is about three times commoner in men, often fades by about 40, and is no insanity defence in Indian law.

11.6 Histrionic and Narcissistic Personality Disorders. Histrionic personality disorder descends from hysteria: Chodoff and Lyons (1958) separated the personality from conversion, and DSM-III made "histrionic" official. Its core is excessive emotionality and attention seeking (five of eight), with seductive behaviour, shallow, rapidly shifting emotion, impressionistic speech, suggestibility and relationships seen as more intimate than they are; Kaplan (1983) and Ford and Widiger (1989) raised the question of sex bias. Narcissistic personality disorder takes its name from Narcissus through Ellis, Näcke and Freud (1914), and Kohut named it (1968). Its core triad is grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of empathy (five of nine); Gabbard and Wink separated grandiose from vulnerable narcissism. Kohut read it as a deficit to be met with empathy, Kernberg as a pathological grandiose self to be confronted. Asking what each person wants from others separates the Cluster B disorders: the admiration seeker who is emotional and seductive, not grandiose, is histrionic.

11.7 Cluster C: Avoidant, Dependent and Obsessive-Compulsive Personality Disorders. In each anxious or fearful disorder a strategy against fear brings short-term relief and long-term cost. Avoidant personality disorder (four of seven) is social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy and hypersensitivity to negative evaluation: the person wants relationships but fears rejection, unlike the indifferent schizoid, and may share a continuum with social anxiety disorder. Dependent personality disorder (five of eight) is an excessive need to be taken care of, with submissive, clinging behaviour and fear of separation; Bornstein linked it to overprotective and authoritarian parenting, and Neki and Roland warned against reading Indian family closeness as disorder. Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (four of eight) is preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and control at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency, driven by a need for control and descended from Freud's anal character. Unlike OCD it is ego-syntonic with limited insight. Cluster C responds reasonably well to psychotherapy (Svartberg, Emmelkamp, Bamelis).

11.8 The Newer Models: Dimensions, ICD-11 and the DSM-5 Alternative Model. Ten categorical boxes produce artificial comorbidity, heterogeneity within a diagnosis, arbitrary thresholds and a crowded residual category (Verheul and Widiger, 2004), and Widiger and Costa (1994) recast the disorders as extremes of the five-factor model. ICD-11, in effect from 1 January 2022 and built by the working group Tyrer chaired, abolished the types: one personality disorder (6D10) graded mild, moderate or severe, with personality difficulty below it, five trait domain qualifiers (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia) and a borderline pattern qualifier. The DSM-5 Alternative Model, kept in Section III, rates Criterion A, the level of personality functioning (identity, self-direction, empathy, intimacy), from 0 to 4, and Criterion B, five pathological trait domains (negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, psychoticism) with 25 facets, and retains six types: antisocial, avoidant, borderline, narcissistic, obsessive-compulsive and schizotypal.

11.9 Oppositional Defiant and Conduct Disorders. DSM-5 created the chapter of disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders, problems of emotional and behavioural self-control. Oppositional defiant disorder is angry or irritable mood, argumentative or defiant behaviour or vindictiveness, four or more symptoms for at least 6 months, graded by the number of settings. Conduct disorder is a repetitive and persistent violation of others' basic rights or major age-appropriate norms: three or more of 15 criteria in 12 months, from four groups (aggression to people and animals, destruction of property, deceitfulness or theft, serious violations of rules). The childhood-onset type (a symptom before 10) predicts persistence, and the specifier with limited prosocial emotions, built on Frick's callous-unemotional traits, belongs to conduct disorder alone. Moffitt (1993) separated life-course-persistent from adolescence-limited antisocial behaviour; Patterson's coercive family process and Dodge's hostile attribution bias explain part of the cause. Most children remit, but adult antisocial personality requires conduct disorder before 15.

11.10 Impulse-Control Disorders: Intermittent Explosive Disorder, Pyromania and Kleptomania. An impulse-control disorder is a failure to resist an urge to a harmful act, often following the impulse cycle: tension, then pleasure, gratification or relief, then sometimes regret. The names grew from monomania: Matthey's klopémanie (1816), Marc's pyromanie (1833) and Esquirol and Marc's kleptomanie (1838). Intermittent explosive disorder is recurrent impulsive verbal or physical aggression grossly out of proportion to the provocation, from age 6, linked to low serotonin (Linnoila, 1983) and treated with anger-focused CBT and fluoxetine. Pyromania is deliberate, purposeful fire setting on more than one occasion for its own gratification, not for money, revenge or ideology, and is very rare. Kleptomania is stealing objects not needed for personal use or their value, with tension before and relief after; only naltrexone has a positive controlled trial. ICD-10 groups the habit and impulse disorders (F63); ICD-11 has four impulse control disorders, including compulsive sexual behaviour disorder.
