From the past papers

  1. Personality disorders are stable long-term conditions which can affect an individual's: (RML 2021, real)

    (a) Cognition (b) Mood (c) Interpersonal functioning (d) All of the above

    Answer: (d) All of the above. The general definition requires the enduring pattern to show in two or more of four areas: cognition, affectivity (mood and emotion), interpersonal functioning and impulse control (Section 11.1.2). Choosing (c) alone treats personality disorders as relationship problems only, and choosing (a) or (b) alone forgets that the definition spans all four areas.

  2. Identify the 'odd one out' from the groups: (Amity 2023, real)

    (a) Paranoid personality disorder (b) Schizoid personality disorder (c) Schizotypal personality disorder (d) Borderline personality disorder

    Answer: (d) Borderline personality disorder. Paranoid, schizoid and schizotypal personality disorder make up Cluster A, the odd or eccentric cluster; borderline belongs to Cluster B, the dramatic, emotional or erratic cluster (Sections 11.1.4 and 11.2.1). Schizotypal is the option that might tempt, because ICD-10 and ICD-11 class it with schizophrenia, but in DSM-5-TR it is a Cluster A personality disorder.

  3. DSM-5 groups the personality disorders into three clusters. Which cluster is described as the 'dramatic, emotional, or erratic' cluster and includes antisocial, borderline, histrionic, and narcissistic personality disorders? (Flight Mock 100, practice)

    (a) Cluster A (b) Cluster B (c) Cluster C (d) Cluster D

    Answer: (b) Cluster B. Cluster A is odd or eccentric (paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal) and Cluster C anxious or fearful (avoidant, dependent, obsessive-compulsive). There is no Cluster D, although it appears as an option in almost every question on the clusters (Section 11.1.4).

  4. Which of the following is NOT a Cluster A personality disorder? (MET Mock 1, practice)

    (a) Paranoid personality disorder (b) Avoidant personality disorder (c) Schizotypal personality disorder (d) Schizoid personality disorder

    Answer: (b) Avoidant personality disorder. Avoidant personality disorder is Cluster C, anxious or fearful (Section 11.7.1). The avoidant person ends up as isolated as the schizoid person, which is the bait, but wants relationships and avoids them for fear of rejection, while the schizoid person does not want them at all (Section 11.2.5).

  5. Borderline personality disorder is primarily characterised by (JEMAS 2025, real)

    (a) Detachment and social withdrawal (b) Emotional instability and impulsivity (c) Obsessive perfectionism (d) Paranoid ideation

    Answer: (b) Emotional instability and impulsivity. DSM-5-TR defines borderline personality disorder as a pervasive pattern of instability of interpersonal relationships, self-image and affects, with marked impulsivity (Section 11.3.2). Detachment and social withdrawal describe schizoid personality disorder, obsessive perfectionism describes OCPD, and paranoid ideation as the main feature is paranoid personality disorder; in borderline personality disorder paranoid ideation is only transient and stress-related (criterion 9).

  6. Which of the following disorders often involves identity disturbance and fear of abandonment? (IHBAS 2025, real)

    (a) Schizoid personality disorder (b) Histrionic personality disorder (c) Borderline personality disorder (d) Antisocial personality disorder

    Answer: (c) Borderline personality disorder. Frantic efforts to avoid real or imagined abandonment and identity disturbance, a markedly and persistently unstable self-image, are criteria 1 and 3 of borderline personality disorder (Section 11.3.3). The schizoid person is indifferent to relationships; the histrionic person seeks attention, but identity disturbance, emptiness and self-harm are not histrionic criteria; and the antisocial person disregards others' rights rather than fearing their loss.

  7. Which of the following is NOT considered a cardinal feature of Borderline Personality Disorder (BPD)? (Amity 2023, real)

    (a) Lack of a well-defined and stable self-image (b) Regular and predictable changes in mood (c) Eccentric behaviour marked by oddities in thinking and communication (d) Enduring pattern of instability in relationships

    Answer: (b) Regular and predictable changes in mood (keyed). Borderline mood is reactive and unpredictable, shifting within hours in response to events (criterion 6), so "regular and predictable" is the direct inversion of a criterion (Section 11.3.4). But (c) is not a borderline feature either: eccentric behaviour with oddities of thinking and communication is schizotypal personality disorder (Section 11.2.6). Two options are strictly correct; mark the keyed (b) and know why (c) is also wrong. Options (a) and (d) are criteria 3 and 2.

  8. Borderline personality disorder requires how many of nine criteria for diagnosis? (MET Mock 2, practice)

    (a) 3 (b) 4 (c) 5 (d) 6

    Answer: (c) 5. Five or more of the nine criteria are needed (Section 11.3.2). The distractors are other disorders' thresholds: 3 (of 7) is antisocial, and 4 is paranoid, schizoid and avoidant (of 7) and obsessive-compulsive (of 8); no personality disorder needs 6.

  9. Which of the following is a core feature of Borderline Personality Disorder that is NOT typically a defining feature of Narcissistic Personality Disorder? (Real PYQ 2024, real)

    (a) Grandiose sense of self-importance (b) Pervasive instability in interpersonal relationships and self-image (c) Need for excessive admiration (d) Belief that one is "special" and can only be understood by other high-status people

    Answer: (b) Pervasive instability in interpersonal relationships and self-image. Instability of relationships and self-image is the core of borderline personality disorder (Section 11.3.7). Options (a), (c) and (d) are all among the nine criteria of narcissistic personality disorder (Section 11.6.5), so they define the narcissist, not the borderline patient.

  10. Borderline Personality Disorder is most characteristically associated with which primary defense mechanism? (Real PYQ 2025, real)

    (a) Repression (b) Splitting (c) Sublimation (d) Rationalization

    Answer: (b) Splitting. Splitting, the keeping apart of all-good and all-bad images of self and others, comes from Melanie Klein and was placed at the centre of borderline personality organisation by Otto Kernberg; it explains the swing between idealisation and devaluation (Section 11.4.1). Repression is the core defence of the neurotic level, sublimation is a mature defence and rationalisation is a defence everyone uses; none of them is the primitive defence of the borderline level.

  11. Which of the following defense mechanism is seen in patients with borderline personality disorder? (RML 2024, real)

    (a) Splitting (b) Regression (c) Undoing (d) Sublimation

    Answer: (a) Splitting. The same answer with new distractors (Sections 11.4.1 and 11.4.2); the defences that accompany splitting in borderline personality disorder are projective identification, primitive idealisation, devaluation, omnipotence and primitive denial. Regression is a general retreat to earlier ways of functioning under stress, undoing is the obsessional defence of cancelling one act with another, and sublimation is mature.

  12. A client tells you that they are "no one" and feels "empty inside." They report chronic fears of abandonment and have a history of intense, unstable relationships. According to psychoanalytic object relations theory, this pathology of the self is most likely a result of: (Real PYQ 2024, real)

    (a) Fixation at the oral stage of psychosexual development (b) A failure in parental mirroring and idealizing, leading to a fragmented self (c) Unresolved Oedipal complex and castration anxiety (d) Harsh superego demands leading to constant guilt

    Answer: (b) A failure in parental mirroring and idealizing, leading to a fragmented self. The vignette is borderline, and the keyed answer is Heinz Kohut's: without a parent's mirroring and a parent to idealise, the child's self never becomes cohesive and stays fragmented and empty (Section 11.4.3). Strictly this is self psychology, a close relative of object relations theory (Klein, Fairbairn, Winnicott and Kernberg), so choose it but know the difference. The other options are classical Freudian accounts: oral fixation of dependency, depression and addiction, the Oedipus complex of neurosis, and the harsh superego of melancholia and obsessional neurosis.

  13. Dialectical Behaviour Therapy is indicated for the treatment of: (IHBAS 2020, real)

    (a) Anxiety Disorders (b) Borderline Personality Disorder (c) Acute and Transient Psychotic Disorder (d) Post-traumatic Stress Disorder

    Answer: (b) Borderline Personality Disorder. Marsha Linehan built dialectical behaviour therapy while treating chronically suicidal women, on her biosocial theory of borderline personality disorder (1993); it combines individual therapy, a skills group (mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, interpersonal effectiveness), telephone coaching and a consultation team, has more randomised trials than any other therapy for the disorder, and works most clearly on self-harm and suicide attempts (Section 11.4.8; Book 2, Section 5.8). Anxiety disorders, acute and transient psychotic disorder and PTSD belong to Chapters 6, 4 and 7, each with its own first-line treatment; DBT was not built for any of them.

  14. Which personality disorder is marked by manipulation, lack of remorse, and irresponsibility? (RML 2025, real)

    (a) Borderline personality disorder (b) Antisocial personality disorder (c) Dependent personality disorder (d) Narcissistic personality disorder

    Answer: (b) Antisocial personality disorder. Deceitfulness and manipulation, consistent irresponsibility and lack of remorse are three of the seven antisocial criteria, of which three are needed (Section 11.5.4). The borderline person may manipulate, but for nurturance and out of fear of abandonment; the dependent person is submissive and clinging; the narcissist exploits others for admiration but is not characteristically impulsive, aggressive or deceitful.

  15. Antisocial personality disorder cannot be diagnosed before what age, and requires evidence of conduct disorder before what age? (MET Mock 2, practice)

    (a) Diagnosed before 16; CD before 12 (b) Diagnosed at or after 18; evidence of CD before age 15 (c) Diagnosed before 18; CD before 15 (d) Diagnosed only after 21

    Answer: (b) Diagnosed at or after 18; evidence of CD before age 15. The person must be at least 18 and must have shown conduct disorder with onset before 15, and the pattern of disregard for others' rights is counted from 15 (Section 11.5.4, Figure 11.9). There is no rule about 12, 16 or 21, and a person under 18 with the pattern receives a diagnosis of conduct disorder instead.

  16. The PCL-R (Psychopathy Checklist-Revised) was developed by: (Biopsych + Testing paper, practice)

    (a) Hervey Cleckley alone, 1941 (b) Aaron Beck (c) Robert Hare (d) Hans Eysenck

    Answer: (c) Robert Hare. Robert D. Hare built the Psychopathy Checklist (1980) and the PCL-R (1991): 20 items scored 0, 1 or 2, maximum 40, usual cut-off 30, building on Cleckley's description (Section 11.5.6). Cleckley wrote The Mask of Sanity (1941) with its sixteen characteristics but built no checklist; Beck is cognitive therapy and the Beck Depression Inventory; Eysenck is the PEN model and an arousal theory of crime.

  17. A client reports a persistent pattern of seeking admiration, but their behavior is characterized by excessive emotionality, attention-seeking, and seductive behavior, rather than grandiosity. This presentation is more characteristic of: (Real PYQ 2024, real)

    (a) Narcissistic Personality Disorder (b) Histrionic Personality Disorder (c) Borderline Personality Disorder (d) Dependent Personality Disorder

    Answer: (b) Histrionic Personality Disorder. Excessive emotionality and attention seeking, with seductive or provocative behaviour, is the definition of histrionic personality disorder (five of eight criteria, Section 11.6.2). Grandiosity is the mark of the narcissist, so an admiration seeker without it is histrionic (Section 11.6.8); borderline personality disorder would need instability, abandonment fears or self-harm, and dependent personality disorder submissiveness and clinging.

  18. Individuals with obsessive-compulsive personality disorder are primarily motivated by a need for which of the following? (RML 2022, real)

    (a) Efficiency (b) Achievement (c) Control (d) Autonomy

    Answer: (c) Control. DSM-5-TR defines OCPD as a preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and mental and interpersonal control at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency (Section 11.7.5). Efficiency is what the disorder sacrifices; achievement suffers because perfectionism stops tasks being finished (working for admiration is closer to narcissism); and autonomy is bait from Erikson's second stage, which matches Freud's anal stage, but the person with OCPD is bound by rules, not seeking freedom.

  19. Compared to obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD) is characterised by symptoms that are typically: (PYQ Mock 2, practice)

    (a) Ego-dystonic and accompanied by significant insight (b) Ego-syntonic with limited insight (c) Only present during manic episodes (d) Acute in onset and rapidly remitting

    Answer: (b) Ego-syntonic with limited insight. In OCPD the rules, the perfectionism and the need for control feel right and part of the self, and there are no true obsessions or compulsions; in OCD the obsessions are intrusive and ego-dystonic (Section 11.7.6). Option (a) describes OCD, and (c) and (d) contradict the definition of every personality disorder, an enduring pattern that begins by adolescence or early adulthood.

  20. The DSM-5-TR specifier 'with limited prosocial emotions' is applied to which diagnosis? (PYQ Mock 2, practice)

    (a) Antisocial personality disorder (b) Conduct disorder (c) Oppositional defiant disorder (d) Intermittent explosive disorder

    Answer: (b) Conduct disorder. The specifier belongs to conduct disorder only: two or more of lack of remorse or guilt, callous lack of empathy, unconcern about performance and shallow or deficient affect, persistently for at least 12 months in more than one relationship or setting (Section 11.9.4). Antisocial personality disorder is the tempting answer, but its Section III counterpart is a different specifier, "with psychopathic features"; oppositional defiant and intermittent explosive disorder have no such specifier.

  21. The hallmark difference between pyromania and intermittent explosive disorder (IED) is: (PYQ Mock 2, practice)

    (a) IED involves only verbal aggression, never physical (b) Pyromania involves planned, deliberate fire-setting for gratification; IED involves impulsive aggression disproportionate to the trigger (c) Pyromania is diagnosed only in adolescents; IED only in adults (d) Both require a comorbid mood disorder

    Answer: (b). Pyromania is deliberate, purposeful fire setting on more than one occasion, often prepared in advance, with tension before and pleasure, gratification or relief after; IED is recurrent impulsive aggression grossly out of proportion to the provocation (Sections 11.10.3 and 11.10.5). IED aggression may be verbal or physical and can be diagnosed from age 6, pyromania has no age limit, and neither needs a comorbid mood disorder.

  22. The term 'kleptomania' was given by: (RML 2024, real)

    (a) Marc (b) Bleuler (c) Munro (d) Morselli

    Answer: (a) Marc. Charles Chrétien Henri Marc, with Jean-Étienne Esquirol, renamed André Matthey's klopémanie (1816) as kleptomanie in 1838; Marc had already coined pyromanie in 1833 (Sections 11.10.2 and 11.10.6). Eugen Bleuler coined schizophrenia, ambivalence and autism; Alistair Munro described monosymptomatic hypochondriacal psychosis; Enrico Morselli named dysmorphophobia (1891).

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Borderline personality disorder is a pervasive pattern of instability of interpersonal relationships, self-image and affects, with marked impulsivity, beginning by early adulthood; not detachment (schizoid), obsessive perfectionism (OCPD), pervasive paranoid ideation (paranoid) or grandiosity (narcissistic). asked 4 times
  2. There are three clusters and no Cluster D: A, odd or eccentric (paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal); B, dramatic, emotional or erratic (antisocial, borderline, histrionic, narcissistic); C, anxious or fearful (avoidant, dependent, obsessive-compulsive). So borderline is the odd one out among the Cluster A disorders, and avoidant is Cluster C, not A. asked 4 times
  3. The nine borderline criteria: frantic efforts to avoid real or imagined abandonment; unstable, intense relationships alternating between idealisation and devaluation; identity disturbance; impulsivity in at least two self-damaging areas; recurrent suicidal behaviour or self-mutilation; affective instability from marked reactivity of mood; chronic emptiness; intense anger; transient stress-related paranoid ideation or severe dissociation. asked 3 times
  4. Splitting (self and others seen as all good or all bad) is the characteristic defence of borderline personality disorder: Melanie Klein's idea, placed at the centre of borderline personality organisation by Otto Kernberg (1967). asked 2 times
  5. Not the borderline defence: repression (the neurotic level), sublimation (mature), rationalisation (used by everyone), regression (a general retreat under stress), undoing (obsessional). The companions of splitting are projective identification, primitive idealisation, devaluation, omnipotence and primitive denial. asked 2 times
  6. Histrionic personality disorder: excessive emotionality and attention seeking (five of eight), with seductive or provocative behaviour, shallow, rapidly shifting emotion, impressionistic speech and suggestibility. The admiration seeker who is emotional and seductive rather than grandiose is histrionic, not narcissistic. asked 2 times
  7. A personality disorder shows in two or more of four areas, cognition, affectivity, interpersonal functioning and impulse control, so it can affect cognition, mood and interpersonal functioning alike ("all of the above"). asked once
  8. Borderline mood is reactive and unpredictable, shifting within hours (criterion 6, affective instability due to marked reactivity of mood): "regular and predictable changes in mood" is not a borderline feature, and eccentric behaviour with odd thinking and communication is schizotypal. asked once
  9. What borderline has that narcissistic lacks is pervasive instability of relationships and self-image; grandiosity, need for admiration and the belief in being special and understood only by high-status people are narcissistic criteria. asked once
  10. Heinz Kohut's self psychology: failure of parental mirroring and idealising leaves a fragmented, empty self (the keyed answer to the "no one, empty inside" vignette, which the paper calls object relations theory); object relations theory proper is Klein, Fairbairn, Winnicott and Kernberg. asked once
  11. Dialectical behaviour therapy (Marsha Linehan, on her biosocial theory of 1993: emotional vulnerability transacting with an invalidating environment) is the treatment indicated for borderline personality disorder; its clearest effect is on self-harm and suicide attempts. asked once
  12. Antisocial personality disorder: deceitfulness and manipulation, consistent irresponsibility and lack of remorse are three of its seven criteria (three or more needed). asked once
  13. OCPD is a preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and mental and interpersonal control at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency; its motive is a need for control, not efficiency, achievement or autonomy. asked once
  14. The term kleptomania was given by Charles Chrétien Henri Marc with Jean-Étienne Esquirol (kleptomanie, 1838); Matthey's earlier word was klopémanie (1816), and Marc also coined pyromanie (1833). asked once
  15. The kleptomania distractors: Bleuler coined schizophrenia, ambivalence and autism; Morselli named dysmorphophobia (1891); Munro described monosymptomatic hypochondriacal psychosis. asked once
  16. Antisocial personality disorder needs an age of at least 18, evidence of conduct disorder with onset before 15, and a pattern of disregard for others' rights since 15 (not 12, 16 or 21). asked 2 times
  17. The DSM-5 core triad of narcissistic personality disorder is grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of empathy (five of nine criteria). asked 2 times
  18. "With limited prosocial emotions" is a specifier for conduct disorder only (not antisocial personality, oppositional defiant or intermittent explosive disorder): two or more of lack of remorse or guilt, callous lack of empathy, unconcern about performance and shallow or deficient affect, for at least 12 months, in more than one relationship or setting. asked 2 times
  19. Borderline personality disorder versus conduct disorder: identity and affective instability with fear of abandonment versus rule violation and aggression (cruelty to animals, serious rule breaking). Conduct disorder before 15 is the precursor of antisocial, not borderline, personality disorder. asked 2 times
  20. Thresholds: borderline 5 of 9; antisocial 3 of 7; paranoid, schizoid and avoidant 4 of 7; obsessive-compulsive 4 of 8; schizotypal and narcissistic 5 of 9; histrionic and dependent 5 of 8; none needs 6. asked once
  21. Compared with OCD, OCPD is ego-syntonic with limited insight and has no true obsessions or compulsions; OCD is ego-dystonic. asked once
  22. The PCL-R was developed by Robert D. Hare (PCL 1980; PCL-R 1991, 20 items scored 0 to 2, maximum 40, cut-off 30), building on Hervey Cleckley's The Mask of Sanity (1941, sixteen characteristics). asked once
  23. Pyromania is deliberate, purposeful fire setting on more than one occasion for the gratification or relief fire brings (not money, revenge or ideology); intermittent explosive disorder is recurrent impulsive aggression, verbal or physical, grossly out of proportion to the provocation, from age 6. asked once
